# Ferro — A Rust Database Access Engine for PHP

**Status:** Draft v0.2 — implementation handoff · 2026-07-23
**Audience:** implementing agent (Claude Code) and maintainer review
**Scope:** engine daemon (`ferrod`), wire protocol, PHP clients (native, Doctrine DBAL, Eloquent), `ferro` CLI, packaging
**Supersedes:** v0.1. Deltas listed in §22.

---

## 0. How to read this document

- **MUST / SHOULD / MAY** are normative (RFC 2119 sense).
- §21 is the **decision log**. Decisions there are settled — do not re-litigate them in code review or refactors. Open items carry an interim default so nothing blocks implementation.
- Build in milestone order (§17). M0 has an explicit task order (§17.1); start there.
- The machine-readable protocol registry in `/proto` (§20.2) is the single source of truth for method ids, flags, error codes, and type tags. Both the Rust and PHP codecs generate constants from it. Any protocol change MUST update the registry, the golden vectors, and both codecs in the same change set.
- Where this spec is silent, prefer: correctness over throughput, explicit configuration over inference, and the smallest surface that turns the acceptance suites green.

## 1. Problem statement

PHP-FPM is shared-nothing: every worker owns its own database connections, prepared-statement caches, and TLS sessions. 200 workers means 200 upstream connections and zero cross-worker reuse. PDO, designed in 2004, compounds this: synchronous only, stringly typed, connection-oriented, and inconsistent across drivers.

Ferro is **not a database**. It is a per-host access engine: one Rust daemon that owns all upstream connections, pools them in transaction mode, multiplexes many PHP workers over a local socket, and exposes a modern typed API — while remaining a drop-in replacement for Doctrine DBAL and Laravel Eloquent so existing applications adopt it by changing configuration only.

**Prior art and positioning.** PgBouncer proves host-level transaction pooling and shares the session-state detection limits that §7.4 confronts explicitly. Prisma ran a Rust query engine out-of-process and later retreated in-process for operational reasons — but Prisma's engine served a single Node client and had no cross-process pooling upside; Ferro's entire point is N workers sharing one daemon, which is exactly the case where out-of-process pays. sqlx supplies the model for §11's compile-time checked queries. ProxySQL/RDS Proxy demonstrate the ops value of a protocol-aware middle tier; Ferro differs by owning **both** ends of the wire (client library and daemon), which is what makes tx-id pinning, typed hydration, and the manifest handshake possible.

## 2. Goals

1. **G1 — Pooling:** N workers share M real connections (M ≪ N) with transaction-mode pooling, correct pinning, and hygiene on release.
2. **G2 — Drop-in:** Doctrine DBAL driver and Laravel/Eloquent connector requiring only config changes. Acceptance: upstream test suites pass (§14, §15).
3. **G3 — Typed:** One canonical type system across backends; native API hydrates readonly PHP DTOs.
4. **G4 — Concurrency:** Request multiplexing; PHP Fibers enable parallel queries from one request. Sync fallback works everywhere.
5. **G5 — Checked SQL:** Build-time verification of queries against a shadow schema + DTO/stub codegen (sqlx-style).
6. **G6 — Observability & security:** Tracing, pool metrics, slow log; credentials isolated in the engine; optional manifest-only query mode.
7. **G7 — Predictable failure:** Engine restarts and backend flaps surface as a small, typed error set with defined client recovery (§9.2, §19). No silent ambiguity about the fate of a write, ever.

## 3. Non-goals

- Not a storage engine, not a SQL rewriter, not a cache of query results.
- No ORM in Rust: identity maps, lazy proxies, object graphs, PHP-closure middleware stay in PHP. The engine's job ends where live object semantics begin.
- No read/write inference from SQL text for replica routing in v1 — routing is explicit (§7.6).
- No transparent engine-side retry of user statements — the engine reports outcomes truthfully; retry is client policy (§19.3).
- Oracle deferred (blocking OCI wrapper on a `spawn_blocking` pool, post-v1). Windows native is TCP-only best-effort (§21 D4).

*[Amended 2026-10-06 (D22, §24): **one scoped exception.** The job transport engine (§24) composes a closed set of statements against an operator-declared table whose layout the application owns, and may write its rows on its own authority only as §24.2 I4 enumerates (in v1: restoring a reservation no client received, and purging expired dedup keys). That is a transport over application-owned storage, not storage owned by the engine; no user statement is read, rewritten or inferred from. Charter rule 6 reads with the same exception: engine-authored statements of the §24 queue verbs are not SQL rewriting, and the `ferro` queue driver is not a drop-in database tier under the rule's "change execution, never SQL generation" (it replaces `DatabaseQueue`'s builder-generated SQL by design; the drop-in database tiers keep stock SQL generation). **PENDING OWNER RATIFICATION:** D22 amends binding scope text, so no G-slice code (G1 onward) starts before the owner ratifies it.]*

## 4. Architecture overview

```
┌────────────── host ──────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PHP-FPM workers (N)                                          │
│   ├─ ferro/client        (native typed API, Fibers-aware)     │
│   ├─ ferro/doctrine-dbal (Driver impl → client)               │
│   └─ ferro/laravel       (Illuminate Connection → client)     │
│              │  UDS /run/ferro/{build-hash}.sock              │
│              ▼                                                │
│  ferrod (Rust, tokio)                                         │
│   ├─ session layer: handshake (epoch), auth, multiplexing,    │
│   │                 flow control, PING/PONG                   │
│   ├─ SQL service: param bind, result framing                  │
│   ├─ TX service: pin management, savepoints, retry hints      │
│   ├─ pin engine: protocol signals + assist lexer (§7.2)       │
│   ├─ pools: pg | mysql | sqlite | mssql  (+ replicas)         │
│   ├─ codegen manifest store (query_id → plan)                 │
│   └─ admin/metrics: OTLP, Prometheus, slow log                │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

One `ferrod` per host (or per pod). Clients hold one UDS connection per worker; all requests are multiplexed over it. Transactions pin engine-side to a `tx_id`, not to the client socket, so a Fiber-suspended request keeps its transaction across frames.

## 5. Transport and framing

**Transport:** Unix domain socket, path versioned by build: `/run/ferro/{schema_hash}.sock`. Deploys with changed contracts spawn a fresh daemon instance; the old one drains and exits on idle TTL (§18). TCP loopback fallback (`FERRO_ADDR`) for containers without shared sockets and for Windows dev (D4).

**Frame header** — 16 bytes, little-endian, followed by a MessagePack payload:

| field | type | notes |
|---|---|---|
| magic | u8 | `0xF7` |
| version | u8 | protocol major |
| flags | u16 | `STREAM`, `END`, `CANCEL`, `OOB_FD`, `COMPRESSED` (reserved, D11) |
| service | u16 | `01` core · `02` sql · `03` tx · `04` stream · `05` admin |
| method | u16 | per-service method id — registry in `/proto/methods.toml` |
| request_id | u32 | client-assigned, multiplexing key (§5.2) |
| payload_len | u32 | MessagePack body length |

**Handshake:** client `HELLO {client_version, type_registry_hash, manifest_hash?, pid, features}` → server `HELLO_ACK {engine_version, boot_epoch, features, pools[], type_registry_hash}`. Since M1-S8a `pools[]` is not a bare name list: each element is a positional triple `[name, kind, server_version]` — the backend FAMILY (`"postgres"`/`"mysql"`; MariaDB is `"mysql"`) and the backend's raw `version()` string, which may legitimately be `nil` (§22.2 (u)/(v), `proto/PROTOCOL.md` §4). The DSN is never on the wire (§12).

- `boot_epoch` (u64): unique per daemon start. Clients cache it; a changed epoch on reconnect voids all session assumptions (§19).
- A type-registry mismatch is a **hard error** (forces regen/redeploy) — this is the versioning story.
- Feature bits (engine): `MEMFD`, `LISTEN_STREAMS`, `MANIFEST`. Feature bits (client): `MEMFD_RX` (able to receive fds, §5.1), `FIBERS` (informational). Unknown bits MUST be ignored.

*[Amended 2026-10-06 (§23, §24): **two service ids are allocated** — `06 http` (Ferro HTTP, §23.5) and `07 queue` (Ferro Queue, §24.4) — together with the engine feature bit `HTTP = 0x08`, which only a build that includes the `http` cargo feature sets (Ferro Queue takes no bit). They are allocated here and **reserved in `/proto` as comments only** (beside `[services]` in `methods.toml`, and in a reserved-codes block in `errors.toml`): no keys, so no generated constant and no registry-hash change. The keys land with each engine's first code slice (F2, G1). (`ADMIN = 5` was different: a real key with no method table from M0 until C3-7b.) **Drain:** on `SIGTERM` a drain signal reaches the sessions (today they are never told; a chassis change), and while HTTP exchanges are in flight `serve`'s wait becomes `FERRO_HTTP_DRAIN_MS + drain_deadline` (30 s + 5 s by default), so an exchange cancelled at the HTTP cap still emits its engine-classified `END` before the hard abort; new HTTP requests are refused at once as Retryable `UpstreamUnavailable` (cause `draining`), unsent; and parked queue `RESERVE`s are answered at once with no jobs (§23.6.1, §24.8). SQL draining is unchanged.]*

### 5.1 Large payloads (memfd) — honest version

Results or params above a threshold (default 1 MiB) MAY move out-of-band: the engine writes into a **sealed** `memfd` (`F_SEAL_SHRINK|GROW|WRITE|SEAL`), passes the fd via `SCM_RIGHTS`, and the frame carries `{fd_index, len, encoding}` with flag `OOB_FD`.

What this buys, precisely: **engine-side** zero-copy and strictly bounded engine memory. On the PHP side there is **one copy**: the client receives the fd via `ext-sockets` (`socket_import_stream` on the UDS stream + `socket_recvmsg` with `SCM_RIGHTS`) and reads it through `php://fd/N` — PHP userland cannot mmap. True client-side zero-copy arrives only with the optional native accelerator (D12). Requirements and gating:

- The path is Linux-only and REQUIRES the client to have advertised `MEMFD_RX` in HELLO (which the client only sets when `ext-sockets` is loaded). Otherwise the engine MUST fall back to inline credit-based streaming. No behavioral difference is observable other than throughput.
- Row streaming (§10) remains the default for cursors; memfd is for bulk single-shot payloads (COPY, large JSON/bytea).

*[Amended M3-D3 (§22.2 (cu)): **built for results, engine → client only.** What moves is a buffered SUCCESS terminal (`fetch:rows`/`fetch:none`, autocommit or tx-scoped — every success terminal goes through one supervisor) whose inline payload, the whole `Outcome::Ok` envelope, is at least the threshold: the frame becomes `END | OOB_FD`, its payload an `OobRef [fd_index, len, encoding]` (`fd_index` always 0, `encoding` the registry's `oob_encoding::FRAME_PAYLOAD` — the memfd holds exactly the bytes the frame would have carried), and the fd rides `SCM_RIGHTS` attached to that frame's FIRST byte (`/proto/PROTOCOL.md` §1.1). A stream's `HEAD`/`DATA` frames never move. **Params do not move**: a client frame carrying `OOB_FD` is still session-fatal. The threshold is `FERRO_MEMFD_THRESHOLD_BYTES` (default 1 MiB; `off` disables the path, and the engine then does not advertise `MEMFD`). **"Strictly bounded engine memory" is narrower than it reads:** the handler still builds the encoded result in the heap, which is then written once into the memfd — replacing the inline path's three further heap copies (envelope, splice, writer buffer) — and freed as soon as the terminal is enqueued; the memfd's pages are shmem charged to `ferrod`'s memory cgroup until the client closes its fd. What the engine gains is its heap and its writer: a multi-megabyte terminal no longer holds the session's one ordered writer for as long as the client takes to drain it. **The result ceiling is unchanged** — a buffered result still has to fit `MAX_FRAME_PAYLOAD` — because raising it only for `MEMFD_RX` clients would be exactly the observable difference this section forbids. The PHP client sets `MEMFD_RX` only when it actually reads with `recvmsg` (Linux, `ext-sockets`, a Unix socket); it then reads every byte with `recvmsg`, from the handshake on, and reads the memfd with `stream_get_contents` on the stream `ext-sockets` hands back rather than through `php://fd/N` — the same single copy.]*

### 5.2 Session and stream rules (normative)

- **request_id:** client-assigned u32. An id is *in-flight* from the first frame the client sends with it until the client has received a frame carrying `END` for it. Ids MUST NOT be reused while in-flight; clients SHOULD allocate monotonically and wrap. The engine MUST reject a reused in-flight id with a `Protocol` error frame on that id and MUST NOT disturb the original request.
- **Ordering:** frames for a given request_id are delivered in order; interleaving across ids is arbitrary.
- **Termination invariant:** every request is terminated by exactly one frame carrying `END` (success payload, error payload, or cancelled payload). All client state machines key off this.
- **CANCEL:** client MAY send `CANCEL {request_id}` for any in-flight id; it is advisory and idempotent. The engine propagates a backend cancel (PG cancel key, MySQL `KILL QUERY`, MSSQL attention signal) best-effort. If the request already completed, CANCEL is a no-op. The client MUST tolerate ordinary result frames arriving after it sent CANCEL, up to the terminal END — which reports either `Cancelled` or the raced-to-completion result. See §9.2 for the write-fate rule.
- **Flow control:** server→client streams are credit-based **per request**: default window 64 frames / 16 MiB (the byte window is deliberately `== MAX_FRAME_PAYLOAD`: a single indivisible row can be as large as the frame ceiling, and the client will not replenish credit before it has seen that frame, so the initial window must always fit one such frame or a large row can never be sent — M1-S5 large-row rule), replenished via `WINDOW_UPDATE {request_id, frames, bytes}`. Additionally a per-session aggregate cap (default 16 MiB, also `>= MAX_FRAME_PAYLOAD`) bounds total buffered engine output; the engine never buffers unbounded results.
- **Liveness:** core `PING`/`PONG`. Clients SHOULD ping after 30 s idle when awaiting nothing; a missed PONG deadline (2× interval) marks the connection dead and enters the reconnect loop (§19.2). `GOODBYE` announces graceful client close so the engine can distinguish drain from death.

## 6. Core SQL service

`EXEC` request:

```
{ pool, sql | query_id, params: [TypedValue], options: {
    timeout_ms?, readonly?, fetch: rows|stream|none,
    isolation?  (only meaningful inside tx),
    trace?: {traceparent} } }
```

`EXEC` response:

```
{ cols: [{name, type}], rows: [[TypedValue]] | stream_ref,
  affected: u64, last_insert_id?: TypedValue,
  warnings: [], stats: {queue_us, exec_us, rows, bytes} }
```

`queue_us` vs `exec_us` is deliberately first-class: pool wait time is the KPI that tells operators to grow `max_size` (§16).

`TX` service: `BEGIN {pool, isolation?, readonly?} → tx_id`, then `EXEC {tx_id, …}`, `SAVEPOINT`, `RELEASE`, `ROLLBACK_TO`, `COMMIT`, `ROLLBACK`. Engine-side deadlines: `idle_in_tx_timeout` (default 10 s) and `max_tx_duration` (default 60 s) roll back and release the pin, returning `Retryable{TxDeadline}`. Interaction with engine restart and write-fate rules: §19.

## 7. Pooling model

Per-pool mode: **transaction** (default), **session** (always pinned; the escape hatch), **statement** (rare, autocommit-only).

### 7.1 Pin architecture: protocol signals first, lexer assist second

Pin decisions are made **post-execution from the backend's own authoritative state report** wherever the backend can report state, with a lightweight statement lexer covering only what is text-visible but protocol-invisible. This inverts v0.1, which over-trusted the lexer.

**The FORM of that report varies by backend; the principle does not.** It is a protocol byte on PostgreSQL, a status flag plus OK-packet trackers on MySQL, and a synchronous in-process **library call** on SQLite. The section heading says "protocol signals" because the first two backends were wire protocols — the invariant being asserted is *authority from the backend, never inference from statement text*, and a library call satisfies it exactly as a protocol byte does (M2, C3; §22.2 (bc)). What a backend may NOT do is report state the engine derived itself.

**PostgreSQL.** The `ReadyForQuery` status byte (`I`/`T`/`E`) is authoritative for transaction state — pin/unpin for tx purposes keys off it, not off lexing `BEGIN`. `ParameterStatus` covers only `GUC_REPORT` parameters (`search_path` is *not* among them) and is treated as an assist signal, never as authority.

**MySQL / MariaDB.** Connection setup MUST enable the session trackers: `session_track_system_variables` (a **curated** list, not `'*'` — see §22.2), `session_track_state_change=ON`, `session_track_transaction_info='STATE'`, with `CLIENT_SESSION_TRACK` negotiated. The signal is **split** (M1-S6): the transaction-info status flag (`SERVER_STATUS_IN_TRANS`) is the tx AUTHORITY (`tx_status → InTx`/`Idle`, never `Failed` — MySQL has no aborted-open-tx state), and the OK-packet `session_state_info` trackers are the session-mutation AUTHORITY (`take_session_mutated`, read-and-cleared), which report mutations at the server — by design covering mutations performed *inside stored programs*. **Verification test DONE (M1-S6 Task 7, §22.2): a `SET SESSION` executed inside a procedure (`CALL p_set_session()`) taints the connection on BOTH MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.8 (verified live, read-back leak-closed).** The assist lexer's unconditional `CALL`/`DO` pin (§22.2) is the conservative backstop for any tracker gap. A connection whose trackers reported no mutation and which never pinned is **known clean** (the hygiene skip that keys off this is DEFERRED — §7.2, §22.2).

**SQLite.** The authority is `sqlite3_get_autocommit()`, reached through `Connection::is_autocommit()` — synchronous, in-process, and needing no round trip, which is what lets it be read after every statement as cheaply as PostgreSQL reads its `ReadyForQuery` byte. It reports *not autocommit* exactly while a transaction is open, so `tx_status` is `InTx`/`Idle`. As with MySQL, **no SQLite transaction-state SIGNAL ever means `TxStatus::Failed`** — SQLite has no aborted-open-transaction state in which later statements are refused — so `Failed` arises via `error_map`, or from the one case where there is no signal to read at all: a connection whose handle was lost to a panicking blocking task. That is the ABSENCE of a signal rather than one, the enum offers no "unknown", and it mirrors `MysqlConn`'s contract for its own handle-less window (M2, C3-3b).

**Reading it after every statement is not a stylistic preference; SQLite ends transactions by itself.** A constraint declared `ON CONFLICT ROLLBACK` rolls the whole transaction back when it is violated, and nothing in the statement text says so. An engine that tracked its own `BEGIN`/`COMMIT` would go on believing a transaction was open, hold the pin, and eventually hand the next tenant a connection it thinks is mid-transaction — the same hazard §7.1 exists to prevent, arrived at from the other direction. **Proven, with its control** (`ferro-sqlite-spike`'s `p6`): the `ON CONFLICT ROLLBACK` violation flips the signal back to autocommit, while the identical duplicate insert against a PLAIN unique constraint (SQLite's default `ON CONFLICT ABORT`) fails only the statement and leaves the signal reporting in-transaction. Without that control the first half would be equally consistent with "any error ends a transaction", which would be a materially different and much weaker claim.

**Assist lexer** (keyword classifier, not a parser) covers, and pins on: `LISTEN`/`UNLISTEN`; advisory-lock function calls in top-level SQL (`pg_advisory_lock` family without `_xact`); raw client `PREPARE`/`EXECUTE`/`DEALLOCATE` (engine-managed prepares are pool-safe and namespaced, §7.5); temp-table DDL; non-local `SET` as a backstop (PG: anything not `SET LOCAL`; MySQL: `SET SESSION` — normally the tracker catches these first); SQLite `ATTACH`/state-changing `PRAGMA`. Unknown/unclassifiable statements pin conservatively (`pin_on_unknown = true` default).

**Escape hatch:** per-pool `pin_functions = ["app_acquire_lock", …]` — statements whose text references a listed function pin for the session. This is the supported path for applications whose own server-side functions mutate session state (§7.4).

**Transaction-control SQL through the guarded statement entries — two classes, one guard (M1-S8a).** The pool's user-facing entries (`Checkout::exec`/`query`/`query_stream`) classify a statement's **LEADING** verb and treat the two classes differently, through ONE shared implementation. **Boundary** verbs (`BEGIN`, `START TRANSACTION`, `COMMIT`, `END`, `ABORT`, a bare `ROLLBACK`, `PREPARE TRANSACTION`) are refused on every entry, in or out of a transaction: they change whether a transaction is OPEN — the pin AUTHORITY — so one slipping through would leave a transaction the pool believes is not open on a connection bound for the next tenant, with no `tx_id`, no actor, no deadline and no rollback-on-session-death. **Savepoint** verbs (`SAVEPOINT n`, `RELEASE [SAVEPOINT] n`, `ROLLBACK TO [SAVEPOINT] n`) pass through on `exec`/`query` **iff the checkout already has a transaction open**: they cannot change transaction status (PG's `ReadyForQuery` byte does not flip on any of them) and every savepoint is destroyed by its enclosing transaction. Outside a transaction they are refused, because MySQL silently IGNORES a bare `SAVEPOINT` under autocommit. **`query_stream` is the exception and refuses them outright, transaction open or not** (`Unsupported("savepoint statement not allowed via query_stream()")`) — a savepoint returns no result set, so there is nothing to stream. That asymmetry is contract, not an oversight: a consuming tier's streaming path (S8b's DBAL `iterate*()`) must issue savepoints through the non-streaming entries. A savepoint passthrough must additionally carry **no bound parameters** and be a **lone statement**, and it runs on the raw **text** protocol — the same leaf the engine's own composed `SAVEPOINT sp_N` uses. `ROLLBACK` is the only verb in both classes; its second word decides. The classifier reads the leading verb only and is **not a parser**: a compound statement's later verbs are caught by the pin AUTHORITY (`apply_tx_status`), not by this guard. Full rationale, scope limits and proofs: §22.2 (r).

### 7.2 Hygiene: at checkout, pipelined, conditional

Hygiene runs at **checkout**, pipelined ahead of the first user statement — the round trip hides behind the user's own query instead of being paid at release. Rules:

- A connection that was **pinned** during its last lease is tainted: it gets the full reset (PG `DISCARD ALL`; MySQL `COM_RESET_CONNECTION`; MSSQL reset-on-next-RPC flag, see D1). Note both are destructive to prepared statements — accepted for tainted connections. **Resolved (was a known defect, M1-S3, found M1-S7, closed in the M1-S8a review round):** PG's `DISCARD ALL` includes `DEALLOCATE ALL`, which also destroyed the statements `tokio-postgres` caches for its own **typeinfo** (`pg_type`) lookups, permanently poisoning that connection's custom-OID diagnostics. `PgBackend::reset` now calls the vendored fork's `Client::clear_typeinfo_statement_cache()` before the reset batch, on BOTH profiles (the `Targeted` arm because a USER-issued `DISCARD ALL` is safe-listed by the assist lexer and recycles non-tainted). Details, rejected alternatives and the live regressions: §22.2 (m); ticket `docs/followups/2026-08-06-discard-all-typeinfo-cache-poisoning.md`, now RESOLVED. **Residual:** a user statement that deallocates followed by a custom-OID lookup *within the same checkout* still poisons that checkout — there is no reset between them; closing it needs a per-statement hook, and it is documented, not silently open.
- A **never-pinned** PG connection gets the targeted profile: `RESET ALL; UNLISTEN *; SELECT pg_advisory_unlock_all(); DISCARD TEMP; DISCARD SEQUENCES;` — this preserves the engine's namespaced prepared statements. (v0.1's targeted list leaked advisory locks and temp tables; this one does not. `DISCARD ALL` remains the superset.)
- A **known-clean** MySQL connection (tracker-verified) skips hygiene entirely. This is what makes the ≥ 95 % statement-cache target (§16) compatible with `COM_RESET_CONNECTION` deallocating prepares: reset only fires when something actually happened. **DEFERRED (M1-S6, §22.2):** the tracker-clean skip is not yet enabled — `clean_reset_profile()` returns `Some(Full)`, so EVERY recycled MySQL connection currently runs `COM_RESET_CONNECTION` regardless of taint (correctness over the cache target, R2). Enabling the `None`-skip waits until the tracker's in-proc coverage across all reuse-relevant session variables is proven; the split-signal + read-back leak-closed verification (§7.1, §22.2) is the first step toward it.
- SQLite: N/A (engine-owned, §7.7).

### 7.3 Statement cache

Per backend connection, LRU keyed by normalized SQL fingerprint, shared across all PHP workers by construction. PG uses named statements under an engine namespace (`__ferro_{fp}`); MySQL `COM_STMT_PREPARE`. Cache size, hit rate, and evictions exported (§13). *[Not built as of M2, so nothing is exported: the only statement cache in the tree is the MySQL driver's own per-connection LRU, which has no hit/miss counter — §13, §22.2 (bv).]*

### 7.4 The transaction-mode contract (documented limitation)

Session state mutated **inside server-side functions/procedures** is undetectable on PostgreSQL: `set_config(…, is_local => false)`, `SET`/advisory locks executed within PL/pgSQL, never appear on the wire, and `ParameterStatus` cannot report them. No lexer fixes this; PgBouncer has the same hole. Therefore, normatively:

- Transaction mode **defines** function-side session mutation as unsupported, except for functions listed in `pin_functions`.
- Applications that need it use **session mode** for that pool.
- MySQL with trackers detects most of this class (pending the M1 verification above) and is documented as the stronger backend for transaction mode.
- User documentation MUST state this contract prominently, in the pooling chapter, with the `pin_functions` and session-mode escape hatches beside it.

### 7.5 Replicas & routing

Pools may declare `replicas = [{dsn, weight}]`; `readonly: true` on EXEC (or a read-only pool alias) routes to replicas with health/lag gating (`max_replica_lag_ms`). Ferro does **not** guess read vs write from SQL in v1 — routing is explicit from the client. Doctrine/Laravel already model read/write connections and map cleanly onto this.

### 7.6 SQLite

The engine owns the file. WAL mode, `PRAGMA busy_timeout` bounded by `checkout_timeout`, many readers, and **every transaction takes SQLite's writer lock at `BEGIN` unless the client DECLARED the request `readonly`** (D13). This removes PHP's worst SQLite failure mode (`SQLITE_BUSY` storms under FPM) and makes SQLite a legitimate small-service backend. Online backup is exposed via the admin service for snapshots: `ADMIN`/`BACKUP` (an OPERATE verb under D15) runs SQLite's own `VACUUM INTO` through the pool, writes a plain FILE NAME into the pool's D14 allowed directory, and moves it into place atomically — a failed backup never leaves a file and never destroys the previous snapshot (`/proto/PROTOCOL.md` §11; §22.2 (cf)).

**This paragraph read "one writer serialized engine-side" until M2, and that wording was unimplementable** — corrected here rather than silently reinterpreted by whoever built the backend. Serializing *the writer* requires knowing which statements write, which is precisely what charter rule 6 and §3 forbid; §1 already says so for the neighbouring case ("No read/write inference from SQL text for replica routing in v1 — routing is explicit (§7.6)"), so §7.6 was contradicting the bullet that pointed at it. D13 records the resolution: the engine never asks what a statement does, it obeys what the client declared, and an UNDECLARED statement is treated as a writer.

### 7.7 Health & lifecycle

Liveness pings on idle, `max_lifetime` recycling, exponential backoff on backend outage, per-backend circuit breaker (half-open probes). `SIGTERM` → drain: refuse new checkouts, let pins finish up to `drain_deadline`, then hard-close. `SIGHUP` → config reload with pool diffing. Note the positive framing for docs: the breaker plus engine-side reconnection *shields* workers from backend flaps — a herd of FPM workers no longer stampedes a recovering database.

## 8. Backend matrix

| Backend | Crate | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL | tokio-postgres | pipelining, LISTEN/NOTIFY streams, COPY via memfd, cancel keys, ReadyForQuery-driven pinning |
| MySQL / MariaDB | mysql_async (patched, `CLIENT_SESSION_TRACK`) | split pin signal — status-flag tx authority + OK-packet session-mutation trackers (§7.1); `CALL`/`DO` lexer backstop; OK-packet `last_insert_id`; `COM_RESET_CONNECTION` currently UNCONDITIONAL on recycle (the tracker-clean skip is deferred, §7.2/§22.2). Tracker-in-procedure verification DONE on MySQL 8.4 + MariaDB 11.8 (§22.2). |
| SQLite | rusqlite (+ engine writer queue) | WAL, serialized writer, online backup API |
| SQL Server | tiberius | **session mode by default** until reset-on-RPC semantics verified (D1) |
| Oracle | post-v1 | blocking OCI on `spawn_blocking` pool |

MongoDB/Redis/ClickHouse are explicitly out of the SQL service; they can later ship as sibling services on the same framing without touching this spec.

## 9. Canonical type system

One tagged value encoding on the wire; per-backend adapters map to native formats. PHP visible types below are what the native API hydrates; DBAL/Eloquent tiers apply their own ecosystem conventions on top (§14–15).

| Canonical | PG | MySQL | SQLite | MSSQL | PHP (native API) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| BOOL | bool | tinyint(1)* | int | bit | `bool` |
| I64 | int2/4/8 | *int signed | integer | int/bigint | `int` |
| U64 | — | bigint unsigned | — | — | `int` or `Ferro\U64` if > PHP_INT_MAX |
| F64 | float4/8 | float/double | real | float | `float` |
| DECIMAL | numeric | decimal | text | decimal | `Ferro\Decimal` (string-backed) |
| TEXT | text/varchar | *text/varchar | text | nvarchar | `string` |
| BYTES | bytea | *blob | blob | varbinary | `string` (binary) |
| DATE / TIME | date/time | date/time | text | date/time | `Ferro\Date`, `Ferro\Time` |
| TIMESTAMP | timestamp | datetime | text | datetime2 | `DateTimeImmutable` (naive, policy §9.1) |
| TIMESTAMPTZ | timestamptz | timestamp | — | datetimeoffset | `DateTimeImmutable` (UTC instant) |
| UUID | uuid | binary(16)/char(36) | text | uniqueidentifier | `Ferro\Uuid` |
| JSON | json/jsonb | json | text | nvarchar | lazy `Ferro\Json` (decode on access) |
| ARRAY<T> | native | — | — | — | `array` |
| INTERVAL / INET / VECTOR | native | — | — | — | dedicated value objects |

*\* configurable legacy mappings (e.g. `tinyint(1)→bool` off for people who use it as a real int).*

**M1-S7 status — implemented end to end.** `NULL`, `BOOL`, `I64`, `U64`, `F64`, `DECIMAL`, `TEXT`, `BYTES`, `DATE`, `TIME`, `TIMESTAMP`, `TIMESTAMPTZ`, `UUID` and `JSON` are implemented **in both directions** (read *and* bind) in the PHP client and on PostgreSQL and MySQL/MariaDB — **except for the three ENGINE asymmetries below** (where the engine has no such type at all). The one CLIENT-side gap — **`BYTES` was read-only from PHP**, because `ExecCodec::bindOne` maps *every* PHP string to `TAG_TEXT`, so no PHP value could produce a `BYTES` param and `ParameterType::BINARY`/`LARGE_OBJECT` could not bind at all — is **CLOSED in M1-S8a** by the explicit `Ferro\Bytes` marker, §22.2 (k)(4). The read side stays a plain PHP `string`, so a round trip is `Ferro\Bytes` out and `string` back. `ARRAY<T>`, `INTERVAL`, `INET` and `VECTOR` remain registry constants only and stay a loud `NonRetryable{Unsupported}` (§22.2), as do the per-engine deferrals listed there. The implemented set is part of the **hashed** type registry (`/proto/types.toml` `implemented` → `registry.lock.json` → `TYPE_REGISTRY_HASH`), so an engine/client pair with different type coverage fails fast at the handshake instead of throwing mid-query on the first row of a newly-supported type. Three engine asymmetries are by design and recorded in §22.2 (e): no MySQL-family backend ever emits the `UUID` tag, a MariaDB `JSON` column classifies as `TEXT`, and PostgreSQL neither produces nor binds `U64` (PG has no unsigned integer type).

**The wire payload is canonical TEXT; the PHP column above is the native-API HYDRATION.** Every tag **added in M1-S7** except `U64` (msgpack uint) rides the msgpack `str` family carrying the canonical text defined in `proto/PROTOCOL.md` §3.2 — the M0 tags keep their native msgpack families (`NULL` = `nil`, `BOOL` = `bool`, `I64` = the signed-int family, `F64` = `float64`, `TEXT` = `str`, `BYTES` = `bin`), and that surviving `str`/`bin` split is precisely *why* the S7 payloads are text-canonical: the two families are indistinguishable in PHP after unpack, so only the tag discriminates them (§22.2 (a)). The engine renders each backend's native binary form into that policy-independent, lossless payload (a `DECIMAL`'s display scale, a 200-digit numeric, `NaN`, the ±`infinity` sentinels and a `24:00:00` time all survive it), and the client turns it into the PHP types above under the §9.1 policies. The two layers are separable on purpose: `Ferro\Client\Value\RawStringValuePolicy` hands the canonical text back verbatim for the DBAL tier (§14).

**The naive/instant rule (read and bind are symmetric).** `TIMESTAMP` hydrates to `Ferro\NaiveTimestamp extends \DateTimeImmutable` — still a `DateTimeImmutable`, so the table above stays literally true — while `TIMESTAMPTZ` hydrates to a plain `\DateTimeImmutable` in UTC. On the bind side a plain `\DateTimeInterface` is an **instant** and binds `TIMESTAMPTZ` normalized to UTC; a `NaiveTimestamp` is a **wall clock** and binds `TIMESTAMP` with its text emitted verbatim, no zone conversion. That pairing is exactly what makes a read → write-back round trip byte-stable; without the subclass every value read from a naive column and written straight back would shift by whatever zone the object carried, silently.

**A `TIMESTAMP`/`TIMESTAMPTZ` column CAN change its PHP type between rows — deliberately.** `infinity` / `-infinity` (PG), `0000-00-00 00:00:00` (a MySQL zero datetime) and a **zero-IN-date** — any value with a zero *year, month or day* component, e.g. `2026-00-05` or `2026-08-00 12:00:00`, legal wherever `sql_mode` omits `NO_ZERO_IN_DATE` (MariaDB 11's default) — are legal stored values that are **not** calendar instants. The sentinel class is therefore defined by a **zero component test, not by string equality against the four fixed forms**, which is how both codecs implement it (`ferro-backend-mysql`'s `mytext.rs`; the client's `CanonicalText::dateIsSentinel` / `timestampIsInstant` / `timestamptzIsInstant`). A `DATE` sentinel keeps its tag (it is carried inside `Ferro\Date`, ask with `isSentinel()`), but a `TIMESTAMP`/`TIMESTAMPTZ` sentinel — whose PHP type is an instant — is handed back as the **canonical text**, so such a column yields a `DateTimeImmutable` for ordinary rows and a `string` for a sentinel row. This is a deliberate exception to the "a column must not change PHP type between rows" rationale that governs the other knobs (`RawStringValuePolicy`): the alternatives are worse — throwing makes a legal column unreadable with no escape hatch, and inventing a date is precisely the silent miscast §9.1 exists to prevent. **The consequence is loud, not silent:** a sentinel row fed to a `\DateTimeImmutable`-typed DTO parameter raises `Ferro\Client\Error\HydrationException` (inside the `FerroException` contract, naming the column and its actual type), and re-binding a sentinel writes it back as `TEXT`, which PG's bind pre-flight refuses against a `timestamp` slot as a known-fate error. An application that must never see the shape either excludes sentinels in SQL or reads the column with `RawStringValuePolicy`.

**The supported calendar range is `0001-01-01 .. 9999-12-31`.** Reachable data outside it — **BC dates and years above 9999** (PG's `date`/`timestamp` span 4713 BC .. 5874897 AD) — is a loud `Unsupported` naming the year, never an invented `" BC"` suffix, negative year or 7-digit year, because `proto/PROTOCOL.md` §3.2's canonical `YYYY-MM-DD` form defines no representation for them and a guessed one would differ between the two codecs. The follow-up, when a real workload needs it, is either an extended-year/BC canonical form added to PROTOCOL.md §3.2 (and both codecs) or a per-pool opt-in raw passthrough; until then the failure is diagnosable rather than a wrong date.

### 9.1 Policies over guesses

Cross-driver ambiguity is resolved by explicit policy, not silent driver behavior: `decimal: object|string`, `naive_datetime_zone: utc|server|error`, `u64_overflow: object|string|error`, `uuid: object|string`. Defaults are the safe object forms. This is where Ferro is deliberately *better* than PDO, whose per-driver casting inconsistencies are a chronic source of production bugs.

**As shipped in M1 these four knobs are CLIENT-SIDE, not pool-level (M1-S7; §22.2).** They are a `Ferro\Client\Value\TypePolicyOptions` value object threaded `Ferro::connect(types:)` → `Connection(types:)` → the decoding `ValuePolicy`. There is no `ferrod` knob, because the S7 wire is lossless canonical text (§9 above): the engine renders a policy-**independent** payload, so nothing in the daemon could act on a `decimal=string` pool setting. **The `HELLO_ACK` pool metadata landed in M1-S8a (Tasks 11/12) and carries `[name, kind, server_version]` — the backend FAMILY and the raw `version()` string, nothing else.** Pool-level *policy defaults* advertised to the client are therefore still not on the wire and remain a later carry. The shipped semantics:

- **`decimal`** — `object` (default) → `Ferro\Decimal` (string-backed, exact); `string` → the canonical text, still validated.
- **`uuid`** — `object` (default) → `Ferro\Uuid`; `string` → the canonical lowercase text, still validated.
- **`u64_overflow`** — `object` (default) → a PHP `int` when the value fits, `Ferro\U64` above `PHP_INT_MAX`; `error` → refuse **only** the overflowing value; `string` → the decimal text for the **whole column**, not merely for overflowing values. That last one is a deliberate wart against the knob's *name*: the point of `string` is that a column's PHP type must not depend on a given row's magnitude (which is what DBAL and a `string` schema column want), so it cannot be applied per value. Choose `object` if you want the narrow behaviour the name suggests.
- **`naive_datetime_zone`** — `utc` (default) → a `Ferro\NaiveTimestamp` pinned to an explicit UTC zone; `error` → refuse the naive tag outright, scoped to `TAG_TIMESTAMP` **alone** (`TIMESTAMPTZ`/`DATE`/`TIME` still decode; an undefined scope would make whole columns unreadable with no escape hatch). **`server` is not implementable in M1** and is rejected loudly by the constructor rather than silently downgraded to `utc`: nothing on the wire carries the backend's session timezone. `HelloAck` is still `[engine_version, boot_epoch, features, pools, type_registry_hash]`, and although M1-S8a Tasks 11/12 filled `pools` with `[name, kind, server_version]` triples, **none of those three is a timezone** — so `server` REMAINS unimplemented and is not merely waiting on that carry. Implementing it needs a new metadata field (and a `SHOW timezone` / `@@session.time_zone` probe beside the version probe), which is a separate change to `/proto`, both codecs and the golden vectors.
- **Refusals are not wire faults.** A policy refusal raises `Ferro\Client\Error\TypePolicyException`; a **malformed** payload (a `DECIMAL` that is not a number, a truncated `TIMESTAMP`) stays `ProtocolException`. The split is by cause, so the S8 DBAL `ExceptionConverter` never reports a configuration choice as a driver protocol failure. Neither is a §9.2 fate branch: both are raised **client-side — never from a wire terminal**, either *pre-send* in `ExecCodec::bindOne` (a `naive_datetime_zone=error` refusal of a naive `TIMESTAMP` param throws during `encode()`, before the request leaves the client, so nothing was executed) or *post-success* in `ExecCodec::decodeRow` (mid-row on the streamed path, after the statement already succeeded).
- **`Connection::typePolicy()` reports the options THIS connection was constructed with.** When a caller injects a ready-made `codec:` or `values:` instead (those forms carry their own embedded policy, and the three arguments are mutually exclusive), the accessor returns the **defaults** — it is a convenience for the common path, not an authoritative read of the policy actually decoding rows.

**MySQL/MariaDB sessions are pinned to `time_zone = '+00:00'` (M1-S7).** §9 maps MySQL's `timestamp` to `TIMESTAMPTZ`, a UTC instant — but the driver hands back zone-*less* date/time components, converted by the server into the *session* zone, so an instant is only definable against a known one; and under pooling an unpinned session zone would make the same column read differently per connection. Ferro therefore pins every MySQL/MariaDB session (re-applied after each `COM_RESET_CONNECTION`), on both the read and the bind side. **Visible consequence: `NOW()`, `CURDATE()` and `CURTIME()` return UTC on every Ferro MySQL connection**, which may differ from a legacy application's previous behaviour. This is the same choice Doctrine and Laravel make, and it is what keeps a pooled `timestamp` column deterministic.

**MySQL/MariaDB column classification (M1-S6, widened by M1-S7).** The MySQL backend keys every column off the wire **column metadata** (column type + flags + charset + display length), not the driver's ambiguous binary `Value`. `TINYINT(1)` (MySQL's `BOOLEAN` idiom, identified by display length 1, signed or unsigned) → `BOOL`; a wider signed `TINYINT`/`SMALLINT`/`MEDIUMINT`/`INT`/`BIGINT` and every *unsigned* integer narrower than 64 bits → `I64` (all fit losslessly); `BIGINT UNSIGNED` → `U64`; `FLOAT`/`DOUBLE` → `F64`; `CHAR`/`VARCHAR`/`TEXT` (non-binary charset) → `TEXT`; `BINARY`/`VARBINARY`/`BLOB` (binary collation 63) → `BYTES`; `DECIMAL`/`NEWDECIMAL` → `DECIMAL`; `DATE` → `DATE`, `TIME` → `TIME`, `DATETIME` → `TIMESTAMP` (naive), `TIMESTAMP` → `TIMESTAMPTZ` (a UTC instant, under the session pin above); MySQL 8's `JSON` → `JSON`; **`ENUM` → `TEXT`** (or `BYTES` on a binary collation — it takes the same charset branch every other string type takes; M1-S8a). **M1-S6's deferrals of `BIGINT UNSIGNED`, `DECIMAL`, the date/time family and `JSON` were closed by M1-S7, and `ENUM` by M1-S8a** — this paragraph previously listed them all as out of scope. An `ENUM` cell's binary-protocol value **IS** its label string in the column's charset, so carrying it as `TEXT` is lossless rather than a promotion, and it is what makes `information_schema` readable (`COLUMNS.COLUMN_KEY` and `referential_constraints.UPDATE_RULE`/`DELETE_RULE` are `ENUM`s on MySQL 8, though not on MariaDB). Still **out of scope**, and still a **loud `Unsupported`** naming the column + its MySQL type rather than a silent miscast: `YEAR`, `BIT`, `SET`, `GEOMETRY`, `VECTOR` — `SET` in particular stays out because its wire form is a **comma-joined multi-value** string, a different type rather than a longer one. Two engine facts are by design, not gaps (§22.2): a MariaDB `JSON` column is a `LONGTEXT` alias and classifies as `TEXT`, and MariaDB's native `UUID`/`INET4`/`INET6` reach the wire as `MYSQL_TYPE_STRING`/utf8mb4 and classify as `TEXT` — so no MySQL-family backend ever emits the `UUID` tag. A cell whose driver `Value` variant does not match its column kind is a client-side decode mismatch → `Backend` (NonRetryable), never `ConnectionLost` (so it can never mint a false §19.3 `Indeterminate`). This is the documented policy the S8/S9 DBAL/Eloquent suite inherits (§14–15) — a diagnosable error, not a surprise miscast. Source: `engine/crates/ferro-backend-mysql/src/rowmap.rs`.

**PostgreSQL column classification (M0/M1-S7, widened by M1-S8a with the CATALOG scalars).** The PG backend keys every column off its `RowDescription` **OID**, through one table (`oid_extract_type`) that backs both the cols-build tag gate and the per-cell extractor so the two can never drift. Alongside the §9 table's scalars, M1-S8a admits the five OIDs PG's own catalog is written in. **Precisely how much of that DBAL actually selects (corrected in the M1-S8a review round, finding F13 — this sentence previously called all of them "the ones DBAL's `AbstractSchemaManager` selects on every introspection", which is false):** of DBAL 4.4.4's `selectTableColumns`, transcribed verbatim into the live gate at `engine/crates/ferrod/tests/catalog_it.rs`, **twelve result columns come back and NONE of them is `oid`, `regtype` or `regclass`** — `c.oid` / `a.atttypid` appear only in JOIN predicates, which never reach a `RowDescription`. The result columns that needed this work are `t.typname` / `bt.typname` (`name`) and `a.attidentity` / `coll.collprovider` (`"char"`), and those two alone were enough to make the whole statement unreadable. The `reg*` coverage is DEFENSIVE — hand-written introspection, `pg_catalog` queries in migrations tooling and `atttypid::regtype` casts do return them — and **S8b must not assume `reg*` appears in stock DBAL traffic**. The mappings: `name`(19) → **`TEXT`** (it is already in `String`'s `FromSql` accept list, so it folds into the existing text arm); `"char"`(18) → **`TEXT`**, but it is a **single BYTE**, rendered the way PG's own `charout` does — `'\0'` is the **empty string** (which is exactly what `pg_attribute.attidentity` holds on a non-identity column), an ASCII byte is that one character, and a non-ASCII byte is a loud `Backend` decode mismatch rather than an invented replacement character; `oid`(26) → **`I64`** (read as `u32`, widened losslessly); and `regtype`(2206)/`regclass`(2205) → **`I64`**, reporting the **numeric OID**, because under Ferro's binary result format their payload literally *is* a 4-byte OID (`regtypesend` **is** `oidsend`) and resolving it to a type NAME would require a catalog round trip the engine must not make (charter rule 6). A caller who wants the name casts in SQL — `::text` or `format_type(...)` — which is what DBAL's own introspection already does. **No new canonical tag and no `/proto` change**: every one of them lands on `TEXT` or `I64`. Still **out of scope** and still a loud `Unsupported`: `timetz`, arrays (incl. `oidvector`), `interval`, `inet`, and every enum/composite/range type (per-database CUSTOM OIDs — see §22.2 (h) and (m)). **M1-S8b's acceptance run measured what that "out of scope" costs, and it is larger than this paragraph implies: `int2vector`(22) — the same vector family as `oidvector` — is `pg_index.indkey`, and DBAL's stock `PostgreSQLSchemaManager::selectIndexColumns()` selects it as a RESULT column, so every stock schema-manager introspection on PostgreSQL fails.** That is 50 of the 78 non-passing PostgreSQL tests at the gate, and it means `doctrine/migrations` and schema diffing do not work on PG today. The `reg*` prediction above held exactly as written (defensive, not present in stock DBAL traffic); it is the VECTOR family that was underestimated. Follow-up: `docs/followups/2026-08-11-pg-int2vector-blocks-the-schema-manager.md`; §22.2 (z). A DOMAIN is reported by PG as its BASE type **in the `RowDescription`** — i.e. on the READ side, which is what this paragraph is about — so a domain column is readable iff its base is. The BIND side is not symmetric (`stmt.params()` reports the domain's own OID for a write into a domain column), which is why the pre-flight has to resolve it explicitly: §22.2 (g). Source: `engine/crates/ferro-backend-pg/src/rowmap.rs`.

### 9.2 Normalized error taxonomy — three branches

Every backend and engine error maps to one tree, carrying the raw SQLSTATE/errno alongside:

```
Retryable      { ConnectionLost{epoch_changed: bool}, PoolTimeout, TxDeadline,
                 Deadlock, SerializationFailure, ReplicaUnavailable }
Indeterminate  { WriteUnconfirmed{cause: link_lost|timeout|engine_restart} }
NonRetryable   { Syntax, Constraint{Unique, ForeignKey, NotNull, Check},
                 Auth, QueryTimeout, Cancelled, Protocol, TxNotFound, Unsupported }
```

**`Indeterminate` is the branch v0.1 was missing.** It is raised when a side-effecting statement was dispatched and its fate is unknown: the backend link (or the engine itself, §19) died between dispatch and response. Rules:

- Reads that die mid-flight are `Retryable{ConnectionLost}` — a read has no fate to be unsure about.
- A cancelled or timed-out **autocommit write** whose non-execution the engine could not confirm surfaces as `Indeterminate{cause: timeout}`, not `Cancelled`/`QueryTimeout`. Inside a transaction, timeout/cancel is safe: the engine rolls the tx back, and the client sees `Retryable`.
- Clients MUST NOT auto-retry `Indeterminate` unless the query's manifest entry declares `idempotent: true` (§11). Raw-SQL writes are never auto-retried.
- The engine never retries user statements transparently in any branch — it reports; the client's policy layer decides (§19.3).

*[Amended 2026-10-06 (D21, §23, §24): **six codes join the tree.** Retryable gains `UpstreamUnavailable` (`0x1007`) and `RateLimited` (`0x1008`), which carry `retry_after_ms` where §23.7.1 says. NonRetryable gains `TlsRefused` (`0x300D`) and `ResponseIncomplete` (`0x300E`) for HTTP, and `LeaseLost` (`0x300F`) and `PoolMismatch` (`0x3010`) for the queue. `Forbidden` (`0x300C`, D15) widens to "refused by the engine's own access policy", which HTTP's SSRF refusals use, and `QueryTimeout` is reused for an HTTP timeout. All six are allocated in §23/§24 and enter `errors.toml` at F2/G1. **The licence bullet reads, under D21:** clients MUST NOT auto-retry `Indeterminate` unless the query's manifest entry declares `idempotent: true` (§11), or, for a Ferro Queue verb, §24.6 licenses the re-send (an autocommit fenced verb, a dedup-keyed `ENQUEUE`, a `RESERVE`). Ferro HTTP licenses through classification instead, and nothing licenses re-sending an `Indeterminate` HTTP request: a request declared idempotent, by its caller or by the operator for its upstream, is never `Indeterminate` — a link-level failure after sending is `Retryable`, and a timeout or cancel follows the read rule above (§23.7.1, §23.7.2); the method alone licenses nothing. Raw-SQL writes are never auto-retried. The definition of `Indeterminate` is unchanged: a lost `RESERVE` is `Indeterminate` because it is a dispatched side-effecting statement.]*

DBAL's ExceptionConverter and Laravel's `QueryException` map from this one tree (§14–15), giving identical exception semantics across all backends. `Indeterminate` maps to a Ferro-specific exception class in both tiers (subclassing each ecosystem's driver-exception base) since neither ecosystem models the concept.

## 10. Native PHP client (`ferro/client`)

Pure PHP, zero required extensions; **PHP ≥ 8.2** (readonly classes). Optional accelerators, runtime-detected: `ext-msgpack` (codec hot path, `composer suggest`), `ext-sockets` (enables the `MEMFD_RX` feature, §5.1). Connection-less by design: you submit work, you never hold a connection.

```php
$db = Ferro::pool('main');

// typed hydration into readonly DTOs
$user  = $db->queryOne(User::class, 'select id, email, created_at from users where id = ?', [$id]);
$rows  = $db->query(OrderRow::class, 'select … where status = ?', [Status::Open]);

// scalars / assoc escape hatches
$count = $db->scalar('select count(*) from orders');
$assoc = $db->rows('select …');                 // array<array<string,mixed>>

// transactions are closures; scope is explicit, retry is policy
$db->transaction(function (Ferro\Tx $tx) {
    $tx->exec('update accounts set balance = balance - ? where id = ?', [$amt, $a]);
    $tx->exec('update accounts set balance = balance + ? where id = ?', [$amt, $b]);
}, Retry::onSerializationFailure(times: 3, backoff: Backoff::jitter(10, 200)));

// streaming: constant memory, credit-driven
foreach ($db->stream(Event::class, 'select … order by id') as $event) { … }

// notifications as iterators (PG)
foreach ($db->listen('jobs') as $note) { … }
```

**Hydration contract:** DTOs are `final readonly` classes with promoted constructor properties; column→property matching by name (snake→camel mapping configurable), types validated against the canonical registry at first use and memoized. No reflection per row on the fast path.

**Resilience:** the client implements the reconnect loop, epoch tracking, and retry policy of §19. The transaction closure above is the recovery surface: `Retryable` inside a closure re-runs the whole closure on a fresh connection/epoch when the policy allows.

The closure is the *idiom*, not the only form: `Connection` also exposes an **imperative** `begin()`/`commit()`/`rollBack()` (+ `inTransaction()`) because a Doctrine/Eloquent driver (§14, §15) is handed those three as unrelated calls and owns the retry decision itself, so it cannot be adapted onto a closure. The imperative form deliberately runs no re-run loop of its own — it hands the caller a *fate* instead (a lost `BEGIN` is `Retryable`, a lost `COMMIT` is the §19.3 `Indeterminate`) — and it does not nest (a driver's nesting is `SAVEPOINT` SQL, §22.2 (r)). See §22.2 (t).

### 10.1 Concurrency

`queryAsync()` returns a `Ferro\Future`. Under plain FPM, `await` blocks on the socket — correct everywhere, zero setup. If a Fiber scheduler is active (`Ferro\Loop::run(...)`, or Revolt/AMPHP detected), awaiting suspends the Fiber and the client multiplexes all in-flight requests over the single UDS connection:

```php
[$profile, $orders, $flags] = Ferro\await([
    $db->queryOneAsync(Profile::class, …),
    $db->queryAsync(OrderRow::class, …),
    $flagsDb->queryAsync(Flag::class, …),
]);
```

Fan-out of k queries costs ≈ max(query) + boundary overhead, not the sum. Transactions are Fiber-safe because pins follow `tx_id`, not the socket. *[Amended M3-D1b (§22.2 (ck)): that holds for the closure form. An IMPERATIVE transaction (`begin()`) lives on the `Connection`, so it belongs to the Fiber that began it, and another Fiber's statement on that Connection is refused while it is open.]*

*[Amended M3-D1a (§22.2 (cj)): the multiplexed session and the blocking half of this section exist. `Connection` has `queryAsync`, `queryOneAsync`, `scalarAsync`, `rowsAsync` and `execAsync`, each returning a `Ferro\Future`, and `Ferro\await()` awaits a keyed list of them. Several requests are in flight on the one socket and every frame is routed by `request_id`, so fan-out costs about max(query) **even under plain FPM, with no Fiber scheduler at all**: all k requests are written before any terminal is read. Fiber suspension landed in D1b: `Ferro\Loop::run([...])` runs tasks as Fibers, and an `await` inside one suspends it until its terminal arrives (§22.2 (ck)); the Revolt adapter is D1c. Three deliberate limits: a statement inside an open transaction settles at once, because it belongs to the transaction's order; an asynchronous statement does not update `lastInsertId()`, because "the most recent statement" means nothing with several in flight; and an open stream is still exclusive on its session.]* **Octane/Swoole caveat:** Swoole coroutines and Fibers don't compose; under Octane the client runs in sync-per-worker mode (documented).

## 11. Checked SQL & codegen (`ferro` CLI)

sqlx's trick, ported: verify every query against a real schema at build time, and generate the types. The CLI is a Rust binary in the engine workspace (D10), sharing the proto and backend crates.

- `ferro schema sync` — applies the project's migrations to a disposable shadow database (or loads a committed schema snapshot).
- `ferro check` — collects queries from: `.sql` files with front-matter and `#[FerroQuery(id: …, sql: …)]` attributes (v1; named, literal arguments — see the amendment below); inline `Ferro::sql('…')` literals via a PHPStan extension (phase 2, D3). Each is `PREPARE`d against the shadow schema; syntax errors, unknown columns, and param-count/type mismatches fail CI.
  *[Amended M3-D2b (§22.2 (cq)): built as `ferro schema-sync --migrations <dir> [--pool <name>]` and `ferro check --manifest <file> [--write <file>]`. Both read their connections exactly as `ferrod` does (`FERRO_POOLS` + `FERRO_POOL_<NAME>_DSN`), so a DSN is never on a command line or in output. `schema-sync` EMPTIES the database and applies `*.sql` files in name order. It is refused unless the database's name ends in `_shadow` (or `--i-know-this-is-disposable` is given) — and that name is the one the CONNECTED SERVER reports (`current_database()`, `DATABASE()`, the file SQLite's `main` is open on), never one parsed out of the DSN, which the drivers resolve differently. The reset is verified empty afterwards; a migration that leaves a transaction open fails. `check` PREPAREs every query on its pool without running it (`PoolBackend::describe`, through the same backend crates and type mapping as the engine): a syntax error, an unknown relation, or a column type outside §9 fails it, every problem listed, nothing written. `--write` records each query's `params` (PostgreSQL's types; a count on MySQL and SQLite, which infer none) and `columns` (name, §9 tag, server type name; no tag on SQLite, which types values, not columns). Neither is part of the manifest hash. Param-count and param-TYPE mismatches against the caller's binds are not checked: nothing in PHP declares a bind's type.]*
- `ferro gen` — emits `gen/`: readonly DTO classes with native property types, PHPStan stubs, and `manifest.json`.
  *[Amended M3-D2c (§22.2 (cr)): built as `ferro gen --manifest <checked manifest> --out <dir> [--queries-class <FQCN>]`. It emits one `final readonly class` per declared `dto`, built for the client's hydrator: one promoted constructor parameter per column, NAMED so that `HydrationPlan` maps it back to that column (its camelCase spelling where the hydrator's `camelToSnake` restores the column, else the column's own name, which the hydrator's exact-name fallback reads; a column neither reaches is refused with an "alias it" message), TYPED as exactly what the DEFAULT §9.1 policy can return for the column's tag — sentinels included, so `TIMESTAMP`/`TIMESTAMPTZ` admit the `string` the policy hands back for `infinity` or a zero datetime — and NULLABLE because preparing a statement does not report nullability. It also emits a class of query-id constants carrying `MANIFEST_HASH`, and `manifest.json`, into one flat directory, refusing any two classes that would share a file name. PHPStan stubs are NOT emitted.]*

**Manifest schema:** `query_id → {sql, param_types, result_shape, pool, readonly: bool, idempotent: bool}`. `idempotent` defaults to `false`; it is declared in the query's front-matter/attribute and is the sole license for auto-retrying an `Indeterminate` write (§9.2, §19.3) — a natural-key upsert can declare it, a balance increment cannot.

*[Amended M3-D2a (§22.2 (cl)): the formats are fixed.]*
- **A `.sql` file** declares exactly ONE query. It starts with a `-- ferro:` block of INDENTED `--   key: value` lines (`--` then two spaces or a tab — `-- note: x` on the first SQL line is a comment, not a key): `id` (required, `[a-z][a-z0-9_.-]*`, up to 128 characters — never all digits, which PHP would turn into an integer key), `pool` (default `default`), `readonly` and `idempotent` (default `false`; exactly `true` or `false`, so a typo cannot license or forbid a retry), and `dto`. Anything that could make the file mean something other than it looks is refused: a front-matter-looking line after a blank line, a second `-- ferro:` block. The SQL is kept byte for byte, line endings included.
- **An attribute** is `#[Ferro\Attribute\FerroQuery(id: …, sql: …, …)]` with NAMED, LITERAL arguments. `vendor/bin/ferro-queries` reads these with PHP's tokenizer, never running the code, resolves the attribute's name against the file's `namespace` and `use` imports as PHP does, and refuses anything it cannot read exactly.
- **`ferro manifest`** collects both into `manifest.json`. The SQL is stored as written, minus front-matter and trimmed, and never rewritten.
- **The hash** is SHA-256 over `{"v":1,"q":{id:{sql,pool,readonly,idempotent}}}` (ids sorted, no whitespace). `dto` and the source location are not part of it, because they do not change what runs. A loader recomputes it and never trusts the copy recorded in the file. A manifest with a repeated query id, an unknown field, or no queries is refused whole.

The manifest hash participates in the handshake (§5). *[Amended M3-D2d (§22.2 (cm)): `ferrod` loads `FERRO_MANIFEST` at startup — a manifest that does not load, or names a pool the daemon lacks, stops startup. A HELLO `manifest_hash` must equal the engine's, else the handshake is refused (so is a hash sent to an engine with none); a client that sends none is admitted but cannot run a `query_id`. EXEC by `query_id` runs the manifest's SQL on a session whose HELLO carried the matching hash, and the request must AGREE with the declaration — its `readonly`, and the pool it actually runs on (the transaction's pinned pool for a tx-scoped request) — or it is refused `Unsupported`: the engine trusts `readonly` for the fate it reports, and a query declared for one pool must not run on another.]* **Manifest-only mode** (`pools.<name>.manifest_only = true`): the engine executes only registered `query_id`s and rejects raw SQL — the injection surface collapses to zero for that pool, and compromised PHP cannot exfiltrate beyond the declared query set. Ships as a hardening option, off by default.

*[Amended 2026-10-06 (D21, §24.10): `idempotent: true` is the sole licence for auto-retrying an `Indeterminate` **SQL** write. Ferro HTTP's and Ferro Queue's licences are defined in their own sections (§23.7.2, §24.6) under D21 — each a declaration or a protocol-defined property of an engine-composed operation, never an inference. **Manifest-only pools accept Ferro Queue verbs:** they are a closed, engine-authored operation set, not raw SQL (§24.10, D22). On any other pool a queue store adds no capability boundary, because an admitted peer can already EXEC against the same table. **Cost, stated:** a store declared on a manifest-only pool widens that pool's surface by all seven queue verbs, none of which the manifest declares — `RESERVE` reads any job payload and `CLEAR` deletes a whole queue (§24.10). Manifest-only mode itself is M4 (ledger E2), after M7.]*

## 12. Security model

- UDS with `SO_PEERCRED` allow-list (uid/gid) + optional bearer token for TCP fallback.
- **Credential isolation:** DB DSNs/passwords live only in `ferrod`'s config/secret store. PHP never sees them; an FPM compromise leaks no database credentials, and with manifest-only mode cannot even issue arbitrary queries. Consequence for ecosystem tooling that shells out with credentials: §14–15 and D8.
- TLS to upstreams (rustls), per-pool CA/cert config, `sslmode` equivalents.
- memfd payloads are sealed (§5.1) and fds pass only over peer-cred-verified sockets; at-rest encryption of these buffers is out of scope for v1 (D6).
- Per-pool guards: `readonly = true` pools set backend read-only session state and reject classified writes; `max_result_bytes`, `default_timeout_ms` are pool policy.

*[Amended 2026-10-06 (§23.3, §23.4, §23.8, §24.10): **Ferro HTTP extends credential isolation to API credentials, with its limits stated.** Attached headers come from operator-owned files, never environment values, and are never logged or exported. The isolation is only as real as the uid split: a PHP-FPM that runs as `ferrod`'s uid can read those files or the daemon's memory, and `ferrod` warns at startup when its allow-lists make that possible (§23.3.3). An upstream endpoint that reflects request headers returns the credential to PHP (§23.3.4). PHP can reach only declared upstreams, at their declared origin, permitted path prefixes and methods, after an address guard that refuses metadata and special-purpose ranges and pins each connection to the address it checked (§23.4, §23.8.5); the query string and body-level method overrides are outside that guarantee. **A recorded gap, not in v1:** per-pool and per-store uid allow-lists. Upstreams have `ALLOW_UIDS`, but any admitted peer may use any database pool or queue store (§23.18 Q4, §24.10). The upstream-TLS bullet above is still unbuilt for the database backends, which run `NoTls`; Ferro HTTP's TLS (§23.8.8) is the first TLS stack in the tree.]*

## 13. Observability

- OTLP traces: one span per EXEC (`query_id`/fingerprint, pool, backend conn id, rows, bytes, `queue_us`/`exec_us` split), linked to the caller's `traceparent` passed in EXEC options. **The link's transport landed first (M2-C4c-1, §22.2 (cd))**: `traceparent` is `ExecRequest`'s ninth field (flat — see (cd)), parsed by the engine against the W3C grammar, dropped and counted in `ferro_traceparent_invalid_total` when it does not parse — never refused — and recorded in the slow log as `trace_id`/`parent_span_id`. The PHP client fills it from `Ferro\Client\TraceContext`'s process-wide provider, read once per EXEC. **The spans landed at M2-C4c-2 (§22.2 (ce)):** exported as OTLP/HTTP JSON to an OpenTelemetry Collector, configured by OpenTelemetry's standard `OTEL_*` variables but OFF until an endpoint is set; a caller's sampled context gets a span as its CHILD and an unsampled one gets none, and with no context nothing is exported unless `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER=parentbased_always_on`. Attributes: `db.system.name`, `db.query.text` (the FINGERPRINT — never raw SQL), `ferro.pool`, `db.response.returned_rows`, `ferro.rows_affected`, `ferro.response_bytes` (§13's "bytes"), `ferro.queue_us`/`ferro.exec_us`, `ferro.fetch`, `ferro.in_tx`, `ferro.readonly`, and on failure `error.type` with the registry CODE NAME (never the message). **The backend connection id is NOT exported in M2**: no backend exposes one through the `PoolBackend` seam (PostgreSQL's pid would need a fork accessor), so it is named here rather than invented. Counters: `ferro_otlp_spans_{exported,dropped,failed}_total`. The span's pool, family and query text come from the ENGINE (the registry, or the tx the statement is pinned to), never the request; with no resolvable pool there is no query text, because the dialect decides where a literal ends.
- Prometheus: pool size/idle/pinned, checkout p50/p99, queue depth, pin duration histogram, **pin-cause counters** (labels: `tx, set, listen, lock, prepare, temp, unknown, pin_function, session_tracker`), **hygiene counters** (`skipped_clean, targeted, full`), statement-cache hit rate, replica lag, error-taxonomy counters including `indeterminate_total`, engine `boot_epoch` gauge and restart counter. **Three of these are structurally not exportable in M2 and are NAMED here rather than exported as a zero** (M2-C4b-2b, §22.2 (bv)): *statement-cache hit rate* — §7.3's engine-owned cache is not built: PostgreSQL and SQLite statements are not cached by the engine (`tokio-postgres`'s internal typeinfo cache is not one), and on the MySQL family the driver's own per-connection `COM_STMT_PREPARE` LRU (`mysql_async`, 32 entries, emptied on every recycle by `COM_RESET_CONNECTION`) exists but exposes no hit/miss counter, so exporting even that needs a vendored-fork accessor — there is no honest rate to report until §7.3 is built; *replica lag* — there are no replicas until §7.5's routing (M4); and a *restart counter* — a process cannot count its own restarts without a stats file, which product-vision §5 forbids, so restarts are derived where they are observed, as `changes(ferro_boot_epoch[...])` in PromQL. Histogram bucket bounds are recorded in `ferro-pool`'s `histogram.rs`.
- Slow log: normalized fingerprints, redacted params by default (`log_params = never|on_error|always`). **`always` means every parameter's TYPE and LENGTH, never its bytes** (M2-C4a): the operator who flips it to debug a slow query is not consenting on behalf of the data subject, and a type plus a length identifies which bind went wrong — which is what the setting is for — without the log becoming a place secrets live. A failed statement's error is likewise labelled by its `PoolError` VARIANT, never its message, because a backend's error text quotes the offending value (PostgreSQL's unique-violation detail is `Key (email)=(alice@example.com) already exists`). Emitted through the daemon's existing `tracing` subscriber; `FERRO_LOG_FORMAT=json` selects product-vision §5's structured-JSON stream. **The fingerprint is read in the statement's own DIALECT and fails closed (M2-C4c-2 review, §22.2 (ce))**: where a literal's end depends on a session setting the engine cannot see (`standard_conforming_strings`, `NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES`), the rest of the statement is hidden; on MySQL and SQLite a double-quoted token is shown as `?` because it may be a string; and only a statement's first 4 KiB are fingerprinted, marked ` …` when cut.
- `ferro top`: live TUI over the admin service (current pins, longest transactions, hot fingerprints).

*[Amended 2026-10-06 (§23.10, §24.9): **each engine exports its own family under the same redaction contract.** Ferro HTTP: one OTLP CLIENT span per `REQUEST`, which exports no URL, path, query or header (a caller-declared route template only); the `ferro_http_*` metrics with a `cause` label from the closed `[http.causes]` vocabulary; and its own slow-log threshold, `FERRO_HTTP_SLOW_LOG_MS`. `ferro_errors_total{code,branch}` keeps counting every service's error terminals at the one terminal builder (§22.2 (bu)), HTTP's and the queue's included; `ferro_http_errors_total` adds HTTP's `upstream` and `cause` labels. Ferro Queue: one span per queue request, the `ferro_queue_*` metrics (a queue name is a label only when the operator lists it) and slow-log records for queue verbs; payloads, dedup keys and job class names are never exported. **Stated cost:** enabling `FERRO_METRICS_ADDR` publishes every configured upstream and store name to every local uid, which D15's indistinguishability rule otherwise hides (§23.10.2).]*

## 14. Doctrine tier (`ferro/doctrine-dbal-driver`)

**Goal:** existing Doctrine DBAL / ORM applications switch by configuration only. The ORM's identity map, lazy proxies, and hydrators are untouched (rows cross the wire as arrays at this tier; typed-DTO hydration is a native-API feature). The wins here are pooling, multiplexing, shared statement cache, and normalized errors.

**Surface.** Implements the DBAL driver SPI, **DBAL `^4.0` first** (D2). The `^3.8` bridge landed in M2 as a SECOND `driverClass`, `Ferro\DBAL\Dbal3\Driver`, over the same connection core — one class cannot implement both majors' SPI, because PHP checks the signatures when the class is declared (§22.2 (by)). The bullets below are written against DBAL 4; **the last one lists every place DBAL 3 differs**, and §22.2 (by) records why each does:

- `Ferro\DBAL\Driver` → `connect()` returns a `Ferro\DBAL\Connection` bound to a pool session. `getDatabasePlatform()` is handed a `Doctrine\DBAL\ServerVersionProvider` — **not** the connection, so it cannot read `HELLO_ACK` itself; the driver remembers the pool KIND from the last `connect()` and combines it with the version string, which is normalised **for PostgreSQL only** (the MySQL-family string is load-bearing: MariaDB is detected by the `MariaDB` substring, so normalising `11.8.8-MariaDB-…` to `11.8.8` would silently select the MySQL platform, i.e. a different dialect). `getExceptionConverter()` maps the §9.2 tree to DBAL exceptions **by DELEGATING to Doctrine's own per-family converters** (SQLSTATE on PostgreSQL, vendor errno on MySQL) and adding exactly three Ferro rules — intercept `Indeterminate` first, take the stock answer second, and upgrade a `Retryable` the stock table returned as a bare `DriverException`. There is deliberately **no Ferro-owned SQLSTATE table**. `Ferro\DBAL\IndeterminateWriteException` serves the third branch and must never implement `RetryableException`.
- `Connection`: `prepare()`, `query()`, `exec()`, `lastInsertId()`, `beginTransaction()/commit()/rollBack()` → TX service frames (savepoints via DBAL's normal savepoint path, riding the same pinned `tx_id`); `quote()` client-side per FAMILY (D5; discouraged, present for compat); `getServerVersion()` per the decision below; `getNativeConnection()` returns the `Ferro\Client\Connection` the driver holds — **documented break** for code expecting a `PDO` instance, and note that it does **not** escape the driver's type boundary (it carries the driver's `ValuePolicy`, so a value the driver refuses is refused there too; open a client connection of your own to read one).
- `Statement`/`Result`: `bindValue()` maps to the canonical tags keyed on the **PAIR** `(ParameterType, PHP type)` — `ParameterType` alone is not a type, since `BOOLEAN` arrives carrying `int(1)` and `STRING` arrives carrying a float, an int, a numeric string, a date string, a JSON document or a UUID. `BINARY` and `LARGE_OBJECT` bind through `Ferro\Bytes`, the only route to `TAG_BYTES` from PHP. `Result::fetch*()` families are backed by row frames; `rowCount()` comes from `affected` and never from `count($rows)`. Streaming is used when the consumer iterates, on **every family and on both the parameterless and the prepared path** — (ac)'s bound to the parameterless path was lifted when its premise measured false ((ag), (ah)), and MySQL streams since Phase B; `exec()` alone stays buffered, because its contract is the affected count.
- **DBAL 3 (`^3.8`) — the differences, and only those.** `Ferro\DBAL\Dbal3\Driver` implements `VersionAwarePlatformDriver`, so a platform comes from `createDatabasePlatformForVersion(string)`, and its version-less `getDatabasePlatform()` REFUSES rather than guess a dialect before any connection — which is why a Symfony app must set `charset` (inert for Ferro) and drop `dbname_suffix`: DoctrineBundle's `ConnectionFactory` calls it otherwise, on DBAL 4 too in its `ServerVersionProvider` form. Its connection implements `ServerInfoAwareConnection`. `lastInsertId()` throws a plain driver exception where DBAL 3 allows `false` (Doctrine ORM 2 casts `false` to the key `0`; 3.x has no `NoIdentityValue`), and accepts but does not use a sequence name. Nested transactions use savepoints only if the application calls `setNestTransactionsWithSavepoints(true)` — DBAL 3's default is off, for every driver. BEGIN/COMMIT/ROLLBACK failures are converted by the DRIVER, because DBAL 3's wrapper never converts them; and a rollback after a COMMIT that failed and ended the transaction is a no-op, because DBAL before 3.9.4 rolls back from a `catch` around the commit and the error would otherwise replace the commit's fate. `quote()` refuses `BINARY`/`LARGE_OBJECT`. The isolation `wrapperClass` is `Ferro\DBAL\Dbal3\FerroConnection`. Naming the wrong major's class fails when `DriverManager::getConnection()` loads it.

**Configuration:**

```php
// Doctrine / Symfony
'connections' => ['default' => [
    'driverClass'   => Ferro\DBAL\Driver::class,
    'unix_socket'   => '/run/ferro/app.sock',    // or driverOptions.socket; host+port for TCP
    'driverOptions' => [
        'pool'            => 'main',
        'readonly'        => false,              // the charter-compliant read/write split
        'connect_timeout' => 2.0,
        'io_timeout'      => 5.0,
    ],
    // 'serverVersion' => 'PostgreSQL 17.10',                 // optional, zero round trip; PG only —
    //                                                        // a bare '17.10', '8.4.11' or '3.53.2' names no family
    // 'charset'       => 'utf8mb4',                          // inert for Ferro; set it under DoctrineBundle
    // 'wrapperClass'  => Ferro\DBAL\Wrapper\FerroConnection::class,  // only for setTransactionIsolation()
]],
```

`driverOptions` is the sanctioned extension point and there is **no top-level `ferro` key**: `Driver::connect()` is `@phpstan-param Params`, `Params` is a SEALED array shape, and reading `$params['ferro']['pool']` measured as two `nullCoalesce.offset` errors at PHPStan level 9 — a charter Definition-of-Done gate (§22.2 (y)).

There is likewise **no `read_pool` key.** Charter rule 6 forbids inferring read-vs-write from a statement, so the charter-compliant shape is a **second, explicitly configured connection** carrying `driverOptions.readonly = true`. That flag is not merely advisory: it is the §19.3 `readonly` declaration, and §22.2 (ac) records exactly what it costs a connection that does not set it.

**Semantics & compat notes:**

- `lastInsertId()`: MySQL from the OK packet of the last EXEC on the session; on **PostgreSQL**, whose protocol carries no such field, it answers INSIDE a transaction with `SELECT lastval()` (`currval(name)` for DBAL 3's named form) on the pinned connection — what `pdo_pgsql` runs, made safe by hygiene's `DISCARD SEQUENCES` at checkout — and OUTSIDE one it THROWS `Doctrine\DBAL\Driver\Exception\NoIdentityValue`, the SPI's own signal, because the emulation would land on a different pooled connection and return a silently wrong key; use `INSERT … RETURNING` or a transaction there. *[Amended M2 (§22.2 (ci)): this bullet said PostgreSQL "always throws", which made Doctrine ORM's default IDENTITY strategy unusable on PostgreSQL; the unit of work always inserts inside a transaction, where the answer is now correct.]* DBAL 4 removed the sequence-name overload (`lastInsertId()` takes no argument and must throw when there is no identity value), so the v0.1 sentence promising one is withdrawn (§22.2 (y)).
- **`getServerVersion()` — DECIDED (D-S8b-1).** `HELLO_ACK`'s `PoolInfo.server_version` is `str | nil` (PROTOCOL.md §4), and `nil` is a normal recurring value on a healthy system, not an error state: the engine learns it lazily and caches it with a TTL, so a TTL expiry racing a slow re-probe, a transient probe failure after expiry, or a backend that is simply down at connect all yield `nil` (§22.2 (v)). The driver converts this string into a PLATFORM — i.e. into which SQL grammar the whole tier emits — so a silent default platform is forbidden. The resolution order is: **from the handshake when the handshake has it; otherwise ONE `SELECT version()`; otherwise a loud `Ferro\DBAL\Exception\ServerVersionUnavailable` naming the pool.** Resolution is DEFERRED to the point of need rather than performed at connect, because failing at connect would turn a routine 5-second probe-backoff window into an outage for every worker reconnecting during it. DBAL's own `params['serverVersion']` is the documented zero-round-trip escape hatch and is named in the failure message.
