- **§20.3/§9.2/§19.3 MySQL chaos-fate parity DONE live on both engines; the S4 `fate.rs` matrix is UNCHANGED (M1-S6 Task 8)** — a live MySQL/MariaDB chaos suite (`engine/crates/ferrod/tests/mysql_chaos_it.rs`, the §20.3 acceptance parity of PG's `chaos_fate_it.rs`) proves the S4 write-fate matrix holds against real MySQL 8.4 + MariaDB 11.8 and that a write is applied AT MOST ONCE (a fresh `SELECT n` read-back of a run-unique counter row ∈ {0,1}, never ≥2 — MySQL has no `UPDATE … RETURNING`). All four §19.3 branches pass on BOTH engines: (1) a cancelled/timed-out **autocommit write** — via `ExecRequest.timeout_ms` OR a client `CANCEL` frame → the backend's out-of-band `KILL QUERY` → errno `1317` → `error_map` → `is_57014` → `WriteUnconfirmed{Indeterminate}`; (2) an **in-tx** timeout → the actor's cancel→drain→ROLLBACK→tombstone → the ONE `TxDeadline{Retryable}` terminal, read-back 0; (3) a **deadlock (`1213`)** between two bare-`BEGIN` txs cross-locking in opposite order → InnoDB kills one victim → `Deadlock{Retryable}`, never Indeterminate, both rows end == 1 (at-most-once); (4) a **connection `KILL <id>`** mid-write → transport error → `is_fatal` → `ConnectionLost` → dispatched autocommit write → `Indeterminate`, session survives. `fate.rs` was reused **verbatim** (charter rule 2/6) — no edit. **Two MySQL-specific chaos mechanics (verified live, recorded for future maintainers):** (a) `KILL QUERY` interrupting a bare `SELECT SLEEP(n)` makes `SLEEP` return `1` WITHOUT erroring the statement (unlike PG's `pg_sleep`→`57014`), so a MySQL chaos write is held in flight on a **row lock** (a raw side connection's `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`), and `KILL QUERY` on a lock-WAITING `UPDATE` errors `1317` — the reliable interruptible-with-error mechanism; (b) **MariaDB strips comments** from `information_schema.processlist.INFO` (MySQL preserves them) but BOTH preserve a **string literal**, so the mandatory in-flight false-green guard (`wait_for_active_conn`) matches a `'<marker>' <> ''` predicate embedded in the write SQL, not an inert comment — and a binary-protocol `COM_STMT_EXECUTE` reports `COMMAND = 'Execute'` (not `'Query'`) so the guard filters on `INFO` alone. The `ferro` testkit user needs no `PROCESS` grant (same-account processlist visibility + `KILL` suffice; `innodb_trx`, which would need `PROCESS`, is avoided). **tx-actor 1213-vs-1205 rollback RE-VERIFICATION (T4-review caveat):** MySQL `1213` (deadlock) auto-rolls-back the WHOLE tx while `1205` (lock-wait timeout, default `innodb_rollback_on_timeout=OFF`) rolls back only the STATEMENT and leaves the tx `InTx`. Both are re-verified live: the actor's explicit `co.rollback_tx()` teardown is idempotent against a `1213`-already-closed tx (a clean Ok, deadlock test) AND correct against a `1205`-left-open tx (dedicated `mysql_lock_wait_timeout_1205_tx_teardown_retryable` test: `1205` → one `SerializationFailure{Retryable}` terminal, then a clean ROLLBACK Ok, no double-terminal, no stuck-open tx). This task is TEST + spec only — no `fate.rs`/backend/tx-actor logic changed.
- **§9/§9.1 the M1 slice plan gains a dedicated CANONICAL TYPE-COVERAGE slice; the tail renumbers S7→S8→S9→S10 (M1 plan amendment, 2026-08-05)** — the M1 execution design originally went S6 (MySQL) → S7 (Doctrine DBAL-4 driver) → S8 (DBAL upstream suite green = the exit gate). S6's close-out research established that the **M0 scalar set (`NULL/BOOL/I64/F64/TEXT/BYTES`) is a hard end-to-end boundary on BOTH backends and in PHP**: PG's `oid_to_tag` raises a loud `Unsupported` for every OID outside it (`ferro-backend-pg/src/rowmap.rs`, e.g. `TIMESTAMPTZ`/`UUID`/`NUMERIC`/`JSONB`), the S6 MySQL policy does the same by column metadata (§9.1 above), and the PHP `M0ValuePolicy` throws `ProtocolException` on every reserved M1 tag (`DECIMAL/DATE/TIME/TIMESTAMP/TIMESTAMPTZ/UUID/JSON/ARRAY/INTERVAL/INET/VECTOR/U64`). Since a Doctrine DBAL suite is saturated with dates, decimals and JSON, the exit gate as written ("the DBAL 4 suite is GREEN modulo a documented allow-list") was **not reachable** — the allow-list would have absorbed most of the suite and the M1 exit condition would have proven little. Type coverage is therefore promoted to its own slice with its own gate, ahead of the driver, because it is a distinct subsystem (§9/§9.1) that BOTH drop-in tiers (§14 DBAL, §15 Eloquent) stand on. **New numbering:** S7 = canonical type coverage (`U64`, `DECIMAL`, `DATE`, `TIME`, `TIMESTAMP`, `TIMESTAMPTZ`, `UUID`, `JSON` end-to-end on both backends + the §9.1 policies `decimal`/`naive_datetime_zone`/`u64_overflow`/`uuid` (**client-side** as shipped — see the slice-wide M1-S7 entry below) + a golden vector per tag in both codecs per charter rule 2; `ARRAY`/`INTERVAL`/`INET`/`VECTOR` stay explicitly deferred as PG-exotic and not required for a green DBAL suite); S8 = the Doctrine DBAL-4 driver, which also carries the S6 deferrals (MySQL `query_stream`, dialect-aware isolation `BEGIN`, errno-on-wire, `HELLO_ACK` pool metadata for backend kind + server version, an imperative `begin/commit/rollBack` client API, `last_insert_id` end-to-end, and the tracker-clean `None`-skip re-evaluation); S9 = the DBAL upstream suite + incompat doc (**still the M1 exit gate**, unchanged in substance); S10 = the conditional accelerator. The §9 canonical table and §9.1 policy set are unchanged — this slice IMPLEMENTS them rather than altering them. See `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-ferro-m1-execution-design.md` §5.
- **§9/§9.1 canonical type coverage as SHIPPED — the slice-wide M1-S7 entry** (supersedes and folds in the Task 6 note) — S7 takes `U64`, `DECIMAL`, `DATE`, `TIME`, `TIMESTAMP`, `TIMESTAMPTZ`, `UUID` and `JSON` from registry constants to working end to end on PostgreSQL and MySQL/MariaDB, in both directions, with the §9.1 policies. Where the build differs from, narrows, or widens the design in the body above:

  **(a) The wire payload is TEXT-CANONICAL, not a native binary or a msgpack map/ext (USER-DECIDED).** Every S7 tag rides the msgpack `str` family carrying the canonical text of `proto/PROTOCOL.md` §3.2; `U64` alone rides the uint family. *Why:* PHP's pure decoder (`PurePacker`) cannot decode msgpack **maps or ext types at all** and §2 of PROTOCOL.md bans ext outright; `str` and `bin` are **indistinguishable in PHP after unpack** (the TypedValue tag is the only discriminator), which would force a `list<int>` special case and make the payload un-round-trippable through the golden-vector JSON; and text is the only form that preserves what actually matters — a `DECIMAL`'s **display scale** (`1.10` ≠ `1.1`, which DBAL string-compares), a 131 072-digit PG numeric, `NaN`/`±Infinity`, PG's legal `24:00:00`, and a MySQL `TIME`'s ±838 h signed range. The codecs move that text verbatim and validate nothing beyond UTF-8: the rendering decision lives in the backend, where the source format is known.

  **(b) The implemented-tag SET is part of the hashed registry (USER-DECIDED).** `/proto/types.toml` gained a real, parsed `implemented` list (replacing the dead, never-parsed `m0_scalar` key) that feeds `registry.lock.json` and therefore `TYPE_REGISTRY_HASH`. An engine/client pair with different type coverage now fails **fast at the handshake** with a registry-mismatch error instead of throwing a confusing `ProtocolException` mid-query on the first `DECIMAL` row. The list is sorted on parse so a cosmetic reorder cannot mint a spurious handshake failure. Because the hash is a *claim of coverage*, the acceptance gate (`engine/crates/ferrod/tests/types_e2e_it.rs`) DERIVES its per-tag assertions from that same list and fails if any declared tag is not both produced and consumed live.

  **(c) MySQL/MariaDB sessions are pinned to `time_zone = '+00:00'`; `NOW()`/`CURDATE()`/`CURTIME()` are UTC.** Forced by §9 mapping MySQL `timestamp` → `TIMESTAMPTZ` (a UTC instant) while the driver hands back zone-*less* components the server converted into the *session* zone — and required for pooling determinism, since an unpinned zone makes the same column read differently per connection. The pin is re-applied after every `COM_RESET_CONNECTION` and is what makes both the read renderer and the typed date/time **bind** truthful; the two are coupled (remove the pin and every `TIMESTAMPTZ` write silently shifts). The UTC `NOW()` is a visible drop-in behaviour change for a legacy application, and it is the same choice Doctrine and Laravel make.

  **(d) The naive/instant bind rule.** `TIMESTAMP` hydrates to `Ferro\NaiveTimestamp extends \DateTimeImmutable`; a plain `\DateTimeInterface` **binds** as `TIMESTAMPTZ` (an instant, normalized to UTC) while a `NaiveTimestamp` binds back as `TIMESTAMP` verbatim. The subclass exists solely as that discriminator — without it, every value read from a naive column and written straight back would shift by whatever zone the object carried, silently. MySQL's temporal params are bound as **typed** `MYSQL_TYPE_DATETIME`/`TIME` components, never as canonical text: both engines reject a `Z`-suffixed datetime literal (MySQL 8 `1292` under `STRICT_TRANS_TABLES`, MariaDB rejects offsets outright).

  **(e) Documented per-engine asymmetries (measured, by design — promoting any of them would be the silent miscast charter rule 6 forbids).** **MySQL 8 has no native UUID type** — `BINARY(16)` stays `BYTES`, `CHAR(36)` stays `TEXT`. **MariaDB's native `UUID`/`INET4`/`INET6` reach the wire as `MYSQL_TYPE_STRING`/utf8mb4**, byte-indistinguishable from a `CHAR(36)` without extended metadata the driver does not expose, so they classify **`TEXT`** (not `Unsupported`, which is what the S7 plan originally predicted). **MariaDB `JSON` is a `LONGTEXT` alias plus a `json_valid()` CHECK** and likewise classifies **`TEXT`**, so S8's DBAL `json` mapping must be designed against that. Net rule: **no MySQL-family backend ever emits the `UUID` tag**, and the `JSON` tag is MySQL-8-only. Both engines still *consume* `UUID`/`JSON` params (bound into `CHAR(36)`/`LONGTEXT`), which the acceptance gate exercises. Separately, **MySQL 8's default `sql_mode` includes `NO_ZERO_DATE`/`NO_ZERO_IN_DATE`** while MariaDB 11's does not, so writing the legal-but-zero `'0000-00-00'` sentinel on MySQL needs an explicit per-statement `SET SESSION sql_mode = ''` (which the live suite does, on both engines, rather than skipping the case on one). **And the third asymmetry is PostgreSQL's: PG has no unsigned integer type, so `U64` is neither PRODUCED nor BOUND there.** No `RowDescription` OID classifies as `U64` (`ferro-backend-pg`'s `rowmap.rs` has no arm for the tag), and on the bind side `PgU64Text::accepts` is `false` for EVERY type (`engine/crates/ferro-backend-pg/src/bind.rs`) — a deliberate **known-fate, pre-send rejection** rather than a widening guess, because `int8` cannot hold the top half of the `u64` range and `numeric` would silently change the column's type semantics. The newtype exists (rather than an `unreachable!()`) only to keep `value_to_boxed` total, so a caller that skipped the pre-flight gets a typed `WrongType` error, never a panic. The acceptance gate (`ferrod`'s `types_e2e_it.rs`) therefore excludes `U64` from PG in BOTH its produce and its bind matrix, citing this entry.

  **(f) Dates outside `0001-01-01 .. 9999-12-31` are a loud `Unsupported`.** The reachable data class is **BC dates and years above 9999** (PG's `date`/`timestamp` span 4713 BC .. 5874897 AD). PROTOCOL.md §3.2's canonical `YYYY-MM-DD` defines no form for them, and inventing one (a `" BC"` suffix, a negative year, a 7-digit year) would differ between the two codecs — so the decoder refuses, naming the year. Follow-up when a real workload needs it: either extend PROTOCOL.md §3.2 with an extended-year/BC canonical form (both codecs, new golden vectors) or add a per-pool opt-in raw passthrough.

  **(g) PG DOMAINs are NOT deferred, and since M1-S8a they are readable AND bindable.** PG resolves a domain to its BASE type when it builds the `RowDescription`, so a domain over a supported base READS correctly with no unwrap anywhere (`numeric(10,2)` domain → `DECIMAL`; a domain over `timetz` → the same `Unsupported` its base gets). It does **not** do that for parameters: `stmt.params()` reports the DOMAIN's own OID and `bind::check_param` matched the target against a NARROW, explicitly-listed set of `Type`s per canonical tag (OID identity, **plus one legacy name-based clause inside `postgres-types`' own `&str` impl** — `matches!(ty.name(), "citext"|"ltree"|"lquery"|"ltxtquery")` — so a domain literally NAMED `citext` did bind as text even before this change; harmless, since pre-flight and impl agreed, but it is why "a domain's own OID is in none of them" is not a literally exhaustive statement of the rule) — a domain's own OID is in none of those lists — so binding the very value just read back into that column was refused. **RESOLVED in M1-S8a (Task 5) — a domain now READS *and* BINDS.** `bind::resolve_domain` is a BOUNDED loop (`MAX_DOMAIN_DEPTH = 8`; a domain over a domain is legal PG, and an unbounded walk on a pathological `Type` must never spin the daemon inside a pre-flight) applied on **BOTH sides of EVERY arm** — in `check_param` AND inside the concrete `ToSql` each `Value` boxes as. **Applying it on one side only would have MANUFACTURED the very defect the pre-flight exists to prevent, and this is the load-bearing part of the entry:** `postgres-types` has ZERO `Kind::Domain` handling of its own (measured on PG 17: `<String as ToSql>::accepts(domain_over_text)`, `<bool …>` over a domain-of-`bool` and `<Vec<u8> …>` over a domain-of-`bytea` are all `false`), so resolving in the pre-flight while boxing `Bool`/`Text`/`Bytes` as bare `postgres-types` impls makes the pre-flight LOOSER than the impl it fronts — §19.3's forbidden direction — and `to_sql_checked` then fails with an error carrying no `DbError`, which `is_session_fatal` reads as `ConnectionLost`, which §19.3 turns into a **false `Indeterminate` on a write that never left the process**. Measured: with that half-applied shape in place, the live domain bind returns `ConnectionLost`. Those three tags therefore box through Ferro-owned wrappers (`PgBool`/`PgText`/`PgBytes`), `PgInt`/`PgFloat` resolve in both of their methods, and the seven canonical-text newtypes resolve in the one macro body. **`check_param` delegates each arm's `accepts` the DECLARED type, never a pre-resolved one** — found by mutation-proving `MAX_DOMAIN_DEPTH`: pre-resolving there and then delegating to a newtype that resolves again unwraps `2 × MAX_DOMAIN_DEPTH` levels against the impl's one `MAX_DOMAIN_DEPTH`, so a domain nested `MAX+1 ..= 2×MAX` deep would pass the pre-flight and be refused by `to_sql_checked` — the same false-`Indeterminate` direction, at a different depth. Delegating `ty` makes the pre-flight bit-identical to `to_sql_checked`'s own predicate by construction. The unwrap WIDENS NOTHING: a domain over an unsupported base (`timetz`) is still a loud pre-send `Sql{Unsupported}`, whose message now names BOTH halves (`s8a_dttz (domain over timetz)`), and a chain nested past the bound is a refusal, never a hang and never a yes. **Guards.** A derived cross-product asserting that for every `(value, non-domain target)` pair the pre-flight, the boxed impl AND the emitted payload BYTES are identical for the base and for a synthetic domain over it; the directional lockstep proof grown with domains over `int4`/`numeric`/`text`/`bool`/`bytea` and a nested one — the `text`/`bool`/`bytea` entries proven load-bearing by the converse mutation (with only `int4`/`numeric` present, the half-applied bug certifies GREEN). **Correction (Task 5 acceptance review, fix round 1): “all mutation-proven” was an OVER-CLAIM as first written, and the correction is recorded rather than quietly reworded.** The cross-product guard does fire as a UNIT — mutating the pre-flight clause reddens it — but its payload-BYTES sub-assertion was **structurally unfalsifiable**: every entry `every_target_type()` then held is bound by a `ToSql` whose `to_sql` IGNORES the `Type` it is handed, so changing `pg_domain_aware_param`'s `to_sql` to encode against the UNRESOLVED declared type left all 19 offline tests GREEN. It bites now because the fixture carries one NAME-SENSITIVE target, `ltree`: `<&str as ToSql>::to_sql` switches on `ty.name()` and prefixes a version byte for `ltree`/`lquery`/`ltxtquery`, so the unresolved path writes `[120]` where the base writes `[1, 120]` — measured RED under exactly that mutation, GREEN restored. **The daemon-level lock carried the same blind spot, one layer up, and was widened in the same round:** `types_e2e_it::pg_domain_reads_and_binds` exercised only a domain over `numeric`, which routes through the Ferro-owned `PgDecimalText` and therefore stayed GREEN with the boxed `Bool`/`Text`/`Bytes` arms reverted to bare `postgres-types` impls; it now also reads and re-binds through domains over `text`/`bool`/`bytea`, and under that revert the daemon returns exactly the false `ConnectionLost`→`Indeterminate` this entry describes (measured: `code 8193, branch 2` on the new bind, while the pre-existing `numeric` bind still passed). Two operator-facing MESSAGE defects were fixed in the same round — both loud and known-fate, neither a safety issue: a chain nested past `MAX_DOMAIN_DEPTH` rendered as `dom_nest_8 (domain over dom_nest_0)`, naming an inner DOMAIN as if it were the base type and never saying the nesting bound was what refused the bind (it now says so, deriving the bound from the const); and the `float4` UNDERFLOW refusal rendered its value with `Display`, spelling `f64`'s smallest subnormal out as ~330 characters of zeros (now `{:e}`). Both have their own mutation-proven assertions. Proven live on PostgreSQL 17: `ferro-backend-pg`'s `pg_types_it::s8a_narrowing_and_domain_binds_round_trip` (domains over `int4`/`text`/`bool`/`bytea`/`float4` and a domain-over-domain, read → bind → read byte-identical) and `ferrod`'s `types_e2e_it::pg_domain_reads_and_binds` end to end through the daemon.

  **(h) Still deferred, still a loud `Unsupported`:** tags `ARRAY`(14)/`INTERVAL`(15)/`INET`(16)/`VECTOR`(17) (PG-exotic, not required for a green DBAL suite; the PHP policy raises a named `ProtocolException` for each); PG `TIMETZ` (12-byte payload, no `FromSql` — it must never fall into the `TIME` arm), PG enums/composites/ranges (per-database CUSTOM OIDs, which a pure-OID match cannot see); MySQL/MariaDB `YEAR`/`BIT`/`SET`/`GEOMETRY`/`VECTOR`. **Correction (M1-S8a Task 6): MySQL/MariaDB `ENUM` left this list — it now classifies as `TEXT` (see §9.1 and entry (q)); PG `name`/`"char"`/`oid`/`regtype`/`regclass` were never in it but were previously unadmitted, and are now `TEXT`/`I64`.** **Correction (fix round 2), now WITHDRAWN (M1-S8a review round).** That correction read: "the PG custom-OID cases are a loud `Unsupported` only on a connection that has not yet run a full hygiene reset — once `DISCARD ALL` has run, a *second, distinct* custom OID on that connection degrades to a bare `26000` instead." That degradation no longer occurs: entry (m) is FIXED, and a custom OID is a loud `Unsupported` on every connection, reset or not, on both reset profiles. The qualification is withdrawn rather than deleted so a reader of an older revision can see it was retired deliberately. Every other case in this list is unaffected (all builtin OIDs; the MySQL classifier keys off column metadata, not a lookup).

  **(i) The four §9.1 policies are CLIENT-SIDE (Task 6, folded in here).** They are a `Ferro\Client\Value\TypePolicyOptions` threaded `Ferro::connect(types:)` → `Connection(types:)` → the decoding `ValuePolicy`; defaults unchanged (the safe object forms, `naive_datetime_zone=utc`). The originally-planned `PoolConfig` fields + `ferrod` env parsing were **dropped, not deferred**: the wire is policy-independent canonical text, so an operator setting one would observe nothing while a typo in that inert setting would stop `ferrod` booting — the worst of both. **Correction (M1-S8a Tasks 11/12): that S8 carry has now LANDED, and it does not unblock these.** `HELLO_ACK`'s pool metadata ships `[name, kind, server_version]` (entries (u) and (v)) — the backend family and the raw `version()` string. It carries no policy defaults and no session TIMEZONE. **`naive_datetime_zone: server` therefore remains UNIMPLEMENTED** — nothing on the wire carries the backend session zone — and is still rejected loudly by the constructor rather than silently downgraded to `utc`; it now needs a new metadata field of its own rather than a carry that already shipped. **The M1-S8a review round (finding F8) found this correction had been applied to the class DOCBLOCK and not to the operator-facing REFUSAL, which still shipped the sentence "(HELLO_ACK advertises no pool metadata yet)" — false in the same release that refuted it.** The message now states the real blocker, in the same words this entry and §9.1 use: `HELLO_ACK` DOES advertise per-pool metadata, and none of `[name, kind, server_version]` is a timezone. Whenever this blocker is restated anywhere, that is the reason — never "HELLO_ACK advertises no pool metadata". The guard behind it does not rest on the wording alone: `TypePolicyOptionsTest::testTheServerZoneRefusalNamesTheRealBlocker` also asserts `PoolInfo`'s field SET, so if a timezone field ever lands the policy becomes implementable and the test goes RED demanding the refusal be removed (measured: adding a `sessionTimeZone` field reddens it). **`error` is scoped to `TAG_TIMESTAMP` alone**, since an undefined scope would make whole columns unreadable with no escape hatch. **`u64_overflow: string` stringifies the WHOLE column**, not only overflowing values — a wart against the knob's name, chosen because a column's PHP type must not depend on a row's magnitude; `error` refuses only the overflow. The exception tree splits by **cause**: a malformed payload stays `ProtocolException` (a wire fault), an operator refusal is the new `TypePolicyException extends FerroException` — deliberately outside the §9.2 branches, because it is raised **client-side, never from a wire terminal**: *pre-send* in `ExecCodec::bindOne` (the symmetric `naive_datetime_zone=error` BIND refusal, thrown during `encode()` before the request leaves the client — nothing was executed) or *post-success* inside `ExecCodec::decodeRow` (mid-row on the streamed path, after the statement already succeeded). Either way it must never be read as a §19.3 fate signal or make S8's `ExceptionConverter` report a configuration choice as a driver protocol failure. Finally, **`Connection::typePolicy()` reports the options that connection was CONSTRUCTED with**: when a caller injects a ready `codec:` or `values:` (mutually exclusive with `types:`, each carrying its own embedded policy) it returns the DEFAULTS — a convenience accessor, not an authoritative read of the policy actually decoding rows.

  **(j) Two consequences of the value objects that S8 must plan around.** *A `TIMESTAMP`/`TIMESTAMPTZ` column can change its PHP type between rows* — a `DateTimeImmutable` normally, the canonical TEXT on a sentinel (`infinity`, a MySQL zero datetime) — which is a deliberate exception to the "a column must not change PHP type between rows" rationale (§9); the failure is LOUD (a `HydrationException` from the DTO path, a known-fate bind refusal on write-back), never a silent miscast. *And MySQL's bind pre-flight is CANONICAL-SHAPE, not type-directed*: `COM_STMT_PREPARE` exposes no inferred parameter types, so there is no `accepts`-style per-slot check the way PG has one — a canonical `TEXT` param binds against ANY column and a mismatch is reported by the SERVER as an ordinary NonRetryable SQL error. What the engine DOES pre-flight client-side is arity plus each payload's canonical shape: a payload with no MySQL representation (PG's `infinity` / `NaN`, an over-range `TIME`) is refused **before the statement is sent**, as a known-fate `Sql{Unsupported}` — because deferring it to the server is only safe under `STRICT_TRANS_TABLES`; under a permissive `sql_mode` (which Doctrine never sets) the server silently COERCES it to `0000-00-00` / `838:59:59` / `0` instead of erroring, which would be a silent corrupt write (§22.2, measured on both engines).

  **(k) Sized S8 carries (named so the S9 DBAL gate is reduced, not moved).** **(1) DBAL's stock format strings do NOT match canonical text** — `PostgreSQLPlatform::getDateTimeTzFormatString()` is `Y-m-d H:i:sO`, which will not `createFromFormat` a canonical `2026-08-05T13:45:07.250000Z`, and `getDateTimeFormatString()`'s `Y-m-d H:i:s` rejects a `.250000` fraction — so the driver tier must supply its own conversion (or bind/return the §9 value objects) rather than leaning on the stock type layer. **(2) The narrowing bind path (the highest-frequency S8 blocker) — CLOSED in M1-S8a. Both halves shipped: the WIDTH half, see (p), and the `Kind::Domain` unwrap from (g).** `Value::I64` used to box as `i64`→`int8`, so binding a PHP `int` against an `int4`/`int2`/serial column was refused, and DBAL generates exactly that on every insert into a `serial` PK. That half is fixed. A domain-typed column is now bindable too — see (g) for the both-sides rule that makes it safe — so nothing of this carry remains. **(3) PG catalog scalars — CLOSED in M1-S8a (Task 6), together with the MySQL/MariaDB `ENUM` half of the same blocker; see entry (q).** `name`(19) and `"char"`(18) map to `TEXT`, `oid`(26)/`regtype`(2206)/`regclass`(2205) to `I64`. The one judgement call worth recording: **`reg*` report the NUMERIC oid, not the type name.** Their binary payload is a bare 4-byte OID (`regtypesend` **is** `oidsend`), PG's result format is binary and not per-statement selectable, and resolving the name would mean a catalog round trip the engine must not make (charter rule 6) — so the numeric OID is the only truthful report. A caller wanting the name casts in SQL (`::text` / `format_type(...)`), which is what DBAL's own introspection already does. **(4) `Ferro\Bytes` / an explicit binary-bind marker — CLOSED in M1-S8a (Task 10).** `ExecCodec::bindOne` mapped *every* PHP string to `TAG_TEXT`, so `TAG_BYTES` was unreachable from PHP and `ParameterType::BINARY`/`LARGE_OBJECT` could not bind at all — a binary payload did not merely mis-bind, it failed at the CODEC (`str` + `String::from_utf8`) as a generic `malformed ExecRequest`. `Ferro\Bytes` (a `readonly string` value object, plus `Bytes::fromStream()` for DBAL's `LARGE_OBJECT` resource) is the marker; a **bare** string still binds `TEXT` unconditionally, because a string's contents are never sniffed for a tag, and there is deliberately **no implicit `is_resource` bind arm** — materialising a stream is the caller's decision, so the refusal names `Ferro\Bytes` instead. The same marker shape is what would let a temporal sentinel re-bind with its tag intact. Reads stay asymmetric on purpose: a `bytea`/`VARBINARY`/`BLOB` column hydrates to a plain PHP `string`, so a round trip is `Bytes` out, `string` back. **Two latent packer defects the new call path made reachable were fixed in the same change** (the `packUint` class, S7): (a) `ExtPacker::packBin` was `\msgpack_pack()`, which emits msgpack **`str`** (measured on pecl msgpack 3.0.0 — the extension's `bin` emitter has no callers), while the engine's BYTES reader is marker-strict `read_bin`; it now delegates to `PurePacker::packBin`. This one is unreachable in production — `PackerFactory::forEncode()` returns `PurePacker` unconditionally — so its only coverage is a unit test that skips without ext-msgpack. (b) `SqlValueCodec::encode`'s BYTES arm was `bytesFromInts($data)` alone, whose `listOf()` returns `[]` for a string, so a raw byte-string payload encoded as `c400` — an **EMPTY bin**, a silent corrupt WRITE — on the DEFAULT (`PurePacker`) encode path. It now accepts either shape (the raw byte string that `bindOne` emits, or the golden-vector/`fromWire` `list<int>`) and throws on anything else. **The READ-side mirror landed in the M1-S8a review round**, because the encode-side fix left its twin open: `SqlValueCodec::fromWire`'s `TAG_BYTES` arm was `intsFromBytes(is_string($data) ? $data : '')`, so ANY non-`bin` payload — `nil`, an int, a float, a bool, an array — decoded silently to an EMPTY blob, making a codec fault and a legitimately-empty blob indistinguishable on the read path and persisting the emptiness on a read → write-back round trip. It now throws `CodecException` (surfacing to the caller as `ProtocolException` through `ExecCodec::decode`, which is where a wire fault belongs), with a POSITIVE control asserting a real `bin` — including the legitimately-empty one — still decodes, so the refusal is not blanket.
  **(l) Fix round 1 — the MySQL non-canonical bind branch is a PRE-SEND rejection, because a permissive `sql_mode` COERCES rather than errors (Task 8b review).** As first built, `ferro-backend-mysql`'s `bind.rs` passed a non-parseable canonical payload through as a byte-string param, on the reasoning that the server would then raise its own clean, known-fate error. That holds only under `STRICT_TRANS_TABLES`. **Measured live on both engines under `SET SESSION sql_mode = ''`** — the mode a legacy stack routinely runs in, since Doctrine sets no `sql_mode` — the server accepted every one of them and silently stored a coerced value: `Date("infinity")`/`Timestamp("infinity")` -> `0000-00-00 [00:00:00]`, `Time("839:00:00")` -> `838:59:59` (MySQL) / `838:59:59.999999` (MariaDB), `Decimal("NaN")`/`Infinity` -> `0`. Reading a PG `date 'infinity'` and writing it to a MySQL pool would therefore have produced a **silent corrupt write** — the miscast charter rule 6 forbids — with the outcome depending on a server session variable. `bind::to_params` now returns `Result<Params, PoolError>` and the four temporal helpers plus a new `is_decimal_text` shape check refuse a payload with no MySQL representation, as a known-fate `Sql{Unsupported}` (branch `NonRetryable`, `sqlstate: None`) raised at the SAME pre-flight point as `validate_arity` in `query::run` — before `COM_STMT_EXECUTE`, so nothing is written and the connection stays clean (it can never mint a false §19.3 `Indeterminate`). Legal MySQL SENTINELS are explicitly NOT in the reject class and still bind verbatim: `'0000-00-00'`, `'0000-00-00 00:00:00'`, a zero-in-date `'2026-00-05'`, `24:00:00`/`26:00:00`/`-838:59:58.000001` TIME, `1.10` display scale, `U64` in both driver forms. Proof: `ferro-backend-mysql`'s `mysql_types_it.rs::non_canonical_payloads_are_rejected_pre_send_under_permissive_sql_mode`, live on MySQL 8 + MariaDB 11 under `sql_mode = ''`, asserting the typed rejection **and** a read-back showing no row. §9.1's "(j)" paragraph above is amended to match; PG, the read path and the PHP client are untouched.

  **(m) FIXED in the M1-S8a review round — was a SHIPPED DEFECT: PG's full hygiene profile (`DISCARD ALL`) permanently poisoned `tokio-postgres`'s typeinfo statement cache, so a custom-OID `Unsupported` degraded to a bare `26000` (belongs to M1-S3, surfaced by M1-S7 Task 9, re-characterised by the S7 acceptance review, re-armed by S8a's domain bind, CLOSED by the S8a review round — the resolution is at the end of this entry).** `DISCARD ALL` includes `DEALLOCATE ALL`, which destroys the prepared statements the driver caches internally for its **typeinfo** (`pg_type`) lookups — the queries it issues to resolve an OID it does not know natively. The driver keeps its own handles and never learns they are gone, so the next typeinfo lookup on that connection fails `SQLSTATE 26000 — prepared statement "sN" does not exist`. Reproduced live on a `max_size=1` PG pool with two DISTINCT custom OIDs across a taint: round 1 (`enum_a`) gives the correct `Unsupported("unsupported type for column \"v\": PG type zzz_enum_a (OID 88246)")`; round 2 (`enum_b`) gives `Sql{branch:3, sqlstate:"26000"}`; round 3 with **no further taint**, and a FRESH lease, gives the same `26000` — the connection is poisoned permanently, not merely order-dependently. Two things mask it: repeating the SAME OID short-circuits on the driver's OID→`Type` cache and never re-issues the lookup, and a DOMAIN over a builtin base **read** from a result set resolves to its base in the `RowDescription`, so no lookup happens on that path. **Correction (M1-S8a review round): the domain half of that sentence was originally written unqualified — "a DOMAIN … resolves to its base in the `RowDescription` so no lookup happens at all" — and that is true of the READ side ONLY.** `stmt.params()` reports a domain's own OID for a WRITE. Measured live on PG 17 via `pg_prepared_statements.parameter_types`: `INSERT INTO t (domcol) VALUES ($1)` → the DOMAIN, `UPDATE t SET domcol = $1` → the DOMAIN, `SELECT $1::domain` → the DOMAIN, but `SELECT … WHERE domcol = $1` → the BASE type. That write path is exactly what S8a (Task 5, entry (g)) made supported, and it is what re-armed this defect on ordinary traffic — a fact the original entry could not have known and the cross-slice reviewer measured on stock HEAD. **Blast radius was bounded and safety was intact throughout:** every tag M1-S7 ships maps to a BUILTIN OID (`numeric`, `date`, `time`, `timestamp`, `timestamptz`, `uuid`, `json`, `jsonb`) and builtin OIDs never perform a typeinfo lookup, so no shipped TYPE was ever affected; the failure is `NonRetryable` — never `Indeterminate`, never `ConnectionLost` — so there was no silent miscast, no double-apply and no cross-tenant leak. **But "no shipped type is affected" stopped being the whole story at S8a, which is the part worth carrying forward:** the trigger is a USER'S OWN domain used as a bind target, not one of the fourteen shipped tags, and Task 5 turned that from a refused corner into an advertised capability — so the reachable surface grew without any of the shipped-type reasoning changing. A "no shipped type is affected" argument is not a bound on reachability when a new capability can mint new OIDs into the traffic. **What WAS broken — until the resolution recorded at the end of this entry — is charter rule 6's contract of a loud, diagnosable failure:** the operator got a `26000` naming an internal statement handle instead of the column-and-type message Task 4b deliberately built, pointing the diagnosis at the wrong layer. §22.2 (h) above is corrected accordingly — **and that correction is itself now WITHDRAWN, see (h)** — and §7.2's `DISCARD ALL` bullet carried the caveat until this round. **This §22 line was owed at commit `d357461` (which filed the ticket alone); writing it is the charter DoD obligation this entry discharged.**

  **Correction to the SCALE claim (M1-S8a review round).** This entry originally said the ambush would come via `AbstractSchemaManager` hitting `information_schema` "and custom types constantly". Measured: reading `information_schema` triggers no typeinfo lookup at all (PG resolves its domains to their bases in the `RowDescription`), and an introspection query's `WHERE table_name = $1` parameter is inferred as plain `name`, not as the `sql_identifier` domain — so the DBAL introspection shape does **not** reach it. `information_schema` defines FIVE distinct domains on PG 17, not four (`cardinal_number` int4, `character_data` varchar, `sql_identifier` name, `time_stamp` timestamptz, `yes_or_no` varchar). The routine trigger is the ordinary one: a WRITE into a domain-typed column, twice with two DIFFERENT domains across one recycle.

  **RESOLVED (M1-S8a review round).** Of the three fix directions the follow-up ticket listed, direction 1 was taken — and it turned out to be ~10 additive lines: a THIRD `tokio-postgres` fork accessor, `Client::clear_typeinfo_statement_cache()`, which drops the three cached typeinfo `Statement` handles (`typeinfo`, `typeinfo_composite`, `typeinfo_enum`) so the next lookup RE-PREPARES, and deliberately leaves the oid→`Type` map alone (`DEALLOCATE ALL` destroys statements, not type definitions — it is the exact opposite half of upstream's existing `clear_type_cache`). `PgBackend::reset` calls it **before the reset batch, on BOTH profiles**: `Full` because `DISCARD ALL ⇒ DEALLOCATE ALL` is the pool-caused poisoning; `Targeted` because the pool is not the only party that deallocates — `ferro-classify` safe-lists a USER-issued `DISCARD ALL`/`DISCARD PLANS` by design (they move session state toward default), so such a connection recycles NON-tainted through `Targeted` carrying the same dead handles. That second arm was not in the review finding; it was found while fixing it and is equally permanent. Before, not after, the batch: each dropped handle sends its usual `Close`, a real deallocation while the statement exists and an accepted no-op once it does not, so the driver can never hold a handle to a statement the server dropped even if the batch fails partway. Directions 2 and 3 were REJECTED: (2) composing the discard without `DEALLOCATE ALL` trades a cache-coherence bug for a §7.4 leak class on connections already known dirty; (3) retiring a fully-reset connection is a reconnect on a hot path (charter rule 5) **and** would not have covered the user-issued-`DISCARD ALL` arm, which recycles non-tainted. Live regression, on PG 17: `ferro-backend-pg`'s `pg_types_it.rs::s8a_f1_distinct_domain_oids_survive_a_full_hygiene_reset` and `::s8a_f1_distinct_domain_oids_survive_a_user_issued_discard_all` — both RED on stock HEAD with the exact `26000`, and the second stays RED under a mutation narrowing the fix to the `Full` arm alone, which is what proves the two arms independent. The follow-up ticket `docs/followups/2026-08-06-discard-all-typeinfo-cache-poisoning.md` is marked RESOLVED and carries the reproduction, the measured trigger table and the acceptance evidence.

  **RESIDUAL, deliberately left and not half-fixed.** A USER statement that deallocates (`DISCARD ALL` or `DEALLOCATE ALL`) followed by a custom-OID lookup **within the same checkout** still poisons that checkout: there is no reset between the two. Closing it needs a per-STATEMENT hook (e.g. routing `PinTrigger::Prepare` through a new `PoolBackend` callback) rather than a reset-time one — real plumbing across the generic pool for a self-inflicted, single-request window. Under `ferrod` an autocommit request takes a fresh checkout per statement, so the window is only reachable inside one explicit transaction that first nukes its own prepared statements. The pool-caused and recycle-visible cases — the ones a drop-in tier actually meets — are closed. Recorded in `conn.rs`'s `reset` docblock and in the follow-up ticket's RESIDUAL section.

  **Fork surface (M1-S8a review round).** The vendored `tokio-postgres` fork now carries THREE Ferro additions, not two: `transaction_status()` (M1-S1), `parameter()` and `clear_typeinfo_statement_cache()`. `/UPSTREAM_PR.md` has an M1-S8a addendum — DRAFTED, NOT FILED, no outward-facing action taken — and its "drop the fork when this lands" checklist now requires all three upstream before the fork can go.

  **(n) MySQL/MariaDB row streaming (`fetch:stream`) is DEFERRED out of M1-S8a — with its real cause, and one refusal authority.** On a MySQL-family pool `fetch:stream` is refused OUTRIGHT (a known-fate `Unsupported`, never a mid-stream error), so the S8b DBAL tier's `iterate*()` must **stream on PostgreSQL and fall back to a buffered `fetch:rows` on MySQL** — a documented asymmetry, not a defect (buffering is correct, just memory-hungry). The blocker is structural, not effort: every `mysql_async` streaming entry point BORROWS the connection (`QueryResult::stream`, `stream_and_drop`, `Queryable::exec_stream`, `Query::stream`), and while the OWNED-`Conn` route does type-check (`ResultSetStream<'static,'static,'static, Row, BinaryProtocol>` satisfies `Send + 'static`, proven live), there is **no public way to recover the `Conn` from a finished stream** — no `into_inner`, no accessor (`query_result` is a private field, `QueryResult.conn` is private, `Connection.inner` is `pub(crate)`), and **dropping the stream closes the connection**. An implementation therefore needs a SECOND Ferro edit in the vendored `mysql_async` fork — which today carries exactly ONE, the `CLIENT_SESSION_TRACK` capability OR of M1-S6 (`vendor/mysql-async/src/opts/mod.rs`); *(this sentence originally said "a THIRD vendored-fork divergence (today the fork carries exactly one Ferro edit)", which contradicts itself and was corrected in the M1-S8a review round. For the record, the OTHER fork — `tokio-postgres` — carries THREE Ferro edits as of that round: `transaction_status()`, `parameter()` and `clear_typeinfo_statement_cache()`; see (m).)* *and* a restructure of `Checkout::finalize_stream`, which reads `tx_status(&B::Conn)` **synchronously** after the drain and has no way to be handed a connection back through the `BackendRows` trait. The GAT alternative is not type-expressible: `RowStreamHandle` holds `&'a mut Checkout<B>` **and** `B::RowStream`, so a conn-borrowing stream makes it self-referential (the in-tree E0505 precedent for this exact class is recorded at `ferrod/src/services/sql.rs`'s tx-scoped streaming comment). **Two measured facts any future implementation must honour:** (1) `ResultSetStream::affected_rows()` returns the **PREVIOUS statement's** count — `setup_stream` snapshots the OK packet *before any row is read*, measured as `3` and `1` in two independent probes — so `BackendRows::rows_affected()` MUST read `Conn::last_ok_packet()` **post-drain**, never the stream's own accessor, or the engine ships a fabricated row count; and (2) the post-drain terminator for a MySQL SELECT reports `affected = 0` where PG's command tag reports the **row count**, a cross-backend divergence for `Statement::rowCount()`. **What M1-S8a DID fix:** the capability is now ONE authority — `PoolBackend::supports_row_streaming()` (default `true`, `false` for MySQL) — read by BOTH the autocommit and the tx-scoped EXEC arms through ONE terminal constructor (`services::sql::stream_unsupported`). Previously only the autocommit arm guarded (on a restated `matches!(pool, AnyPool::Mysql(_))`), so a **tx-scoped** stream was refused AFTER checkout + BEGIN, inside `Checkout::query_stream`'s Err arm, which **force-taints the pinned transaction connection** — a needless taint for a request that never should have been dispatched. **The cost of that taint is currently ZERO, and this entry originally overstated it (corrected by the M1-S8a Task 1 review):** `MysqlBackend::clean_reset_profile()` returns `Some(ResetProfile::Full)` unconditionally (`ferro-backend-mysql/src/conn.rs:329-331`), and the recycle path computes `if tainted { Some(Full) } else { clean_reset_profile() }` (`ferro-pool/src/pool.rs:160-164`) — so on a MySQL-family pool **both branches already run the full `COM_RESET_CONNECTION`**, taint or no taint, and `tx_open` is cleared by the actor's COMMIT/ROLLBACK regardless. The taint becomes a real per-recycle cost only once the deferred tracker-clean `None`-skip (R2, M1-S6 Task 7 (b)) lands and a clean connection can legitimately skip the reset. Proof: `ferrod`'s `mysql_it.rs::mysql_stream_is_refused_identically_on_both_arms_and_the_tx_survives`, which asserts the observed terminal against the daemon's own `stream_unsupported()` constructor (so a late, backend-declared refusal is RED) and that exactly ONE frame follows the tx-scoped request (charter rule 4). **AMENDED (dev-loop B2a, 2026-09-09): the fork-level half of the blocker is CLOSED.** The vendored `mysql_async` fork now carries a second edit (drafted for upstream in `UPSTREAM_PR_MYSQL_ASYNC.md`, Draft 2): the stream's terminal `None`/error no longer drops an owned connection (a `done` flag retains the state; `FusedStream` semantics preserved), and `ResultSetStream::into_conn()` / `QueryResult::into_conn()` drain whatever remains and hand the owned `Conn` back — the second covering the no-result-set case where `stream_and_drop()` answers `None` and would otherwise eat the connection (the dispatch signal is `columns_ref().is_empty()`). Proven live on MySQL 8.4 + MariaDB 11.8 by `ferro-backend-mysql`'s `stream_recovery_it.rs` four-part gate, each part asserting SESSION IDENTITY (`CONNECTION_ID()` + a session user variable), with the (n) row-count rule pinned (`Conn::affected_rows()` post-drain, never the stream's setup-time accessor). **The `finalize_stream` restructure is DONE (dev-loop B2b-1, 2026-09-09).** The second structural blocker this entry named — that `RowStreamHandle::finish` reads `tx_status(&B::Conn)` synchronously after the drain, with no way to hand a moved-out connection back through the `BackendRows` trait — is closed by a new `PoolBackend::reclaim_stream(&mut conn, rows) -> Result<u64>` hook: `finish` calls it AFTER the drain and BEFORE `finalize_stream`, so a backend whose `RowStream` OWNS the driver connection (MySQL, B2b-2: `query_stream` will MOVE the `Conn` out of `MysqlConn` into the stream) restores it into `conn` there, and answers `affected` from the connection's own post-drain packet (this entry's row-count rule). The default hook returns the stream's `rows_affected()` (PG/fake byte-identical). A reclaim `Err` obliges the backend to leave `conn` `is_closed`-dead and makes `finish` force-taint, so the pool discards the husk (charter rule 6); proven on the fake by `ferro-pool`'s `query_stream.rs::reclaim_hook_clean_recycles_but_a_failed_reclaim_discards_the_conn`. **The reclaim await is BOUNDED** (`tokio::time::timeout(config.checkout_timeout, ..)`, the same knob the checkout-time recycle uses for its ROLLBACK/RESET cleanup): a conn-owning backend's restore is a `COM_RESET_CONNECTION`-class round trip that can hang on a half-dead socket, and `ferrod`'s stream producer awaits `finish()` UNRACED — it is the one backend-touching await there with no cancel/deadline arm — so an unbounded reclaim would strand the request's single terminal `END` (charter rule 4). A timeout is handled exactly like a reclaim failure (`affected = 0`, force-taint, discard) and `finish` always returns; proven by `a_hung_reclaim_cannot_strand_finish` and mutation-proven (removing the bound hangs the test rather than failing it). The DRAIN loop beside it remains unbounded — pre-existing since S5, and bounding it would mean dropping a mid-protocol PG stream, so it is deferred to the slice that has a backend able to hang a drain. **The `MysqlConn` half is DONE (dev-loop B2b-2a, 2026-09-09):** the driver handle now lives in an `Option<Conn>` with `park`/`unpark`, and every method the pool can reach while parked answers safely rather than unwrapping — `is_closed` reports DEAD (the signal that makes the pool discard the husk instead of recycling it; `tainted` alone would recycle), `tx_status` answers `Failed` (a falsely-clean `Idle` is the one unsafe answer), `take_session_mutated` `false`, `last_insert_id` `None`, and `cancel_handle` reads a connect-time connection id so a cancel stays obtainable mid-stream. Proven live on MySQL 8.4 + MariaDB 11.8 by `ferro-backend-mysql`'s `conn_it.rs::{mysql,mariadb}_parked_conn_contract`. **THE ENGINE DEFERRAL IS CLOSED (dev-loop B2b-2b, 2026-09-10): MySQL/MariaDB stream rows incrementally.** `ferro-backend-mysql`'s `stream` module supplies a two-shape `MysqlRowStream` — a `Rows` arm that OWNS the parked `Conn` and drives the fork's `ResultSetStream`, and a `NoRows` arm reached when the PREPARED statement declares zero result columns, which is the dispatch that keeps a no-result-set statement off the owned route (`stream_and_drop()` answers `None` there **and consumes the `QueryResult`, closing the connection with it**); that arm runs buffered on the borrowed conn and yields nothing. `reclaim_stream` drains through `into_conn`, reads `affected` from the CONNECTION post-drain (this entry's measured rule — the stream's own accessor carries the PREVIOUS statement's packet), unparks, and records the §7.1 tracker taint. `supports_row_streaming()` is now `true`, so BOTH EXEC arms follow the one authority, and `ferrod`'s `mysql_it.rs` proves it on both: rows in order, exactly one END, and — the load-bearing assertion — the SESSION (autocommit) and the TRANSACTION including its COMMIT (tx-scoped) survive the park/reclaim round trip. Documented residuals: a `fetch:stream` `CALL` discards its rows, because a prepared `CALL` declares zero result columns even when the procedure emits a result set — the BUFFERED path has the same blind spot from the same cause, so this predates streaming; and this entry's second measured fact still holds, namely that a MySQL SELECT's post-drain terminator reports `affected = 0` where PG's command tag reports the row count. **THIS ENTRY IS NOW FULLY CLOSED (dev-loop B2c, 2026-09-10): the DRIVER tier streams on MySQL too.** `ferro/doctrine-dbal-driver`'s pool-kind gates in `query()` and `runPrepared()` are gone — both paths call `streamRaw()` on every family — which **retires controller decision D-S8b-2**. The driver's live guard that asserted the asymmetry (`StreamingLiveTest::testMysqlIteratesCorrectlyEvenThoughItBuffers`, written to be the thing that fails "the day MySQL streaming lands") did exactly that and is repointed: MySQL's interleaved iteration now MATERIALISES the remainder like PostgreSQL's, asserted on the same `settledRowCount()` counter that used to read `0`. **One consequence recorded rather than assumed:** the driver now assumes EVERY Ferro backend can stream, because the client is told a pool's `kind` but never its capabilities. Both shipped backends stream; a future backend that cannot (a SQLite one) would make `streamRaw()` fail rather than fall back, and closing that needs either streaming in that backend or the streaming capability advertised on `HELLO_ACK`. **`rowCount()` on a MySQL SELECT still answers `0`, now for a different reason** — not because the driver buffered, but because the post-drain OK packet a MySQL SELECT ends with reports `0` (this entry's second measured fact). The observable is unchanged; only the mechanism is.

  **(o) The vendor `errno` now rides the wire (M1-S8a Task 3) — MySQL/MariaDB only, `None` on PG by construction.** `PoolError::Sql` gained an `errno: Option<i32>` slot (`ferro-pool/src/error.rs`), filled at exactly ONE producing site — `ferro-backend-mysql`'s `error_map::map`, from `mysql_async::ServerError.code` (a `u16`, widened losslessly) — and mirrored into `ErrorPayload.errno` **verbatim on the `PoolError::Sql` arm** of `ferrod`'s `services::fate::classify_fate`, the ONE place a `PoolError` becomes a wire error — with ONE exception, stated in full below, that this entry originally omitted. Nothing downstream re-classifies from it: the taxonomy `code`/`branch` were already derived upstream by `classify_errno`, so the raw errno is now BOTH that classification key and an independent wire field. **Why it is load-bearing:** MySQL's SQLSTATEs are far coarser than its errnos — measured live on MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.8, a duplicate key (`1062`) and a NOT NULL violation (`1048`) BOTH arrive as SQLSTATE `23000` — and Doctrine DBAL's stock MySQL `ExceptionConverter` matches on the **errno EXCLUSIVELY**, so without this the S8b driver could not map a single MySQL error. **PostgreSQL has no integer error code** (its error identity IS the five-character SQLSTATE; `DbError` exposes no numeric vendor code), so `errno` stays `None` on every PG path forever, as it does on every client-side bind pre-flight rejection on either backend (no server ever saw the statement). **Correction (M1-S8a review, finding F11): there is a THIRD `None` case, and it is exactly the three MySQL errnos the cancel/timeout path produces.** `classify_fate` runs its §19.3 `is_57014` override BEFORE the `match err` that mirrors `errno`, and that override builds its `ErrorPayload` from scratch — `errno: None` **and** `sqlstate: None` on all four of its branches. `ferro-backend-mysql::classify_errno` deliberately maps `1317` (`KILL QUERY`), `3024` (MySQL `MAX_EXECUTION_TIME`) and `1969` (MariaDB `max_statement_time`) to `errc::CANCELLED` precisely so that override fires — so those three, alone among MySQL's errnos, never reach the wire. Measured on the real `classify_fate` for all three reachable `OpContext`s (`WRITE_UNCONFIRMED`, `CANCELLED`, `TX_DEADLINE`): `errno = None` every time, while the control (`1062`) keeps `Some(1062)` and its SQLSTATE. That measurement is now a GUARD rather than a note — `fate.rs`'s `the_57014_override_drops_the_vendor_errno_and_sqlstate`, over all three errnos × all three contexts, with the non-cancel control beside it and a precondition assertion so it cannot pass without actually taking the override; it goes RED under the obvious "helpful" change of forwarding the vendor errno/SQLSTATE through the override. This is DELIBERATE, not an oversight to repair: the §19.3 re-label replaces the vendor's account of what happened with the engine's own fate, and forwarding a raw errno beside a fate the vendor did not compute would invite a consumer to key on it. **The consequence S8b must plan around:** a Doctrine `ExceptionConverter` mapping keyed on errno `3024`/`1969`/`1317` will NEVER fire — a statement timeout is signalled by the fate `code` (`WRITE_UNCONFIRMED` / `TX_DEADLINE` / `CANCELLED`), not by the vendor code. The same false "verbatim" sentence was shipped in `error_map.rs`'s module doc and is corrected there in the same round. That negative is proven against a REAL server — `ferro-backend-pg`'s `pg_query_it::a_real_pg_server_error_carries_no_errno` reads the value off `error_map::map` on a genuine `42601` — deliberately not against a hand-built `PoolError`, which could not fail. Charter rule 2 is satisfied in the same change set: `proto/vectors/error_mysql_errno.json` is the first golden vector locking a non-null `errno` (`1062`) beside a real `sqlstate` (`23000`), byte-locked in BOTH codecs, and the PHP taxonomy exceptions expose `errno(): ?int` (`CarriesErrorPayload`) plus the value in their message. The conformance suite's vector-accounting guard also became **derived** rather than declared: it scrapes its own source for `loadVector('<name>.json')` call sites, so registering a name can no longer certify a vector that has no byte-lock test (mutation-proven — under the previous hand-written array, deleting the byte-lock test left the guard GREEN).

  **(p) The PG narrowing bind landed (M1-S8a Task 4) — `I64` → `int2`/`int4`/`int8`, `F64` → `float4`/`float8`, with the range check in the PRE-FLIGHT.** `bind::accepts` was `Type`-IDENTITY through the concrete `ToSql` (`<i64 as ToSql>` accepts `int8` ONLY, `<f64 as ToSql>` accepts `float8` ONLY), so a PHP `int` bound against an `int4`/`serial` PK was a hard `NonRetryable` refusal — the single highest-frequency DBAL blocker, hit by every insert into a `serial` and every identifier lookup. Two newtypes (`PgInt`, `PgFloat`) now write the **native binary** form for each width (delegating to `<i16/i32/i64>` and `<f32/f64>`, not re-rendering a decimal string PG would have to re-parse), and the pre-flight became `bind::check_param(v, ty) -> Result<(), String>` so a refusal carries an operator-facing REASON; `accepts` is retained verbatim as `check_param(..).is_ok()` so every existing call site and the directional lockstep proof read the SAME predicate. **The value-aware range gate is the load-bearing half.** `ToSql::accepts` sees only the `Type`, so a narrowing overflow is invisible to it; `check_param` sees the `Value`, and refuses an `i64` the target width cannot hold (`2147483648` against `int4`) and a FINITE `f64` that would become `inf` in `float4` (`1e39`) — a silent corrupt write. Both are **pre-send, known-fate `Sql{Unsupported}`** rejections naming the offending value, and both carry `errno: None` (no server answered). **Doing this in `to_sql` instead would be the defect, and the mechanism is MISCLASSIFICATION, not transmission:** `encode_bind_raw` serialises every param into a local buffer BEFORE anything touches the socket, so a `to_sql` failure means the statement provably never left the process — but it surfaces as `Error::to_sql(..)`, whose `as_db_error()` is `None`, which `ferro-backend-pg`'s `is_session_fatal` reads as a transport failure → `PoolError::ConnectionLost` → which §19.3 turns into `WriteUnconfirmed{Indeterminate}` on a sent, non-readonly, non-in-tx op. A statement that never left the process would then be reported as a write of UNKNOWN fate. §19.3's directional rule is therefore intact and mechanically proven: the pre-flight is STRICTER than the boxed impl (exactly as strict for the integer widths, strictly stricter for `float4`), never looser. **Precision loss WITHIN the REPRESENTABLE `f32` range is accepted and is not a miscast** — it is the column's own precision, and near the upper boundary PG's own input parser rounds a text literal identically (`3.4028235004135232e38` maps to the same `f32` in both). **Correction (Task 4 acceptance review, folded into Task 5's commit): the `float4` gate originally refused only OVERFLOW, and the UNDERFLOW mirror was a shipped silent corrupt write.** Measured live on PG 17 through the real bind path: `F64(1e-46)` bound to a `float4` returned `Ok(1)` and stored `0.0` (and `-0.0` for the negative), while PG's own parser on the identical literal `'1e-46'` returns `22003 "1e-46" is out of range for type real`. `check_range` now also refuses a finite NON-ZERO `f64` that truncates to zero in `f32`, with a message naming the value and the corruption it prevented. The boundary is REPRESENTABILITY, not normality: `1e-45` still binds, landing on the smallest `f32` subnormal (`1.401298464324817e-45`) — byte-for-byte what PG stores for `'1e-45'`, which PG accepts. **Why the shipped proofs missed it, which is the reusable lesson:** the directional lockstep rule was never violated — `check_param` and `to_sql` AGREED to truncate — so a cross-product proof over `accepts`-vs-impl is STRUCTURALLY blind to this class. It is a SEMANTIC hole (the engine disagreeing with the database it fronts), and the guard for it is a value-level assertion (`s8a_out_of_range_narrowing_is_refused_before_send`) plus a live assertion that PG's OWN parser refuses the same literal, both mutation-proven in two directions (removing the condition goes RED; making it over-strict — refusing every subnormal — also goes RED). **Guards.** The cross-product lockstep proof now drives the out-of-range magnitudes (`i64::MAX`, `i32::MAX+1`, `i16::MAX+1`, `1e39`, `NaN`) and goes RED if the range gate is removed — mutation-proven, and proven load-bearing by the converse mutation (shrink the fixture and the same broken gate stays GREEN). Because `every_variant()` is a hand-written `Vec` whose length assertion is a TAUTOLOGY with respect to a MISSING variant, a compile-forced `_exhaustive(v: &Value)` match with **no `_` arm** now lives beside it, so a 15th `Value` variant breaks the build (proven by adding one). `every_target_type()` deliberately gets no such guard and says so in-source: `tokio_postgres::types::Type` is external and open, so no exhaustive match over it exists and behavioural growth plus mutation is the only available guard. Proven live on PostgreSQL 17: `ferro-backend-pg`'s `pg_query_it::query_i64_against_int4_is_known_fate_not_connection_lost` (an in-range `I64` now inserts into an `int4` PK and reads back; an out-of-range one is refused pre-send with `errno: None`) and `ferrod`'s `sql_exec_it::exec_wrong_param_type_not_indeterminate` end to end through the daemon.

  **(q) Catalog + `ENUM` type coverage — the schema-introspection blocker, closed on BOTH engine families (M1-S8a Task 6).** DBAL's `AbstractSchemaManager` (and `doctrine/migrations`) run catalog SQL constantly, and it failed on both. **PostgreSQL:** `pg_type.typname` is `name`(19), `pg_attribute.attidentity` is `"char"`(18), `pg_class.oid` is `oid`(26) and `atttypid::regtype` is `regtype`(2206) — all four were a loud `Unsupported`. **What made DBAL 4.4.4's whole `selectTableColumns` statement unreadable was the first two, not all four (corrected in the M1-S8a review round, finding F13; §9's PG paragraph carries the same correction and the measurement).** That statement's twelve RESULT columns include `name` (`t.typname`/`bt.typname`) and `"char"` (`a.attidentity`, `coll.collprovider`) but no `oid`/`reg*` — those appear only in JOIN predicates, which never reach a `RowDescription`. `oid`/`regtype`/`regclass` support is therefore DEFENSIVE coverage for hand-written and migrations-tool introspection, and **S8b must not assume `reg*` appears in stock DBAL traffic**; Task 6's own report said exactly this and it never reached the spec until now. **MySQL/MariaDB:** `ENUM` was rejected outright. Everything here maps onto **EXISTING** canonical tags — `TEXT` and `I64` — so there is **no new tag and no `/proto` change**; the mappings and their rationale are in §9's two classification paragraphs (`"char"` is one BYTE whose `'\0'` renders as the empty string; `reg*` report the numeric OID because their payload IS an OID and resolving the name would need a catalog round trip). **The MySQL correction that shaped the work:** `MYSQL_TYPE_ENUM` **never reaches the client**. Measured on MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.8, `information_schema.COLUMNS.COLUMN_KEY` *and* a user-declared `ENUM('a','b')` both arrive as `MYSQL_TYPE_STRING` carrying `ENUM_FLAG`, so the **string-family** early-return was the one and only rejection doing any harm, and fixing it alone unblocks `information_schema` completely. The standalone `ColumnType::MYSQL_TYPE_ENUM` arm is fixed too, as **defence in depth** against a server or driver version that does send the dedicated code — but it is deliberately covered by the OFFLINE `column_kind` unit tests ONLY, because no live traffic can reach it and a live test aimed at it would be a guard that CANNOT FAIL. That is recorded rather than papered over, and it was measured: restoring only that arm leaves every live test GREEN and reddens only the offline test. **`SET` stays a loud `Unsupported` in both spellings** — its wire form is a comma-joined MULTI-value string, a different type rather than a longer one — and `ENUM` was dropped from `unsupported()`'s trailing "Deferred: …" list, which is what makes the live `SET` assertion falsifiable at all (that tail names every deferred type, so a bare `contains("SET")` was green for a `YEAR` column; the discriminating substring is the interpolated head `"MySQL SET ("`). **The live gate is `ferrod`'s `catalog_it.rs`**, on PostgreSQL 17 + MySQL 8.4 + MariaDB 11.8. Its assertions are DERIVED — for every column a query returns, the tag `HEAD` promised must equal the tag the producer emitted, swept over every non-`NULL` cell of every row with the compared-cell COUNT asserted against a measured number so an all-`NULL` result cannot satisfy it vacuously. The PG arm runs **DBAL 4.4.4's verbatim `selectTableColumns` SQL** (transcribed, not depended on — S8a takes no composer dependency) beside an explicit per-type query. **The MariaDB arm is driven through a USER `ENUM(...)` column, not through `information_schema`, and that is load-bearing:** MariaDB's `COLUMN_KEY` is a `MYSQL_TYPE_VAR_STRING`, not an `ENUM`, so it read fine before this slice — measured under the pre-fix classifier, the `information_schema` read is RED on MySQL and **GREEN on MariaDB**, which would have made a MariaDB `information_schema` assertion prove nothing. A user `ENUM` column is `MYSQL_TYPE_STRING | ENUM_FLAG` on BOTH engines and goes RED on both. Every guard was mutation-proven in both directions, including the compiler-invisible one: promising `"char"` as `I64` in `oid_to_tag` while the extractor still emits `Value::Text` compiles cleanly and is caught only by the derived live assertion.

  **(r) Savepoint SQL passes through INSIDE a transaction; a LEADING boundary verb never does (M1-S8a Task 7).** The guarded `Checkout` entries (`exec`/`query`/`query_stream`) used to refuse every transaction-control verb uniformly, which made Doctrine's nested transactions impossible: `Doctrine\DBAL\Connection` implements them by executing the literal strings `SAVEPOINT DOCTRINE_<n>` / `RELEASE SAVEPOINT …` / `ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT …` as PLAIN SQL through `exec()`, not through a driver API. The guard is now two-class (`pin::TxControlClass`) and shared by all three entries through ONE `Checkout::guard_tx_control` (before this it was copy-pasted three times, which is how an entry could have been added with a subtly different rule). **Boundary verbs** (`BEGIN`, `START TRANSACTION`, `COMMIT`, `END`, `ABORT`, a bare `ROLLBACK`, `PREPARE TRANSACTION`) stay refused on every entry, in or out of a transaction: they change whether a transaction is OPEN — the pin AUTHORITY — so one slipping through would leave a transaction the pool believes is not open on a connection that then goes back to the pool for the next tenant, with no `tx_id`, no actor, no deadline and no rollback-on-session-death. **Savepoint verbs** (`SAVEPOINT n`, `RELEASE [SAVEPOINT] n`, `ROLLBACK TO [SAVEPOINT] n`) are allowed **iff the checkout already has a transaction open**, because they cannot change transaction status (real Postgres's `ReadyForQuery` byte does not flip on any of them — the engine's own `leading_tx_verb` model has classified them "preserve" since M1-S1) and because every savepoint is destroyed by the enclosing transaction's commit or rollback, which the tx actor owns on every exit path. **Outside a transaction they are still refused, and NOT for symmetry:** MySQL silently IGNORES a bare `SAVEPOINT` under autocommit — no transaction is started and the savepoint has no effect — so delegating to the server would hand the caller a rollback point that does not exist. (`ROLLBACK TO`/`RELEASE` under autocommit are already loud — measured `1305`/`42000` on MariaDB, `25P01` on PG; the rule covers all three because a rule that is loud for one verb and delegated for two is unreasonable-about, and PG is silent for none of them.) `ROLLBACK` is the only verb in both classes; its SECOND word decides. **`ROLLBACK [WORK|TRANSACTION] TO …` is legal PG that this two-word scan calls Boundary and therefore REFUSES** — a false refusal in the safe direction, and no DBAL platform emits it.

  **A savepoint passthrough is ROUTED to the raw TEXT leaf (`PoolBackend::simple_query`), not to the prepared/extended path — a DEVIATION from the Task 7 plan, forced by a measured fact the plan did not have.** MySQL 8 cannot run these statements on the prepared path at all: measured on 8.4.11, `COM_STMT_PREPARE` of `SAVEPOINT` / `ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT` / `RELEASE SAVEPOINT` fails with errno **1295** ("This command is not supported in the prepared statement protocol yet"), while MariaDB 11.8 and PG 17 accept all three. Since `ferrod`'s EXEC path (autocommit and tx-scoped alike) runs user SQL through `Checkout::query`, relaxing the guard alone would have left DBAL's nested transactions dead on the single most important DBAL backend — green on MariaDB and PG, red only on MySQL 8, which is exactly the shape of bug a single-engine test lane ships. The routing is also the consistent choice: a savepoint takes no parameters and returns no rows, so the prepared protocol buys nothing, and passthrough savepoints now run on the SAME leaf as the engine's own composed `SAVEPOINT sp_N` (`Checkout::tx_control`). Two REFUSALS make the routing safe, both strictly stricter than the old behaviour: a savepoint passthrough may carry **no bound parameters** (the text leaf cannot bind, and silently dropping them would be the miscast charter rule 6 forbids) and must be a **lone statement** (`pin::is_lone_statement` — no `;` beyond an optional trailing one; conservative, so a `;` inside a quoted identifier is refused too). `query_stream` refuses savepoints outright even inside a transaction: a savepoint returns no result set, so there is nothing to stream.

  **SCOPE OF THE GUARD, stated precisely because the obvious stronger claim is false.** It classifies the statement's **LEADING** verb only. A COMPOUND statement is not parsed: measured on MySQL, where `CLIENT_MULTI_STATEMENTS` is negotiated, `SELECT 1; COMMIT` succeeds and leaves `tx_status = Idle`, and `SAVEPOINT s; START TRANSACTION` succeeds and leaves `InTx`. So the guard may NOT be described as refusing boundary verbs "unconditionally on every entry" — the truthful claim is "refused when they lead the statement, reading the first one or two **contiguous** words". The *contiguous* qualifier is load-bearing and was added by the M1-S8a Task 7 review: `skip_leading_noise` strips only **leading** comments, so a comment split BETWEEN the two words defeats the two-word check — measured, `START /*x*/ TRANSACTION` is classified `None` and passes the guard. That is likewise pre-existing and likewise not a leak (verified on a `max_size=1` pool: `apply_tx_status` sees the `T` and forces the recycle, so the next tenant on the same backend pid gets `tx_open = false`), and the classifier table now pins the limit — `START /*x*/ TRANSACTION`, `START -- c\nTRANSACTION` and `PREPARE /*x*/ TRANSACTION` are all asserted rather than assumed, so a future tightening is a deliberate change to a red test rather than a silent behaviour shift. This is **pre-existing** behaviour and it is **not a leak**: `apply_tx_status` reads the real post-statement status off the protocol signal after every statement, so the pin engine still sees the open transaction and still pins/taints the connection — the guard is a diagnostic front door, the protocol signal is the authority. What M1-S8a adds is that a statement may now also LEAD with a savepoint verb, which is a new vector aimed at that same guard; it is closed for the savepoint class specifically by the lone-statement rule above (`SAVEPOINT s; COMMIT` is refused, live-proven on PG, MySQL and MariaDB), so the savepoint allowance does not widen the pre-existing compound exposure. The classifier's unit table pins the compound cases explicitly, including `("SELECT 1; COMMIT", None)`, so a later slice that decides to parse compound statements must change that row deliberately.

  **Known interaction, documented not fixed:** the engine's own `SavepointStack` (the `SERVICE_TX` `SAVEPOINT`/`RELEASE`/`ROLLBACK_TO` API, which composes engine-owned `sp_N` names) does not observe passthrough savepoints, so MIXING the two forms inside one transaction can leave that stack disagreeing with the server. The DBAL tier uses passthrough exclusively. **A passthrough savepoint must NOT be lexed — and the original form of this entry got the direction wrong (corrected by the M1-S8a Task 7 review).** As first shipped, a passthrough savepoint reached the §7.1 assist lexer, whose safe-list contains no `SAVEPOINT`/`RELEASE`/`ROLLBACK`, so under `pin_on_unknown = true` it tainted the connection (`PinCause::Unknown`). This entry called that "one extra hygiene reset … the conservative direction (charter rule 5) … left for a later perf slice." **On PostgreSQL it is the LESS safe direction, and it armed the ambush (m) predicted.** The taint selects the **Full** profile, whose `DISCARD ALL` runs `DEALLOCATE ALL` — precisely the trigger for the typeinfo-cache poisoning recorded in (m). Since DBAL emits `SAVEPOINT DOCTRINE_<n>` for *every* nested transaction, that converted an occasional defect into a routine one: measured live on PG 17 with a `max_size=1` pool and no error in the run, a prepared statement created before a passthrough savepoint was **gone after the recycle** with `sqlstate 26000`, while the engine's own byte-identical `SAVEPOINT sp_N` left it intact.

The taint was never load-bearing: `tx_control` has always issued the identical text **un-lexed**, and `guard_tx_control` has by that point already established the statement is a **lone, param-free, savepoint-class statement inside an open transaction**. The fix is therefore verdict-keyed rather than keyword-keyed — `Checkout::apply_classify_for` skips the lexer only for a `SavepointPassthrough` verdict (an exhaustive match), and **`ferro-classify`'s `SAFE_LEADING_KEYWORDS` is deliberately NOT widened**, because that would exempt those verbs on every path, including ones that never passed the three refusals. Proven live on PG: all four DBAL forms leave `tainted=false`, the prepared statement survives the recycle on the same backend pid, and removing the skip turns the test red with the `26000` back. The leak class is unaffected — a user `SET search_path` in the **same** transaction still taints (`PinCause::Set`) and a later savepoint does not clear it.

  **Correction (M1-S8a review round): the charter-rule-6 half of that proof — that the skip is per-STATEMENT and not a lexer off-switch — closes on a PREPARED STATEMENT, not on `search_path`, and as first shipped it closed on the wrong one.** Both PG reset profiles run `RESET ALL` (`Targeted` is `DISCARD ALL` minus `DEALLOCATE ALL`/`DISCARD PLANS`), so a "the next tenant did not inherit the `search_path`" read cannot distinguish `Full` from `Targeted` — it only evidences that SOME reset ran, and it was measured GREEN under a mutated taint-routing (`profile = backend.clean_reset_profile()` unconditionally, i.e. a tainted connection wrongly recycled through `Targeted`). Nowhere in this spec should `search_path` non-inheritance be cited as evidence of a profile SELECTION; the pre-existing S3 test `pg_s3_tainted_conn_still_gets_full_reset` already carries an in-code caveat to exactly that effect. The falsifiable pair is `pg_s8a_savepoint_passthrough_does_not_taint_and_keeps_prepares_across_recycle` (untainted → the prepare SURVIVES the recycle) and `pg_s8a_savepoint_skip_does_not_disable_the_lexer_for_other_statements` (tainted → the prepare is GONE, asserted as SQLSTATE `26000` rather than "an error", so a dropped connection or a syntax error cannot masquerade as the proof) — the two directions of one claim, each RED under the taint-routing mutation the other is green under. **And a note on why (m) shows up here at all:** the paragraph above justifies this skip partly by "it armed the ambush (m) predicted", which is historically accurate and stays. (m) is itself FIXED as of the same review round, so the skip now stands on its own merits — an unnecessary `Full` reset is wasteful and needlessly destructive to the engine's namespaced prepares — not on (m) being open.

  Proof: `ferro-pool`'s `pin::tests::s8a_tx_control_class_splits_boundary_from_savepoint` (the classifier table, incl. case/whitespace/comment/quoted-identifier and compound rows) and `s8a_is_bare_tx_control_is_unchanged_from_pre_s8a` (the pre-S8a behaviour written out by hand, so it is not the tautology `is_bare_tx_control(x) == tx_control_class(x).is_some()` would have been); `ferro-pool`'s `tests/query_guard.rs` guard matrix over all three entries × both classes × both transaction states, plus the compound/params refusals; and live on PG 17, MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.8 — `ferrod`'s `tx_it.rs` / `mysql_it.rs` `savepoint_sql_passes_through_inside_a_transaction` (a read-back showing the rolled-back row absent: `[1, 3]`), `boundary_sql_stays_refused_inside_a_transaction` (nine disguised boundary spellings plus `SAVEPOINT X1; COMMIT`, all refused, the transaction still usable afterwards) and `savepoint_sql_outside_a_transaction_is_refused`.

  **(s) MySQL/MariaDB isolation/readonly `BEGIN` is a SINGLE-STATEMENT BATCH, and that is load-bearing (M1-S8a Task 8).** `actor::compose_begin_sql` is now dialect-aware (`compose_begin_sql(dialect, isolation, readonly)`, fed from `Pool::backend().dialect()` at the one call site in `begin_on_pool`). On MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.8 `BEGIN READ ONLY`, `BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL …` and `START TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL …` are ALL `ERROR 1064 (42000)` (measured), so the composed form is `START TRANSACTION [READ ONLY]`, prefixed for the isolation cases with `SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL …;` **in the same statement string**. Two reasons it is one string and not two calls: `Checkout::begin_tx_with` issues exactly ONE `simple_query` and wraps it in the whole pin/RFQ/tracker/Rule-A sequence (a second call would re-run all of it); and — the measured one — a **STANDALONE** `SET TRANSACTION …` returns an OK packet with `SERVER_SESSION_STATE_CHANGED` and **no trackers**, which `tracker::is_mutation` reads as a genuine session mutation, so every isolation/readonly transaction would taint into a full `COM_RESET_CONNECTION` at the next recycle. **BATCHED** (over the already-negotiated `CLIENT_MULTI_STATEMENTS`), `query_drop` drains both result sets and the FINAL OK packet carries a `TransactionState` tracker, which gates the bare-flag path off: no taint, `tx_status` reads `InTx`, and `SERVER_STATUS_IN_TRANS_READONLY` confirms read-only took. The batch consequently MASKS the intermediate statement's own trackers — acceptable **only** because the engine composes this string itself; a user batch still goes through the unchanged `Checkout::exec`, and the engine-composed BEGIN never passes through the M1-S8a Task 7 tx-control/lone-statement guard at all (`begin_tx_with` drives the RAW `PoolBackend::simple_query`). It is also one of the three concrete holes any future tracker-clean hygiene skip (§7.2, R2) must reconcile. **The `SESSION` form is FORBIDDEN, and the prohibition is load-bearing:** `SET SESSION TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL …` would persist the level on the pooled connection past `COMMIT`, so the next tenant would inherit it — a cross-tenant connection-state leak (charter rule 6). The next-transaction-only spelling is correct *because* it does not persist, and the observable consequence is that **the level is NOT visible in `@@transaction_isolation`** (which keeps reporting the session default, rendered by MySQL with a hyphen: `REPEATABLE-READ`). Anyone verifying this behaviour must therefore prove it by a **lock conflict** — under `SERIALIZABLE` InnoDB converts a plain in-transaction `SELECT` into a locking read, so a concurrent `UPDATE` of that row blocks, while under the default `REPEATABLE READ` it does not — never by reading the variable back. That trap is real: the naive variable assertion is false, and its natural "repair" is the leak. **The CONVERSE — that the SESSION-scoped level was NOT moved — is exactly what reading the variable DOES prove, and it is the load-bearing leak guard.** It must be read INSIDE the pinned transaction, where no checkout hygiene can have run, against the session default read beforehand: MySQL/MariaDB `SELECT @@SESSION.transaction_isolation`, PostgreSQL `current_setting('default_transaction_isolation')` (NOT `transaction_isolation`, which is the current transaction's level and is legitimately `serializable` there). A NEXT-TENANT read is NOT a guard and must not be written as one: it was measured GREEN under a genuine SESSION-scoped leak on all three engines, because `COM_RESET_CONNECTION` and the targeted profile's `RESET ALL` wipe the level before the next tenant sees it — hygiene is defence in depth here, never the property under test. (An earlier form of this entry concluded no pool-level guard was possible; that was true only of the next-tenant phrasing, and the M1-S8a review built the in-transaction one.)
