Measured RED on all three engines under both the `SET SESSION` mutation and the respelling below; GREEN unmutated. **The `SET SESSION`/`GLOBAL` scan in `compose_begin_sql_table` is a cheap TRIPWIRE, not the guarantee** — it is a source-text containment check, and `SET @@SESSION.transaction_isolation = '…'` (on PG, `SELECT set_config('default_transaction_isolation', …, false)`) contains neither substring, sets the session variable on both families, and — with the table's expected strings updated to agree, which is what an implementer changing the emitted form would do — leaves the whole shipped unit suite GREEN while the engine emits a session-persistent isolation. Measured. The behavioural in-transaction guards are what hold the line. The PostgreSQL strings are byte-identical to M1-S6's (all 8 still pinned verbatim), and `Dialect::Sqlite` is an explicit LOUD refusal rather than a silent PG fallback; the `match` over `Dialect` has **no `_` arm**, so a fourth dialect breaks the build (proven by adding one: `E0004` at `compose_begin_sql`). **PHP-reachable NAMED isolation constants are explicitly NOT in S8a**: they would need a new `[isolation]` section in the `/proto` registry (touching `registry.rs`'s `MethodsToml` and `Registry` **and** `build.rs`'s own `deny_unknown_fields` `Registry`), and Doctrine does not need them — `Connection::setTransactionIsolation()` emits `SET SESSION TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL …` as plain SQL, not a driver flag.

  **Where the cross-tenant-leak guard lives, and where it deliberately does NOT (measured, not assumed).** A "the next tenant did not inherit the level" assertion at the POOL level is a guard that **cannot fail**, on both engine families: with the composer mutated to emit the forbidden `SET SESSION TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL …` (MySQL) and `SET SESSION CHARACTERISTICS AS TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL …` (PG), the e2e assertions stayed GREEN — MySQL's `clean_reset_profile() = Some(Full)` runs `COM_RESET_CONNECTION` at every recycle and PG's targeted profile runs `RESET ALL`, so hygiene wipes the leak before any later tenant can observe it. Both such assertions were therefore DELETED rather than shipped as dead guards, with the measurement recorded in-source at both sites. The falsifiable leak guard lives one layer down, on a RAW backend connection with no hygiene in the way: `ferro-backend-mysql`'s `begin_dialect_it::the_batched_isolation_never_survives_the_transaction`, which reads the session level BEFORE the batch and again after `COMMIT` and after `ROLLBACK`, and goes RED (`SERIALIZABLE` vs `REPEATABLE-READ`) under exactly that mutation. Hygiene masking the leak is defence in depth, not the property being asserted.

  **A cancelled contending write is NOT a raw `57014` on the wire, and the lock-conflict proof asserts the §19.3 cell instead.** The concurrent `UPDATE` that proves `SERIALIZABLE` took is an autocommit WRITE bounded by `timeout_ms`, and the S4 fate matrix deliberately re-labels a cancelled/timed-out dispatched write as `WriteUnconfirmed{Indeterminate}` (`fate.rs`'s `is_57014` override), replacing the raw SQLSTATE with the engine's own payload — so `sqlstate` is `None` there by construction and the gate asserts `errc::WRITE_UNCONFIRMED` + `branch::INDETERMINATE` + the cancel message (which separates it from the other producer of that code, a `ConnectionLost` on a sent write).

  Proof: `ferrod`'s `tx::actor::tests::compose_begin_sql_table` (the full dialect × isolation × readonly matrix verbatim, plus a scan over every composed cell for a `SESSION`/`GLOBAL` scope — independently mutation-proven by making the expected table agree with a SESSION-emitting composer, at which point only the scan fires); `ferro-backend-mysql`'s `begin_dialect_it.rs` (the standalone-taints / batched-does-not pair, and the raw-connection no-leak guard) on BOTH engines; `ferrod`'s `mysql_it::mysql_begin_honours_isolation_and_readonly` (READ ONLY → SQLSTATE `25006`; `SERIALIZABLE` → a lock conflict; an `isolation: None` CONTROL run that must NOT conflict — proven load-bearing by a mutation in which the level is silently ignored AND an unrelated lock is held, where the lock-conflict block passes for the wrong reason and ONLY the control run catches it); and `ferrod`'s `tx_it::pg_begin_isolation_and_readonly_are_unchanged` (PG's `BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE READ ONLY` end to end, unchanged).

  **(t) The PHP client grew an IMPERATIVE transaction trio, and the closure form's §19.1 re-run loop is deliberately NOT reachable from it (M1-S8a Task 9).** `Connection::begin(readonly)` / `commit()` / `rollBack()` / `inTransaction()` exist because `Doctrine\DBAL\Driver\Connection` is handed exactly those three as UNRELATED calls with the application's code in between — DBAL owns its own nesting counter and its own retry decision and never hands a closure to a driver — so `Connection::transaction(callable)` (§10) cannot be adapted onto the SPI. The two forms share one object: `begin()` holds a real `TxHandle` privately and every statement method (`exec`/`query`/`queryOne`/`scalar`/`rows`, and `stream`) routes through it while it is set, so an in-transaction statement carries the `tx_id` and inherits `TxHandle`'s bare send-and-classify — **no transparent reconnect, no re-issue** (charter rule 3). That is the load-bearing difference from `transaction()`, whose §19.1 loop may reconnect and re-run the WHOLE closure: a reconnect in the middle of an imperative transaction would void the `tx_id` silently, and the caller — who is holding the transaction across three separate calls — would never learn of it. `transaction()` itself is unchanged except for one guard refusing to run while an imperative transaction is open.

  **Every failure hands the caller a FATE, which is the only thing that makes "the caller owns retry" mean anything.** A lost `BEGIN` is routed through the same `FateClassifier::classifyLoss(OpKind::TxBegin, …)` the closure form uses and arrives as `RetryableException` (it opened nothing); a rejected `BEGIN` propagates its taxonomy exception verbatim; a lost `COMMIT` is the §19.3 carve-out and arrives as `IndeterminateException`, never retried by anyone. **A lost `ROLLBACK` is the one deliberate asymmetry: it does NOT throw.** DBAL's `transactional()` — and essentially every hand-written `try { … } catch { $c->rollBack(); throw; }` — calls it from a `catch`/`finally` that is ALREADY carrying the error that matters, so a raw throw there would REPLACE the caller's real error with a transport failure. It is also outcome-equivalent: a rollback whose reply was lost has the same result as one that succeeded, because the engine rolls back and tombstones the `tx_id` on session death, on deadline and on drop (`tx_registry.abort_session`), so no PHP destructor is needed and nothing is left dangling. **The same silence covers "that transaction is already gone", which is not a link failure and is in fact the COMMONEST way a mid-transaction failure lands.** When an in-transaction statement trips `idle_in_tx` / `max_tx` / an in-tx cancel, the engine rolls back, releases the pinned connection and TOMBSTONES the `tx_id`, emitting one `TxDeadline{Retryable}` terminal; the caller's `finally { rollBack(); }` then rolls back a dead id and gets a well-formed `Outcome::Error` — `TxDeadline` or `TxNotFound`. Until M1-S8a's fix round those threw OUT OF THE `finally` and REPLACED the caller's real error — measured; the exact failure this asymmetry exists to prevent. `rollBack()` therefore also swallows those two wire CODES (`ERR_TX_DEADLINE`, `ERR_TX_NOT_FOUND` — codes, never message matching), **and nothing else**: any other server-side rejection still throws, because swallowing every class would leave `rollBack()` unable to report anything and the swallow itself unfalsifiable. *[The known cost recorded here — a malformed `TxControl` body, i.e. a CLIENT codec defect, arriving as `Protocol` on this path and being swallowed with the tx-gone cases — is PAID OFF as of the M1 Phase-B `TxNotFound` slice; see (ai) below. `Protocol` is out of the swallow list and throws again.]*

  **The handle is cleared on every exit, including every failure**, so `inTransaction()` cannot stick at `true` and strand the Connection pointing at a tombstoned `tx_id`: `commit()`/`rollBack()` clear it BEFORE the wire operation, and `begin()` assigns it LAST. The one place it deliberately SURVIVES a failure is a mid-transaction STATEMENT error — clearing there would make the caller's `finally { rollBack(); }` throw `InvalidTransactionStateException` and mask the original error, the exact failure the swallowing arm above exists to prevent. Misuse (`commit`/`rollBack` with nothing open, a nested `begin`, `transaction()` while one is open) throws the new LEAF class `Ferro\Client\Error\InvalidTransactionStateException` and sends no frame; a leaf rather than the root `FerroException`, because the root passes for ANY Ferro error — measured: with the misuse guard removed and the assertion widened to the root, a test "proving" the refusal passed on an unrelated `RetryableException` from a dead link.

  **Two carried defects closed here.** `Connection::lastInsertId()` was autocommit-only (measured at Task 2: autocommit INSERT → 1, in-transaction INSERT → 2 via the handle, the accessor still → 1); the imperative path now propagates the key, so a driver's `lastInsertId()` is right for a transactional INSERT. The CLOSURE form still does not propagate — the key lives on the handle the closure was given (`TxHandle::lastInsertId()`), and a value read after the closure returned would belong to a transaction that no longer exists. And `Connection::stream()` never cleared the key (unreachable live today — PG always reports `null` and MySQL `fetch:stream` is `Unsupported`, (n) — but live the moment MySQL streaming lands); it now clears it inside the generator body, which is also where the request is written, so an un-iterated `stream()` clears nothing.

  **A THIRD, found in the M1-S8a review round: the key survived a FAILED statement, and the docblock claiming it could not was false.** `Connection::lastInsertId()` (and `TxHandle::lastInsertId()`) now CLEAR on the way INTO every statement — at `Connection::dispatch`, the one statement chokepoint, so autocommit, the imperative transaction and the §19.1 retry loop are all covered — so a statement that fails, is cancelled, or is `Indeterminate` reports `null` rather than the previous statement's generated key. **This is a DELIBERATE divergence from PDO, which keeps the stale key**, and the reason is the §19.3 case specifically: an `Indeterminate` write's key is UNKNOWN by definition, so a stale `int` there is not merely stale, it is FABRICATED — the silent-wrong-answer class this engine exists to refuse — while `null` is the truth. The divergence is unobservable through DBAL or the ORM, both of which read the key immediately after a SUCCESSFUL `executeStatement()` and never after a throw; the only shape where the two now differ is code that catches the exception and reads the key anyway. Transaction CONTROL (`begin`/`commit`/`rollBack`) deliberately does NOT clear it — tx control is not a statement — so `begin; INSERT; commit; lastInsertId()` still reports the key, which is what DBAL needs. "Match PDO" was never the governing rule for this accessor in any case: it already diverges (PG reports `null` rather than `lastval()`; a plain read clears the key rather than leaving it), and the governing rule is "the last statement's key, or nothing" — of which the failure path was the one hole. Guarded from the CALLER's vantage (catch, then read) on all four paths, each with a control assertion proving a key really was there to go stale: `ConnectionLastInsertIdTest`'s `testAFailedStatementClearsItRatherThanServingAStaleKey`, `testAnIndeterminateWriteClearsItRatherThanFabricatingAKey`, `testAFailedStatementInsideAnImperativeTransactionClearsItToo` and `testAFailedStatementClearsTheTxHandlesOwnCopyToo` — all four RED (`Failed asserting that 42 is null`) with the two clears removed.

  **`stream()` inside an imperative transaction is tx-scoped on BOTH axes:** it carries the `tx_id` (an autocommit stream would run outside the open transaction and see none of its uncommitted writes — a silent wrong answer; the engine routes a tx-scoped streamed fetch to the owning actor) and it rides the TRANSACTION's session rather than `Connection::session()`, the reconnect loop's current one. The two are the same object on every path `Connection` can take today, since nothing reconnects while a transaction is open; the binding is explicit anyway because the engine's tx registry is per-session and a `tx_id` on a foreign session is `NotFoundOrForbidden`. **Isolation is deliberately not a `begin()` parameter** — see (s): named constants would mean hand-written protocol numbers on the PHP side, and Doctrine sets isolation with plain SQL.

  Proof: `php/client`'s `ConnectionImperativeTxTest` (17 offline cases over the scripted `FakeSession`: the `tx_id` decoded back off the recorded `ExecRequest` for all five statement methods and for `stream`, the lost-BEGIN/lost-COMMIT/lost-ROLLBACK classifications, the `finally`-cannot-mask property, the `last_insert_id` propagation and its closure-form negative, and the misuse matrix) and `ImperativeTransactionLiveTest` against real Postgres (commit persists / rollback discards, an in-transaction read sees its own uncommitted write and loses it after `rollBack()`, Doctrine's literal `SAVEPOINT DOCTRINE_1` / `ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT` / `RELEASE SAVEPOINT` sequence through the trio, and the closure form still working while refusing to nest).

  **(u) `HELLO_ACK` advertises per-pool METADATA, and `protocol_version` went 1 → 2 to make the skew fail at the first byte (M1-S8a Task 11).** `HelloAck.pools` was a bare `array<str>` of names; it is now an array of positional `PoolInfo` triples — `[name, kind, server_version]` (`proto/PROTOCOL.md` §4). `kind` is the backend FAMILY (`"postgres"` / `"mysql"`; MariaDB is `"mysql"` — same family, same wire protocol, distinguishable only from `server_version`) and it was **free**: `PoolSpec.kind` has been inferred from the DSN SCHEME by `config::infer_pool_kind` since M1-S6 and was merely never put on the wire. `server_version` rode as `nil` in this task; **Task 12 fills it — see entry (v)**. The DSN is still never exposed (§12). A DBAL-4 driver needs both — `Driver::getDatabasePlatform(ServerVersionProvider)` picks a platform family and `Connection::getServerVersion(): string` is mandatory — and learning the family from the handshake is what keeps the driver from probing with a dialect-specific query it would have to guess the dialect to write.

  **Why the version bump, and what its failure actually LOOKS like.** Adding a field to a message does **not** move `TYPE_REGISTRY_HASH`: the hash is FNV-1a over `proto/registry.lock.json`, which carries protocol version / magic / flags / services / methods / error codes / type tags and **no message layouts**. A skewed pair would therefore have handshaked "successfully" (`handshake::validate_hello` compares only the hash) and then thrown deep inside `HelloAck::decode` — a late, confusing failure instead of an early, deterministic one. `PROTOCOL_VERSION` is byte 1 of **every** frame header and is checked in `Header::decode` on BOTH sides **before any payload is parsed**, so the bump makes a skewed pair fail at the first byte pair of the first frame in both directions. It also changes the lock file, so the hash moves as a second, independent tripwire. **Recorded honestly, MEASURED end to end against a live `ferrod` at version 2, and — since the M1-S8a review round (finding F14) — ASSERTED there too**: a `HELLO` whose version byte is `1` is answered with `errc::PROTOCOL` (`0x3009`) — `"unsupported protocol version: expected 2, got 1"` — while a well-formed v2 `HELLO` carrying a stale `type_registry_hash` is answered with `errc::UNSUPPORTED` (`0x300A`). Three consequences. (1) The code is `PROTOCOL`, not `UNSUPPORTED`, so anything keying on `UNSUPPORTED` to mean "we disagree" does NOT fire on a version skew. (2) The engine's own terminal message names both versions, so the engine-side diagnosis is good. (3) The old client cannot READ that terminal — the reply frame itself carries version `2` and dies in the client's own `Header::decode` as `CodecException('bad version 2')` — so the client-side symptom is a bare codec error with no server text. Carrying a version inside the `HELLO` PAYLOAD and rejecting it typed is a strictly larger change and is not in M1-S8a; `proto/PROTOCOL.md` §1 records the same table, so the missing typed error is not later mis-filed as a defect.

  **Cost, paid deliberately: every committed golden vector's `frame_hex` changed in byte 1**, and the four `proto/vectors/negative/*.bin` fixtures were regenerated with it (charter rule 2 — registry, vectors and BOTH codecs move in one change set). The negatives were hand-checked because each exists to be wrong in exactly ONE way: `bad_magic.bin` and `oversize_len.bin` now carry the CURRENT version so nothing fires before their own defect; `reserved_flag.bin` stays structurally valid so it is still rejected at the flags layer and not at the version; and `bad_version.bin` is byte-identical to before — its version byte is an explicit `0x99` that must stay wrong (had `gen_vectors` written `PROTOCOL_VERSION` into it, it would have become a VALID frame and the test would have inverted). **Correction (M1-S8a review, finding F7): as first written this entry ended "No `PoolRegistry` is threaded anywhere: the session has none and needs none, because everything advertised is already in `Config`." That was true of Task 11 and is FALSE at HEAD, and it directly contradicted entry (v) below.** Task 12 threads ONE `Arc<PoolRegistry>` through `serve()` → `Session::run_with_handler` (verified: `engine/crates/ferrod/src/serve.rs` lines 41/51/61/101/108) and builds the ack's pool metadata off it, precisely so the version cache is per-DAEMON rather than per-connection; the config-derived second derivation (`session::handshake::pool_info_from_config`) was DELETED in that same change, so there is now exactly one. (v) is the authority on this; the sentence was left stale because Task 12 updated (u)'s `server_version` clause and not this one — which is what the parallel-authoring hazard in the §22.2 preamble is about. **Do not re-thread a second registry handle or re-derive pool metadata from `Config`:** two derivations of one wire field is the drift (v) deleted `pool_info_from_config` to prevent.

  Proof: `ferro-proto`'s `messages::hello_ack_carries_structured_pool_metadata` and `header::a_frame_from_the_previous_protocol_version_is_rejected_by_the_header` (`expected`/`got` both derived from `consts::PROTOCOL_VERSION`, never a literal — a hand-written protocol constant is a charter rule 2 defect in a test too); its PHP mirror `HeaderTest::testRejectsThePreviousProtocolVersionBeforeReadingAnyPayload`; **and, added by the M1-S8a review round (finding F14), the DELIVERY half that the table above previously asserted from a measurement nobody had written down — `ferrod`'s `handshake.rs::a_previous_version_hello_is_answered_with_the_documented_protocol_terminal`, a live session over a real socket that pins `request_id = 0`, `END`, `errc::PROTOCOL` + its branch, the exact message and the one-frame-then-EOF shape, every constant derived from `consts::PROTOCOL_VERSION`. It is genuinely falsifiable: relabelling `session::classify`'s Fatal arm `errc::UNSUPPORTED` and, separately, dropping the terminal for a silent close each turn it RED. Before it, three of this slice's own artifacts disagreed about whether the table had been measured — the codec-side guard (`golden_vectors.rs`) says in its own docblock that it locks only the STRING, and the Task 11 fix-round report called the delivery "INFERRED rather than measured"; the guard's docblock now names its live counterpart instead of a carry**; `ferrod`'s family-mapping + §12 assertions (the latter made on the ENCODED ack bytes rather than the struct — **relocated by Task 12 into `pools::tests`**, where they run through the real `PoolRegistry::pool_info` path against unreachable pools instead of a config-only shortcut); `php/client`'s `PoolInfoTest` + the reshaped `SessionHandshakeTest`; and the cross-language byte lock over the reshaped `hello_ack` vector. **The non-empty vector fixture is itself mutation-proven**: with the pre-S8a `pools: []`, a deliberately swapped `name`/`kind` order in `Message.php` passes the byte lock (measured); with the two triples it fails. `HandshakeLiveTest` derives its expectation from the harness's own launched pools AND — new here — from the DSN schemes the harness passed, so an engine that drops, renames, invents or MISLABELS a pool still fails live.


  **(v) Each pool's SERVER VERSION is learned lazily at the first handshake that asks — bounded as a WHOLE, probed CONCURRENTLY, TTL'd, and negative-cached (M1-S8a Task 12).** `HELLO_ACK`'s `PoolInfo.server_version` is now the backend's raw `version()` string. `ferrod` knew no backend's version before this and its pools are LAZY (`Pool::new` dials nothing, there is no warmup or `min_size`), so it could not be learned at build time without making BOOT depend on database reachability — a property worth keeping — and "at first checkout" is too late for a DBAL driver, which must pick a **platform** at connect time. So it is learned on the first handshake that asks, off the ONE `Arc<PoolRegistry>` now threaded through `serve()` → `Session::run_with_handler` (which is what makes the cache per-DAEMON rather than per-connection). `SELECT version()` works verbatim on PostgreSQL, MySQL and MariaDB, so the probe goes through the ordinary GUARDED `Checkout::query` with no per-backend method and no raw-connection back door; the string is advertised **RAW**, because normalising it would bake one ecosystem's platform-selection rules into the engine and a Doctrine driver needs `mariadb` to survive inside it.

  **Four properties are load-bearing, and each looks like a simplification it is not. A future slice must not undo any of them.** (1) **The probe sits on the handshake critical path, INSIDE the client's own I/O deadline** — `Ferro::connect` defaults `ioTimeout` to 5 s and applies it to the `HELLO_ACK` read — so all pools are probed CONCURRENTLY under ONE 1.5 s budget: N unreachable pools cost what one does. A per-pool bound with serial probing (the design this replaced) blows the client's deadline at three unreachable pools and fails `Ferro::connect` outright, destroying the very property the lazy design exists to preserve. (2) **A success EXPIRES after a TTL (10 min) rather than sealing for the daemon's life.** A `OnceCell` is the obvious tool and the wrong one: sealed on success, a rolling backend upgrade leaves `ferrod` advertising a pre-restart version that a driver converts into a platform (i.e. which SQL grammar it emits) — silently. (3) **A FAILURE is remembered for a short backoff (5 s), not not-at-all.** Uncached, every handshake pays a full probe budget for as long as a backend is down; cached forever, a backend that comes back needs a daemon restart. (4) **A probe already IN FLIGHT is never duplicated and never waited on.** `tokio::sync::OnceCell::get_or_try_init` QUEUES concurrent initialisers, so an FPM reconnect storm after a `boot_epoch` change (§19.1) would serialise N handshakes behind one probe; the state is a plain `Mutex<VersionCache>` with an `in_flight` flag that a losing caller reads past rather than blocking on. **The handshake NEVER fails because of a probe, in any state** — an unreachable, slow or unparseable backend advertises `server_version: nil` and the `HELLO_ACK` goes out regardless. The pool's `kind` is unaffected throughout: it comes from the DSN scheme and is known for a backend that has never been dialled.

  **Two engine-discipline details.** The per-pool probes are DETACHED `tokio::spawn`s that the budget stops WAITING for, never inlined futures the budget DROPS — the engine's standing "drain, don't drop" rule (`services::sql::run_autocommit_exec`): dropping an inlined future would drop a live `Checkout` with a statement still in flight, and `Checkout::drop` would push that connection back onto the idle stack UNTAINTED for the next tenant (charter rule 6). Detached, the task finishes normally, releases its checkout the normal way, and its answer lands in the cache for the NEXT handshake. The probe's own statement is bounded the same way every interruptible statement in `ferrod` is: fire the out-of-band CANCEL, then AWAIT the query to completion. **That detached-vs-dropped distinction was reported at the time as having "no black-box observable"; the M1-S8a review round showed that is FALSE and built the guard.** The observable is the pool's own idle stack (`Pool::poison_idle_for_test`, whose closure runs only when the stack is non-empty), and it discriminates at the PRODUCTION pool config: `pools::tests::the_probe_is_detached_so_a_budget_expiry_never_recycles_a_mid_statement_connection` shrinks only the whole-call budget so it expires MID-statement, then asserts (1) `server_version == None`, i.e. the budget really did beat the statement — without which the rest is vacuous; (2) the idle stack is EMPTY at that instant, i.e. the probe still owns its checkout; and (3) the same closure DOES fire once the detached task finishes, so "empty" cannot mean "the probe never ran". Replacing the `tokio::spawn` with a plain inlined future — the natural simplification, which still compiles — turns it RED. And `pool_info` iterates its pools in NAME order, not `HashMap` order — which pins the advertised order, and is also what makes "the pools are probed concurrently" a falsifiable claim rather than a coin flip under a serialising mutation. **The name-order guard was itself a coin flip until the review round** (a two-pool literal is right half the time whatever `pool_info` does): `pools::tests::pool_info_is_advertised_in_name_order_whatever_the_maps_own_order` now BUILDS registries until it finds one whose `HashMap` iteration order is not already sorted, ASSERTS that precondition, and only then requires the sorted answer — deterministic rather than 1-in-6, and measured 8/8 RED with the `sort_by` deleted, 8/8 green with it back.

  **The probe is TOTAL, and its `in_flight` marker is RAII (M1-S8a review round, findings F15–F17).** Every await `probe_version_on` performs is bounded: the DIAL (`VERSION_CHECKOUT_BUDGET` 5 s), the statement (`VERSION_STATEMENT_BUDGET` 10 s), the out-of-band CANCEL (`VERSION_CANCEL_BUDGET` 2 s — `CancelToken::cancel_query` dials a FRESH connection, also unbounded before this) and the post-cancel DRAIN (`VERSION_DRAIN_BUDGET` 5 s). Dropping the dial or the cancel is safe (neither holds a pooled connection; the semaphore permit releases with the future); **dropping the DRAIN is not, so when the drain bound expires the checkout is force-TAINTED before release** — the same net M1-S5's `RowStreamHandle::drop` uses — selecting the full `ROLLBACK` + `DISCARD ALL` recycle rather than handing a mid-protocol connection to the next tenant clean (charter rule 6). `in_flight` is now cleared from `ProbeGuard::drop` rather than from a statement after the await, so a probe that returns, errors, PANICS or is dropped all clear it, and a guard dropped without a version records `Failed` (so a panicking probe takes the backoff instead of re-firing every handshake). Without both halves a single panicking or hanging probe sealed the pool permanently un-probeable — the `OnceCell`-forever failure mode property (2) exists to prevent, wearing a hat. The TTL and backoff dials moved behind a `ProbeTuning` value — production is `ProbeTuning::default()`, *literally* the same constants, and no logic those durations feed carries a `#[cfg(test)]` branch, so a test that shrinks one drives the code the daemon runs (the ONE test-only member is a panic-injection hook, `#[cfg(test)] fault`, compiled out of every non-test build; it exists because a panic INSIDE the probe task is the one failure shape no real backend can produce, and it is exactly the shape that used to leak `in_flight` for the daemon's life) — so the state machine is testable at ms scale, which is what made the EXPIRY direction guardable at both of its sites — `an_expired_version_is_re_probed_rather_than_trusted_for_the_daemons_life` and `an_expired_version_is_advertised_as_unknown_not_as_a_stale_string`, each RED under its own site's `OnceCell`-forever mutation and green under the other's. **One limitation, recorded rather than covered:** an ARBITRARY hang injected INSIDE the probe (`std::future::pending()`) would still seal the pool, because there is deliberately no outer drop-based timeout — such a timeout would drop a live `Checkout` mid-statement and reintroduce exactly the hazard the paragraph above closes. Every REACHABLE hang shape is bounded and `probe_version_on` is total by construction; a future unbounded await added inside it is the thing to catch in review, and its doc comment says so.

  **Carry (a) — the backend DIAL is unbounded EVERYWHERE EXCEPT this probe.** `Pool::checkout`'s `checkout_timeout` covers only the semaphore acquire; `PgBackend::connect` and `MysqlBackend::connect` pass no connect timeout and TCP keepalive is off, so a black-holed dial runs to the OS TCP timeout (~127 s measured) while holding one of the pool's permits. The probe's `VERSION_CHECKOUT_BUDGET` is a bandage on ONE caller: `run_autocommit_exec`, the TX actor and the stream producer all still dial unbounded. This belongs to M0/M1-S3 (`ferro-pool`), not to S8a — `docs/followups/2026-08-10-unbounded-backend-dial.md`, three fix directions, none chosen.

  **Carry (b) — `server_version: nil` was a CONSUMER decision S8b had to make before it wrote a driver. It is now DECIDED and IMPLEMENTED (D-S8b-1, M1-S8b).** `proto/PROTOCOL.md` §4 documents that `nil` is a legitimate steady state; what it does not say is that S8b turns this string into a DBAL PLATFORM, i.e. into which SQL grammar the tier emits. `nil` recurs on a HEALTHY system (TTL expiry racing a slow re-probe, a transient failure after expiry, a backend down at connect), so a driver meeting it must fail loudly or defer resolution — it must NOT silently fall back to a default platform, which is a wrong dialect rather than a clean error. **The decision taken: DEFER, then RESOLVE, then FAIL LOUDLY** — resolution is postponed to the point of need; if the version is still unknown there, the driver issues ONE `SELECT version()`; if that fails it raises `Ferro\DBAL\Exception\ServerVersionUnavailable` naming the pool, and names DBAL's own `params['serverVersion']` as the zero-round-trip escape hatch. Failing at CONNECT was rejected because `nil` is normal: it would turn a routine 5-second probe-backoff window into an outage for every worker reconnecting during it, and §19.1's `boot_epoch` reconnect storms make that concrete. Option (d) (advertise-stale-with-a-flag) remains rejected and would still need a `/proto` change that must not be made as a side effect of driver work. The asymmetry that makes this load-bearing rather than cosmetic: `kind` is never nil, but for the MySQL family `kind` is `mysql` for BOTH MySQL and MariaDB, so a nil version costs exactly the MariaDB-vs-MySQL dialect fork — the one distinction that matters most. Rationale and the rejected options: `docs/followups/2026-08-10-s8b-nil-server-version-decision.md`; §14 carries the implemented rule.

  **`PoolRegistry::probes_issued()` exists SOLELY so the caching claim is testable.** "Two handshakes report the same string" proves STABILITY, not caching — an engine that re-probed every time passes it identically, and it was measured passing under exactly that mutation. The counter is the observable the live gate (`ferrod`'s `hello_meta_it.rs`, on PostgreSQL 17 + MySQL 8.4 + MariaDB 11.8) asserts on: one probe per pool on the first handshake and none on the second; a refused backend re-probed only after the backoff; a BLACK-HOLED backend probed once across two handshakes with the second returning immediately; and four pools — one live, one refused, two black-holed — handshaking in under one budget with three `nil`s. `pool_info_from_config` was DELETED in the same change rather than kept as the "pool-less fallback" the plan sketched: every `PoolRegistry` is built from a `Config`, so a pool-bearing config cannot yield a pool-less registry and that branch could not have fired — and two derivations of one wire field is how the two drift. The `kind` token now has ONE source, `PoolKind::wire_name()`, whose `match` has no `_` arm. **That the probe does not TAINT the connection it borrows is asserted directly**, on a `Checkout` the test still owns (the checkout-time recycle clears `tainted`, so the same assertion after a re-checkout would be green no matter what the statement did) and on BOTH engine families, since PG taints off the RFQ byte and MySQL off the OK-packet session trackers.

  **(w) The `Isolation` wire enum is hand-duplicated in Rust and PHP, and NOTHING pinned the two together until the M1-S8a review round.** `BeginRequest.isolation` is an optional `u8` whose mapping (`ReadCommitted = 0`, `RepeatableRead = 1`, `Serializable = 2`, `nil` = engine/pool default) is written out by hand in BOTH `ferro-proto`'s `messages::tx::Isolation` and PHP's `Ferro\Protocol\Isolation`, on the argument — made explicitly in both docblocks and in `proto/PROTOCOL.md` §9.1 — that a message-field VALUE is outside charter rule 2's `/proto` source-of-truth scope (it is neither a method id, a flag, an error code nor a type tag). **The review found that exemption had left the two copies genuinely unpinned:** the `tx_begin_request` golden vector carries `isolation` as a RAW INT that `BeginRequest::encode` copied straight through, so the byte lock never touched the enum, and swapping the PHP values (`ReadCommitted = 2`, `Serializable = 0`) left the entire PHP suite green — 541 tests, 0 failures, PHPStan level 9 clean. Live impact at S8a was nil (both PHP BEGIN sites hardcode `isolation => null`, so the enum was unreachable dead code) — it was a trap armed for exactly the slice that will first use it, where `setTransactionIsolation(SERIALIZABLE)` would silently degrade to `READ COMMITTED` with no loud signal anywhere. **What the review round shipped is the minimum, not the durable fix.** `BeginRequest::encode` now accepts an `Isolation` case (`$iso instanceof Isolation => $iso->value`), which is what puts the enum on a byte-locked path at all, and `IsolationCrossLanguageTest` carries two KINDS of lock: (A) a golden-vector byte lock routing the ENUM CASE through `encode` — `Serializable` only, because the vector carries one isolation value — written as two separate methods on purpose, since folding the value assertion and the byte lock together lets the first short-circuit the second on exactly the drift both exist to catch; and (B) an all-three-cases comparison against the Rust peer's own discriminants AND its `TryFrom<u8>` arms, cross-checked against each other first, with every parse step failing LOUDLY rather than skipping (a lock that goes vacuous when the peer file moves is worse than no lock). Measured RED under four mutations: the PHP swap (all three locks, plus the full suite, which the same swap left green before), a moved peer file, and Rust-side drift in both a self-consistent and an inconsistent form. **Still OPEN:** promoting `isolation` into `/proto` (registry + lock + both generators) is the durable answer and was deferred — it moves `TYPE_REGISTRY_HASH` and could not be done from a `php/client`-scoped fix round running in parallel with other agents. The charter-rule-2 exemption argued in both docblocks is what allowed the gap and should be revisited when the promotion lands.

  **(x) The `F64` bind/read width is now falsifiable — it was not, on any backend.** Every `F64` fixture in the engine was `2.5` or `1.5`, both EXACTLY representable in `f32`, so narrowing the MySQL binder to `MyValue::Double((*f as f32) as f64)` — a silent corrupt write of every double a DBAL app binds — measured GREEN across the entire MySQL suite **and** the S7 canonical acceptance gate. The blindness was ENGINE-WIDE rather than backend-specific: the read path's fixture was equally f32-clean, and the shared all-engine gate's was too, so PG was no better guarded than MySQL. The `ferro-backend-mysql` fixtures are now f32-LOSSY by construction (`0.1+0.2`, `1/3`, a large integer-plus-fraction, a negative, `f64::MAX` which narrows to `inf` and `f64::MIN_POSITIVE` which narrows to `0`, so an exponent bug and a mantissa bug are separately witnessed), every comparison is on the IEEE-754 BIT PATTERN, and each fixture carries a self-check asserting it is genuinely f32-lossy so a future edit cannot re-open the blind spot by substituting a benign magnitude. Proved live on MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.8 by `query_it.rs::f64_bind_round_trip_is_bit_exact` (an independent text-literal oracle first, then the bind, then a bit-exact read-back) and offline by `bind.rs::f64_binds_the_exact_double_never_narrowed_through_f32`. **The shared S7 acceptance gate was closed in the same round** (`ferrod`'s `types_e2e_it.rs`): its single `F64` fixture is now `f64_fixture()` — `0.1 + 0.2`, with the f32-lossy self-check beside it — and the seeded SQL literal is a separate constant so the server's own parser stays an INDEPENDENT oracle for the produce direction rather than a re-rendering of the expectation. That closes the PG half: narrowing `PgFloat::to_sql`'s `FLOAT8` arm now fails the read → re-bind → read byte-stability assertion, narrowing `rowmap`'s `ExtractType::F64` arm fails the produce assertion, and swapping the fixture back to `1.5` fails on the fixture itself. The lesson generalises past floats: **a fixture whose value cannot distinguish the correct implementation from the broken one makes every test that uses it decorative**, however many of them there are.

  **(y) SPEC §14 was written against a DBAL-3-shaped SPI, and four of its sentences were unimplementable (M1-S8b).** Read from `doctrine/dbal 4.4.4`'s own `src/`, not from memory. `ServerInfoAwareConnection` and `VersionAwarePlatformDriver` **do not exist** in 4.x — `getServerVersion(): string` is on `Doctrine\DBAL\ServerVersionProvider`, which `Driver\Connection` extends, and it is **non-nullable**, so a `nil` `PoolInfo.server_version` cannot be represented and the driver can only resolve it or throw. `Driver::getDatabasePlatform()` is handed a `ServerVersionProvider`, **not** the connection, so it cannot read `HELLO_ACK` metadata at all and the driver must remember the pool KIND from the last `connect()` (and `Doctrine\DBAL\Connection::getDatabasePlatform()` can resolve the platform WITHOUT connecting, when `params['serverVersion']` is set, so that branch must be handled loudly rather than by guessing a family). `lastInsertId()` takes **no** argument (UPGRADE.md: *"Removed support for `Connection::lastInsertId($name)`"*) and must THROW when there is no identity value, which makes §14's "sequence-name argument supported for PG" impossible to express. And §14's configuration example was wrong for an independent reason: `Driver::connect()` is `@phpstan-param Params`, `Params` is a SEALED array shape with **no `ferro` key**, and reading `$params['ferro']['pool']` measured as two `nullCoalesce.offset` errors at PHPStan level 9 — a charter Definition-of-Done gate. `driverOptions?: array<mixed>` is the sanctioned slot and every key read out of it carries an explicit `is_string()`/`is_int()`/`is_bool()` narrowing. Two smaller corrections belong with them: `getNativeConnection()` returns the **`Ferro\Client\Connection`** the driver holds (§14 said `Session`; both satisfy the SPI's `resource|object`, and the `Connection` is the useful one) — and it does **not** escape the driver's type boundary, because it carries the driver's own `ValuePolicy`, so a value the driver refuses is refused through it too; and §14's `read_pool` idea has **no charter-compliant consumer** — charter rule 6 forbids inferring read-vs-write — so it ships as `driverOptions.readonly` on a second, explicitly-configured connection. All of these are amended in §14 in the same change set.

  **(z) The §14 acceptance bar is NOT reachable as written; the reachable part is MEASURED and recorded rather than silently narrowed (M1-S8b Task 14).** Five obstacles, each measured. **(1) SQLite is impossible** — `engine/crates` has `pg` + `mysql` only (`AnyPool { Pg | Mysql }`), so one third of "green on PG + MySQL + SQLite" waits for `ferro-backend-sqlite`. **(2) The upstream suite cannot select a third-party driver.** `TestUtil::getConnectionParams()` checks only `$params['driver']` and otherwise returns `['driver' => 'pdo_sqlite', 'memory' => true]`; `pdo_sqlite` is loaded, so the functional suite runs GREEN against in-memory SQLite with ZERO Ferro contact and neither `--fail-on-skipped` nor `ci/assert-no-skips.sh` catches it — nothing skips, everything genuinely passes, on the wrong engine. **This was reproduced deliberately at the gate rather than argued**: with the contact assertion disarmed and upstream's `TestUtil` restored, `tests/Functional/DataAccessTest` reported `OK (105 tests, 211 assertions)` against SQLite `3.45.1`. `/testkit` therefore ships a REPLACEMENT `TestUtil` (which THROWS when `db_driverClass` is absent, so the fallback cannot happen silently) and `testkit/dbal/bootstrap.php`, whose FIRST action is to assert `getNativeConnection() instanceof Ferro\Client\Connection`, print the resolved driver/platform/version, and round-trip a real `SELECT 1`. Restoring only the bootstrap turns the same run into `FERRO CONTACT ASSERTION FAILED: the suite's connection is a Pdo\Sqlite, not a Ferro one.` **(3) MySQL could not run the suite at all**: upstream's `initializeDatabase()` does `dropDatabase`/`createDatabase` and the testkit user had only `GRANT ALL ON ferro.*` — measured as 1057 errors out of 1077 functional tests. Ferro cannot serve that shape in any case (the DSN lives in the engine, PHP holds no credentials — §12/D8), so the database is pre-provisioned in `testkit/mysql-init.sql` + `testkit/postgres/init.sql` and the patched `initializeDatabase()` is a no-op. **(4) Even the STOCK `pdo_pgsql` driver is not green on these containers** (1 environmental failure over the whole tree). **(5) Removing `initializeDatabase()` removes the suite's ONLY reset**, and an unreset suite produces a number that is not reproducible: measured against a KNOWN-GOOD driver, the same command gave `Errors 23` then `Errors 33`. The runner therefore performs a container-side reset before phpunit (no PHP credentials involved — the same shape as the MySQL grant), gives every family its OWN `doctrine_tests` database — **never the shared `ferro` one**, which the suite would silt up with ~40 tables, 8+ sequences, several schemas, a domain type and views for every other live suite in this repo — and prints a `[ferro] reset: …` line that a recordable log must contain.

  **The bar is restated as: "the curated subset in `testkit/dbal/allowlist.txt`, run through the real `DriverManager` against a real backend with driver identity asserted before the first test, green modulo a recorded and triaged list."** `isDriverOneOf()` answers FALSE for every name — a deliberate choice with 60 upstream call sites behind it, since claiming `pdo_pgsql`/`pdo_mysql` would opt Ferro into PDO-specific expectations — which is why the skipped counts below are large and are not hidden failures. **Recorded, two-run reproducible on all three backends (result line AND the full ordered failure set diffed, six comparisons, no differences):** PostgreSQL 17.10 `730 collected / 374 executed / 296 passed / 7 failures / 71 errors`; MySQL 8.4.11 `730 / 385 / 372 / 9 / 4`; MariaDB 11.8.8 `730 / 384 / 371 / 9 / 4`. Numbers, environment manifest, the reproducibility evidence and a per-test triage are in `docs/dbal-suite/2026-08-11-results.md`. **Two findings dominate the PostgreSQL column and neither is a driver defect.** 50 of its 78 non-passing tests share ONE cause: DBAL's stock `PostgreSQLSchemaManager::selectIndexColumns()` selects `pg_index.indkey`, an `int2vector` the engine's PG read path has no mapping for, so **the stock PostgreSQL schema manager — and therefore `doctrine/migrations` and schema diffing — does not work on PG today** (loudly, `NonRetryable`; the MySQL family is unaffected). 16 more are the engine's PG bind matrix being narrower than libpq in the direction (aa) did not widen — a bound interval inside the platform's own date-arithmetic SQL makes PostgreSQL infer `text` for an `INTEGER` bind, and Doctrine's `BooleanType` hands the driver `int(1)` for a `bool` column. Both are engine changes with §19.3 lockstep obligations and are deliberately NOT smuggled into a driver slice: `docs/followups/2026-08-11-pg-int2vector-blocks-the-schema-manager.md` and `docs/followups/2026-08-11-pg-bind-matrix-narrower-than-libpq.md`. The gate also independently re-measured a pre-existing HIGH-severity CLIENT defect first journalled in Task 7: **an `I64` at or above 2^32 is unreadable by `php/client` on every value policy and every backend** — `write_sint` emits the unsigned `0xcf` ladder for a large non-negative, `PurePacker::be(..., signed: false)` returns a decimal STRING, and `CanonicalText::requireInt` accepts `is_int()` only, so any `bigint` past 4.29e9 and every epoch-milliseconds column throws (`docs/followups/2026-08-11-i64-above-2e32-unreadable-in-php-client.md`). **The ORM functional suite is NOT run** — `doctrine/orm` is not a dependency of this repository — and one blocker is already known and documented rather than engineered around: `Doctrine\ORM\Id\IdentityGenerator::generateId()` is `(int) $conn->lastInsertId()` and DBAL 4 defaults PostgreSQL to the IDENTITY strategy, which cannot work while PG reports no generated key. The remedy is ORM configuration (the SEQUENCE strategy), and it means **drop-in is config-only for DBAL and explicitly NOT config-only for ORM on PostgreSQL** (D-S8b-5). Capturing `lastval()` on the pinned checkout inside the engine WOULD make ORM-on-PG genuinely drop-in and was REJECTED for M1: it reverses a deliberate decision and puts engine work inside a slice scoped as a PHP package. Revisitable in M2 with explicit sign-off; not a side effect of driver work.

  **What this does and does NOT discharge for the M1 exit gate.** The M1 plan (the renumbering entry above) makes **S9 = "the DBAL upstream suite + incompat doc" the M1 exit gate**, on a "green modulo a documented allow-list" bar. S8b Task 14 built the RUNNER, produced the FIRST recorded measurement and wrote the incompat stub — but it does **not** close S9, and the numbers say why: the MySQL family is within sight of the bar (13 non-passing of 385 executed, every one explained), while PostgreSQL is not (78 of 374), and the gap is two engine gaps rather than a longer allow-list. S9's honest remaining work is therefore (1) `int2vector`, (2) the `I64 → text`/`bool` bind widening, (3) the `I64 ≥ 2^32` client read, and only then the allow-list argument. Recording that here rather than letting a green-looking MySQL column stand in for the gate.

  **(aa) A canonical `TAG_TEXT` param now binds wherever PostgreSQL's own TEXT INPUT SYNTAX is what it carries — the drop-in blocker, closed without loosening §19.3 (M1-S8b Task 4).** Stock Doctrine's type layer stringifies every `datetime`/`date`/`time`/`decimal`/`json`/`guid` value and binds it as `ParameterType::STRING`, which reaches the engine as `TAG_TEXT`; `PgText`'s `accepts` was `String`'s (`varchar`/`text`/`bpchar`/`name`/`unknown` + the name-keyed `citext`/`ltree`/…), so **every such INSERT was refused pre-send on PostgreSQL** while MySQL — which has no bind pre-flight at all — accepted the identical driver. `PgText` is now an explicit impl accepting those types **plus** `numeric`, `date`, `time`, `timestamp`, `timestamptz`, `uuid`, `json` and `jsonb`. **The format change is not cosmetic:** the `pg_domain_aware_param!` macro delegated `encode_format` to `<String as ToSql>`, which takes the trait's `Format::Binary` default, so widening `accepts` ALONE would have told PostgreSQL that the UTF-8 bytes of `2026-08-05` were a 4-byte binary `date`. **Both `to_sql` and `encode_format` therefore BRANCH on the resolved base** — verbatim text and `Format::Text` for the eight new targets, the unchanged delegated path (and `Format::Binary`) for everything `String` already accepted — and the branch is load-bearing twice over. It is required for CORRECTNESS, because "text bytes are the binary bytes for every string type this already took" is FALSE: `<&str as ToSql>::accepts` also admits `citext`, `ltree`, `lquery` and `ltxtquery` by NAME, and for the last three the binary form is `0x01 || text`. And it is required for FALSIFIABILITY, because that same name-sensitive encoder is the only thing that makes the payload-bytes clause of `s8a_every_arm_treats_a_domain_exactly_as_its_base` able to fail — the `ltree` fixture entry S8a's review round added for exactly that purpose. A type-blind `to_sql` would make a domain and its base write identical bytes by construction and silently revert that repair: MEASURED, the mutation that is RED at HEAD goes GREEN. The regression surface on everything that already worked is consequently empty rather than believed-harmless. **§19.3's direction is intact:** `check_param`'s `Value::Text` arm delegates to this same `accepts`, so the pre-flight and the impl are bit-identical by construction and moved in ONE edit; and what the widening admits is not an unclassifiable failure but a real server-side `22007`/`22P02` `DbError`, which `is_session_fatal` reads as non-fatal and `error_map` classifies `NonRetryable`. **The sentinel discipline the old narrowness protected is PRESERVED as a value-aware gate**: PostgreSQL's special input literals (`infinity`, `-infinity`, `now`, `today`, `tomorrow`, `yesterday`, `epoch`, `allballs`, and `NaN`/`Infinity` for `numeric`) are still refused for a temporal or numeric slot, naming the tagged route (`Ferro\Date`, `Ferro\NaiveTimestamp`, `Ferro\Decimal`) that expresses a sentinel deliberately — while the identical string remains an ordinary value in a `text` column. That is a REFUSAL keyed on the slot's declared type, not an inference of a tag from content: nothing decides that `'2026-08-05'` "is a date". **What this did NOT widen, measured at the acceptance gate:** the OTHER direction. `TAG_I64` into a PostgreSQL `text` or `bool` slot is still refused, which two ordinary stock-Doctrine shapes hit — see (z) and `docs/followups/2026-08-11-pg-bind-matrix-narrower-than-libpq.md`.

  **(ab) Doctrine's stock type layer is a silently-corrupting calendar parser, and the driver REFUSES rather than converts (M1-S8b Task 9).** Measured on 4.4.4, with **no exception raised**: `date '2026-00-05'` → `DateTime(2025-12-05)`; `datetime '0000-00-00 00:00:00'` → `DateTime(-0001-11-30)`; `time '24:00:00'` — a value PostgreSQL genuinely stores — → `00:00:00`. `proto/PROTOCOL.md` §3.2 warned about this parser class in prose; that is the measurement. The mirror problem is `datetimetz`, which is unreadable in the other direction: `DateTimeTzType` has no fallback and accepts only `Y-m-d H:i:sO` on PostgreSQL and `Y-m-d H:i:s` on the MySQL family, so **every** canonical RFC3339 form throws on **every** platform, and every microsecond form throws too. The driver's own `ValuePolicy` handles both — `ValuePolicy::decode(int $tag, mixed $data)` is per-cell TAG-AWARE by construction, so no client API change was needed to see column tags. A whole-second `TIMESTAMPTZ` is re-rendered into the platform's own format (per family, from two literals LOCKED against the stock accessors by a unit test, so a DBAL release that changes either goes red); a sub-second one is **refused rather than truncated**, because silent precision loss is the same defect class as the corruption above; and sentinels, zero dates, negative times, sub-second times and `24:00:00` are refused with a message naming the native API as the way to read them. Charter rule 6 is untouched: this is the driver's own conversion step, which `RawStringValuePolicy`'s docblock already blessed. **One claim in that docblock was FALSIFIED and corrected in the same change set:** `AbstractPlatform::getDateTimeFormatString()` does NOT reject a canonical fractional `TIMESTAMP` — `DateTimeType::convertToPHPValue` falls back to `new DateTime($value)`, measured twice independently — so the naive-timestamp fraction survives untouched and only the `datetimetz` and `time` claims stand. **Every refusal is `Ferro\DBAL\Exception\NonRepresentableValue`, and it carries no §9.2 fate branch** — like the client's own `TypePolicyException`, it reaches the exception converter branch-less and comes out a plain `DriverException`, so a policy refusal can never be reported as an indeterminate write nor upgraded to retryable. The catalogue of refused values is a first-class incompatibility, not a footnote: through this driver, PG `time '24:00:00'`, PG `date`/`timestamp` `infinity`/`-infinity`, MySQL zero and zero-in dates, MySQL negative `TIME` intervals, sub-second `TIME` and sub-second `TIMESTAMPTZ` all raise instead of returning a value — every one of them a value stock PDO+DBAL returns, corrupted, silently. The honest framing, and the one `docs/known-incompatibilities.md` uses, is **"we refuse what PDO corrupts"**, with the measurements.

  **(ac) `iterate*()` streams on the PARAMETERLESS read path only, and every DBAL statement is fate-declared a WRITE (M1-S8b Tasks 1, 12).** Two bounded honesty statements about §14's wording, the second of which costs more than it first appears. **Streaming.** `Doctrine\DBAL\Result::iterateAssociative()` is literally a loop over `fetchAssociative()`, so "never buffer" reduces to pulling one row at a time — but `Connection::executeStatement()` with parameters is `$stmt->execute()->rowCount()`, and a streamed request's terminal carries **no `affected` field**, so streaming the prepared path would make every parameterized write return 0. The driver therefore streamed on `query()` — the zero-parameter `executeQuery`, where DBAL never asks for a row count — and buffered elsewhere; and MySQL/MariaDB buffered everywhere, because engine-side row streaming there was still deferred ((n), controller decision D-S8b-2). **BOTH halves of that are now obsolete, and both were removed by measurement rather than by decision:** the "a streamed terminal carries no `affected`" premise was measured FALSE ((ag)), so the prepared path streams ((ah)); and (n)'s engine deferral closed, so MySQL/MariaDB stream too, retiring D-S8b-2 (B2c). Nothing in the tier buffers for iteration any more. The paragraph is kept because the REASONING is still the record of why it was ever true. **This REFINES (n)** rather than contradicting it: (n) records that the tier streams on PG and buffers on MySQL, and this is the additional bound inside the PG half. Adding `affected` to the stream terminal is the durable fix and is a `/proto` change (registry + golden vectors + both codecs), deliberately NOT smuggled into a driver slice. The consequence a user sees is that **`rowCount()` after a `SELECT` now diverges by ROUTE as well as by FAMILY**: on PostgreSQL the parameterised (buffered) route reports the row count and the zero-parameter (streamed) route reports 0; MySQL reports 0 either way. It is not normalised, because normalising means counting rows — the exact conflation §14 warns about — and DBAL itself documents `rowCount()`-on-a-`SELECT` as driver-specific. (`free()` keeping `rowCount()` while emptying rows and columns is likewise a CHOICE: upstream is split, `Driver\SQLite3\Result` keeps its count and `Driver\PgSQL\Result` answers 0.) Because the client session is strictly single-in-flight, an open streamed `Result` drains its remainder before the connection issues anything else — so the canonical `foreach (iterate…) { executeStatement(…) }` idiom keeps working (it degrades to what PDO does unconditionally) instead of throwing a `ProtocolException` every user would read as a driver bug. A second, smaller change of vantage point comes with streaming: **on a streamed read a statement's ERROR surfaces mid-iteration, not from `executeQuery()`**, because the open reads only the HEAD. The FATES are unchanged (`fate.rs` is reused verbatim); where you catch them is not.

  **Abandonment is a different case and must not collapse into that one.** DBAL never calls a driver `Result::free()` when a consumer stops iterating (`Doctrine\DBAL\Result` has no `__destruct`; the only reference is the returned Generator's bound `$this`), so the driver holds a `\WeakReference` to the open result and the result cancels its own stream on destruction. A driver holding a STRONG reference would be the only thing keeping an abandoned stream alive, and `break`-ing out of a large `iterateAssociative()` would then transfer the ENTIRE remaining result set on the next statement — invisible in a 100 000-row test, an OOM on a real table. **The measured LIMIT of that design, stated because it is what an operator will hit:** it cancels for the CANONICAL idiom, `foreach ($conn->iterateAssociative($sql) as $row) { … break; }`, where the generator is a temporary and PHP frees the driver `Result` by refcount at the `break`. It does NOT cancel for a BOUND iterator — `$it = $conn->iterateAssociative($sql); foreach ($it as $row) { break; }` — where `$it` keeps the result alive and the remainder is transferred on the next statement (MEASURED: 99 975 of 100 000 rows). A live reference is indistinguishable from a caller who may still fetch, so this is a PHP refcount fact, not a design choice; both shapes are pinned by tests, and the operator's rule is *iterate the call directly, or `unset()` the iterator*. `Ferro\DBAL\Connection::settledRowCount()` makes all three cases observable: 0 for pure iteration and for the canonical abandon, non-zero only for interleaving.

  **Fate.** The DBAL 4 SPI carries NO read/write signal — `executeQuery('INSERT … RETURNING id')` with no parameters reaches the driver's `query()`, and the prepared path serves `executeQuery` and `executeStatement` alike — and charter rule 6 forbids inferring one from the SQL text. Every result-producing method on `Ferro\Client\Connection` hard-codes `readonly = true`, so a driver built on them would have reported a lost `INSERT … RETURNING` as **Retryable**, i.e. "provably did not apply", for a write whose fate is genuinely unknown. `Connection::fetchRaw()`/`streamRaw()` exist to let the CALLER declare the fate, the driver declares **write** for everything, and a read-only connection is an explicit `driverOptions['readonly' => true]` — configuration, never inference. Conservative by construction: it never costs safety. **What it DOES cost is stated in full, because it is more than retryability.** `readonly` is read in TWO places in `fate.rs`, and the second is the **57014 override**: with `!in_tx`, a cancelled or timed-out statement is `Cancelled{NonRetryable}` when the client declared `readonly` and `WriteUnconfirmed{Indeterminate}` when it did not. So under this driver **a plain `SELECT` killed by an operator's `statement_timeout` — a normal production setting — surfaces as `Ferro\DBAL\IndeterminateWriteException`**, "your write may or may not have landed", for a statement that wrote nothing. That is cry-wolf on the one exception that must never be routinely retried, and it is a NEW failure shape rather than a lost one: the same statement through the native client API gets a clean "statement cancelled or timed out". **A second cost rides with it, measured on both cells:** the §19.3 override REBUILDS the error payload with `sqlstate: None` and `errno: None` (see (o)), so a cancelled statement reaches DBAL with a **NULL SQLSTATE and `getCode() === 0`, where PDO would give `57014`**. The fate is carried by the exception CLASS and by the `/proto` code on the chained client exception, not by the SQLSTATE. Three consequences are discharged in this same change set. **(1)** The property `run_streamed_exec`'s and `abort_stream`'s docblocks state — "A streamed READ never becomes `Indeterminate` (classify_fate routes it by `readonly`)" — was never unconditional, and `CLAUDE.md`'s M1-S5 paragraph restated it without the condition; **all three are amended here** to say the guarantee belongs to a client that DECLARES `readonly`, and that the M1-S8b Doctrine driver deliberately does not. **(2)** It is listed in `docs/known-incompatibilities.md` as the FIRST entry of its Errors section, including the instruction not to add a blanket retry on `IndeterminateWriteException` — that is exactly the at-most-once violation the branch exists to prevent — and the one-line configuration (`driverOptions['readonly' => true]`) that restores the clean answer for a connection that genuinely only reads. **(3)** It is pinned by a live guard (`ExceptionMappingLiveTest::testACancelledSelectIsIndeterminateOnAWriteConnectionAndNotOnAReadonlyOne`) that asserts BOTH cells, so the cost is falsifiable and cannot be silently "fixed" later by inferring read-vs-write from SQL text. **A related, deliberate non-mapping:** neither `IndeterminateWriteException` nor `Ferro\DBAL\RetryableDriverException` extends `Doctrine\DBAL\Exception\ConnectionLost`. Upstream's `TransactionTest` kills the backend session and expects `ConnectionLost`; under Ferro it gets the §19.3 refinement instead, and that is the point — frameworks treat `ConnectionLost` as reconnect-and-retry, so inheriting from it would invite precisely the retry charter rule 3 forbids.

  **(ad) `setTransactionIsolation()` is captured TYPED in an optional `wrapperClass`, and the raw statement is REFUSED (M1-S8b Task 13).** Doctrine's own implementation runs `executeStatement($platform->getSetTransactionIsolationSQL($level))` — `SET SESSION TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL …` on MySQL, `SET SESSION CHARACTERISTICS AS TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL …` on PostgreSQL — which are exactly the two forms (s) names as FORBIDDEN. On a transaction-mode pool the statement lands on an arbitrary pooled connection, taints it, and hygiene wipes it before the next `BEGIN`: it reports SUCCESS and changes nothing, while `getTransactionIsolation()` keeps returning the level Doctrine cached. (s) also records that the obvious cross-tenant test CANNOT FAIL, because hygiene masks the leak in both directions. `Ferro\DBAL\Wrapper\FerroConnection` overrides the method, captures the level as a typed enum ABOVE the SQL layer, emits nothing, and hands it to the driver connection to ride `BeginRequest.isolation` on the next transaction — where `compose_begin_sql` composes the correct per-transaction form for the dialect. No SQL is inspected, rewritten or generated: the wrapper simply never emits the statement. Without the wrapper the driver REFUSES the statement, prefix-anchored on the two fixed platform-generated strings (anchored specifically so a literal inside an `INSERT` cannot trip it — a false refusal on ordinary SQL would be worse than the bug), with a message naming the one-line configuration fix. **The refusal is enforced at THREE entry points, not two**: `exec()`, `runPrepared()` **and** `query()`. The plan specified two, and the hole was measured: PostgreSQL's `query()` routes to `streamRaw()` while MySQL's delegates to `runPrepared()`, so a two-family test guarding only MySQL would have called the two-site form correct. `READ UNCOMMITTED` maps to `ReadCommitted`, per `Ferro\Protocol\Isolation`'s own documented mapping — a genuine tightening on MySQL, never a weakening, and listed in `docs/known-incompatibilities.md`. This slice is also the **first real caller** of the hand-duplicated `Isolation` enum that (w) describes, so its cross-language lock is finally exercised by behaviour rather than merely present: swapping the `Serializable` arm to `RepeatableRead` turns the live guard RED on BOTH families, and on MySQL it does so behaviourally (REPEATABLE READ does not turn a plain SELECT into a locking read) rather than by message text.

  **(ae) The PG catalog VECTORS `int2vector` (OID 22) and `oidvector` (OID 30) are admitted on the READ path as `TAG_TEXT`, rendering PG's own space-separated text (M1-S9).** This is the first of (z)'s three named M1-exit gaps: DBAL's stock `PostgreSQLSchemaManager::selectIndexColumns()` selects `pg_index.indkey` raw, and its refusal was the ONE cause behind 50 of PostgreSQL's 78 non-passing suite tests — the stock schema manager, `doctrine/migrations` and every schema-diff workflow. The rendering is the exact string `int2vectorout`/`oidvectorout` produce and `pdo_pgsql` hands stock Doctrine (`"1 3"`, empty vector = empty string — never the regular-array `"{1,3}"` form), decoded from the array-wire binary payload (`int2vectorsend` IS `array_send`) by `pgtext::{int2vector,oidvector}_to_text` and proven live against PG's own `::text` output byte-for-byte on real `pg_index.indkey` and `pg_proc.proargtypes` cells in the same query. **This does NOT open the array class**: a vector is 1-D, NULL-free and 0-based by PG's own recv functions — the renderer refuses any payload that isn't (a distinct named `Backend` decode-mismatch per §9.1, never `Indeterminate`) — and the live deferral guard's boundary sentinel moved from `oidvector` to `int2[]` (OID 1005), the true array one step away, so the admission is by OID, not by family association. **The bind direction is deliberately NOT widened** (the follow-up doc's question 3): neither vector is ever a parameter in any schema-manager query, `bind.rs` is untouched, and §19.3's directional rule stands — no `/proto` change of any kind (the tags ride `TAG_TEXT`, so charter rule 2 is satisfied vacuously). Follow-up doc: `docs/followups/2026-08-11-pg-int2vector-blocks-the-schema-manager.md` (both of its open questions answered: DBAL 4.4's schema manager carries `indkey` and unnests server-side, and byte-equality with `pdo_pgsql`'s rendering is proven by the `::text` oracle either way; `oidvector` decided WITH `int2vector` per question 2).

  **(af) The PG bind matrix gained the two libpq-parity widenings ordinary stock-Doctrine shapes reach — `I64 → text` and `I64 → bool` (value-gated) — the second of (z)'s three named M1-exit gaps (M1-S9).** 16 measured suite failures, all `canonical I64 cannot bind to PG type text/bool`. `I64 → text`: PostgreSQL infers `text` for a parameter concatenated into a string, which is DBAL's own `getDateAddSecondsExpression` SQL (`? || ' SECOND'`) — the decimal rendering binds verbatim in TEXT format, `Type::TEXT` ONLY (no `varchar`/`bpchar`: no measured caller, and unmeasured widening is how a pre-flight rots). `I64 → bool`: Doctrine's own `BooleanType::convertToDatabaseValue(true, pg)` returns `int(1)` (measured) and an untyped `Connection::insert()` binds it through as `TAG_I64` — admitted with the EXPLICIT decision the follow-up doc demanded: a **VALUE gate** in `check_range` admits ONLY 0 and 1 (the exact values Doctrine emits) and refuses every other integer PRE-SEND with the value, the reason and both routes named (`Types::BOOLEAN`, or pass 0/1), so a stray `5` can never silently become a boolean — the §9.1 coercion class stays refused, and the fate stays KNOWN (PG would have refused `'5'` too, but server-side, after the send). `accepts`, `to_sql` and `encode_format` moved in ONE edit (`PgInt` branches on the resolved base: decimal-text + `Format::Text` for `text`, native binary `bool` for 0/1, delegated binary for the integer widths; the non-0/1-into-bool `to_sql` arm is an `Err` backstop, so the pre-flight is EQUAL to the impl there, never looser). **The §19.3 directional lockstep proof was re-derived, not re-run**, per the follow-up doc: the cross-product proof `continue`s past every pair `accepts` refuses, so a VALUE-gated widening whose accept-side value is missing from `every_variant` is silently never exercised — `Value::I64(1)` joined the fixture as the only value for which `accepts(I64, BOOL)` is true, which is what makes the widened pair reach the boxed impl at all (the same structural-blindness class S8a found once already, in its third costume). The F64 arms are UNTOUCHED and still refuse `text`/`bool`. Proven live in the two measured shapes verbatim: `SELECT ? || ' SECOND'` with `I64(300)`, the full date-add expression end to end, `I64(1)`/`I64(0)` round-tripping a real `bool` column as `Bool`, and `I64(5)` refused pre-send with the connection proven untouched. Follow-up doc: `docs/followups/2026-08-11-pg-bind-matrix-narrower-than-libpq.md` (RESOLVED).

  **(ag) CORRECTION to (ac), measured at M1-S9 (B1a): the stream terminal has ALWAYS carried a real `affected` — the missing half was the CLIENT, and no `/proto` change exists to make.** (ac)'s clause "a streamed request's terminal carries no `affected` field" and its remedy "adding `affected` to the stream terminal … is a `/proto` change" were both FALSE when written: `PROTOCOL.md` §10 has said since M1-S5 that the stream `END` is the same `Outcome::Ok(ExecOk)` terminal every EXEC uses with "only `affected`/`stats` populated", and the engine fills it truthfully — `StreamEnd.affected` is the command tag read post-drain (`RowStream::rows_affected()`, "never a hardcoded 0"), encoded by `build_stream_terminal_body`. What actually blocked the prepared path from streaming is that `php/client`'s stream pump decoded the Ok terminal only to check it for an error and DROPPED the body. B1a closes that half: the pump settles a shared `StreamTerminal` cell on the Ok terminal, surfaced as `RawStream::{settled, affected, lastInsertId}` — null-until-settled, never a premature or invented 0 (an abandoned stream, an error terminal and a body-less defensive Ok all stay unsettled, because a command tag that was never read has no honest value). Proven live: a fully drained 500-row streamed SELECT settles with `affected == 500` from PG's own command tag through a real `ferrod`. What (ac) got RIGHT is untouched: the driver still buffers the prepared path today, `rowCount()`'s route divergence still stands, and switching the prepared path to stream (B1b) is a DRIVER change with one real decision left — `rowCount()` on an undrained streamed result must either drain-then-answer or answer "unsettled", and that choice belongs to the driver slice that makes it. Process note, recorded because the loop will hit this class again: (ac) was written in the S8b driver slice ABOUT the engine's wire contract without re-reading `PROTOCOL.md` §10 — a cross-layer claim recorded from memory. The correction was found by re-verifying the premise before implementing the "fix" (the ledger's verify-the-defect-first rule), which turned a planned `/proto` change into a 40-line client change.

  **(ah) The Doctrine tier's PREPARED path streams on PostgreSQL, and `rowCount()` on a streamed result is DRAIN-THEN-ANSWER (M1-S9 B1b).** The two halves of the old B1, finished: (ag) established that the stream terminal always carried the command-tag `affected` and B1a surfaced it on `RawStream`; this slice makes the driver USE it. `Connection::runPrepared()` now opens `streamRaw()` on a PostgreSQL pool exactly as `query()` does (MySQL keeps buffering — §22.2 (n) is untouched; `exec()` also keeps `fetch:none`, deliberately: its contract IS the affected count, so a stream would be opened and drained inside one call on the path Doctrine's own savepoints ride). The one open decision (ag) left to this slice is made and pinned: **`Result::rowCount()` on a still-open streamed result drains, then answers** — `executeStatement()` is `$stmt->execute()->rowCount()`, so the prepared path could not stream at all without it, and it is the honest trade: a parameterized WRITE produces no DATA frames (its "drain" is the terminal that was arriving anyway, so the count is as immediate as the buffered path's), a streamed SELECT's `rowCount()` costs the remainder of the stream and answers what `pdo_pgsql` answers, and pure iteration still never buffers. The drained rows stay fetchable (drain buffers, never discards). A result freed before its terminal keeps `0` — the command tag was never read. **This closes §14's never-buffer clause on PostgreSQL** (every result-producing path streams) and REPLACES (ac)'s `rowCount()` route-divergence table: on PG both routes now report the row count; MySQL's `0`-for-`SELECT` stands. The `tx_id` routing invariant is untouched and re-proven live — `streamRaw()` rides the open transaction's session and pinned `tx_id` exactly as `fetchRaw()` did, and the nested-savepoint live test now exercises the STREAMED prepared path end to end. Pinned live in the shapes that matter: `executeStatement('UPDATE … WHERE id <= ?', [7])` returns 7 through the streamed path, `executeQuery(…)->rowCount()` on a parameterized SELECT answers the row count where it used to answer 0, and the memory-probe/interleave/abandonment trio all run through the prepared path now. The DBAL-suite effect (the ~14 `rowCount`-adjacent tests plus the never-buffer clause) is measured at A5, not asserted here.

  **(ai) `/proto` gained `TxNotFound` (`0x300B`, NonRetryable): "that `tx_id` is gone" and "your frame is malformed" are no longer the same code (M1 Phase B, B3).** The follow-up (t) named and (ac)'s neighbours inherited. `ferrod` answered BOTH facts with `errc::PROTOCOL`, which forced `php/client`'s `rollBack()` into an unwinnable choice: it MUST swallow "that transaction is gone" (it is called from a `finally` already carrying the caller's real error, and replacing that error is the exact failure the asymmetry exists to prevent — measured in the M1-S8a review), so it swallowed `Protocol` wholesale and with it a malformed `TxControl` body, i.e. the client's own codec defect, on the one path where a client most needs to hear about one. **Two engine sites move, and only two**: `resolve_active`'s `NotFoundOrForbidden` arm (unknown / already-ended / another session's — indistinguishable by design, §7, and reached by a tx-scoped `EXEC` as much as by the TX methods) and `actor_gone_terminal`'s non-tombstone arm ("transaction is no longer active"). Everything else that was `Protocol` STAYS `Protocol` — a body that will not decode, an unknown savepoint name, a bad isolation composition — which is what makes the narrowed swallow list mean something. The deadline tombstone is deliberately NOT folded in: it stays `TxDeadline` (`0x1003`, **Retryable**) so an owner can still tell "the engine ended this" from "this was never yours", and both codes remain in the client's swallow list for the same reason as before.

  **The falsifier is the deliverable, not the code.** A test that a `TxNotFound` terminal is swallowed passes just as happily if the two codes collapse back into one; what cannot is a test that a `Protocol` terminal on `rollBack()` THROWS. That test now exists at all three tiers — `php/client` (`testAProtocolTerminalOnRollbackIsNoLongerSwallowed`), the DBAL driver (a new row wedged between the tx-gone row and the generic-failure row of the same mirror test), and live against real `ferrod`+PG, where ONE session gets a `TxNotFound` for a never-issued `tx_id` and a `Protocol` for a `0xC1` body and the two are asserted to differ. Both single-tier guards were mutation-proven (put `ERR_PROTOCOL` back in `TX_ALREADY_GONE`, or point `tx_not_found()` at `errc::PROTOCOL`, and they go red).

  **Two consequences worth stating rather than discovering.** (1) This is a REGISTRY change, so `TYPE_REGISTRY_HASH` moved: a client and engine built from different revisions now fail at the handshake (§5) instead of disagreeing about this code at runtime — which is the designed behaviour and is what makes the client-side swallow safe to rely on. (2) `error_tx_not_found` is the first committed golden vector that is a TX-service RESPONSE, so the Rust vector harness's `(service, method)` dispatch had to learn the `END`-flag guard the `BEGIN` arm already had; `COMMIT` and `SAVEPOINT` both got it.

  **(aj) The backend DIAL is bounded, and `checkout_timeout` is a PER-STEP bound rather than a total budget (M1 Phase B, B5).** `Pool::checkout` bounded the semaphore acquire and nothing else, and neither `tokio_postgres::connect` nor `mysql_async`'s dial carries a connect timeout — so against a host that neither answers nor resets (a black-holed address, a drop-rule firewall, a wedged hypervisor) the dial ran to the OS TCP connect timeout, measured at ~127 s, **while holding one of the pool's `max_size` permits**. Sixteen such callers exhausted a pool for two minutes with nothing running on any connection. The dial is now wrapped in `config.checkout_timeout` and a timeout returns `PoolError::Timeout`. **The knob was reused rather than joined by a new `connect_timeout`, and the reason is a fact about the shipped code, not a preference:** the BOUNDED-recycle block ten lines below already reuses `checkout_timeout` as a per-conn cleanup bound, so a checkout could always spend it on the acquire and then again on each conn it recycled — `checkout_timeout` has never been a total, and minting a second knob would have implied it was. `Timeout` rather than `ConnectionLost` because both are Retryable and neither ever sent a user statement (`sent = false`, no `Indeterminate` question either way), so the split costs nothing and is what lets an operator tell "the backend refused us" from "the backend never answered" — the whole signature of the case the bound exists for.

  **The cancel dial was the worse half.** Both backends' out-of-band `Cancel::cancel()` open a SIDE connection, unbounded for the same reason — and `ferrod` awaits `cancel_handle.cancel().await` INLINE on the timeout/cancel arms of `run_autocommit_exec` before re-awaiting the query future, so an unreachable backend stalled the very teardown the deadline was supposed to start. Both now bound that dial at a fixed 2 s `CANCEL_DIAL_BUDGET` — a constant and not a config knob, because a cancel is best-effort BY CONTRACT: its timeout and its failure have identical handling, the actor tears the transaction down regardless, and no statement is ever re-run (charter rule 3), so giving up loses nothing the caller was promised.

  **The guard asserts the released permit, not the elapsed time**, because a `checkout()` that returned `Timeout` while LEAKING its permit would satisfy a naive timing assertion and still leave the pool a slot short forever — which is precisely the recorded harm. The test drives a `max_size: 1` pool and requires a SECOND checkout to succeed; a companion test pins the success path so the bound cannot silently become a ban; both are mutation-proven. Still open and deliberately not folded in: removing `ferrod`'s now-redundant per-caller `VERSION_CHECKOUT_BUDGET`. **TCP keepalive on ESTABLISHED connections is CLOSED as an engine item — see (au), which also corrects this entry's "TCP keepalive is off": it is off for MySQL and ON for PostgreSQL.**

  **(ak) The PHP client enforced the frame cap on DECODE only, so an oversize bind killed the session instead of failing locally (M1 Phase B, B6a).** `Header::decode` has always rejected a `payload_len` above `MAX_FRAME_PAYLOAD`; `Codec::encodeFrame` had no such guard, so the client would put on the wire a frame **its own decoder would reject** — measured: `encode()` of `MAX_FRAME_PAYLOAD + 1` succeeds and feeding those 16 bytes to `decode()` throws.
