The Rust codec has guarded its `Encoder<OutFrame>` since M0, which makes this a cross-codec asymmetry of exactly the kind charter rule 2 exists to prevent; `ferrod`'s own `session_rules::oversize_payload_len_is_fatal` even notes in passing that the Rust encoder "would refuse to build such a frame". **The severity is the engine's correct response, not the send.** An oversize `payload_len` is `Classification::Fatal` — `ferrod` emits a `Protocol` terminal on `request_id 0` and CLOSES the connection, and it has no alternative, because the framing is desynchronised and a payload it declined to read cannot be skipped. So binding a `Ferro\Bytes` above 16 MiB — reachable from stock Doctrine through `ParameterType::LARGE_OBJECT` — dropped the session rather than raising an error. The guard now mirrors Rust's, at the mirror site, and fires **before any byte reaches the transport**: the caller gets a local exception naming both the limit and the actual size, and the session is untouched and still usable, which is the property the test asserts (nothing written, then a normal request on the SAME session completes). The inclusive boundary is pinned too — a payload of exactly `MAX_FRAME_PAYLOAD` must still encode, since §5.2 deliberately sizes the initial credit window so any single valid frame fits it, and a cap that rejected its own maximum would break that large-row rule rather than merely being conservative.

  **This bounds a FRAME, not a bind, and the difference is the deferral.** Carrying one payload across several frames — chunked `LARGE_OBJECT` — is a `/proto` design change (registry + golden vectors + both codecs in one change set) plus an engine-side reassembly path needing its own memory bound, so it is deferred to M2 rather than smuggled in here. What B6a changes is that the existing ceiling is now honest: a clear refusal naming the number instead of a dropped connection.

  **(al) The Eloquent tier hands Illuminate PDO-shaped SCALARS, and skipping `prepareBindings()` was a real defect (M2, C2).** Two things the tier's own unit and live tests could not see, both caught by the first run of `laravel/framework` v11.51.0's own integration subset, both fixed. **First, the binding path.** `FerroPostgresConnection`'s three execution paths passed `array_values($bindings)` straight to the client, skipping `Connection::prepareBindings()` — Illuminate's OWN normalisation, which converts a `DateTimeInterface` to the grammar's date format and a `bool` to an int. Eloquent binds `Carbon` instances directly for datetime attributes, so a plain `Model::create(['created_at' => $carbon])` arrived tagged canonical `TIMESTAMPTZ` and was correctly refused against the naive `timestamp` column `$table->timestamps()` creates. Every binding now goes through `prepareBindings()`; a suite of scalar-bound unit tests is structurally blind to this, which is why the framework's own suite is the gate. **Second, and larger: the DECODE policy.** The client's default `M1ValuePolicy` returns the SPEC §9 value objects, and Illuminate is written against PDO, which returns a driver-native STRING for every non-trivial type. Stock framework code puts those values where only a scalar works — `Builder::pluck($column, $key)` uses the key column's value as an ARRAY KEY (`TypeError: Cannot access offset of type Ferro\NaiveTimestamp on array`) and then calls `substr()` on it, so "stringifies on demand" is not the property required. The tier now passes `RawStringValuePolicy`, the same driver-native hand-off the Doctrine tier takes, and charter rule 6 is unaffected: this is the driver's own decode step, not a change to Grammar, Processor or schema. **ONE divergence from `pdo_pgsql` is kept because it is safer:** `TIMESTAMPTZ` arrives as canonical RFC3339 (`…T…Z`) rather than PDO's `Y-m-d H:i:s+00`. `Model::asDateTime()` runs `Date::createFromFormat('Y-m-d H:i:s', $value)`, and PHP's format parser IGNORES trailing data — so PDO's form parses with the `+00` silently discarded and the instant reinterpreted in the application timezone, while the canonical form fails that parse and falls through to Illuminate's own `Date::parse()`, which reads the `Z`. Do not "fix" it to match PDO.

  **(am) `ferro/laravel` claims a distinct `driver` NAME by default, with the stock name available as an opt-in ALIAS — and the name is measurably observable (M2, C2).** Illuminate resolves a connection by driver name (`ConnectionFactory::createConnection()` consults `Connection::getResolver($driver)` before its own `match`, verified in v11.51.0), so §15's "config-only adoption" necessarily means the application now reports a driver the ecosystem does not recognise. That is not cosmetic: **six** of upstream's own `QueryBuilderTest` cases gate their assertions on `in_array($this->driver, ['pgsql', 'sqlsrv'])` and assert NOTHING under any other name, and third-party packages branch on `getDriverName()` the same way. `FerroConnections::register(['pgsql' => 'ferro-pgsql'])` registers Ferro under the stock name for applications that want the fuller drop-in; the sharp edge is stated at the call site — it hijacks EVERY `pgsql` connection in the application, including one meant to dial PostgreSQL directly. Nothing is dialled either way: the factory hands the resolver a LAZY PDO closure, which these connections discard unresolved. **Both names are therefore recorded as separate columns** in `docs/laravel-suite/`, because a single number would hide which half of the gap is the engine and which half is a name — measured 62/71 under `ferro-pgsql` and 68/71 under the alias, the six-test difference being exactly the gated cases. It also proves the tier's exception really is a `PDOException` subclass reaching those assertions.

  **(an) `I64` binds PostgreSQL's `numeric` and `float8`, the second half of the libpq-parity widening (M2, C2b).** The mirror of (af), found the same way — by an upstream framework suite rather than by reading the matrix — and it was the ONLY engine gap in the `laravel/framework` v11.51.0 subset. `numeric` because PostgreSQL types `extract(year from …)` as `numeric` (changed from `double precision` in PG 14) and Laravel's stock grammar compiles `whereYear` to exactly that; `float8` because `$table->float()` compiles to `double precision` and inserting a PHP `int` into it is what anyone writes for a round amount. **The two targets need different value handling, and that difference is the design.** `numeric` is arbitrary-precision, so an `i64`'s decimal rendering is exact at every magnitude and needs NO gate — it rides `Format::Text`, the same shape `PgDecimalText` writes. `float8` is exact only where the value round-trips, so it carries a **pre-send exactness gate**: this is STRICTER than `pdo_pgsql`, deliberately, because PDO sends the decimal text and PostgreSQL rounds it without a word — a `bigint` id past 2^53 landing one apart in a `double precision` column is the same silent-corrupt-write class the `F64 → float4` overflow/underflow arms already refuse, and §9.1's rule is that a non-representable value is loud. `FLOAT4` was NOT widened: no measured caller reaches `real`, and (af)'s membership rule is that an unmeasured widening is how a pre-flight rots. **Two things worth carrying.** The gate cannot be spelled `(n as f64) as i64 == n`: Rust's float→int `as` cast SATURATES, so that form reports TRUE for `i64::MAX` — the one value most obviously not representable — and it compares through `i128` instead; and it is a round trip rather than a `|n| <= 2^53` bound, because every even integer above 2^53 is representable too and a magnitude bound would refuse provably lossless binds. The lockstep proof's fixture gained `2^53` and `2^53 + 1` and the growth is mutation-proven load-bearing, exactly as (af)'s `I64(1)` entry was: the proof `continue`s past every pair the value-aware pre-flight refuses, so a value-gated widening with no accept-side fixture value is never exercised at all. Suite effect measured: **65/71** under `ferro-pgsql` and **71/71** under the `pgsql` alias — a clean sweep of the curated subset, and the last non-passes are the (am) driver-name artifact alone.

  **(ao) The Eloquent tier reported the RAW `version()` banner, and `version_compare` reads that as OLDER THAN EVERYTHING (M2, C2c).** `ferrod` caches the backend's own `version()` output verbatim — `PoolInfo`'s docblock says normalising it is the consuming tier's job — and PostgreSQL's leads with the product name: `PostgreSQL 16.13 (Ubuntu …) on x86_64-…`. PHP's `version_compare` treats a leading non-numeric part as older than any number, so MEASURED against that exact string, `version_compare($raw, '12.0', '<')` and `version_compare($raw, '14.0', '<')` are BOTH `true` on a PostgreSQL 16 server. **It does not throw and does not warn; it answers wrong.** The harm is silently wrong introspection SQL, not a failed query: stock `PostgresGrammar::compileColumns()` selects `'' as generated` instead of `a.attgenerated` when it believes the server is pre-12, so `Schema::getColumns()` reports EVERY column as non-generated on a modern PostgreSQL and a `GENERATED ALWAYS AS … STORED` column looks like an ordinary writable one to anything diffing or dumping the schema. **This is exactly the failure `FerroPdoShim::getAttribute`'s own docblock said it existed to prevent** — its nil-version guard was right and incomplete, because a version that is present but unparseable is just as wrong and much quieter, which is why it survived a green tier suite and surfaced only through upstream `laravel/framework`'s own `SchemaBuilderTest::testGetAndDropTypes`. The fix reuses the sibling Doctrine tier's rule UNCHANGED (`Ferro\DBAL\PlatformVersion::normalise`, which measured the same string breaking DBAL's anchored parser): strip PostgreSQL's leading product name and nothing else — minimal because every extra rule is a chance to discard a load-bearing suffix, and the MySQL family is precisely that case, where MariaDB is detected ONLY by the `-MariaDB-` substring and must pass through byte-identical. The two packages cannot share code, so they share the rule and its rationale in both docblocks. **The guard asserts the CONSEQUENCE, not the number**: a live test creates a `GENERATED ALWAYS AS … STORED` column and requires stock introspection to report it as generated — mutation-proven, and the general lesson is (ac)'s in a new place: a value the tier hands upward is only correct if the code that CONSUMES it reads it the way you assumed.

  **(ap) The last two PG bind gaps ordinary Eloquent reaches: `I64 → varchar` and `TEXT → int2/int4/int8` (M2, C2d).** Mirrors of each other — an integer into a string column, and a string into an integer column — and both measured in `laravel/framework` v11.51.0's own integration suite: `EloquentBelongsToManyTest` binds an `int` into a pivot's `$table->string('flag')` (5 cases), and `EloquentPivotSerializationTest` restores a serialized pivot, so Eloquent re-binds keys that round-tripped through PHP as STRINGS against `int4` columns (4 cases). `pdo_pgsql` sends every parameter as text and PostgreSQL parses it. **Two membership decisions worth keeping, because both are the rule working rather than being bent.** First, `bpchar` stays OUT of the `I64` arm while `varchar` comes in: `char(n)` PADS, so a read-back would not equal what was written — a difference in SEMANTICS, which is what (af)'s "name the exact `Type`" rule is actually about, as against `varchar`, whose input syntax simply IS the decimal rendering. Second, `int2` and `int8` ride along with the measured `int4`, and that IS a departure, argued rather than assumed: the three widths share exactly one input syntax and differ only in RANGE, which PostgreSQL itself enforces and reports as `22003`, while the caller shape — a stringly-typed key — is width-independent; `$table->id()` is `bigint`, so admitting only `int4` would leave the identical defect armed on the commonest Laravel primary key. **`bool`, the float widths and `bytea` stay out of the TEXT arm, each for its own stated reason rather than by omission:** `'1'`/`'t'`/`'yes'` ACQUIRING a boolean meaning is the §9.1 coercion class the S9 `I64 → bool` value gate exists to refuse and text must not be a way around it; a text float silently ROUNDS, the harm (an) added a pre-send exactness gate for; and `bytea`'s hex-vs-escape input is a real encoding DECISION, not a rendering. **A non-numeric string is deliberately NOT pre-refused** — PostgreSQL's own parser answers `22P02`, server-side and known-fate (a server error carries a `DbError`, so it classifies `NonRetryable`, never `Indeterminate`), the same arrangement the eight (aa) targets have always had; a digits-only pre-check would be STRICTER than libpq and would refuse forms PG accepts. One Doctrine-tier test asserted the OLD refusal and was rewritten rather than deleted: `testTheSameNumericStringIsAnIntUnderIntegerAndIsRefusedUnderString` became `…LandsUnderBothIntegerAndString`, still pinning that the two `ParameterType`s take different wire paths (`TAG_I64` binary vs `TAG_TEXT` text) and now that both arrive as the same integer.

  **(aq) `search_path` belongs to the POOL, and Laravel needs it in two places (M2, C2e).** A stock Laravel application sets `search_path` in `config/database.php` and the PDO connector issues `SET search_path` on connect. A Ferro application cannot: the session is POOLED and multiplexed, so a per-connection `SET` would be session state a later tenant inherits — the cross-tenant class §7 exists to prevent. It therefore belongs on the `ferrod` pool DSN, where libpq's `options=-csearch_path=…` puts it, which is the same place §12/D8 already puts every other connection parameter. MEASURED against `laravel/framework`'s own `SchemaBuilderSchemaNameTest`, which creates objects in a non-default schema and then renames them by UNQUALIFIED name: without it those cases fail, with it they pass. **The part worth writing down is that Laravel ALSO needs the config key**, and for a reason that is easy to get wrong from first principles: `PostgresBuilder::getSchemas()` reads `search_path` out of the CONFIG ARRAY and never asks the server, so the schema builder's idea of which schemas to enumerate is independent of the session's. An application using a non-default schema sets it in BOTH places — a documented configuration requirement, not an incompatibility.

  **(ar) The C2 suite grew a CONTROL column, and it changed what a non-pass means (M2, C2e).** Widening the allowlist from 5 files to all 89 driver-agnostic ones took the suite from 71 cases to 633, and at that size the question "is this non-pass ours, or this framework/server pair's?" stops being answerable by reading each one. `FERRO_LARAVEL_DRIVER=stock-pgsql` runs the identical tests against the identical server through upstream's own `pdo_pgsql`, no Ferro anywhere. It earned its place before it was built: two `SchemaBuilderSchemaNameTest` cases looked like a Ferro schema gap and reproduced identically through stock PDO. **Its contact assertion INVERTS** — under the control the run refuses to start if the resolved connection turns out to be a Ferro one, checked on the connection's actual class rather than on the env var being verified, because a control that quietly ran through Ferro would report Ferro's behaviour as upstream's baseline, which is worse than having no baseline. Mutation-proven twice: registering Ferro's resolver for `pgsql` kills the run one step EARLIER (Ferro's own config refusal, since the control array carries no `ferro_socket`), and adding that socket so a real Ferro connection is built is what makes the inverted check fire; both refuse to run, neither reports a number. It is the one place PHP holds database credentials, inherent to being PDO and measurement-only. **The result it produced is the headline: under the stock driver NAME, Ferro is indistinguishable from `pdo_pgsql` on this set — 605/607 on both, identical ordered failure sets, and the two remaining are upstream's own.** Under Ferro's own driver name the column is 570/579, and every one of the nine differences is positively identified in upstream source as code branching on `$this->driver` (an `expectException` never armed, a `match` picking the wrong expected type, a `markTestSkipped` that never fires) — which is (am)'s cost restated with a control behind it rather than an argument.

  **(as) `FerroPdoShim::quote()` exists now, because the caller turned out to be ordinary (M2, C2f).** It used to be named in the shim's own docblock as deliberately absent — "implementing it means owning dialect-specific SQL escaping, security-critical code with no known caller", the sibling Doctrine tier's reasoning. Running upstream's PostgreSQL-SPECIFIC test directory (which the (ar) control column made attributable at all) produced the caller: `Postgres/EscapeTest::testEscapeString` was the single failure there, and the control passed it. `Connection::escapeString()` is `getReadPdo()->quote($value)`, reached from `DB::escape()` and — more commonly — from `Grammar::substituteBindingsIntoRawSql()`, i.e. every `Builder::toRawSql()`, every `->dd()` on a query builder, and the SQL query-log tooling renders. The security half of that old sentence is why the implementation looks the way it does rather than a reason it does not exist. **Illuminate does the dangerous half first** — `Connection::escape()` rejects NUL bytes and invalid UTF-8 itself and routes null/int/float/bool/binary/array elsewhere — so what reaches `quote()` is a valid UTF-8 string with no NULs, and the rule is doubling `'` and nothing else. **That rule is complete only while `standard_conforming_strings` is `on`, so it is VERIFIED rather than assumed**: one `SHOW` per connection, cached, refusing loudly otherwise. Reading it from a POOLED checkout is sound and the reason is worth keeping — the checkout that answers is not the one that later executes what the caller interpolates, but a non-local `SET` taints the connection (§7.1) and hygiene restores the server default, so every checkout begins from the value the probe observed. MEASURED against `pdo_pgsql` on PostgreSQL 16 over eight cases including `backslash-then-quote \'` and `quote-then-backslash '\`: byte-identical output, every case round-tripping through `SELECT <literal>` to the original bytes. `PDO::PARAM_LOB` is refused rather than ignored — PostgreSQL's binary literal is a different shape and Illuminate builds it in PHP via `escapeBinary()`. **Two process notes.** The probe first read `(unreadable)` because it was routed through the shim's `guard()` helper, which COERCES its result to `int|true` for the boolean-returning PDO methods it was written for; the check is deliberately written so that anything but an unambiguous `'on'` refuses, so that defect surfaced as a loud failure rather than a wrong answer, and `guardValue()` now separates translation from coercion. And the `standard_conforming_strings` verification is UNFALSIFIABLE by the live suite — every server this project runs against has it `on`, so deleting the check was measured GREEN live; an offline test over a scripted session is where that mutation goes red, and the live/offline split is stated rather than left implicit. **And one thing was got WRONG in the process, recorded because the process matters more than the defect:** §21 **D5** already decided `quote()` — "client-side with per-platform tables; no engine round trip" — and it was not read before implementing, so the shipped verification is one cached round trip D5 forbids. Charter ground rule 1 makes §21 binding and says an impossible decision is to be RAISED, not routed around; this one is not even impossible — `standard_conforming_strings` is `GUC_REPORT`, so the engine already holds it with ZERO round trips via the M1-S1 fork's `Client::parameter()`, and advertising it in `HELLO_ACK` is both D5-compliant and MORE correct than a cached `SHOW` (a snapshot of one checkout, where `ParameterStatus` tracks it live). That is a `/proto` slice under charter rule 2, so it is filed rather than smuggled in: `docs/followups/2026-09-10-quote-scs-probe-vs-d5.md`, with D5 itself carrying a pointer. The shipped behaviour is safe throughout; what is outstanding is the conflict.

  **(at) `HELLO_ACK` advertises `literals_are_standard` per pool, and D5 is satisfied rather than merely noted (M2, C2g).** `PoolInfo` gained a FOURTH element (`protocol_version` 2 → 3 — it is a positional fixarray and both decoders reject any other arity, so the bump is what makes a skewed pair fail at the first byte of the first frame instead of deep inside `HelloAck::decode`; M1-S8a bumped to 2 for exactly the same reason). **The field is deliberately not named for either backend's own setting.** What a client building a SQL literal needs is one bit — is a backslash inside a single-quoted literal an ordinary character? — and two families answer it under different names (PostgreSQL's `standard_conforming_strings`, MySQL's `NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES` in `sql_mode`); putting either NAME on the wire would bake one vocabulary into the protocol and force a client to know which spelling to look for. **MySQL needs the bit MORE than PostgreSQL does** — PG has defaulted to the safe value since 9.1, MySQL defaults to the unsafe one — which is the argument against deferring the shape until a MySQL tier exists. It costs the engine nothing: PG reports the GUC as `GUC_REPORT`, so the M1-S1 fork's mirrored `Client::parameter` answers synchronously, and the value rides the SAME lazy/concurrent/TTL'd per-pool probe as `server_version` (one cache, so an expiry can never apply to one value and not the other). **The MySQL arm is deliberately unfilled and rides as `nil`** — reading `sql_mode` would cost a statement the probe does not currently make, and no MySQL caller has been measured; `nil` is the fail-closed answer, so an unfilled arm refuses rather than guesses. **`nil` is the arm that matters** and inherits `server_version`'s contract exactly (never learned / expired / not learned yet are one thing to a client: unknown), so the shim proceeds only on an unambiguous `true` — never reading `nil` as `false` (over-strict) and never as `true` (an escaping rule the server never confirmed). Three process notes. The golden vector needed a THIRD pool entry, because the new field has three states and two entries cannot cover them; its two Options are deliberately non-covariant across the fixture, so a codec reading one where it meant the other still moves the bytes. `gen_vectors` writes the encoded BYTES and the human-readable `message` JSON **separately**, so the two drifted the moment the field landed — what caught it was the PHP conformance test re-encoding that JSON against `frame_hex`, which is precisely the guard's purpose. And two assertions that hardcoded a COUNT had to be rewritten to pin their claim instead (the version-skew message's `expected 2`, and `TypePolicyOptions`' "none of those three is a timezone"): an assertion that must be edited on every bump is one someone eventually "fixes" by pasting whatever the code now says.

  **(au) TCP keepalive is an operator DSN parameter on both families, not engine code — and (aj)'s "TCP keepalive is off" was half wrong (M2).** MEASURED, by parsing a DSN through each driver's own config type rather than by reading docs:

  | family | default | reachable from the pool DSN |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | PostgreSQL | keepalive **ON**, idle **7200 s** | `?keepalives_idle=30` → 30 s (also `keepalives_interval`, `keepalives_retries`, `keepalives=0` to disable) |
  | MySQL/MariaDB | keepalive **OFF** (`None`) | `?tcp_keepalive=30000` → 30 s |

  `PgBackend::connect` passes the DSN straight to `tokio_postgres::connect`, and `MysqlBackend::connect` to `Opts::from_url`, so both parse these without a line of Ferro code. **Hardcoding a value would REMOVE an operator knob**, which is the opposite of what §12/D8 does with every other connection parameter and of the position (aq) took on `search_path`: connection parameters belong on the pool DSN, because the pool owns the connection. So this is documented, not implemented.

  **And the hazard it was filed against is already bounded by four other things**, which is why the PostgreSQL default being on changes nothing in practice: an idle pooled connection that dies silently is caught at checkout by `is_closed`/`ping` or recycled by `max_lifetime` (30 s… 30 min default) long before a 2-hour keepalive probe could fire; an IN-FLIGHT statement is bounded by the §19.3 `timeout_ms` + `CANCEL` that M1-S4 made mandatory on both exec paths; and a pinned transaction idle between statements is bounded by its tx deadline. What keepalive would add on top is narrow, and an operator who wants it has the knob. **The knob worth reaching for first is usually `tcp_user_timeout` rather than keepalive** — keepalive probes only IDLE sockets, while `tcp_user_timeout` bounds an unacknowledged in-flight write — and PostgreSQL's config exposes that too; neither is set by Ferro, for the same reason. Charter rule 5 applies: no measurement demands either.

  **(av) A MySQL `CALL` returns real cells: `cols` falls back to the EXECUTED result set's metadata when the PREPARED statement declares none (M2, S2).** C1a recorded the `CALL` blind spot as a MySQL constraint end to end. Half of it is a MySQL fact and half was a Ferro choice: `query.rs` built `cols` from `stmt.columns()` (the `COM_STMT_PREPARE` metadata) and then mapped each row's cells by **iterating that same list**, so an empty list did not merely lose column NAMES — it produced rows with **zero cells**. Measured live at S1 before any code (`call_columns_spike_it.rs`): prepare-time columns `0`, execution-time columns `2`, and the driver's own row already carrying `Some(2)` cells. No vendored fork was needed, unlike the signals S1 and S6 required.

  **The prepared list stays PREFERRED, and that is a fate decision rather than an optimization.** The obvious change — read the executed set's metadata uniformly — would have moved the §9.1 out-of-scope-column refusal from pre-send to post-send for *every* statement, turning §19.3's story for a type error from "never sent" to "sent, then refused" across the whole buffered MySQL read path. Preferring the prepared list confines that change to statements the server declares no columns for, which is precisely the `CALL` shape that was broken anyway. `query_it.rs`'s `out_of_scope_column_is_unsupported` — which asserts the refusal happens *before* the query runs and that the conn stays clean — therefore passes **unchanged**, and the hot path for every ordinary buffered read is untouched (the prepared columns are still borrowed, never cloned; the executed set's come back as the driver's own `Arc<[Column]>`, a refcount bump).

  **On the fallback arm the refusal is still KNOWN-FATE and never `Indeterminate`**: it is raised after a FULL drain, so the statement's outcome is settled and the connection is clean and reusable. What genuinely changes is that a procedure which writes *and* selects an out-of-scope column type now has its write APPLIED before the client sees `Unsupported` — narrow, but real, and stated here rather than left to be discovered.

  **An ordering trap worth the comment it got.** `QueryResult::columns()` reads the driver's PENDING result set, and draining clears it (`next_row` calls `set_pending_result(None)` at the terminator), so it must be read BEFORE `collect()` — the exact opposite of `affected_rows()`/`last_insert_id()` two lines below it, which the M1-S8a review pinned as readable only AFTER. Two load-bearing ordering constraints pointing opposite ways in one function.

  **Still open, deliberately:** `query_stream` is unchanged (S3) — its no-result-set dispatch also branches on the prepared list, so a *streamed* `CALL` still discards its rows; and `selectResultSets()` still needs a `/proto` multi-result-set representation (S4). S2 also widened the spike to a TWO-`SELECT` procedure, since S1 measured only the N=1 case and S4's design rests on that count; it asserts `> 1` rather than a fixed number and prints the exact count, because whether a trailing OK packet surfaces as a set is engine-specific and pinning it would turn one engine red without saying more than the printed line does.

  **(aw) …and the streamed path too: the `query_stream` dispatch moved to the EXECUTED metadata, which required parking BEFORE knowing the shape (M2, S3).** (av) fixed the buffered path and left `query_stream` alone, because its use of the prepared column list is not a cosmetic read: `stream.rs::open` branched its **park/no-park dispatch** on `columns.is_empty()`, and `stream_and_drop()` answers `None` for a statement with no result set **while consuming the `QueryResult` and closing the connection with it**. So the prepared list was doing real work — keeping a no-result-set statement off the owned route — and a `CALL` (empty prepared list, rows at run time) took that arm, ran buffered, and had its rows discarded.

  **The signal (av) used is not available at the moment this decision has to be made.** Executed metadata exists only after the statement runs, and by then the park choice is already made. What resolves it is that the B2a fork already exposes `QueryResult::into_conn`, whose own docblock names precisely this case — the owned-route exit for a statement that produced no result set. So the arm parks, RUNS, then reads `columns_ref()`: a non-empty executed set streams (a `CALL` now yields real rows, **incrementally** — not buffered), an empty one hands the connection straight back through `into_conn` + `unpark`. **No new fork edit was needed**; the capability had been built at B2a and simply never used from this direction.

  **The narrowing from (av) is preserved exactly.** A statement whose prepared list is non-empty — every ordinary `SELECT`, i.e. the hot path — takes the same code it always did. Only the empty-prepared-list arm changed, and it pays the same §19.3 trade for the same reason: the metadata does not exist until the statement has run, so an out-of-scope column type is refused after the send. Here the refusal additionally hands the connection back **clean** via `into_conn` rather than discarding it, so an `Unsupported` costs no connection.

  **What the arm change costs, stated rather than discovered later:** a streamed `INSERT` now parks and recovers where it previously ran buffered on a borrowed connection, so a *failed* streamed INSERT discards its connection instead of reusing it. That is a cost in efficiency, never in correctness — a parked wrapper reads `is_closed`-dead, which is the FB-2 contract the pool already relies on. The recovery mechanism itself was **already proven live on both engines** before this slice built on it, by `stream_recovery_it.rs`'s `no_result_set_recovers_via_query_result`, session-identity gate included.

  **`MysqlRowStream::NoRows`'s contract changed and its docblock says so**: it used to mean "decided on the prepared column count, never parked", and now means "parked, ran, reported no result set, handed back". The `Rows` invariant is restated in the way that survives: `columns` is always the very list `cols` was built from — prepared metadata for a `SELECT`, executed for a `CALL` — so the two are produced together rather than coming from one source.

  **Still open (S4):** a multi-`SELECT` procedure streams only its FIRST result set; `into_conn` drains the rest. `ExecOk` carries exactly one `cols`+`rows`, so representing more is a `/proto` change.

  **(ax) The stream drain inside `finish()` is bounded too — and what made that safe is a discard latch the pool owns, not the bound (M2, FB-3b).** `RowStreamHandle::finish` drained the remainder with an unbounded `while self.next().await.is_some() {}` from M1-S5 until now, while `ferrod` awaits `finish` UNRACED — so a backend that can hang a row pull stranded the request's single terminal END frame, a charter-rule-4 violation. (aj)'s sibling fix bounded the RECLAIM await immediately beside it and deliberately left this alone, on the stated ground that bounding it means abandoning a MID-PROTOCOL stream and "must not be done blind".

  **The reason it could not be a one-line fix is the other half of the hazard.** Abandoning a drain leaves a connection that is *alive and healthy but mid-result-set*, and every discard decision in `ferro-pool` is made by `backend.is_closed(&conn)` at `Checkout::drop` — which cannot express that state. PostgreSQL is exactly the case that breaks: its row stream is channel-backed, so the client stays perfectly fine and the connection would be pushed back onto the idle stack still carrying the previous tenant's unread rows. **A naive bound would have converted a hang into a cross-tenant leak**, which is strictly worse.

  So the slice adds `Checkout::discard`, a pool-owned latch meaning "this connection is alive and must never be reused", checked in `Drop` alongside `is_closed`. The drain is then bounded by `checkout_timeout` — the same knob (aj) chose for the reclaim beside it and `Pool::checkout` uses for recycle cleanup, because this is the same class of post-statement work and an operator should not have to discover a third. It is a HANG bound, not a throughput budget: a drain slow enough to exceed it costs ONE connection, which is strictly better than never emitting the terminal.

  The two existing reclaim-failure arms now set the same latch. They were correct before — the reclaim contract obliges a backend to leave a failed connection `is_closed`-dead, and every backend honours it — but the invariant no longer *depends* on that being true.

  **Both halves are mutation-proven, and the second is why the test is worth having:** remove the bound and the guard hangs forever (the stranded terminal); keep the bound but drop the latch and it fails on the recycle assertion, because the fake's connection is deliberately NOT closed — a hung drain damages the SESSION, not the socket.

  **(ay) The same latch closes the ABANDONED-stream half of the invariant, which (ax) left resting on backend cooperation (M2, FB-2).** `RowStreamHandle`'s `Drop` net — the safety net for a stream abandoned by a panic, an unwind, or a dropped future — set `tainted = true` and nothing else, and its own comment asserted that "`tainted` alone trips the recycle guard". **That was true of the wrong guard.** `tainted` trips the guard that decides whether the NEXT checkout runs the full `ROLLBACK` + `DISCARD ALL` hygiene profile; it has never had any bearing on `Checkout::drop`, which decides whether the connection reaches a next tenant at all and consults only `is_closed()` (and, since (ax), `discard`). So the documented "abandoned owning stream ⇒ conn discarded" invariant rested entirely on every backend reporting a moved-out connection dead — precisely the dependency (ax) removed one function lower down, for precisely the reason that applies here too: PostgreSQL's channel-backed stream leaves an abandoned mid-result-set connection looking perfectly healthy to `is_closed`.

  **Measured, not reasoned about.** The regression test was written and run BEFORE the fix, against a fake whose connection is deliberately left alive: it asserted `tainted == true` and `closed == false` — both of which passed — and then failed on the recycle assertion, with the next checkout handed back the same connection id. The pre-fix state *is* the interesting mutation ("keep the taint, drop the latch"), so the proof that the latch rather than the taint is what protects the next tenant came for free.

  The fix is `self.checkout.discard = true` beside the existing taint. `tainted` is deliberately KEPT: it records the truth about the connection, and a later change that stopped discarding should not silently lose it. The cost is at most ONE connection on a path that is abnormal by construction — `finish()` is mandatory and `ferrod` calls it on BOTH the success and the cancel/timeout paths, so the `Drop` net is never reached in ordinary operation.

  **(az) §7.6 was unimplementable as written, and the fix is a DECLARATION rule, not a cleverer inference (M2, C3 / D13).** §7.6 said "one writer serialized engine-side". Serializing the writer means identifying the writer, and identifying the writer from SQL text is exactly what charter rule 6 and §3 forbid — while §1's own bullet already said so for the neighbouring case and pointed AT §7.6 as the explicit-routing authority. So the spec contradicted itself across two sections, and a reader had no way to know: the contradiction only surfaces when someone tries to build the backend, at which point the cheapest move is to quietly infer and call it an implementation detail. That is how a charter rule gets eroded, which is why this was raised under ground rule 1 rather than resolved in code.

  **D13 takes the declaration route.** Every transaction takes SQLite's writer lock at `BEGIN`; a transaction the client DECLARED `readonly` takes a deferred reader lock instead. The engine never asks what a statement does. An undeclared statement is a writer — conservative, never wrong, and the same trade (ac) already records for the DBAL tier.

  **The rejected option is the one that looks cheapest.** `busy_timeout` alone needs no new signal and no new seam, and it does deliver the stated benefit for ordinary traffic. It fails on deferred upgrade: a transaction that begins as a reader and later writes can get `SQLITE_BUSY_SNAPSHOT`, which `busy_timeout` does **not** retry — the transaction must be rolled back and replayed, and charter rule 3 forbids the engine replaying it. Taking the lock at BEGIN removes that class by construction rather than handing the application a failure mode the engine created. Its WAL and `busy_timeout` pragmas are kept regardless. *(The `SQLITE_BUSY_SNAPSHOT` behaviour is documented, NOT yet reproduced against SQLite's source — it is marked UNVERIFIED in `C3-SQLITE-SCOPE.md` §1 and is the crux of the rejected option, so it must be reproduced before the first C3 code slice, not assumed because the decision already went the other way.)*

  **What the decision actually bought, beyond SQLite.** Half of D13 is nearly free: the TX service already composes BEGIN from the request's `readonly`, and PostgreSQL already emits `START TRANSACTION READ ONLY` from it. The other half is a genuinely new seam — `readonly` reaches `ferrod`'s SQL service but NOT `ferro-pool`, where it appears only in two comments, so routing an autocommit statement to a reader connection needs plumbing through `Pool::checkout`. That seam is not SQLite-specific: single-flight coalescing of concurrent identical declared-readonly reads, and read-your-writes replica routing, each need the same thing. D13 is therefore the first decision that makes an HONEST `readonly` declaration pay for itself, which is the standing argument for fixing (ac) rather than living with it.

  **(ba) D13's reproduction obligation is DISCHARGED, and the premise held (M2, C3-1).** (az) left one thing outstanding on purpose: the `SQLITE_BUSY_SNAPSHOT` behaviour D13 rejected Option A over was *documented, not reproduced*, and D13's own revisit note required it be proven before any C3 code slice — the decision going the other way being no licence to skip it. It is now reproduced against real `rusqlite` on a real WAL database (`engine/crates/ferro-sqlite-spike/`, a spike crate that is NOT the backend): **a deferred upgrade fails `SQLITE_BUSY_SNAPSHOT` (extended code 517) in under 500 ms with a 5-SECOND `busy_timeout` armed on the connection that fails**, so the retry loop is never entered.

  **The elapsed time is the load-bearing half, and it needs a control to mean anything.** Observing the error alone is equally consistent with "busy_timeout retried for five seconds and then gave up" — a far more benign claim that would not justify rejecting Option A. So the spike pairs it: with the lock taken at `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, contention moves to the OTHER connection, surfaces as a plain `SQLITE_BUSY` (5), and `busy_timeout` parks for the full duration before failing. Same build, same API, same knob — effective once the lock is taken at BEGIN, inapplicable to an upgrade. That pair is the executable form of D13's argument, and it is worth keeping past C3 rather than deleting with the spike, because it would fail loudly if a future SQLite release changed the behaviour the decision rests on.

  **The other three premises hold too, so C3 is unblocked rather than merely decided:** no vendored driver fork is needed *for the capabilities the `PoolBackend` seam demands* (stated that way because "no fork will ever be needed" is not checkable — and this is the assumption that was proven FALSE for `mysql_async` at M1-S6 and `tokio-postgres` at M1-S1, both times before any code was written); rows stream across `spawn_blocking` without buffering, with the connection handed back usable, so the B2b-2a park/unpark shape fits; and `InterruptHandle` satisfies `Send + Sync + 'static` and really interrupts, so `Cancel` has a backing.

  **One finding belongs in the spec rather than only the dev-loop docs, because §9.2's SQLite `error_map` will trip over it:** `rusqlite`'s `ffi::ErrorCode` is an ordinary Rust enum whose discriminants are its own declaration order, NOT SQLite's numeric codes — `DatabaseBusy` is 3 while `SQLITE_BUSY` is 5, `OperationInterrupted` is 7 while `SQLITE_INTERRUPT` is 9. Casting it to an integer and comparing against a SQLite code compiles cleanly and is always wrong. Compare the typed `code` field against the `ErrorCode` variant, or compare `extended_code` against a spelled-out number; never cast the enum. Two of the spike's own assertions were written the wrong way and were caught only because the premises held and produced the real codes.

  **(bb) D13's BEGIN composition has landed, and the placeholder it replaced was actively wrong (M2, C3-2).** `compose_begin_sql`'s SQLite arm emitted a bare `"BEGIN"` for an undeclared request. SQLite reads a bare `BEGIN` as DEFERRED, so the placeholder emitted *precisely* the deferred-upgrade shape (ba) proved is unretryable — it was not a weaker spelling of `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, it was the one string that reintroduces the class D13 exists to remove. The arm now emits `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` for an undeclared or declared-write request and `BEGIN DEFERRED` for a declared-`readonly` one, mutation-proven in both directions.

  **The isolation level is accepted on SQLite and emits nothing, which is a decision rather than an omission.** SQLite has no `SET TRANSACTION` to emit a level with, and under D13 there is nothing left for a client to ask for: every write transaction is serialized against every other by the lock it takes at BEGIN, and a reader sees a consistent snapshot, so no two writers ever interleave and the strongest level a client can name is what the discipline already provides. That precision is deliberate — "SQLite is serializable" is the loose claim, since WAL readers get snapshot isolation and snapshot isolation alone permits write skew; it is D13's BEGIN-time lock making writers mutually exclusive that closes the gap. The level also cannot change blocking behaviour, since `readonly` alone decides the lock mode. Refusing would break drop-in for an application that merely configures a level, and would buy no safety in exchange. An unknown isolation BYTE remains a client error on every dialect: that is a protocol fault, not a capability gap. The unit test asserts the full isolation x readonly cross-product, because a level that silently flipped `IMMEDIATE` to `DEFERRED` would be a correctness bug that no isolation-only assertion would catch.

  **A fifth premise was proven BEFORE this arm shipped, because this arm is what creates the hazard.** D13's readonly arm hands the client a DEFERRED transaction, which is the C3-1 spike's `p4a` setup exactly — so a client that declares `readonly` and then writes reaches the failure class charter rule 3 forbids the engine resolving, and it would surface only under concurrency, the worst shape for a bug. `PRAGMA query_only=ON` converts it into an up-front `SQLITE_READONLY` (8): deterministic, provably not executed (so its §9.2 fate is `NonRetryable`, never `Indeterminate`), and reversible on the connection, which is what makes it usable on a pooled one. Proven on `p4a`'s own fixture for direct comparability, and with no contention at all — without that second half the first would be equally consistent with "READONLY happened to win the race". **The backend slice therefore owes the pragma on any connection checked out for a declared-readonly request, and owes disarming it on recycle.**

  **Two things this slice does NOT close, recorded so they are not mistaken for done:** the autocommit half of D13 (`readonly` reaching `ferro-pool`'s `Pool::checkout`) is untouched and is the next slice; and nothing links the engine's emitted string to the spike's proof that the string takes the writer lock, so a respelling would update one and leave the other passing on its own literal. The MySQL lane closed that with a live lockstep test (`ferro-backend-mysql/tests/begin_dialect_it.rs`); the SQLite equivalent is not writable until a SQLite pool exists, and `Dialect::Sqlite` is unreachable at runtime until then — which is why a unit test is the only gate this slice can honestly offer.

  **(bc) §7.1 is AMENDED for a library-call pin authority, and the C3 order was corrected before it cost a slice (M2, C3-3 planning).** §7.1 said pin decisions are made "from backend protocol signals" and carried no SQLite paragraph for pin AUTHORITY at all — SQLite appeared only in the assist-lexer's list. Both backends built so far were wire protocols, so the wording had never been tested against anything else. SQLite's authority is `sqlite3_get_autocommit()` via `Connection::is_autocommit()`: synchronous, in-process, no round trip. It satisfies the actual invariant — *authority from the backend, never inference from statement text* — exactly as a protocol byte does, so the fix is to generalise the framing rather than carve out an exception. What a backend still may not do is report state the engine derived itself.

  **The amendment rests on a new premise rather than on reasoning.** `p6` proves the signal must be read after EVERY statement rather than tracked engine-side, because SQLite ends transactions BY ITSELF: a constraint declared `ON CONFLICT ROLLBACK` rolls the whole transaction back when violated, and nothing in the statement text says so. An engine tracking its own `BEGIN`/`COMMIT` would hold a pin for a transaction that no longer exists and eventually hand the next tenant a connection it believes is mid-transaction — the §7.1 hazard reached from the other direction. The **control** is the identical duplicate insert against a plain unique constraint (SQLite's default `ON CONFLICT ABORT`), which fails only the statement and leaves the signal reporting in-transaction; it was mutation-proven by making the control table `ON CONFLICT ROLLBACK` too, which fails it. Without that half the first would be equally consistent with "any error ends a transaction", a materially weaker claim.

  **The planning correction is recorded because it is the kind of error that is invisible once built.** The `readonly`/`Pool::checkout` seam had been scheduled as the next slice. `readonly` genuinely does not reach `ferro-pool` — it occurs there exactly twice, both in comments — but no backend would have READ it: PostgreSQL has nowhere to apply a per-statement readonly outside a transaction, MySQL likewise, and the SQLite backend does not exist. Building it first meant adding a parameter nothing consumes and testing only that it arrives, which is scaffolding whose shape is guessed rather than fitted; it now follows the backend that gives it a consumer. Separately, "the backend crate" was one undifferentiated item, and the two existing backends measure 5623 lines (PG) and 4040 (MySQL) — SQLite will be smaller, but not by the order of magnitude that makes it one slice. It is now split a–e by what can be tested without the next piece existing, with the `AnyPool` arm LAST so that nothing before it changes `ferrod` and a failure in the backend cannot be confused with a failure in the daemon. `docs/dev-loop/C3-SQLITE-SCOPE.md` §6, §10.

  **(bd) The SQLite backend crate exists, and connection setup is CHECKED rather than requested (M2, C3-3a).** `ferro-backend-sqlite` is created, separate from the `ferro-sqlite-spike` premises suite. It carries no `impl PoolBackend` yet: the trait has a dozen required methods and this slice builds four, so implementing it now would mean eight bodies returning `Unsupported` — and a stub returning `Unsupported` is indistinguishable at the type level from a finished method, so the incompleteness would stop being visible exactly when it matters. The methods carry the trait's signatures; C3-3b/c adds the impl and the compiler checks them then.

  **WAL is verified, not assumed.** `PRAGMA journal_mode=WAL` returns the mode actually in force and SQLite can decline the change, so the backend compares the returned value and treats a non-WAL result as a hard connect error — otherwise the engine would believe it had the reader concurrency D13 assumes while running in rollback mode. Stated honestly: the guard's failure arm is not reachable in the development container, since declining WAL requires a filesystem without shared-memory support, so the test proves the connection is in WAL rather than exercising the guard.

  **An in-memory DSN is refused, because SQLite gives every `:memory:` connection its OWN database.** Pooled, that would silently hand different tenants different databases — one worker's writes invisible to the next, with no error anywhere, and squarely against §7.6's "the engine owns the file". The refusal is justified rather than asserted: the test demonstrates the hazard first (two in-memory connections cannot see each other's table) and only then asserts the refusal, across five spellings including the `mode=memory` URI form. A refusal with no proof behind it is superstition a later reader deletes.

  **Two pieces of speculative state were written and then removed on review, and the second is the more instructive.** A `dead` flag beside the connection `Option` was unobservable, since `is_closed` already reports true from the `Option` and a caller holds `&mut` across every blocking window. And an explicit "mark the handle lost" call in the panic arm was DEAD CODE that a mutation exposed: deleting it left the panic-contract test green, because parking had already moved the handle out and the error arm simply never unparks it. The contract therefore holds by construction, and the test now says it is a regression guard on the behaviour rather than proof that a particular line is load-bearing. That is (ba)'s non-buffering lesson a second time: a green test is not evidence that the code beneath it does anything.

  **(be) The SQLite pin authority, hygiene reset and affected count are built — and BOTH of SQLite's row counters are wrong on their own (M2, C3-3b).** `tx_status` is a LIVE read of `is_autocommit()`, never a cached flag, and the spike's `p6` hazard is now proven through the real backend with its control: an `ON CONFLICT ROLLBACK` violation ends the transaction underneath the engine and the signal reports it, while the identical duplicate against a plain unique constraint fails only the statement and leaves the signal in-transaction. Mutation-proven — a `tx_status` that reported what the engine's own BEGIN implied fails the first assertion.

  **The affected count needed measurement, not reasoning, and the obvious choice is wrong in the commonest case.** `changes()` reports the last DATA-MODIFYING statement's count, so it goes STALE: measured, three rows inserted and then a `BEGIN` still reports 3 — and `simple_query` is exactly the path the pin hook runs `BEGIN`/`COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK` through, so that is the common case rather than a corner. Its counterpart `total_changes()` never goes stale but OVER-reports, because it includes rows written by triggers and foreign-key cascades: measured, inserting one row into a table with an `AFTER INSERT` trigger moves it by 2, and deleting one parent with two `ON DELETE CASCADE` children moves it by 3. So the delta of `total_changes()` decides WHETHER the statement changed anything and `changes()` reports HOW MANY, which keeps a `BEGIN` at 0 and a triggered INSERT at the statement's own 1 — the same value PostgreSQL and MySQL report for those shapes. Both failure modes are mutation-proven against the shipped tests. **Trade-off stated:** a multi-statement batch reports its LAST statement's count rather than the sum, which is also what PG and MySQL do for a simple-query batch.

  **`reset` is an explicit list because SQLite has no `DISCARD ALL` analogue**, and porting PostgreSQL's would have been meaningless. A pooled SQLite connection can carry four things into the next tenant: an open transaction (rolled back, gated on the live signal because `ROLLBACK` errors when none is open), a `PRAGMA query_only` armed by a declared-`readonly` checkout, ATTACHed databases (§7.1's assist lexer already names `ATTACH` pin-worthy, which is the admission that it leaves state behind), and temp objects, which live in the per-connection `temp` schema and survive until the CONNECTION closes rather than the checkout. **Both reset profiles do the same thing, stated rather than hidden:** PostgreSQL uses `Targeted` to avoid `DISCARD ALL` destroying its prepared statements, but SQLite's reset is in-process, issues no round trip and destroys no prepared statements, so a `Targeted` that differed from `Full` would be a distinction with no behaviour behind it. `clean_reset_profile` is therefore `Some(Full)`; `None` (skip hygiene entirely) is not taken even though SQLite has no stored procedures and so none of the §7.4 function-body blind spot — that is the same optimization still deferred for MySQL (R2), and nothing here is expensive enough to justify the risk.

  **`impl PoolBackend` is still deliberately absent, with the line now drawn.** After this slice nine of the twelve methods are real, but `cancel_handle`, `query`, `query_stream` and `reclaim_stream` are not, and two associated types have no implementation. The trait lands at C3-3d, when only the STREAMING pair would be `Unsupported` — which is precisely the shape the MySQL backend shipped at M1-S6 and is therefore a precedent rather than an excuse.

  **(bf) SQLite's row mapping keys on the VALUE's storage class, not the column's declared type — and the obvious objection turns out to have no consumer (M2, C3-3c).** SQLite is dynamically typed: a declared column type is only an affinity and is not enforced, so `CREATE TABLE t(v INTEGER)` stores the string `'abc'` in `v` quite happily. Measured on one table: a column declared `INTEGER` returns INTEGER in row 1 and TEXT in row 2, and EVERY expression column — `1+1`, `'lit'`, `count(*)` — reports no declared type at all. Between them those rule out a per-column mapping entirely: no single tag can describe a heterogeneous column, and for a large ordinary class of queries there is no declared type to read. (It is also behind rusqlite's opt-in `column_decltype` feature, which is itself a hint.)

  **The objection to per-value tagging is that it makes the `cols` header a lie, and it would — except `ColMeta.tag` has no consumer.** The PHP client drops it deliberately in BOTH the buffered and streaming paths and says so in its own comment (F25/hazard 47): *"The decode authority is the PER-CELL tag … not the column metadata."* Nothing in `ferrod` reads it either. Every cell is self-describing on the wire, so per-cell tagging is not a compromise — it is what the client already relies on. `ColMeta.tag` is filled from the FIRST row's storage class (NULL for an empty result), describing the data returned rather than a declaration SQLite does not enforce, and is documented as advisory.

  **NINE of the fourteen §9 tags are UNREACHABLE from a SQLite read, by construction rather than by omission.** SQLite has five storage classes and none carries the information the others need: `BOOL` (no boolean type; `0`/`1` read back as `I64`, and a column declared `BOOLEAN` is not enforced), `U64` (INTEGER is SIGNED 64-bit), `DECIMAL` (NUMERIC affinity stores INTEGER or REAL — `42` in a NUMERIC column arrives as INTEGER), `DATE`/`TIME`/`TIMESTAMP`/`TIMESTAMPTZ` (no date-time storage class exists; dates are TEXT, INTEGER or REAL by convention), `UUID`, and `JSON` (the JSON functions operate on TEXT). Named rather than papered over, following S7's precedent for `UUID` on the MySQL family. **Drop-in consequence, stated now rather than discovered at the acceptance suite:** a SQLite column holding an ISO date reads back as `TEXT`, not as a §9 `Date` — which is also what PDO's SQLite driver does, but is a real asymmetry against the PG and MySQL columns.

  **The bind direction is not symmetric and does not need to be:** a client may bind any of the fourteen, and the canonical-text tags land in TEXT, which is how every SQLite application stores them. `U64` above `i64::MAX` is REFUSED pre-send rather than wrapped to a negative, the same rule and the same reason as PG's refusal (§9.1: non-representable values are loud, never coerced).

  **`last_insert_rowid()` is STICKY exactly like `changes()`**, so it is reported only when the statement actually moved it. After a SELECT it still holds the previous INSERT's rowid, and carrying that over would be a silently WRONG key — which (m)'s `lastval()` finding already records as strictly worse than no key. Residual, stated: an INSERT that explicitly reuses the previous rowid reports `None`, which errs in the safe direction. Both this guard and the `U64` refusal are mutation-proven.

  **One process note worth keeping:** a test in this slice was written as "a lost handle reports Failed" and asserted `Idle`, because a failing statement does not lose the handle — only a panicking blocking task does. It was renamed to what it verifies and the real contract moved to where it is reachable. A test whose name and body disagree is worse than no test, since the name is what a later reader greps for.

  **Recorded so it is not slipped in silently later:** SQLite defaults `foreign_keys` to OFF while both Laravel and Doctrine turn it ON. That is a drop-in behaviour decision with an acceptance-suite consequence, not a connection-setup detail, so it is left to the acceptance slice with its evidence rather than decided here. A second wiring debt: the busy timeout defaults to `PoolConfig`'s default `checkout_timeout` because a backend cannot see `PoolConfig`, so the C3-3e `AnyPool` slice must pass the pool's real value.

  **(bg) The SQLite fate table, the cancel handle, and `impl PoolBackend` (M2, C3-3d).** The trait lands here by the line drawn at C3-3b — when only the STREAMING pair would be `Unsupported`, which is exactly the shape the MySQL backend shipped at M1-S6. `query_stream`'s row type wraps `Infallible` rather than stubbing its methods with `unimplemented!()`, so the impossibility is enforced by the TYPE SYSTEM instead of by a runtime panic nobody has exercised. `error_map` keys on the EXTENDED code and the S4 `fate.rs` matrix is reused verbatim, exactly as the MySQL map does.

  **SQLite has an errno but no SQLSTATE — the mirror image of PostgreSQL**, which has a SQLSTATE and no errno (MySQL has both). So `sqlstate` is `None` and `errno` carries the extended code, which is what lets a consumer tell `SQLITE_BUSY` (5) from `SQLITE_BUSY_SNAPSHOT` (517) even though both classify to the same retryable-contention bucket — the same reason M1-S8a put MySQL's errno on the wire beside its coarser SQLSTATE. Every extended-code constant is ASSERTED against an error a real SQLite produced rather than derived from `primary | (N<<8)` on paper, and a companion test demonstrates the trap the constants exist to avoid: `ErrorCode::DatabaseBusy as i32` is 3, not 5.

  **`SQLITE_BUSY_SNAPSHOT` classifies Retryable, which is NOT where the reasoning started.** The obvious argument says NonRetryable: (ba)'s `p4a` proved `busy_timeout` never retries a 517, and re-sending the statement inside the same still-open transaction fails identically forever because the snapshot does not advance while the transaction lives. Only ROLLBACK and replay can succeed, and charter rule 3 forbids the engine performing that. **But that is precisely PostgreSQL's `40001`, which this engine already classifies Retryable** — a serialization failure also cannot be fixed by re-sending the statement (PG refuses the aborted transaction with `25P02`) and also needs the caller to replay. `Branch`'s own documentation settles it: *"Retryable only licenses the CALLER to retry per its own policy"*, which is transaction replay rather than statement re-send. Classifying 517 NonRetryable would say something different about SQLite than the engine says about PostgreSQL for an identical semantic. **Under D13 it should be unreachable anyway, which makes it diagnostic:** every undeclared or declared-write transaction takes the writer lock at `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` and so holds no stale snapshot to upgrade from, so reaching 517 means a transaction the client DECLARED `readonly` then wrote. The error message says exactly that, because it is a client bug the developer must see rather than a database condition to retry around.

  **A finding that outlives the test that produced it: `tokio::time::timeout` cannot rescue a runaway `spawn_blocking` statement.** A mutation that never fires the cancel was expected to time out and fail; it HUNG and had to be killed. Dropping the outer future returns from the timeout, but the statement is still running on a blocking thread that nothing in the async world can reclaim, and tokio waits for blocking tasks at shutdown. **So on this backend the interrupt handle is not a convenience — it is the ONLY mechanism that can stop a statement**, and a per-request `timeout_ms` alone would leak a thread per runaway query. `ferrod` must fire the cancel, not merely abandon the future. This is a consequence of the `spawn_blocking` bridge (bd) introduced and is worth stating at the spec level rather than in a test comment.

  **`cancel_handle` bounds nothing, deliberately.** PostgreSQL and MySQL both bound their cancel at a fixed 2 s because both open a SIDE CONNECTION to deliver it, and an unreachable backend would otherwise stall the teardown the deadline started (aj). SQLite's `sqlite3_interrupt()` is an in-process flag set on a handle the struct already owns: nothing is opened and nothing is awaited. Copying that constant would bound nothing and imply a hazard that does not exist here.

  **One process note, because the diff nearly shipped without it:** `error_map.rs` was written, unit-tested and green while having NO CALLER — the same "does anything read this?" check that killed the C3-3 seam (bc) and dissolved (bf)'s central objection, applied late to my own diff instead of early to the plan. A fate table nothing consults is indistinguishable from a correct one at every gate this repo runs, so it is now wired at all five statement sites in `simple_query` and `query`. The rule generalises: ask what READS a thing before asking whether it is right.

  **(bh) The third `AnyPool` arm, and a test that was green for the wrong reason (M2, C3-3e).** The SQLite pool is now reachable from `ferrod`: `sqlite://` infers `PoolKind::Sqlite`, the registry builds `AnyPool::Sqlite(Pool<SqliteBackend>)`, and the SQL and TX services dispatch to the SAME generic bodies (`run_exec_on_pool` / `begin_on_pool`) they already had. **Nothing about the registry's shape changed to accommodate a backend that is a library rather than a wire protocol** — which is the check the `PoolBackend` seam had to pass to be worth having, and it passed.

  **A SQLite pool must be spelled `sqlite://`, which is narrower than the backend accepts.** `resolve_path` also takes a bare filesystem path, but a bare path reaches `infer_pool_kind` with no scheme and lands in the warn-and-default arm. Widening the inference to "looks like a path" is the repair NOT taken: it is inference rather than declaration, and it would swallow exactly the typo'd DSNs that arm exists to surface (§12's schemeless-DSN leak was that function's one review finding at M1-S6).

  **C3-3a's wiring debt is discharged: the pool's `checkout_timeout` IS the connection's `busy_timeout`.** The two must agree, and they fail in opposite directions — a busy wait LONGER than the checkout timeout parks a tenant past the deadline that was meant to bound it, and a SHORTER one abandons contention the pool was still willing to wait out. Proven behaviourally at 250 ms against the 5 s standalone default, because the registry-level equality assertion is **currently vacuous and is documented as such**: `DEFAULT_BUSY_TIMEOUT` and `DEFAULT_POOL_CHECKOUT_TIMEOUT` are both 5 s, so that assertion would hold whether or not the wiring existed. It pins the intent and breaks the moment either constant moves; the behavioural test is what distinguishes wired from defaulted. Closing the gap outright needs a per-pool `checkout_timeout` knob, which `daemon_pool_config` does not have — a config change, recorded rather than smuggled in here.

  **A premise this engine had carried since M1-S6 was measured FALSE.** `VERSION_SQL`'s own comment read "Works VERBATIM on PostgreSQL, MySQL and MariaDB … so no per-backend method is needed". SQLite has no `version()` at all — `SELECT version()` answers `no such function` (extended code 1) — so the probe is now per-family (`SELECT sqlite_version()`), held in a SEPARATE tuning field rather than a branch, so a test can steer either statement. Without it every probe on a SQLite pool would have failed and `HELLO_ACK` would have advertised a nil `server_version`: safe, because both driver tiers refuse a nil version loudly by naming the pool (D-S8b-1), but it would have surfaced at C3-6 as a mystery instead of here as a one-line difference. The test asserts the real version AND re-runs the identical registry with `SELECT version()` substituted, so the claim is a measurement rather than an assumption.

  **The C3-2 debt is discharged, and the first version of the lockstep test was GREEN UNDER THE MUTATION IT EXISTED TO CATCH.** Nothing had linked `compose_begin_sql`'s emitted string to `p4b`'s proof that that string takes the writer lock, so a respelling would have updated the unit test's literal and left `p4b` proving a fact about a string the engine no longer emits. The new test names no BEGIN string at all: it asks the pool for its dialect, asks the composer what to emit, runs it through the same `Checkout::begin_tx_with` the TX service uses, and asks a raw side connection whether the writer lock is held.

  **Why the first version failed to discriminate is the finding worth keeping.** It issued a write inside the transaction before observing — and a write inside a DEFERRED transaction UPGRADES it to a writer and succeeds when nothing else is contending, so after any statement has run both spellings hold the lock and the observation cannot tell them apart. Respelling the undeclared arm to a bare `BEGIN` left it passing. **D13's claim is not "the transaction ends up holding the lock" but that the lock is taken AT BEGIN**, so that no upgrade is ever needed and `SQLITE_BUSY_SNAPSHOT` is unreachable; that is visible only in the window between BEGIN and the first statement, which is where the side connection now looks. Both arms are mutation-proven in that form (`BEGIN` fails the undeclared arm; `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` in the readonly arm fails the control), and the control is what stops "B cannot take the lock" from being consistent with a broken fixture.

  **An operational property found by a failing test rather than by reasoning: `PRAGMA journal_mode=WAL` cannot switch a database while another connection holds a lock on it.** When the side connection created the file as a rollback-journal database and took the writer lock before the pool had ever dialled, the backend's WAL verification (bd) failed and the checkout returned `ConnectionLost` rather than parking. For an already-WAL database this does not arise, and a pool dials at startup with contention arriving later, so it is not a hazard in the ordinary case — but it means a SQLite pool's FIRST connection should not be raced against an external writer, and it is the reason the timeout test warms the pool before contending.

  **A defect this arm MADE REACHABLE, caught in the pre-push diff re-read.** `PoolBackend::supports_row_streaming` defaults to `true` and `SqliteBackend` was inheriting it, while its `query_stream` is `Unsupported` until C3-5. `ferrod` reads that one method as the single streaming-capability authority precisely so a `fetch:stream` against a non-streaming backend is refused EARLY, before any checkout, rather than failing part-way through a result set the client has begun consuming — so the inherited default would have converted a clean, precise refusal into a mid-stream error the moment a SQLite pool could be built, which is this slice. It was unreachable before only because nothing could construct such a pool. Now overridden to `false` with the pairing asserted (and mutation-proven), so both halves flip together at C3-5.

  **(bi) The `readonly` declaration reaches the pool, and two tests were proven wrong on the way (M2, C3-4).** `Pool::checkout_declared(readonly)` carries the client's declared flag to a new `PoolBackend::apply_readonly`, which SQLite implements as `PRAGMA query_only` — giving `set_query_only` its first caller since C3-3a. `Pool::checkout()` delegates with `false`, so every existing caller is unchanged: the additive shape M1-S6 used for `take_session_mutated`. **This is a declaration being honoured, never an inference** (charter rule 6): the same flag is already trusted by `fate.rs` to suppress `Indeterminate` (§19.3) and by `compose_begin_sql` to choose `BEGIN DEFERRED` (D13) — which is (ba)'s `p4a` setup — so a lying declaration does not merely mislabel a statement, it steers the engine into the one failure class rule 3 forbids it resolving. The pragma converts that into an up-front `SQLITE_READONLY` (8).

  **The default is a no-op and that is correct rather than convenient, but the C3-3d lesson still applies in one direction.** Outside an explicit transaction neither PostgreSQL nor MySQL has a per-statement read-only scope; the spellings that would persist are connection state on a pooled connection, i.e. the cross-tenant leak (s) already rules out for isolation, and their in-transaction half is handled in the BEGIN string. So unlike `supports_row_streaming`'s `true` default (bh), this default cannot make an existing backend wrong — it is what both wire backends would write by hand. What it CAN do is let a FUTURE backend with a connection-scoped read-only mode inherit silence, and the trait doc says so. **Recorded as a real possibility rather than a closed question:** PostgreSQL's session-scoped `default_transaction_read_only` GUC, armed per checkout and cleared by the existing hygiene `RESET ALL`, would let the PG arm enforce the same declaration outside a transaction. Not built here — C3-4 is the seam plus the backend that already needed it — but the seam is what makes it a small slice rather than a redesign, and it is the first thing since (ac) that would make an honest `readonly` cost something on PostgreSQL.

  **Both checkout exits apply it, and the split is not decoration.** `checkout_declared` returns from two places — a freshly dialled connection and a recycled one — and the recycled exit must apply AFTER the hygiene reset, which would otherwise undo the arming. Enforcement wired to only one exit would depend on pool occupancy: green in any test that warms the pool, absent for exactly the requests a cold or saturated pool serves with a new connection. Each arm has a test that fails when it alone is deleted, and neither test fails for the other's mutation.

  **A test claiming to exercise the fresh-dial exit through `ferrod` was measured FALSE.** The HELLO handshake calls `PoolRegistry::pool_info`, whose version probe checks a connection out and returns it, so by the time any EXEC runs the pool always has an idle connection: no daemon e2e in that directory can reach the fresh exit at all. The mutation is what established it — deleting the fresh-dial arm left the test green — and the proof moved to pool level, where which connection serves a checkout is decided by the test rather than by a handshake.

  **The second wrong test took TWO rounds to diagnose, and the first diagnosis was also wrong — which is the more useful finding.** The recycle test's docstring claimed the hygiene reset's `PRAGMA query_only=OFF` was what let the next tenant write, and deleting that line left it GREEN. The first conclusion drawn from that was "the seam disarms, not hygiene, because every checkout re-declares". **That was wrong too.** The real cause is that the test had stopped testing recycling at all: its middle step ends in a REFUSED statement, and a connection whose statement failed is not the one the next checkout receives — so the final step was served a freshly dialled connection, read-write by construction, and passed for a reason unrelated to disarming. Adding one SUCCEEDING readonly checkout between them, which returns its connection to the pool intact, makes the same mutation fail it.

  **So hygiene IS what disarms a recycled connection, and the seam cannot do it.** `reset` clears `apply_readonly`'s tracked flag unconditionally, so after a reset the flag reads "off" whether or not the pragma is, and `apply_readonly(conn, false)` short-circuits and issues nothing. The flag and the pragma are kept in step by that one line and nothing else. The §7.4 case — a tenant arming `PRAGMA query_only=ON` ITSELF, declaring nothing, so the flag never reads `true` — is covered by the same line, and now has its own test. **The reusable lesson is about this pool, not about SQLite: a test step that ends in a failed statement silently converts a recycle test into a fresh-dial test**, because the failed connection does not come back. Any future test that means to exercise a recycled connection must end its setup step on a statement that SUCCEEDED.

  **(bj) SQLite streams, and a non-buffering proof that had to be calibrated before it proved anything (M2, C3-5).** `query_stream` + `reclaim_stream` are implemented and `supports_row_streaming` flipped to `true` in the SAME change, which is what (bh) asked to be asserted rather than remembered — the test that said so is now on its other side, and the C3-3d test asserting "streaming is the only `Unsupported` left" was UPDATED rather than deleted, because its real subject was always "the impl is complete and its gaps are exactly the ones we claim". The backend is now complete against `PoolBackend` with nothing `Unsupported`.

  **SQLite is a conn-owning backend in MySQL's exact sense, and not by choice.** `Statement` and `Rows` borrow the `Connection`, and `Connection` is `Send` but not `Sync`, so a stream that yields rows one at a time must keep the connection alive, borrowed, and on ONE blocking thread for its whole life. The connection is therefore moved out of `SqliteConn` at `query_stream` and moved back at `reclaim_stream` — which is why that hook exists at all (n), and why `affected`/`last_insert_id` are answered from the CONNECTION post-drain rather than from the stream: SQLite's `changes()` reports the last data-modifying statement and goes stale (be), so anything read before the statement finished would be the previous one's. The borrow constraint is not merely conceptual — written inline first, every early-return arm was a borrow-check error, since the connection cannot be handed back from inside the scope that borrows it; the producer is a separate fn taking `&Connection` for exactly that reason.

  **The non-buffering test was worthless twice before it worked, and both failures are the same failure.** The first version took ten rows, slept, and asserted that the next row was the one that follows — which holds just as well when the producer has buffered the entire result during the sleep, so it passed under the widened-channel mutation it existed to catch. That is (ba)'s `p2` lesson arriving again on a test written in full knowledge of it. The fix was to make the outcome DEPEND on the producer still being mid-statement: fire the connection's interrupt after the idle period, and assert the stream is truncated. **That still did not discriminate**, and the reason is worth recording because it is not obvious: at 200 000 rows a widened producer only reaches ~157 000 within 250 ms, so the interrupt truncated the result either way. `TOTAL` is now 50 000 against a 400 ms window, both MEASURED — a widened producer completes well inside it — so "did the interrupt find anything to stop?" has two different answers. **The general rule: a stall probe is only a proof when the thing it measures cannot also be produced by the mutated code being merely slow.**

  **Abandonment costs nothing and a drain timeout still kills.** A dropped receiver makes `blocking_send` fail and the producer stops stepping rather than running the result to completion for nobody. Dropping the whole stream without reclaiming is the other arm: the join handle goes with it, the detached task finishes and drops the `Connection`, and `SqliteConn` is left handle-less — which `is_closed` reports, so the pool discards the husk. That is exactly the mechanism `RowStreamHandle::finish` already relies on when a drain times out and calls dropping the stream "the kill", so no new machinery was needed to satisfy it.

  **C3-3a's removed `dead` flag stays removed, and the condition it named was checked rather than assumed.** That slice deleted an unobservable flag and said C3-5 should reintroduce it only if the parked window turned out to be POOL-VISIBLE. It is not: between `query_stream` and `reclaim_stream` the connection is checked out, so the pool's `is_closed` sweep never sees it, and `finalize_stream` reads `tx_status` only after the reclaim has restored the handle. The park/unpark pair gained stream-specific names (`park_for_stream`/`unpark_from_stream`) rather than becoming `pub(crate)`, so the distinction between "parked for one blocking call" and "parked for a whole stream" is where a later reader will look for it.

  **`ColMeta.tag` is `NULL` on every streamed column, and that is a decision inherited from (bf) rather than a shortfall.** The decode authority is the per-cell tag; the PHP client drops the column tag in both paths by design and nothing in `ferrod` reads it. The buffered path fills it from the first row only because it has one in hand — a stream does not, and peeking a row to populate a field with no consumer would cost the first row's latency for nothing.

  **(bk) Two process corrections, both worth more than the code change that surfaced them (M2, C3-5 follow-up).**

  **CI logs ARE readable, and have been all along.** The dev loop has carried "this environment cannot read a CI failure — `get_job_logs` returns only a job's tail, the raw archive 404s" as lesson (1) for many iterations, and every red lane since has been diagnosed by INFERENCE with the inference labelled as such. It is wrong: `get_job_logs` with `return_content: true` returns the job's log content directly, and the C3-5 red lane was diagnosed from the actual panic message in seconds rather than guessed at.
