A stale caution is not harmless — it made every CI failure more expensive than it needed to be and pushed the loop toward speculative fixes, which is exactly what the drive-to-green rules warn against.

  **A test can be flaky because it borrows a knob that means something else.** C3-4's busy-timeout test set the pool's `checkout_timeout` to 250 ms so that "waited it out" and "used the 5 s default" would be twenty times apart. But `Pool::checkout` also bounds the DIAL by `checkout_timeout` (aj/B5), so that 250 ms was simultaneously the budget for opening a file and switching it to WAL — which a loaded CI runner does not always make. It went red with `warm the pool into WAL: Timeout`: the pool working exactly as designed, and the test asking it to dial faster than it can. Reproduced locally by setting the bound to zero, which produces the identical message. The fix separates the two values in the test, which costs nothing it was actually asserting — the WIRING is pinned by the registry's own unit test, and what this one measures is that the backend USES the busy timeout it was given. **The general shape: when a test tightens a configuration value to make one behaviour observable, check what ELSE that value governs.**

  **(bl) C3-6a — the Doctrine tier learns the SQLite family, and the acceptance column it unlocks says two useful things and one uncomfortable one (M2, C3-6a).**

  **The tier change is small and mostly delegation.** `PlatformVersion` gained `KIND_SQLITE` and an `AbstractSQLiteDriver` delegate (which IGNORES the version provider — there is one SQLite platform), `TemporalFormat` a `Y-m-d H:i:s` arm that is bind-side only (no SQLite backend ever EMITS a `TIMESTAMPTZ` — all four temporal tags are unreachable on the read path per (bf)), `ExceptionConverter` an arm for the stock SQLite table, and `lastInsertId()` a third message. `quote()` takes the existing non-MySQL branch by decision rather than fallthrough: `SQLitePlatform` does not override `quoteStringLiteral()`, and SQLite has no backslash-escape mode, which is why `literals_are_standard` (at) does not arise here. **Two unit tests that used `'sqlite'` as their example of an UNKNOWN family went red the moment it became a known one** — the swap to `'mssql'` is the small proof they were testing the arm rather than a string.

  **`familyFromVersion()` does NOT learn SQLite, and that is the honest answer rather than a gap.** SQLite's banner is a bare `3.53.2`; MySQL's is a bare `8.4.11`. No rule separates them that is not a guess, so both take the null arm and the platform-before-connect path fails loudly. The practical rule — already true of every MySQL pool — is: do not set `serverVersion` in the connection params, because it short-circuits the handshake that is the only authority on the family. Only PostgreSQL, whose banner names the product, survives that short-circuit.

  **The stock SQLite exception table keys on the MESSAGE TEXT**, not on SQLSTATE or errno, which is what every bundled SQLite driver feeds it. So the arm works only because the engine's `error_map` passes SQLite's own message through unaltered — asserted rather than assumed, because a reworded message would degrade every specialised exception to a bare `DriverException` silently, and no test of the code path would notice.

  **THE COLUMN: 329 passed / 357 executed, against a CONTROL that is 357/357** (`docs/dbal-suite/2026-09-15-c3-6a-sqlite-results.md`; two runs each, result line and ordered failure set identical). The control is upstream's own `pdo_sqlite` against the same database file with the contact assertion INVERTED — the C2e shape (ar) — and it is what makes the 28 attributable instead of arguable. Three guards were mutation-proven: pointing the control at Ferro is refused, running the Ferro column against `pdo_sqlite` is refused, and setting both `db_driver` and `db_driverClass` is refused (DriverManager silently prefers `driverClass`, so a "control" configured that way would have been measuring Ferro). **SQLite is the first family whose whole acceptance column needs no server**, so the runner's reset is a file delete — with the `-wal`/`-shm` sidecars, and BEFORE `ferrod` opens the file rather than after.

  **All 28 have two causes and neither is a driver defect. The first is the uncomfortable one: 24 are §7.4.** DBAL's `SQLitePlatform::getAlterTableSQL()` rebuilds a table through `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE __temp__x AS SELECT …` followed by four more statements, and `AbstractSchemaManager::alterTable()` runs them as five SEPARATE `executeStatement()` calls outside any transaction — so on a transaction-mode pool the temp table is gone by statement 2. **Measured, PostgreSQL behaves identically** (an autocommit `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE` is `42P01` on the next request; both families survive inside one transaction), so this is the pooling contract, not a SQLite shortfall — SQLite is simply the first family whose STOCK Doctrine schema manager depends on session state between statements. **The remedy is verified, not proposed:** the identical sequence inside `$conn->transactional(…)` succeeds, because the transaction pins the connection to its `tx_id`. SQLite has transactional DDL, so the wrap costs nothing. The consequence for C6 is stated plainly: a non-simple `ALTER TABLE` through the stock SQLite schema manager must run inside a transaction when the connection is a Ferro one. The other 4 send TWO `CREATE TABLE`s in one `executeStatement()`; Ferro prepares every statement and refuses, and **PostgreSQL refuses the same shape with `42601`** — one statement per request is a uniform contract, category (c).

  **`foreign_keys` was already ON, and nobody had decided it.** C3-3a left the question open believing SQLite's OFF default applied. Measured through the driver, a Ferro SQLite connection reports `PRAGMA foreign_keys = 1` and refuses an orphan INSERT with errno 787, with no pragma anywhere in the backend: `rusqlite`'s `bundled` feature compiles libsqlite3-sys with `SQLITE_DEFAULT_FOREIGN_KEYS=1`, where `pdo_sqlite` against the system library reports 0. An engine-wide integrity guarantee resting on a dependency's compile flag is one `cargo update` from silently inverting, so it is now SET and READ BACK at dial (the WAL discipline), refusing the connection if the build cannot enforce it. The value is ON; nothing observable changed. **The half that is a real finding: neither tier's own mechanism can work here** — Doctrine's opt-in `EnableForeignKeys` middleware and Laravel's SQLite connector both apply a SESSION pragma at driver-connect, i.e. to whichever pooled connection serves that one call. Measured: with the middleware and without it, the result is identical, because the guarantee comes from the pool. A per-connection setting that must hold for every tenant belongs at dial — the same place and reasoning as the MySQL family's `time_zone = '+00:00'` (S7). **Its guard test is honest about being currently redundant**: it asserts the raw bundled default too, so a future flag change fails the measurement rather than the guarantee — C3-3a's `lose_handle` lesson applied on purpose rather than discovered.

  **The dates-read-back-as-`TEXT` asymmetry (bf) costs the Doctrine tier nothing, and the suite is why that is known.** Every temporal row of `Functional/TypeConversionTest` passes on the SQLite column — `datetime`, `datetimetz`, `date`, `time` — because DBAL's own types parse the platform's format strings out of strings, and `SQLitePlatform` declares all of them as plain `Y-m-d H:i:s`/`Y-m-d`/`H:i:s`. The control agrees, and `pdo_sqlite` does the same thing, so it is not an asymmetry a Doctrine user can observe. It stays real for a caller using `ferro/client` directly, and C3-6b must re-ask it for Illuminate.

  **Not established, said rather than implied:** §14's bar remains unmet (329/357 with 28 triaged non-passes, and the ORM suite is still not run at all), and `php/doctrine-dbal`'s own live tier still has no SQLite lane — `LiveTestCase` spawns a `ferrod` with a mandatory PostgreSQL pool, and adding a third is a `php/client` harness change that was deliberately left out of this slice.

  **(bm) A cross-tenant SQLite session leak, found while scoping C3-6b, and the reset that closes it (M2, C3-6a follow-up).**

  **The defect: every connection-scoped `PRAGMA` a tenant set survived the hygiene reset and reached the next tenant.** C3-3b built that reset as an explicit four-item list — open transaction, the engine's own `query_only` arming, ATTACHed databases, temp objects — reasoning from what the ENGINE leaves on a connection. That reasoning is complete for the engine and silent about the tenant, and SQLite's pragmas are per-connection, so a tenant's are simply carried forward. Probed through a real pool, one pragma per tenant pair, **17 of 23 survived a recycle**: `foreign_keys=OFF` (which silently disarms the integrity guarantee (bl) had just made a declared one, for everyone afterwards), `writable_schema=1` (which lets the NEXT tenant `DELETE FROM sqlite_master`, i.e. destroy the schema, without ever asking for the privilege), `trusted_schema=0`, `ignore_check_constraints=1`, `read_uncommitted=1`, `recursive_triggers`, `legacy_alter_table`, `cell_size_check`, `automatic_index=0`, `reverse_unordered_selects`, `secure_delete`, `synchronous=0`, `fullfsync`, `checkpoint_fullfsync`, `cache_size`, `hard_heap_limit`, `analysis_limit`, `threads` — and `busy_timeout`, which is the pool's own checkout bound (bh). It was reproduced first through two real PHP client sessions, which is what "cross-tenant" means to a user.

  **The fix is not a longer list, and that was a decision rather than a shortcut.** SQLite has around sixty pragmas and gains more with each release, so a hand-kept list rots silently — the same failure (bl) had just found in `foreign_keys` resting on a build flag nobody had decided. The right analogue is the one the MySQL backend already uses: `COM_RESET_CONNECTION` (M1-S6). SQLite has no such command, but it also has no server to reconnect to — the "reconnect" is a local file open — so **`ResetProfile::Full` now CLOSES the connection and opens a fresh one**, which is complete by construction and additionally re-applies the DECLARED setup (`open_configured`: WAL verified, the pool's `busy_timeout`, `foreign_keys` on and read back) rather than trusting what the previous tenant left.

  **That makes the two profiles genuinely differ for the first time, and C3-3b's `Some(Full)` had to move with it.** A reopen was measured at **~250 µs** (debug build) against §16's p50 < 60 µs boundary target, so paying it on every recycled checkout would blow the latency budget on hygiene alone; `clean_reset_profile` is now `Some(Targeted)` — the four-item list, unchanged. **The gate is sound and that is why narrowing is safe**: the only statements that can set connection-scoped state are `PRAGMA` and `ATTACH`, and `ferro-classify`'s SQLite dialect taints on both UNCONDITIONALLY, ahead of its safe-list and independent of `pin_on_unknown` (MySQL's `CALL`/`DO` shape). That property had no test; it has one now, with a control proving `pin_on_unknown=false` really is off in the same call. **Nothing was weakened:** a clean connection gets exactly the reset it got before, and a tainted one gets a strictly stronger one.

  **Two hazards were checked rather than assumed.** `PRAGMA journal_mode=WAL` cannot switch a database while another connection holds a lock — a failing test found that at (bh) — and `open_configured` treats a non-WAL answer as a hard connect error, so a reopen under a sibling's write lock could have made hygiene fail. Measured: on an ALREADY-WAL database that pragma returns `"wal"` under another connection's write lock; (bh)'s failure was the rollback→WAL *switch*, which a live pool's reopen never performs. And a failed reopen must leave the connection dead rather than half-reset, which it does by construction — the handle is parked first and restored only on the success arm, the same contract `with_conn`'s panic arm relies on.

  **The sharpest test in the set is the one that distinguishes a reopen from a scrub.** `last_insert_rowid()` is per-connection and sticky ((bf)), so it is an identity probe: a scrubbed connection still answers the previous tenant's rowid, a reopened one answers 0. Without it an implementation that enumerated a handful of pragmas would pass every other test in the file while carrying the other fifty forward — which is precisely the outcome being ruled out. A second test asserts the opposite direction on a CLEAN recycle (the rowid must survive), so the two profiles cannot silently collapse back into one. A third guards an interaction that would be a safety hole rather than an inefficiency: `apply_readonly` short-circuits on a tracked flag (bi), so a reopen that left the flag reading `true` would make the very next declared-readonly checkout arm nothing at all. All mutation-proven, each failing exactly the tests that claim it.

  **Process note worth keeping: this was found by probing for something else.** The iteration set out to scope C3-6b (the Illuminate SQLite column), and the first question asked of the engine was how Laravel's `SQLiteBuilder::dropAllTables()` behaves through Ferro — which runs `PRAGMA writable_schema = 1` and then `DELETE FROM sqlite_master` as separate statements. It worked, which was the surprise: on the transaction-mode pool those are separate checkouts, so the pragma should not have been in force for the second. Asking why a thing *succeeded* is what surfaced it.

  **(bn) C3-6b — the Illuminate tier learns SQLite, and the framework suite's SQLite column with its control (M2).**

  **The execution layer turned out to be family-agnostic in exactly one character.** `FerroPostgresConnection`'s whole body — `select`, `cursor`, `statement`, `affectingStatement`, `unprepared`, the binding normalisation and the hydration — is written against the Ferro client and Illuminate's own `run()`, neither of which knows what a family is; only `extends PostgresConnection` is PostgreSQL-specific. It is now a TRAIT (`FerroConnectionBody`) rather than a shared base class, because the thing each family must NOT share is its parent: `FerroSQLiteConnection extends SQLiteConnection` inherits a different stock Grammar, Processor and Schema builder, which is what charter rule 6 keeps stock. A common ancestor could only have been `Connection` itself, throwing that inheritance away.

  **The measurement was built first and it named every piece of tier surface that followed** — the (bl) discipline, and it earned its keep four times rather than once. (1) The first smoke run died 11 of 11 on `getPdo()->lastInsertId()`: `PostgresProcessor` uses `insert … returning id` and never touches PDO, while SQLite goes through the BASE `Processor::processInsertGetId`, which calls it directly. (2) The second case failed on `table "users" already exists`, exactly as the previous iteration predicted. (3) The first FULL run then reported **182 of 225 errors on one line** — see below. (4) 40 more were the alias hijack. None of these was designed for in advance; all four were reported by upstream's own tests.

  **`lastInsertId()` is where the pooled engine and PDO genuinely disagree, and the disagreement is resolved in the PDO shim.** `Ferro\Client\Connection::lastInsertId()` is deliberately a PER-STATEMENT value, cleared on the way in to every request so a statement that generates no key leaves it `null` rather than carrying a stale one (M1-S8a, (bf)) — right for the engine, because a pooled statement can land on a different backend connection and a carried-over rowid would be a silently wrong key. PDO's `lastInsertId()` is a property of the HANDLE and keeps answering until another insert replaces it. `Processor::processInsertGetId()` looks like it reads the value immediately — `insert(); getPdo()->lastInsertId();` — but `Connection::insert()` fires `QueryExecuted` first, and any listener that runs a query clears the client's value in between, which is what 182 of those errors were. **So the connection remembers each non-null key from its own writes and never clears it**, and the shim reads that. The remembered value is what `pdo_sqlite` would return in every case, including after a rollback, where PDO also still answers the rolled-back rowid. The engine, the client and the Doctrine tier's stricter "read it immediately" contract are all untouched. It THROWS rather than returning PDO's falsy `false`, on the sibling tier's reasoning: `processInsertGetId` would hand a `false` straight back as a model's primary key, and (m) already records a wrong key as strictly worse than none. The discriminating mutation is the naive implementation — shim reads the client directly — which passes a plain insert-then-read test and fails only the two that encode PDO's stickiness.

  **`dropAllTables()` needed a schema-builder override, and the defect produced itself as an artifact.** Upstream takes the statement branch only for an in-memory database; for a file one it calls `refreshDatabaseFile()`, which is `file_put_contents($connection->getDatabaseName(), '')` — and under Ferro that name is the config LABEL, since §12/D8 keeps the path in the engine and an in-memory DSN is a hard refusal (bd). So `migrate:fresh` would create and truncate a junk file and leave every table in place. During the mutation round, with the stock builder restored, the live tests left two empty files named `laravel_tests` and `ferro_sqlite_label` in the REPOSITORY ROOT while every table survived. `FerroSQLiteBuilder` therefore takes the statement branch unconditionally and makes `refreshDatabaseFile()` throw — a safety refusal, since silently truncating an arbitrary path derived from a config label is destructive. The four statements run in ONE transaction (`PRAGMA writable_schema` is connection-scoped, so each `select()` would otherwise be its own checkout — the (bm) leak is closed, so the sequence is now correctly REFUSED in autocommit), with `vacuum` AFTER the commit, because SQLite cannot VACUUM inside a transaction. Both halves mutation-proven, each failing with SQLite's own message naming the mechanism (`table sqlite_master may not be modified`; `cannot VACUUM from within a transaction`). Nothing here generates SQL: every statement still comes from the stock `SQLiteGrammar`.

  **THE COLUMNS: `ferro-sqlite` 575 / 579 executed, against a CONTROL at 586 / 588** (`docs/laravel-suite/2026-09-15-c3-6b-sqlite-results.md`). The control is upstream's own `pdo_sqlite` opening the SAME file with NO ferrod started at all — stronger than pointing it elsewhere, since a control with no Ferro to reach cannot quietly route through one. **On SQLite the contact assertion cannot be satisfied by the trap it exists for**, which is the part that needed new thinking: on PostgreSQL "something answered and it said PostgreSQL" is enough, but testbench's injected fallback IS SQLite. So the probe additionally reads `PRAGMA database_list` and requires the `main` database to be open on the expected FILE — mutation-proven against a decoy path, against `:memory:` (where it names the testbench trap), and against the stock connection class. `sqlite_version()` replaces `version()`, which SQLite does not have at all — the premise that measured FALSE at (bh). One harness trap worth recording: the SQLite path must be derived with the ENGINE's rule (`strip_prefix("sqlite://")`), because PHP's `parse_url()` returns FALSE outright for `sqlite:///abs/path`, so the obvious URL parse would refuse every valid DSN.

  **All four `ferro-sqlite` non-passes are triaged and only one is a real incompatibility.** The single FAILURE reproduces in the control — upstream's own. Two errors are the (am)/(ar) driver-NAME artifact arriving on the SQLite side: upstream's `markTestSkipped` is gated on `$this->driver === 'sqlite'`, so under a different name the case runs against a limitation SQLite genuinely has — **and they SKIP in the alias column**, which is the proof rather than the argument.

  **The fourth is §7.4 again, through a new mechanism, and three remedies were ruled out by measurement.** `SQLiteGrammar::compileAlter()` emits six statements for a column change and `Blueprint::build()` runs them as six separate `statement()` calls: `PRAGMA foreign_keys = OFF`, build `__temp__x`, copy, `drop table x`, rename, `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON`. The pragma is connection-scoped, so the OFF never reaches the drop, and `DROP TABLE`'s implicit `DELETE FROM` is refused by the child row's foreign key (errno 787). Probed against a real pool: as Illuminate runs it, refused; **all six inside ONE transaction, still refused**, because `PRAGMA foreign_keys` is a no-op inside a transaction — which is exactly what makes this case different from (bl)'s `__temp__` remedy and from `dropAllTables()`, where a transaction IS the whole fix; and transaction plus `PRAGMA defer_foreign_keys = ON`, which is transaction-legal, still refused. There is no execution-layer remedy, and inventing one would mean the tier substituting SQL the stock grammar did not emit. Recorded for C6 with the mechanism named and the obvious fixes eliminated.

  **The stock-name alias is NOT a drop-in escape hatch on SQLite the way it is on PostgreSQL**, and that asymmetry is structural rather than a defect. `register()` documents that an alias hijacks every connection whose `driver` is that name; upstream's own tests use throwaway SQLite connections as a convenience regardless of the family under test, and none of them carries a `ferro_socket`. The alias column costs **42 extra errors** for the two name-gated skips it buys. Under the PostgreSQL columns nothing hijacked the `sqlite` name, so those tests passed there and still do. One fix came out of it: the harness now registers the alias only in the column that actually RUNS under the stock name — registering it while measuring `ferro-sqlite` had hijacked testbench's stock `sqlite` connection for 40 errors about a configuration that was not under test. **The PostgreSQL column was re-run afterwards as a regression check and its result line is identical to (ar)'s recorded one** (633 / 6 errors / 3 failures / 54 skipped / 1 risky).

  **Not established, said rather than implied:** §15's bar still names MySQL, which this tier does not register at all, and the ORM suite is not run on any family.

  **(bo) C3-7 scoping: the online-backup MECHANISM already works, so C3-7 is an ADMIN SERVICE slice — and it is a `/proto` change (M2).**

  §7.6's last sentence asks for an "online backup API exposed via admin service for snapshots", and the obvious reading is that `ferro-backend-sqlite` needs `sqlite3_backup_*` wired through a new engine method. **Probed before writing any of it (the C3-3 discipline), that turns out to be unnecessary.** `VACUUM INTO '<path>'` is a plain statement, so the pool already runs it; it is SQLite's own recommended way to snapshot a live database; and the per-request `timeout_ms` + interrupt handle that (bg) proved is the ONLY mechanism able to stop a runaway SQLite statement already bounds it. `sqlite3_backup_*` would additionally need a connection pair OUTSIDE the pool, which is exactly the kind of second lifetime the `PoolBackend` seam exists to avoid.

  **What C3-7 is actually missing is the admin service, and it does not exist at all.** `ADMIN = 5` is a reserved service id in `/proto` with NO `[methods.admin]` table, and `dispatch::route(service::ADMIN, _)` answers `Unsupported` with a test asserting exactly that. Adding methods there is a charter-rule-2 change (registry + golden vectors + both codecs) plus an authorization question — who may snapshot, and who chooses the destination — so it is split: **C3-7a** is this scoping plus the measured properties pinned as tests; **C3-7b** is the admin service itself. §13's `ferro top` is the same missing transport, so C3-7b is the slice that opens it for C4 as well.

  **Four properties an admin surface has to be designed around, each measured rather than assumed.** (1) **No pool coordination is needed** — the worry worth checking was that a snapshot reads pages while another tenant holds SQLite's writer lock, so the API might have to drain or quiesce the pool; it does not, and a snapshot taken under a concurrent OPEN write transaction succeeds and correctly EXCLUDES that transaction's uncommitted row. (2) **SQLite refuses an existing target**, so "overwrite the previous snapshot" cannot be offered without the admin surface deleting the file first, which is a destructive filesystem action and belongs to C3-7b's authorization rather than happening implicitly. (3) **A refused snapshot leaves a ZERO-BYTE file behind** (SQLite creates the target before discovering the source is unwritable) — and the obvious follow-on worry is FALSE: measured, that leftover does NOT block the retry, because a zero-byte file is treated as an empty database rather than an existing one. Both halves are recorded because an implementer would otherwise have to guess at the pair. (4) **`PRAGMA query_only` bounds writes to OTHER FILES too**, which its name does not suggest: a checkout the client DECLARED `readonly` refuses `VACUUM INTO` with `SQLITE_READONLY`. That cuts both ways — an honest `readonly` declaration already prevents a tenant snapshotting on that checkout, and C3-7b must NOT serve the admin surface on a declared-readonly checkout or the snapshot will simply fail.

  **The scoping also surfaced a capability boundary, and the interesting part is why it is RAISED rather than patched.** §12/D8 keeps the database path in the engine so PHP never learns it, yet `VACUUM INTO` lets the CLIENT name a path the engine writes — a complete copy of the database, anywhere the daemon can write. Refusing that verb was the obvious fix. It would have been security theatre, and only a measurement showed it: `ATTACH DATABASE '<path>'` plus an ordinary `CREATE TABLE side.copy AS SELECT …` produces the same copy at the same arbitrary path, and `ATTACH` is permitted BY DESIGN — (be)'s hygiene list exists to `DETACH` it afterwards. **With the negative control that makes it precise:** outside a transaction the attachment does NOT survive to the next statement, because `ATTACH` taints unconditionally ((bm)), so it is the TRANSACTION — the pool pinning every statement to one connection — that carries the capability. Closing the boundary therefore means deciding a general rule about path-naming statements, which is a §21 question now raised with three options and an interim default of "unchanged". A tripwire test asserts the capability, so a decision to close it turns that test RED rather than passing silently — the (bl) `foreign_keys` precedent, applied on purpose.

  **No engine code changed in C3-7a**, which is the honest summary: the slice's output is a decision, a split, and seven tests that pin what the next slice builds on.

  **(bp) D14 implemented: the path guard, and the mechanism that made it cheap (M2, C3-7a follow-up).**

  C3-7a raised the path-naming boundary as a §21 open item with an interim default of *unchanged*; the owner chose confinement, and this is **D14** built. **The enforcement point is SQLite's own authorizer callback**, and finding that is what turned an expensive decision into a contained one: `VACUUM INTO` and `ATTACH` both fire `SQLITE_ATTACH` carrying the **resolved filename** before SQLite opens anything, so one guard covers both — and any future verb that attaches a file — without parsing a single statement. Charter rule 6 never comes into it: nothing is rewritten and nothing is inferred from SQL text; SQLite says which file it is about to open and the engine answers.

  **Measured on the BUNDLED SQLite 3.53.2, not the system one**, because C3-7a's own probe ran against 3.45.1 and flagged the difference as a thing to check rather than assume — the eighth premise this project has refused to take on trust. Both verbs fire the event; a `Deny` yields `SQLITE_AUTH` (extended 23) and **creates no file at all**, which is a cleaner refusal than the declared-`readonly` path, which leaves a zero-byte one ((bo) property 3). `rusqlite`'s `hooks` feature carries no additional dependency.

  **The default is what makes the guard safe without configuration:** `allow_dir` is the database file's own directory unless the operator sets `FERRO_POOL_<NAME>_ALLOW_DIR`, so `VACUUM INTO 'snap.db'` beside the database works out of the box and no existing deployment changes. The root is canonicalised ONCE at dial — a root that does not resolve is a connect failure rather than a guard that silently allows nothing — and a candidate is checked by canonicalising its PARENT, because a snapshot's target does not exist yet. An EMPTY filename is allowed: that is how SQLite reports a temporary or in-memory attachment, which writes no file. A blank env value reads as UNSET rather than as the empty path, so a stray `export FERRO_POOL_X_ALLOW_DIR=` cannot turn into a pool that refuses every `ATTACH`.

  **C3-7a's tripwire did its job and is now inverted.** That test asserted the CAPABILITY, green on purpose, so the open decision could not be forgotten; it now asserts the refusal, which is the whole point of having written it that way ((bl)'s `foreign_keys` precedent). **Three mutations, each failing exactly the tests that claim it:** not installing the guard fails the five guard tests and leaves the seven mechanism tests green; installing it on the fresh-dial path but not the reopen fails ONLY `the_guard_survives_a_recycle` — the C3-4 lesson, since (bm) made `ResetProfile::Full` a close-and-reopen and enforcement on one exit alone would depend on pool occupancy; and comparing path strings instead of canonicalising fails ONLY the `..`-traversal test, which is the one line a naive guard would fall to while passing everything else.

  **(bq) The §14–15 incompatibilities page gets its second tier and a CI gate, after it was caught telling readers a fixed defect was still broken (M2, C6).**

  §14 budgeted "the full per-package catalogue" for M2 and `docs/known-incompatibilities.md` had only ever had one package in it: every entry was written from the Doctrine tier at M1-S8b, and `ferro/laravel` — three phases of measured behaviour — was absent. That half is now written, from the recorded C2/C3-6b acceptance columns rather than re-derived: the driver-NAME artifact and its two-column proof, the SQLite alias asymmetry that costs 42 errors, the `quote()`/`DB::escape()` refusal on a pool that does not advertise `literals_are_standard`, the sticky `lastInsertId()`, the raw-string value hand-off with its one deliberate RFC3339 divergence, `search_path` needing to be in two places, `FerroSQLiteBuilder`, the `compileAlter()` foreign-key refusal with its three measured dead ends, and the plain statement that **MySQL is not registered by this tier, so §15's bar is not met**. D14 gained a *Files and paths* section beside it.

  **The gate is the part worth keeping, and it exists because this page had already rotted in the worst available direction.** It told readers that a `bigint` at or above 2^32 could not be READ — a HIGH-severity defect fixed at M1-S9 with a live test proving the whole int64 range — and that a dead backend could wedge a first query for the ~127 s OS connect timeout, bounded at Phase B (aj). A stale "this is broken" is worse than a stale README: it is believed, and it is worked around. Nothing was watching, because prose cannot be checked.

  **Citations can be.** `ci/check-incompatibilities-doc.sh` (a file check, no toolchain or backend, beside `ci/check-d12-recorded.sh` in the `rust` lane) fails the build when a Markdown link or `docs/…` path stops resolving, when a cited `§22.2 (xx)` letter is absent from this changelog, when a cited `Ferro\…` name or `SomeTest::testFoo` no longer exists, when a follow-up under `docs/followups/` declares no status, or — the rule that catches the rot above — **when the page cites a follow-up that has since been RESOLVED**. A fixed follow-up is not deleted, because the measurement it records is worth keeping; it stops being *citable as a live defect*, and the page points at the §22.2 entry that closed it instead. So fixing a defect and forgetting this page now turns CI red.

  Running it named the work: **all nine follow-ups in `docs/followups/` are resolved**, three said so in three different vocabularies and four said nothing at all, and the page was citing two of them as live defects. Every status was re-derived against the tree rather than copied from the document asserting it — which is how a second stale claim surfaced, this one in `CLAUDE.md`: the MySQL `CALL` blind spot it still described as open was closed at (av)/(aw), and only the `/proto` multi-result-set change remains deferred, on the separate and stated ground that no tier can reach a second result set. Both corrected here.

  **Six checks, ten mutations, and the round paid for itself three times.** (1) The spec-letter check read only the FIRST letter of a citation list like `§22.2 (am), (ar)`, so a rotted second letter passed — a green check doing nothing. (2) The PHP-name and test checks grepped all of `php/`, which includes the **gitignored `vendor/`** that the `rust` lane never installs, and matched on the LEAF name alone: `Connection` is declared a dozen times inside `doctrine/dbal`'s own tree, so a cited `Ferro\Nope\Connection` would have passed — locally and, for names that happened to collide, in CI too. Both now resolve against this repository's own sources with the NAMESPACE matched as well as the leaf. (3) Tightening them exposed a citation that could never have resolved in CI: `TransactionTest::testCommitFailure` is **upstream's** test, living only under `vendor/`, and the page now names it fully qualified — a bare backticked `SomeTest` means one of ours, by rule. A fourth defect came out of the same work and is worth the line because it is silent: `decl_re` built its regex with `printf '…%s\b'`, and **printf expands `\b` in a FORMAT to a backspace**, so the pattern matched nothing and every cited name was reported missing — visible only because the failure was loud rather than the other way round. Entries whose defect is fixed are now **rewritten in place and marked FIXED with the milestone that closed them**, never quietly deleted, because an entry that was public long enough to be believed needs a correction rather than a silence.

  **(br) The §13 slow log ships first of the four observability bullets, because it is the only one whose CONSUMER already exists — and it carries the fingerprint the other three will reuse (M2, C4a).**

  §13 lists four things: OTLP traces, Prometheus metrics, the slow log, and `ferro top`. The obvious order is the order they are written in; the order that can actually be BUILT is different, and the difference is transport. Prometheus is an HTTP scrape endpoint and `ferro top` is a TUI over the admin service — and `ADMIN = 5` is a reserved `/proto` service id with no method table, no dispatch route and no authorization model ((bo)). A log line needs the `tracing` subscriber the daemon has had since M0, which is exactly what product-vision §5 means by *"slow log as structured JSON to stdout/journald"*. So the slow log ships first, and it ships the piece the other three all need: the fingerprint.

  **The redaction contract is STRUCTURAL, not procedural.** product-vision §5 says *"normalized fingerprints only, never raw SQL, in metrics/spans"*. `ferro_classify::Fingerprint` is a newtype whose only constructor is the normalizer — there is no `From<String>`, no public field, and a grep of the workspace confirms exactly one construction site. So the obvious mutation, "log the raw SQL", is not merely caught by a test: **it cannot be written at the call site at all**, which is a stronger guarantee than a test and is why the newtype exists rather than a `String` and a review rule. Parameters are not a field of the record — `params` is a COUNT, and the values appear only through the type-and-length renderer §13 now documents.

  **It reuses the pin lexer's region pass rather than writing a second one**, and that is a redaction decision, not a tidiness one. `ferro-classify`'s scanner is the code that already knows what a string literal is on each dialect — E-strings, `''` escapes, dollar-quoting, nested block comments — and is total on any input. A separate "good enough" literal finder would be a second place for the contract to be wrong, and the failure mode is a value in a log file. The one change the pass needed is that it now records each hidden span's KIND: a literal becomes `?`, a comment is dropped, and the difference is underivable from the masked copy alone, because a space INSIDE a literal is byte-identical to a space in code.

  **Measured, not assumed, in three places.** `exec_us` and `queue_us` were already computed and already on the wire, so the record observes the §13 split rather than adding timing. The threshold is on **queue + exec**, not on the backend call alone: a statement that spent two seconds waiting for a connection is slow to the caller, and a slow log that counted only `exec_us` would be silent on pool exhaustion — the commonest production stall there is. And a write reports rows **affected**, because `rows.len()` is 0 for every INSERT.

  **The mutation round found two weak tests of this slice's own, which is what it is for.** Removing the call site entirely left all six unit tests green and failed only the end-to-end — the C3-4 shape, and the reason the e2e exists. But "render params regardless of `log_params`" also passed at first, because every statement in the e2e carried its values as LITERALS and so had no bound parameters at all; and the `log_params` matrix was therefore unreachable end to end. A parameterized statement was added and the mutation then fails. Recorded because the lesson generalises: a redaction knob can only be tested by a statement that actually carries the thing being redacted.

  The e2e runs on a **SQLite** pool, so it needs no server and runs in CI, with the threshold at `Some(0)` — "log everything" — so the assertion is about wiring and never about whether a statement happened to exceed some number of milliseconds on a loaded runner. Writing it surfaced D14/C3-4 working as designed: `common::req` declares `readonly: true` by default, and on SQLite that declaration is ENFORCED, so the test's own CREATE TABLE was refused with `SQLITE_READONLY` until it declared itself honestly.

  **(bs) The §13 Prometheus endpoint — and the correction that C4b was never blocked on the admin service (M2, C4b).**

  §22.2 (br) split C4 by TRANSPORT and got the principle right, then drew one conclusion from it that the tree does not support: that Prometheus and `ferro top` *both* wait on C3-7b's admin service. **They do not.** §13 itself separates them — `ferro top` is *"live TUI over the admin service"* in so many words, while the Prometheus bullet names no transport at all, because a scrape is an HTTP GET and needs no `/proto` surface. product-vision §5 settles the rest: *"admin/metrics bind loopback/UDS by default"* is a stated default, so the endpoint does not need the cross-host authorization model that is genuinely owner-only for C3-7b. The lesson is not about metrics: **a correct principle produced a wrong conclusion, and one iteration later it came back as a premise.** The guard is the one this project already applies to the engine — check the claim against the tree, including when the claim is your own.

  **No HTTP dependency was added.** The workspace had no `hyper`, `axum`, `http` or metrics crate, and a scrape endpoint is one GET on one path. A minimal responder — request line, three response shapes, no body read, an 8 KiB bounded head and a 5-second read deadline — is a few dozen lines against a dependency tree that would sit inside a daemon holding every tenant's database credentials, and hand-rolling this class of thing is what D9's pool and the M0 codec already do. **The exposition text is built separately from the socket**, so the format an operator's dashboards depend on is tested as a pure function and the listener is tested for what a listener gets wrong. Connections are served ONE at a time on purpose: loopback is not nobody, every process on the host can reach the port, and a sequential loop with a deadline bounds the surface to one in-flight request — a scrape queueing behind a scrape is correct anyway.

  **Binding is opt-in, which is a deliberate reading of §5 rather than a contradiction of it.** *"Loopback by default"* governs WHERE the endpoint binds once asked for, not WHETHER a daemon that has only ever had one listener starts opening a port because a package was upgraded. So `FERRO_METRICS_ADDR` is unset by default, a blank value reads as unset (D14's `allow_dir` rule), a bind failure is logged at `error` and **never takes the daemon down** — the engine's work is on the UDS, and turning an observability fault into an outage is the wrong trade — and a non-loopback bind is warned about by name, since the endpoint is unauthenticated and pool names describe a topology even though no DSN or SQL is exposed.

  **The pin-cause vocabulary bug is closed, and closed so it cannot recur** — C4a recorded it as “FB-7”, an ID C1b had already used, so that label was a duplicate from the start and is retired with the fix rather than renumbered. §13 declared the pin-cause vocabulary CLOSED while `PinCause` had gained `SessionTracker` at M1-S6 — nine variants against eight labels — and the reason nobody noticed is that the vocabulary was a hand-kept list. It is now four interlocking guards: `index()` is an exhaustive match, so a new variant **does not compile** until it is given a slot; a slot outside `COUNT`, or a variant missing from `ALL`, fails a coverage test; and a test **parses §13's own sentence** out of the spec file and compares it to the enum, so document and code cannot drift in either direction. A counter that cannot be forgotten is the same idea one level down: `last_pin_cause` was a plain `Option<PinCause>` written at four sites, and is now a `PinCauseCell` whose inner field is private to `pin.rs`, so a pin site that sets a cause without counting it **fails to compile** — verified by writing one, not asserted (the C4a `Fingerprint` precedent).

  **Every cause is exported for every pool, including the zeroes**, because a series that springs into existence on its first event is one no operator can alert on: "no data" and "it has not happened yet" must not be the same observation. The `cause` label is a closed vocabulary of bare identifiers, but the `pool` label is operator-supplied, so it is escaped — the one value that could otherwise close its quotes and forge a series.

  **Deferred, and named rather than implied:** §13's remaining metrics (pool size/idle/pinned, checkout p50/p99, queue depth, the pin-duration histogram, hygiene counters, statement-cache hit rate, replica lag, the error-taxonomy counters including `indeterminate_total`, and the `boot_epoch` restart counter) are **C4b-2**. This slice is the transport plus the one family whose events the pool already raises. `ferro top` (C4d) still waits on C3-7b, which is the only part of (br)'s conclusion that survives.

  **(bt) D15 recorded: the admin service's authorization model — and M2's remaining scope re-derived from §17 and §15 rather than from the loop's own candidate list (M2, C3-7b-0).**

  C3-7a split C3-7 and named what its second half owed, including an AUTHORIZATION model with no precedent in the tree, which the loop held as owner-only and did not invent. **The owner decided it on 2026-10-02 and it is D15:** UDS-only in M2, READ verbs to any admitted peer but bounded by the redaction contract, OPERATE verbs gated on a new `FERRO_ADMIN_UIDS`, every admin verb refused on a transport that cannot attest a uid. No code changes here; the decision is recorded before the slice that implements it, as D13 was at C3-0.

  **This entry went through an adversarial review before it was pushed, and the review found the decision's default broken.** The first draft said an empty `FERRO_ADMIN_UIDS` meant "only `ferrod`'s own uid", by analogy with `FERRO_ALLOW_UIDS`. But `uid_allowed` reads an EMPTY allow-list as self-only and a NON-EMPTY one as excluding self, so the analogy produced the two worst outcomes available: in the default deployment every PHP worker IS self and would have held every OPERATE verb, and in a production deployment `ferrod`'s own uid cannot get past the accept gate, so OPERATE would have been unreachable by anyone. Empty now means OPERATE is disabled — opt-in, fail-closed, no implicit member. The same review bound READ verbs to the redaction contract, because "writes or changes state" is the wrong axis for a verb that only READS a secret (D8's contemplated credential lease would have classed as READ), and made explicit that the OPERATE gate is not snapshot confinement — D14 already lets any admitted peer `VACUUM INTO` inside `allow_dir` over the data plane.

  **Premises checked against the tree, with their consequences stated precisely.** `serve.rs` checks the peer uid at accept and does not pass it to `Session::run_with_handler`; the session does hold its own `UnixStream`, so a per-verb check can re-read it with `peercred::peer_uid` rather than needing it plumbed. `SO_PEERCRED` reports the uid that created the connection, so an fd inherited across `fork` + `setuid` keeps the original — D15 says "kernel-attested" in exactly that sense. **And `FERRO_ADDR` is not implemented anywhere:** it appears only in PHP docblocks, no code reads it as an environment variable, and `ferrod` binds no TCP listener for `/proto` (its only TCP listener is §22.2 (bs)'s opt-in metrics endpoint). §5 describes a TCP loopback fallback the engine does not serve; recorded as a found bug, outside §17's M2 bullet.

  **Re-deriving the finish line moved items in both directions.** `ferro top` is in §17's M4 list, not M2 — and the ledger already carried it as E4, so the C4 row's "C4d" was a DUPLICATE inside an M2 row, now struck; replica lag, also on the C4b-2 list, needs E3's replica routing and is M4 for the same reason. In the other direction, three M2 obligations were on no list at all: §15's `getRawPdo()` "throws with a pointer to this section" — nothing in `php/laravel` overrides it, so Illuminate's own returns; §15's acceptance names a Laravel demo app running with only the config diff; and the Doctrine ORM functional suite, which the ledger assigned to Phase C in writing ("stated, not absorbed") and §14's restated acceptance still records as not run. C3-7b itself is on M2's path through §7.6's *"Online backup API exposed via admin service"*.

  **(bu) §13's hygiene, error-taxonomy and pool-size metrics — counted where the work already happens, with every label generated or exhaustive (M2, C4b-2a).**

  C4b-1 built the scrape endpoint and one family; this adds three more, each observed at a point the engine already passes through rather than measured anew. **Hygiene** (`ferro_hygiene_total{pool,profile}`, `profile ∈ skipped_clean, targeted, full` — §13's own sentence, held by a test that parses it) is recorded at the ONE place the pool decides a recycled connection's reset, at the last point before the hand-out — after `apply_readonly`, so a connection evicted by a failed cleanup OR a failed arming is not counted. The decision is computed once, before the guard, so the value that drives the cleanup is the value that is counted; the guard is logically unchanged (`tainted` always yields `Full`). **`skipped_clean` means no reset PROFILE ran** — a defensive `ROLLBACK` of a still-open transaction may still have run, but that is transaction cleanup the tx authority owns, not session hygiene. **It reads 0 on every backend that exists**, and the e2e asserts exactly that: PostgreSQL and SQLite report `Targeted` for a clean connection and MySQL `Full`, so the skip — §7.2's deferred R2/B7 optimisation — is unreachable until a backend can prove a connection clean; a fake-backend test makes it reachable and counts it exactly.

  **Errors** (`ferro_errors_total{code,branch}`, `ferro_indeterminate_total`, `ferro_errors_unregistered_total`) are counted where every error `END` is BUILT — `session::supervisor::build_terminal_frame` — not where errors are classified: `classify_fate`'s call sites are spread across `services/sql.rs` and it never sees session-level errors (a codec fault, an unknown flag bit, an unrouted service, a peercred denial). There were TWO builders, so `session::error` now delegates. The counters measure terminals PRODUCED, not delivered: a terminal for a client that has already disconnected is built, counted, then dropped by the closed channel. **The label vocabulary is generated, not kept:** `ferro-proto`'s build script now also emits `errc::ALL` and `branch::ALL` from `registry.lock.json` (itself generated from `/proto/errors.toml`; no new ids, no wire change, no PHP change — the PHP constants file needs no `ALL` table). `indeterminate_total` follows the branch on the wire, not the code's usual branch. A terminal carrying an unregistered code is counted separately: nonzero there is a hand-written protocol constant, which charter rule 2 calls a defect. **Pool size** is three gauges (`max`, `in_use`, `idle`); `in_use` counts held permits, so a connection being dialled or pinged by the liveness reaper is in use too — capacity no checkout can have at that instant.

  **The adversarial review is why the tests can be trusted, and it found the first version's tests could not.** Five findings, every one reproduced before being fixed. **(1)** The e2e exercised the SESSION terminal path twice — its "dispatch" error was a misnomer, since an unrouted service is answered by `session::error` too — and the HANDLER path, where every SQL error and every `Indeterminate` is produced, never: a second END builder there passed all 163 lib tests and the e2e. The e2e now drives a syntax error and a timed-out autocommit write through a real EXEC, asserting the write comes back `Indeterminate` and moves `ferro_indeterminate_total`. **(2)** The "one END construction site" guard was too narrow — TWICE. v1 matched one exact spelling, and the slice's own mutation round slipped a fully qualified path past it; v2 matched `flags:` field initialisers, and the reviewer built a frame with `header.flags |= flags::END`. v3 does not care how the flag is applied, only that it is NAMED: any use of `flags::END` or import of a bare `END` in production code outside `build_terminal_frame` fails it, with a test of the matcher over eight spellings it must see and six it must not. **(3)** No test counted hygiene EXACTLY: counting on the fresh-dial exit, or before the cleanup so evicted connections counted, both passed. A new `ferro-pool` suite on the fake backend owns its pool outright and pins every cell — fresh dial 0, clean recycle `targeted`, tainted `full`, no-profile `skipped_clean`, ROLLBACK-only `skipped_clean`, evicted 0. **(4)** The record sat before `apply_readonly`, whose failure evicts, so one checkout could count twice; moved. **(5)** Prose: "29 call sites", "from errors.toml", "sent", and an `in_use` that omitted the reaper — all corrected. All five surviving mutations now fail.

  **Deferred to C4b-2b, named:** the pinned-connection gauge, the checkout-latency and pin-duration histograms, and queue depth. **Structurally not M2, to be stated in §13 at C4b-2b:** statement-cache hit rate (the engine has no statement cache; `tokio-postgres`'s typeinfo cache is not one — *[corrected at (bv): the MySQL driver keeps its own per-connection LRU, with no counter]*), replica lag (needs E3's routing — M4), and a restart COUNTER (a process cannot count its own restarts without a stats file, which product-vision §5 forbids; it is `changes(ferro_boot_epoch[...])` in PromQL).

  **(bv) The rest of §13's Prometheus list — pinned, queue depth, checkout and pin-duration histograms — and §13 amended to NAME the three it cannot honestly export in M2 (M2, C4b-2b).**

  Four families: `ferro_pool_pinned_connections`, `ferro_pool_waiting_checkouts` (§13's "queue depth"), `ferro_checkout_duration_seconds` (§13's "checkout p50/p99", computed by Prometheus from the buckets) and `ferro_pin_duration_seconds`, all per pool. **Each is observed where the engine already passes, and each balance is held by construction rather than by remembering its sites.** The pin is now a `PinSlot` private to `pin.rs` — the only way to pin or release — which holds its pool's metrics and releases in its OWN `Drop`, so every pin that starts ends exactly once whether it ends at COMMIT, at ROLLBACK, or with the checkout dropped mid-transaction; a re-pin of an already-pinned checkout keeps its start time and counts once. Queue depth is an RAII guard around the permit wait, because that wait ends three ways — a permit, a timeout, or the caller dropping the future (a CANCEL, a session teardown) — and only `Drop` sees the third. Checkout latency is observed in `Checkout::new`, the one constructor both exits return through, with the `queue_us` the EXEC reply already carries.

  **The histogram is hand-rolled, like the endpoint** (§22.2 (bs)): one atomic bucket array plus an atomic sum, recorded in microseconds — the unit `queue_us` is already in — and rendered in seconds by integer arithmetic, so a bound never prints as `0.000024999`. Buckets are stored non-cumulatively (one `fetch_add` per observation) and accumulated at render, and `_count` is the `+Inf` bucket of that same read — never a separate counter. **The bounds are a decision, recorded:** checkout from 25 µs to 30 s — dense below a millisecond because §16 sets p50 < 60 µs and an operator must see which side of it the pool sits, and running PAST the default 5 s `checkout_timeout` because that bounds the permit wait, the dial and each recycle cleanup SEPARATELY, so a successful checkout can exceed it (only successful checkouts are observed; a failed one is an error terminal, which `ferro_errors_total` counts); pin duration from 1 ms to 300 s — the 60 s bound is `ferrod`'s default `max_tx` (§6), and 120 s and 300 s are headroom for an operator who raised it — because its question is which transactions hold connections long enough to starve a pool.

  **"Pinned" means a connection held by an explicit transaction** (`PinState::PinnedTx`) — the transaction-mode-pooling sense, the one that starves a pool. A tainted connection is not pinned; it returns to the pool and is the hygiene family's business.

  **§13 now names what it cannot export.** Statement-cache hit rate: §7.3's engine-owned cache is not built — PostgreSQL and SQLite statements are not cached by the engine, and the MySQL family's driver-internal `COM_STMT_PREPARE` LRU exposes no hit/miss counter (exporting it needs a fork accessor) — so there is no rate until §7.3 is built, and §7.3 and §16 now say so. Replica lag: no replicas until §7.5's routing, which is M4. A restart counter: a process cannot count its own restarts without a stats file, which product-vision §5 forbids, so restarts are derived where observed, as `changes(ferro_boot_epoch[...])`. Exporting each as a constant zero would have been a lie an operator could alert on.

  **A daemon-level test told the truth about a path by failing to fail.** The e2e drives a client disconnecting mid-transaction, expecting that to exercise the `Drop` release. Deleting the `Drop` release left it GREEN — because session teardown does not drop the pinned checkout; the tx actor runs `rollback_tx()` under `tx_teardown_timeout`, so the pin ends at the ROLLBACK site. `Drop` is the backstop for a teardown ROLLBACK that fails or times out, which the `ferro-pool` suite proves exactly; the e2e keeps the disconnect case, now described correctly, because it covers the teardown path. Five mutations — `Drop` not releasing, a re-pin counting twice, release not observing, queue depth by increment/decrement instead of RAII (the abandoned-wait path leaks), and latency observed on one exit only — each fails exactly the tests that claim it.

  **The adversarial review found eight things, all reproduced, and four of them were in the tests.** **(F1)** "The engine has no statement cache" was false for MySQL: `mysql_async`'s `prep()` keeps a per-connection 32-entry LRU, emptied by every `COM_RESET_CONNECTION`. Meanwhile §7.3 and §16 still promised the opposite of what §13 now said. All three agree now, and the cross-reference moved from §11 to §7.3, whose namespaced prepares those are. **(F2)** No test checked a VALUE or a UNIT. Three mutations survived: observing a constant 0 for checkout latency, the same for pin duration, and rendering raw microseconds, which is a 10⁶× error on every bound. Four tests close them:
  - a pool test asserts the checkout sum equals the `queue_us` each checkout reported, with one checkout made to wait 20 ms so a constant cannot match by accident;
  - another asserts a pin held for 30 ms records at least 30 ms;
  - a unit test pins the exact rendered lines;
  - the e2e asserts that every `le` label is its bound in seconds.

  **(F3)** A BEGIN that FAILED still started a pin if one line moved, and nothing noticed. That is D13's `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` → `SQLITE_BUSY` path, and the result is a phantom pinned connection. It is now tested. **(F4)** `_count` and `+Inf` disagreed in roughly three scrapes in four under four observing threads, because the count was its own atomic, read at a different instant. It is now the `+Inf` bucket of the same pass, so the exposition invariant holds by construction. A concurrent test fails when the count is taken from a second pass. **(F5)** The release decremented the gauge before observing the duration, so a scrape could see a pin end with no duration recorded; a 30 ms preemption reproduced it. The order is reversed under Release/Acquire, and the scrape reads gauges before histograms. The same preemption injected under the correct order passes, which shows the fix is the ordering and not luck. **(F6)** The pin bounds' rationale cited a 300 s default `max_tx`; the default is 60 s. **(F7)** "Capped at `checkout_timeout`, past which a checkout has failed" was false: a checkout succeeded after 243 ms against a 150 ms timeout. The tail now runs to 30 s. **(F8)** "By construction" was overstated: `self.pin = PinSlot::default()` compiled and leaked a pin. `PinSlot` now holds the metrics, releases in its own `Drop`, and has no `Default`. That exact overwrite is now correct, and run as a positive control it passes. Every mutation the review left standing now fails the test that claims it.

  **(bw) Reconnection: the PDO shim owns the client, the closure dials it, and four silent defects that could not have been found without Illuminate's own `DatabaseManager` (M2, C1e-2).** C1e-2 was scheduled as a documentation fix: §15 said `getRawPdo()` "throws", nothing overrode it, and Illuminate's `DatabaseManager` calls it on every reconnect (`refreshPdoConnections()` and the reconnector closure, verified in v11.51.0), so the clause was simply wrong. Proving that the un-overridden method WORKS is what found the real defects — and only a REAL `DatabaseManager` could, because a connection built straight from the resolver has no reconnector. **Illuminate reconnects by replacing the PDO, never the connection:** it builds a whole fresh connection and transplants that connection's `getRawPdo()` into the one the application holds. The tier kept its Ferro client in a property on the connection AND handed the shim its own reference, so after a transplant every BEGIN/COMMIT/ROLLBACK (which `ManagesTransactions` routes through `getPdo()`) moved to a NEW client while every statement stayed on the OLD one. **Measured against a live engine, every one silent:**
  - after `DB::reconnect()`, `transaction(fn () => insert; throw)` rolled back an EMPTY transaction while the insert autocommitted — **a row the application was told was discarded was committed**;
  - after `DB::disconnect()` inside a transaction (Illuminate's `setPdo(null)`, which also zeroes its counter), the old client still held that transaction open, so the next ordinary insert ran inside a transaction nothing would ever commit — **the write was lost**;
  - on SQLite, where Illuminate reads a generated key through `getPdo()->lastInsertId()`, the sticky key (C3-6b) was read through a closure bound to the connection that BUILT the shim — after a reconnect, the throwaway one the manager discards — so **every `insertGetId()`, and therefore every `Model::create()`, threw after a reconnect**.

  **The fix is ownership, and then lifetime.** The shim owns the client and the sticky key (PDO keeps `lastInsertId` on the HANDLE, so a fresh handle starts with none); the connection keeps no client at all, and every execution path resolves it through `getPdo()` INSIDE its `run()` callback, because Illuminate's lost-connection retry reconnects and then re-invokes the SAME callback — one that captured the client before `run()` re-sent on the session that had just failed (mutation-proven). The first version still captured a client DIALLED AT CONSTRUCTION inside the PDO closure, and **the adversarial review measured what that costs**, because Illuminate passes the closure AROUND. `refreshPdoConnections()` transplants it out of a throwaway connection that sits in a reference cycle with its grammar, so the throwaway kept the superseded client — and its open transaction — alive after `DB::disconnect()` until the cycle collector ran: a same-key insert blocked for the engine's full 10 s idle-in-transaction deadline (0 s without a prior reconnect), and 19 of 20 superseded clients survived 20 reconnects. Worse, after `DB::purge()` the same closure is RESOLVED BY TWO CONNECTION OBJECTS, which then shared one session: one connection's `rollBack()` discarded the other's autocommitted write, where PDO keeps it. **The closure now DIALS when resolved, once per resolution** — the semantics of the closure `ConnectionFactory::createPdoResolver()` hands a stock connection — so it owns nothing until it runs and each run owns its own session. The config is still validated at `DB::connection()`; only the dial moved.

  **The lost-connection guard decides by TYPE, and the fix arms the path it guards.** `tryAgainIfCausedByLostConnection()` re-runs a statement whose error looks like a lost connection, by MESSAGE SUBSTRING. For a write whose fate is unknown that is exactly the transparent retry charter rule 3 and §19.3 forbid, and whether it fires was an accident of wording: the engine's link-loss text is a lower-case "connection lost during …", which misses Illuminate's `'Connection lost'` by ONE LETTER; the engine also forwards backend messages verbatim; and the client's own dial failure DOES match (`'No such file or directory'`). Before this slice the retry also landed on the old, failed client; it now lands on a fresh one. So `causedByLostConnection()` walks the exception chain and the FIRST Ferro-relevant link decides: a tier `ConnectFailed` (the client could not be DIALLED — nothing was sent, and reachable inside `run()` only because dialling is now lazy) → yes, the same reconnect-and-retry a PDO connect failure gets; a `RetryableException` carrying `ERR_CONNECTION_LOST` (the engine itself classified the loss as known-fate) → yes, whatever its text; any other `RetryableException` → the stock detector; ANY OTHER Ferro failure — `Indeterminate`, a raw `ConnectionLostException`/`TransportException` that `cursor()`'s stream-open path can surface unclassified, `NonRetryable` — → no, whatever its text. The first version refused only `IndeterminateException`, and the review showed the raw types would still have been retried on matching text. `causedByConcurrencyError()` gets the same type rule for `Indeterminate`, because a "yes" there makes `DB::transaction(attempts:)` re-run the WHOLE transaction — from `handleCommitTransactionException()` too, i.e. after a COMMIT whose reply was lost. **The cost, stated:** Illuminate's PDO-style retry of a SELECT lost mid-flight no longer happens, because every statement on this tier is declared a write (§22.2 (ac)) and the client reports such a loss `Indeterminate`.

  **Recorded, NOT fixed here — next slice (C1e-3): after a `ferrod` restart a long-lived connection never recovers by itself.** Measured: every statement fails `Indeterminate` — `autocommit write lost mid-flight … write failed after 0 of 47 bytes` — so the guard (correctly, by type) refuses the reconnect, and the client, built with `RetryPolicy::none()`, never re-dials on its own; Octane and queue workers stay broken until `DB::reconnect()` or a restart. The root cause is in the CLIENT, not this tier: `FateClassifier::classifyLoss` treats a request that failed while still being WRITTEN as indeterminate, although an incompletely-written frame cannot have been executed — the same `sent` axis `fate.rs` already applies engine-side, where a never-transmitted statement is `Retryable`. Once the client says so, this tier's guard already answers yes to it.

  **Four tests of this slice were found unable to fail by its own mutation round, and why is worth keeping.** Three asserted INSIDE a `transaction()` closure and threw `\RuntimeException` to force the rollback — and PHPUnit's `AssertionFailedError` extends `PHPUnit\Framework\Exception extends \RuntimeException`, so the test's own `catch` swallowed every assertion that FAILED inside the closure. A mutation pinning `cursor()` to a stale client failed its assertion and the test passed; tracing it showed the stale client was correctly NOT seeing the transaction's row — the claim was right and the test could not have said otherwise. They throw a dedicated `RollbackSignal` now, and the same mutation fails them. Separately, the guard's type-matrix rows choose each row's text AGAINST its expected answer — a refused failure whose text matches Illuminate's substrings, an accepted one whose text matches none — because a row whose text agrees with its answer passes whether or not the type rule exists (the first dial-failure row did exactly that).

  **Static analysis was right to object, and the boundary now says why.** Illuminate DOCUMENTS `getPdo()` as `\PDO`, but the method has no native return type and returns whatever was stored — on this tier, the shim. PHPStan believed the docblock and called the runtime `instanceof` impossible; the narrowing happens at an explicit `mixed` boundary that states the docblock is wrong for this tier, rather than behind a suppression. **The framework's own suite is unchanged by the slice** on all three local columns — SQLite 575/579, PostgreSQL `pgsql` alias 605/607, `ferro-pgsql` 570/579, identical outcome counts and failure sets to the recorded ones.

  **(bx) A request that never left the client is `Retryable` — §19.3's "not-yet-dispatched" rule, applied to the client's half of the link — and a failed session poisons itself so that is TRUE (M2, C1e-3).** C1e-2's review measured what the gap cost: after a `ferrod` restart, every statement on a long-lived Laravel connection failed `Indeterminate` with the client's own text *"autocommit write lost mid-flight … write failed after 0 of 47 bytes"*. **Zero bytes had been written**, yet the fate was reported unknown, so every layer above correctly refused to retry it, and an Octane or queue worker stayed broken until restarted. §19.3 already had the rule. Its client bullets speak of requests *in flight*, its COMMIT carve-out of a COMMIT frame *sent*, and its engine-side mirror says "not-yet-dispatched → `Retryable`". The client applied the in-flight rule to a request that never got out.

  **The fact that decides is which PHASE failed, and only the session knows it.** `Session::writeFrame` now raises `TransportException::requestNotSent()` when the write fails. The transport contract (`TransportInterface::writeAll`, now written down) says a write throws only when its bytes were NOT all written. `FateClassifier::classifyLoss` gained a `sent` axis, checked FIRST and decisive for every op kind, the COMMIT included: an unsent request is `Retryable` `ERR_CONNECTION_LOST`. A failure while READING the reply keeps every fate it had.

  **"An unsent request cannot have executed" was not yet true, and making it true is half the slice.** The session never marked itself dead after a transport failure. With `RetryPolicy::none()`, which both drop-in tiers use, the next request wrote to the same socket. After a write TIMEOUT against a live peer, that request's bytes would have COMPLETED the earlier partial frame, and the engine would have decoded a request stitched from two. After a read failure, a later request could read the earlier one's late reply. So the first transport failure now closes the socket (the engine sees EOF and discards any partial frame), and every later frame is refused BEFORE a byte is written, which is itself correctly `requestNotSent`.

  **Two more paths were brought under the rule:**
  - The stream-open path (`stream()`/`streamRaw()`) was the one request path with no `classifyLoss` at all. It surfaced raw `TransportException`/`ConnectionLostException` — review finding F6 from C1e-2, fixed at its source — and is now classified like every buffered path, an in-transaction stream as `TxStatement`.
  - `Retryable` still licenses only the CALLER: `mayRetry` never re-issues a write. So the client's own behaviour is unchanged, and the decision moves to the layer that owns it. In the Laravel tier that layer is C1e-2's type guard, which answers yes to `Retryable` `ERR_CONNECTION_LOST`. **Measured live: the first write after a `ferrod` restart is now reconnected, re-run and applied exactly once, where it used to fail forever.** The Doctrine tier's existing contract already says a never-transmitted statement is `RetryableDriverException`; the client now actually produces that for the client half of the link.

  **Mutations, each failing what claims it:**
  - the session not marking a write failure unsent fails the client tests AND the live Laravel restart test, which is the defect reproduced;
  - removing the poisoning fails the poisoned-session test;
  - ignoring the `sent` axis fails six rows;
  - leaving the stream open unclassified fails both stream tests.

  **The adversarial review of (bx) found two of its own claims false, and the fix is the rest of this entry.** **(F5)** "Every later frame is refused as not-sent" was false while a STREAM was open: the stream guard was checked before the poisoned check and nothing cleared it, so every later request failed `ProtocolException` ("a stream is open on this session"), which no layer can recognise as a dead session — reproduced live with a Laravel `cursor()` across a `ferrod` restart, i.e. exactly the case the incompatibilities page had just marked FIXED. Poisoning now clears the stream guard (a closed socket has no frames left to interleave with), which also makes a later `abandonStream()` a no-op instead of a throw out of the `finally` that is carrying the real error. **(F2)** "Not sent" was set on CONTROL frames too — WINDOW_UPDATE, CANCEL, GOODBYE, PING — including a WINDOW_UPDATE that follows rows the engine has already produced; only REQUEST frames (HELLO, a buffered request, a stream open) carry the flag now. **(F4)** `Retryable` could not be ACTED on by a write-only caller: the client's reconnect loop ran only on a read RETRY, so a queue worker on the native client — or a Doctrine connection — was told "not sent" forever. A session a failure already closed is now REPLACED before the next request (`Connection::requestSession`) — not a retry of anything, since the request about to go out has been sent nowhere — and never while a transaction is open, whose session IS the transaction. `Ferro::connect` always builds a reconnect loop, so this reaches both drop-in tiers. **(F1)** one call site passed the sent flag positionally into `$epochChanged`; every site uses the named argument now. **(F3)** `stream()`, the in-transaction stream (`TxStatement`) and the closure-form COMMIT had no test; each has one.

  **Two independent recovery paths, and the live test proves the pair.** The live mid-cursor test passes with EITHER F5's guard-clearing (Illuminate's type-guarded retry then reconnects) OR F4's replace-before-request; mutating BOTH fails it with the review's exact `ProtocolException`, and the unit tests isolate each. Of 12 unit-level mutations (4 for the slice, 8 for the review's fixes) 11 fail the test that claims them; the twelfth survived because it exposed a DEAD early return in `abandonStream` (poisoning had already cleared the guard it tested), which was removed rather than kept. **Two qualifications, stated:** the first write after a restart can still surface `Indeterminate` over TCP or during an engine drain, when the kernel accepts it — a write that may have been delivered is correctly unknown, and the NEXT statement recovers; and the Doctrine tier's streamed statements now surface a lost OPEN as `RetryableDriverException`/`IndeterminateWriteException` rather than a generic `DriverException`, as its documented table already said they should. Recorded, not fixed: a codec failure on a header does not poison the session (FB-10; fails safe through the `request_id` echo, not fast).

  **(by) The DBAL `^3.8` bridge is a SECOND `driverClass` over ONE shared core, because PHP checks an SPI's signatures when the class is DECLARED, not when a method is called (M2, C5).** D2 deferred the bridge to M2; the question was how much of the driver could be shared. DBAL 3 and DBAL 4 name the same interfaces with incompatible shapes: DBAL 3 connects to learn the server version before choosing a platform ONLY for a driver implementing `VersionAwarePlatformDriver` — for any other driver it calls `getDatabasePlatform()` with no version and no connection, i.e. it would have Ferro choose a dialect without even knowing the family — and DBAL 4 deleted that interface; DBAL 3's version detection also asks only through `ServerInfoAwareConnection`, likewise deleted; and `quote($value, $type)`/`quote(string)`, `lastInsertId($name = null)`/`lastInsertId()` and the `bool`/`void` transaction returns cannot all be satisfied by one class. A class naming a missing interface, or declaring an incompatible method, cannot be declared — so a single adaptive class is not available, and naming the wrong major's class fails when `DriverManager::getConnection()` loads it (a fatal `Declaration … must be compatible` for the DBAL 4 class under DBAL 3; a catchable `Interface "Doctrine\DBAL\VersionAwarePlatformDriver" not found` for the DBAL 3 class under DBAL 4), before any connection, rather than misbehaving. **The split is by signature, not by behaviour:** `AbstractConnection` holds every method both majors accept (the fate declaration, pinned-tx routing, streaming, the isolation refusal, the nil-version decision) and protected helpers for the rest; `Connection` (DBAL 4) and `Dbal3\Connection` add only the signature-bound methods and forward, so the behaviour cannot drift between majors without a diff to the shared file. Likewise `AbstractDriver` (handshake, family, exception converter), `CanonicalBinder`/`BindKind` (what a bind kind does to a value) under per-major `ParameterBinder`s (DBAL 4's enum `match` stays exhaustive with no `default`; DBAL 3's int constants get a REFUSING `default`, keeping the property that an unknown type is never funnelled into the string path), `IsolationStatement` (moved off the DBAL 4 wrapper, which cannot even be declared under DBAL 3 — leaving it there would have made the isolation refusal FATAL on DBAL 3 instead of loud) and `PlatformVersion::delegateFor` (the stock abstract drivers are shared; only how a platform is ASKED of them differs). **Decisions, each with its reason:** DBAL 3's `lastInsertId()` THROWS where its SPI docblock allows `false`, because Doctrine ORM 2's `IdentityGenerator` is `(int) $conn->lastInsertId($seq)` and `false` would become the primary key `0`; the sequence name is accepted and not used on any family (on PostgreSQL its only use would be a follow-up `currval()`: OUTSIDE a transaction, on another pooled connection — the cross-connection hazard the DBAL 4 driver already refuses `lastval()` for; INSIDE one, which is where Doctrine ORM 2's identity generator runs, it would be CORRECT on the pinned connection, and that is recorded for the ORM-suite slice rather than widened into this one, for both majors at once); the version-less `getDatabasePlatform()` REFUSES where the stock DBAL 3 drivers answer their family's oldest platform (before a connect the driver does not know even the family); `getSchemaManager()` — on DBAL 3's DEFAULT path, via `LegacySchemaManagerFactory` — answers `$platform->createSchemaManager()`, the stock manager for whatever platform Doctrine chose; `bindParam()` and `execute($params)` are implemented, deprecated as they are. **Measured, not assumed:** (1) the package's live suite is SHARED — every live test drives the installed major's `driverClass` — and under 3.10.6 it passes, after test-side adaptations for real API differences (DBAL 3's `Result` has no `getColumnName()`; its wrapper's `getServerVersion()` is private; its platform detection CONVERTS a driver exception, so `ServerVersionUnavailable` arrives as the `previous` of a stock `DriverException` — still loud, still naming the pool; DBAL 3 nests with savepoints only when told to); (2) PHPStan level 9 runs against BOTH majors, and `COMPOSER=composer.dbal3.json` is LOAD-BEARING — without it PHPStan builds its locator from `composer.json` and silently analyses the DBAL 3 lane against DBAL 4's types (17 bogus errors); (3) the `^3.8` FLOOR is real: CI downgrades the lane to 3.8.0 and re-runs PHPStan and `tests/Dbal3` there, and that analysis was shown to see 3.8.0's types (a probe instantiating `MySQL84Platform`, which 3.8.0 lacks, is reported not found under the floor and clean under 3.10.6). The floor run's first result was 6 errors — all in this slice's OWN test fixtures, which named platform classes newer than 3.8.0; they now assert the OLDEST class that still discriminates (the version-less answer is an instance of none of them). (4) DBAL 3's wrapper is in a reference cycle with itself (`ExpressionBuilder` holds the connection), so a dropped connection keeps its Ferro session until the cycle collector runs — measured with a `WeakReference` on the client (alive after `unset()` on 3.10.6, gone on 4.4.4). Upstream behaviour, shared by PDO under DBAL 3, so it is not a driver change; but it held every live test's session open into `ferrod`'s graceful drain, ~5 s each, until the shared live base class collected cycles before stopping the daemon (one version test 10.7 s → 0.85 s). Writing the README section found two of its sections STALE: "Known gaps" listed three defects fixed in M1 and "Streaming" said MySQL buffers; both corrected. **The adversarial review found the slice's headline measurement HOLLOW, and three defects behind it** — 12 findings, every one reproduced, journal and probes kept. **The claim "the live suite passes in full under 3.10.6" had been measured with every MySQL-gated test SKIPPED** (no MySQL in the container), and two of those tests failed under DBAL 3 the moment they ran: DBAL 3.8–3.10 default `nestTransactionsWithSavepoints` to FALSE, so a nested BEGIN emits no SAVEPOINT at all (upstream behaviour for every driver; the test opts in under DBAL 3, and the §14 bullet says applications must), and a second test still called DBAL 4's `Result::getColumnName()`.
