The container then got a MariaDB 10.11 from the Ubuntu archive on CI's port with the testkit's own init SQL, and both lanes now run with NO skips (MariaDB 10.11, not CI's MySQL 8.4 — a regression aid, not a recorded number). **F3: DBAL 3's wrapper never converts a driver exception from `beginTransaction()`/`commit()`/`rollBack()`** (DBAL 4's converts all three), so a PostgreSQL `40001` at COMMIT — the shape SSI produces by design — reached a DBAL 3 application as a bare driver exception instead of a retryable `DeadlockException`, and an indeterminate COMMIT never as `IndeterminateWriteException`; the DBAL 3 connection now converts its own, legal under DBAL 3's `@throws Driver\Exception` because DBAL 3's `DriverException` implements it, and the converter returns an already-converted exception unchanged, since `transactional()` hands it back for the rollback decision. A live test drives both COMMIT-time failures (a DEFERRED unique constraint, deterministic on one connection; SERIALIZABLE write skew) on both majors, imperatively and through `transactional()`. **Fixing F3 found two more, one upstream and one ours.** DBAL from 3.9.4 (the release that fixed `transactional()` for auto-rolled-back transactions) resets the nesting level in `commit()`'s `finally` even when the COMMIT fails, and `transactional()` then rolls back unless the failure is one of five listed classes — so its own rollback throws "There is no active transaction." and PHP chains the real failure UNDER it: **through `transactional()`, an indeterminate COMMIT reaches the caller masked, on both majors**. The safe half holds (nothing retryable reaches the top); the class does not. The wrapper throws before calling the driver, so no driver can fix it; it is pinned by a tripwire on each lane and filed with its one-line upstream remedy (`docs/followups/2026-10-02-transactional-masks-a-failed-commit.md`). And on the `^3.8` FLOOR, where `transactional()` rolls back THROUGH the driver from a `catch`, the client's "rollBack() with no open transaction" REPLACED the commit's exception outright — even a retryable `40001` was lost (measured on 3.8.0; `pdo_pgsql` does the same). A rollback after a COMMIT that failed and ended the transaction is now a no-op in the shared core (the commit's exception already reports the fate), while any other rollback with no transaction still throws — the control. **F7: DBAL 3's `quote($value, BINARY)` was a silent corruption** — both stock PostgreSQL drivers escape it as `bytea`, so `"\\x41"` quoted as text stored ONE byte where `pdo_pgsql` stores four; binary types are refused now, pointing at the bound parameter. **F8, pre-existing on DBAL 4 since M1-S8b: the isolation `wrapperClass` recognised its driver connection by `instanceof` on the OUTERMOST one, which any driver middleware replaces** — and DoctrineBundle installs one on every connection by default (`IdleConnectionMiddleware`; logging and profiling under `kernel.debug`), so in a stock Symfony app `setTransactionIsolation()` was refused with advice to configure the wrapper already configured. It now finds the connection through `getNativeConnection()`, which every middleware base class forwards, via a `WeakMap` keyed by the Ferro client with `WeakReference` values (a strong value would pin its own key). **F11: DoctrineBundle 2's `ConnectionFactory` asks the driver for a platform before anything connects** — version-less on DBAL 3, with `serverVersion ?? ''` on DBAL 4 — whenever `charset` is unset or `dbname_suffix` is set, only to default the charset; both drivers refuse (no family is known yet), so both messages now name the fix (`charset`, inert for Ferro; no `dbname_suffix`), and the documented Symfony configuration carries it. The rest were test gaps and prose: three surviving mutations in the DBAL 3 statement (merge-vs-replace, the unpinned sort, a dead `unset`, now removed), the handshake-family branch tested only by MySQL-gated live tests, the isolation refusal on `runPrepared()` covered by no test anywhere (pre-existing), and §14's "holds on both majors" sentence false for five bullets, two of which (the streaming bound, the `int2vector` schema-manager block) had been stale since M1-S9. Recorded, not built: a connect-time `TransportException` reaches the caller unconverted on both majors (FB-11), and the documented `'serverVersion' => '17.10'` throws `BackendFamilyUnknown` — the examples now spell the PostgreSQL banner. **Not established:** the upstream doctrine/dbal 3.x functional suite has not been run through the bridge (the M1-S8b acceptance shape, with a SQLite control column) — the next slice — and neither has Doctrine ORM 2.

  **(bz) Upstream doctrine/dbal 3.10.6's functional subset runs through the DBAL 3 bridge — and comparing SKIP sets, not only failures, found a defect both majors had carried since M1-S8b (M2, C5b).** `testkit/dbal-suite.sh` derives the DBAL major from the pinned tag and selects everything major-specific from it: the driver package's `vendor-dbal3/` tree, a 3.10.6 `TestUtil` (its public surface differs from 4.4.4's — `generateResultSetQuery(rows, platform)` takes two parameters — so its body is transcribed byte for byte from the pinned clone) and `driverClass = Ferro\DBAL\Dbal3\Driver`; the 11-entry allowlist is unchanged. Recorded in `docs/dbal-suite/2026-10-02-c5b-dbal3-results.md` with the A5 protocol (two runs per family, contact assertion and reset lines verified). **The runner's PHPUnit is 11, not the 9.6 the 3.10.6 tree pins, and that reverses test-METHOD order** (PHPUnit 11 runs inherited methods first): two tests that fail through Ferro AND the stock-driver control — SQLite's `testListForeignKeysFromExistingDatabase`, PostgreSQL's `testDropWithAutoincrement`, whose own comment says it must run first — each pass alone and fail after the inherited test they conflict with, so they are the HARNESS's, not Ferro's and not upstream's. Every non-pass is triaged and none is a driver defect. DBAL 3 adds two refusals that 4.4.4's tree has no test for — `testDispatchEventWhenDatabasePlatformIsExplicitlyPassed` (a version-less platform request, refused before a connect AND after one: by then the family is known, but the stock answer, the family's OLDEST platform, is not the one the connection uses, so it is a POLICY of not handing out a second dialect, (by) F11) and SQLite's `testCreateAndDropDatabase` (a database FILE at a client-chosen path, structurally unsatisfiable under D8/D14) — **and one real incompatibility (review F1): `testGetSearchPath`.** DBAL 3's PostgreSQL schema manager takes the CURRENT schema from `SHOW search_path` with `"$user"` replaced by the connection's `user` PARAMETER, which a Ferro connection does not have; when the role owns a schema of its own name, DBAL 3 picks the next schema in the path, names the role's tables qualified and `public`'s bare, and the comparator plans a CREATE plus a DROP — a destructive migration, measured. DBAL 4 asks the server (`SELECT current_schema()`). The workaround — DBAL's `user` param set to the backend role, inert for Ferro's own connection, or no `"$user"` in the pool's `search_path` — is documented on the incompatibilities page, and `SearchPathLiveTest` pins all three cells against PostgreSQL's own `current_schema()`.

  **The finding.** `getServerVersion()` returned the backend's VERBATIM banner — M1-S8b put normalisation inside platform selection (D-S8b-1) and nowhere else — and upstream's own `PostgreSQLSchemaManagerTest::testListTableColumnsOidConflictWithNonTableObject` gates itself on `version_compare($conn->getServerVersion(), '12.0', '<')`, which PHP answers `true` for `PostgreSQL 17.10 (…)` (measured; `pdo_pgsql`'s `17.10 (…)` answers `false`). So the test SKIPPED under Ferro while it RAN under `pdo_pgsql`, on both majors — A5's recorded PostgreSQL column included. **A skip is not a failure, so a triage that classifies every NON-PASS cannot see it**: A5's could not, and this slice's first pass did not; it surfaced only when the Ferro column's skip set was diffed against a `pdo_pgsql` control's. It is (ao)'s defect — found in the Laravel tier at C2c and fixed there — still live in the sibling tier, which had normalised the version it CONSUMED and not the one it ANSWERED. Fixed: `getServerVersion()` returns `PlatformVersion::normalise()`'s form on both the advertised and the resolved path (PostgreSQL: product name stripped; MySQL family byte-identical, since `-MariaDB` is what selects the dialect). After the fix, measured against PostgreSQL 16 on BOTH majors and compared as SETS by a committed script (`testkit/dbal/compare-columns.php`, reports in `docs/dbal-suite/2026-10-02-c5b-skip-sets/`), the Ferro column's skip set is IDENTICAL to the `pdo_pgsql` control's, the only failure the two share is the harness's test-order artifact, and every Ferro-only failure is in the triage; against MariaDB 10.11 no test the `pdo_mysql` control runs is skipped under Ferro. The runner gained server-family controls for it (`FERRO_DBAL_CONTROL=1` with the family's DSN: a HARNESS column may hold the credentials the product's PHP never does, as the Laravel runner's `stock-pgsql` has since C2e). **Two costs, stated (review F6):** code that read the product name out of `getServerVersion()` no longer finds it; and the new string no longer works as DBAL's `serverVersion` parameter, since a platform requested before any connection can learn the family only from that string (`17.10 (…)` → `BackendFamilyUnknown`, loud; `PostgreSQL 17.10` still works, as §14 documents). **The rule this leaves for every future suite column: compare the SKIP set against a control as well as the failure set; a test that runs under the stock driver and skips under Ferro is a defect until shown otherwise.** Running the test exposed (ca).

  **(ca) `I64 → oid`, value-gated to `0..=u32::MAX` — the bind mirror of M1-S8a's `oid → I64` read (M2, C5b).** The test (bz) un-skipped reads `pg_class.oid` — which arrives as an `I64` — and binds it straight back, first into `UPDATE pg_attribute SET attrelid = ? WHERE attrelid = ?` (`attrelid` is an `oid`); it failed `canonical I64 cannot bind to PG type oid` on both majors. An OID could be read and not written back: readable-but-not-bindable, the asymmetry (g) closed for domains. The bind writes PostgreSQL's native 4-byte unsigned binary form. **Stricter than `pdo_pgsql`, on (an)'s grounds:** PostgreSQL's `oidin` accepts a NEGATIVE integer and silently WRAPS it (measured: `'-1'::oid` is `4294967295`), so `pdo_pgsql`'s text bind of `-1` lands on a different object id than the one written; refused pre-send instead, together with anything past `u32::MAX` (which PostgreSQL itself refuses, `22003`) — known fate either way. **`regclass`/`regtype` stay OUT** although they read back as `I64` too: no measured caller, (af)'s membership rule; `regclass` rides the lockstep fixture as the negative control. A unit test, a live test with the read-back/bind-back round trip and both boundaries, each mutation-checked; the upstream test passes on both majors.

  **(cb) The Illuminate tier learns the MySQL family, and the framework suite has a MySQL column with its control (M2, C1f).** `Ferro\Laravel\FerroMySqlConnection extends MySqlConnection` and `FerroMariaDbConnection extends MariaDbConnection`, each with the shared execution trait plus one MySQL-family trait (`FerroMySqlFamily`), registered as `ferro-mysql` and `ferro-mariadb` — one per Laravel family, because Laravel 11 resolves MariaDB through its own `mariadb` driver and grammar (the first version registered `ferro-mysql` for both servers; see the review below). **The measurement was built before the tier surface and named all of it**, against a local MariaDB 10.11 (`docs/laravel-suite/2026-10-02-c1f-mysql-results.md`): registration alone measured 456 errors of 633 cases, and three changes took the column to 577/579.

  **(1) `insert()`, because stock MySQL executes through `getPdo()->prepare()`** — 443 of the 456 errors, the shim refusing a method no pooled engine can honour. The override keeps stock's two load-bearing details: the key is read INSIDE `run()`, before `QueryExecuted` fires (a listener that runs a query clears the client's per-statement key), and it is the STATEMENT's own key — `pdo_mysql`'s contract, measured: `"0"` after an insert that generated none, after a `SELECT`, after an `UPDATE`. That is the OPPOSITE of the SQLite shim's handle-sticky key ((bn)), because the two PDO drivers differ, and each family follows its own. **(2) Duplicate-key detection by errno 1062**, because stock `isUniqueConstraintError()` matches `pdo_mysql`'s WORDING (`Integrity constraint violation: 1062`), which a Ferro error does not carry — so `createOrFirst()` re-threw the duplicate it exists to catch (9 errors). The (bw) rule again: what Illuminate decides by wording, the tier decides by type, and the stock detector still answers for anything that is not a Ferro error; 1062 alone, the code stock names. **(3) A MySQL `TIMESTAMP` reads back as the naive UTC wall clock, the OPPOSITE call from (al)'s PostgreSQL one, and for a reason about the WRITE path.** `$table->timestamp()`/`timestamps()` create MySQL `TIMESTAMP` columns, which Ferro reads as `TIMESTAMPTZ` with canonical RFC3339 text; `pdo_mysql` returns the session-timezone wall clock with no offset, and every Ferro MySQL session is pinned to `+00:00` (M1-S7). Eloquent WRITES a naive `Y-m-d H:i:s` string, which the server reads in that session — so only a naive read makes a write → read round trip byte-stable, and RFC3339 would make Illuminate read a UTC INSTANT where it wrote an application-timezone wall clock: a silent shift for any app not running in UTC. On PostgreSQL `pdo_pgsql`'s own string carries an offset, which is why (al) stands there. Implemented as a per-family value policy chosen by driver (`Ferro\Laravel\Value\MySqlValuePolicy`), the per-platform shape the Doctrine tier's `DbalValuePolicy` already has; every other tag is the raw policy's answer, which its unit test asserts as the control.

  **The (bz) rule caught the column's flattering number before it was recorded.** Compared as SETS against the `pdo_mysql` control, `ferro-mysql` SKIPPED 8 tests the control ran — each carrying a `#[RequiresDatabase]` that names the family (4 `['mysql', 'mariadb']`, 3 data sets `['sqlite', 'mysql', 'mariadb']`, 1 `['mysql', 'mariadb', 'pgsql']`), which testbench resolves from `getDriverName()` — and two of them FAIL under the control, so the Ferro column looked better than its control by running less: the C2e driver-name artifact ((am), (ar)) on a second family. Under the alias the skip sets are identical to the control's. The first version of this entry went on to call the alias column IDENTICAL to the control — the same 3 failures, the same digests — **and that was a coincidence of names, which the review below took apart.** **Harness:** a `mysql` / `mariadb` / `mysql-local` arm in `testkit/laravel-suite.sh`; the family in the patched `DatabaseTestCase` with a `stock-mysql` control pinned to UTC like the engine (a control differing in two variables would make every difference uninterpretable); a contact probe that requires `@@version_comment` (an error on PostgreSQL, where `@@` is a prefix operator and the column does not exist, and on SQLite) and the family's name in it or in `version()` — both, because an Ubuntu-packaged MariaDB answers the comment with `Ubuntu 24.04`; a `laravel_tests` database in `testkit/mysql-init.sql` and a reset whose character set is EXPLICIT, since MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.8 default to utf8mb4 and MariaDB 10.11 to latin1; and a `measure-mysql` job in the `laravel-suite` workflow (MySQL 8.4, MariaDB 11.8), which runs the set comparison itself. `php/laravel` gained a MySQL live lane on `FERRO_TEST_MYSQL_URL` (`MySqlConnectionLiveTest`: the key, the listener ordering, the unique detector with a NOT NULL control, the `TIMESTAMP` round trip with a `DATETIME` control), each behaviour mutation-proven. **Not built:** the MySQL family still does not advertise `literals_are_standard` — it would have to be learned from the session's `sql_mode` (`NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES`) — so `DB::escape()`/`toRawSql()`/`castAsJson()`/`DB::pretend()` with string bindings stay refused on a MySQL pool, fail-closed (C1g).

  **The adversarial review found 14 defects, five of them major, and the slice's record — not only its code — was what failed.** All reproduced before fixing. **F14 (the most severe):** `Schema::disableForeignKeyConstraints()`/`withoutForeignKeyConstraints()` did nothing, because `SET FOREIGN_KEY_CHECKS=0` taints its own checkout and the next statement gets a reset connection — so on MariaDB `dropAllTables()` (`migrate:fresh`, `db:wipe`, `RefreshDatabase`) failed with `1451` on any schema whose parent table sorts before its child (`MySqlBuilder` drops in name order), and on both servers the seeder idiom `disableForeignKeyConstraints(); Parent::truncate()` failed, as did dropping a referenced parent alone (MySQL 8.4 accepts the single multi-table `DROP` `dropAllTables()` emits with checks on — measured in CI, where this slice's first control assumed otherwise — but refuses the parent alone with `3730`). The framework suite never saw it because its FK schemas sort child-first. The SQLite sibling was found at C3-6b ((bn)); the MySQL one had not been probed. Now `FerroMySqlBuilder`/`FerroMariaDbBuilder` run `dropAllTables()` and `withoutForeignKeyConstraints()` inside one transaction — a Ferro transaction pins its connection across MySQL's implicit-commit DDL, measured: the checks stay off after a `DROP` inside it, and the statement after the transaction gets a clean connection — and a bare `disableForeignKeyConstraints()` outside a transaction is REFUSED rather than silently doing nothing; its cost is that a non-DDL statement inside `withoutForeignKeyConstraints()` now rolls back if the closure throws. Unlike (bn)'s SQLite `compileAlter()`, a remedy existed: MySQL's setting, unlike SQLite's pragma, is honoured inside a transaction. **F12:** `database` is not a label on this family — `MySqlBuilder` binds it as `information_schema.table_schema` — so a label differing from the pool's database made the schema builder introspect the wrong schema (`hasTable()` false for an existing table, `migrate` re-creating its repository); the tier now checks `select database()` the first time the schema builder is requested and refuses a mismatch, and §15's example was amended. **F1:** `getPdo()->lastInsertId()` still returned the SQLite shim's handle-sticky key on a MySQL pool — STALE after a keyless insert, a `SELECT` or an `INSERT IGNORE`, where `pdo_mysql` answers `"0"`; the shim now follows the pool's family. **F3:** Laravel 11 has a separate `mariadb` driver, so MariaDB through `ferro-mysql` ran the MySQL GRAMMAR — and the MariaDB control was configured the same way, so its three "upstream's own" failures (`threadCount()` `null`; `castAsJson()`'s `cast(? as json)`, a MariaDB syntax error) were the grammar's; under the stock `mariadb` driver the control is clean. Hence `ferro-mariadb`, and the suite measures MariaDB under its own family. **F4:** the alias column "identical to the control" shared the control's two `testBasicUpdateForJson` FAILURES for a DIFFERENT reason — the control on that syntax error, Ferro on the `quote()` refusal `castAsJson()` reaches — so `compare-columns.php` now compares a shared failure's exception TYPE and exits 1 on a difference; against the pair this entry first recorded it exits 1 where it exited 0. **F6, F13 (documentation, both major):** Laravel's MySQL session keys — `strict`/`modes`, `isolation_level`, `timezone`, `charset`/`collation` — are silently ignored, since the connector that issues them never runs and a pooled session is reset between tenants (measured on MariaDB 10.11 with Laravel's shipped `'strict' => true`: `ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY` and `NO_ZERO_DATE` absent, the server's default collation, `REPEATABLE READ`); and a `TIMESTAMP` written through a non-UTC session reads back in UTC after adoption — the same instant, a different naive string, which Eloquent reads as a wall clock. The page's old sentence "Doctrine and Laravel make the same choice [of UTC]" was false for both and is corrected. **F2:** the `TIMESTAMP` rendering is `pdo_mysql`'s only to the second — a fractional column renders the canonical fraction (none, or six digits), not the column's precision — now stated and pinned by a live test, and `MySqlValuePolicy` fails CLOSED on a non-canonical payload instead of handing up the RFC3339 shape it exists to remove. **F5:** the `insert()` override's `pretend()` guard had no test (deleting it left the lane green); it has one, mutation-proven, and `recordsHaveBeenModified()` now precedes the write, as stock's does. **F8:** both suite runners' MySQL resets ran `client 2>&1 | grep -v … || true`, which swallowed a FAILED reset and still printed the line CI asserts on (reproduced with a failing stand-in client); `MYSQL_PWD` now, with nothing to filter. **F9:** `unix_socket` — mysqld's own socket in a stock config — was read as the ferrod socket; ignored now, like `host`. **F7, F10, F11:** §15's config example and `lastInsertId` bullet amended; the incompatibilities page says plainly that §15's bar is not met; "8 failures" was 9 errors. The harness also learned to check that a column reached the server it claims (MySQL and MariaDB sit on adjacent ports) and to refuse a DSN that is not the database the reset recreates. **Recorded after the review** (the `laravel-suite` workflow, two runs per column, identical skip and failure sets): MySQL 8.4 `ferro-mysql` 578/579, `mysql` 585/587, `stock-mysql` 587/587; MariaDB 11.8 `ferro-mariadb` 578/579, `mariadb` 586/588, `stock-mariadb` 588/588. Both controls are clean; each Ferro column's non-passes are the driver-name artifact, and each alias column's two are the `quote()` refusal — the one real gap, C1g.

  **(cc) `quote()` works on every family, built from forms whose meaning does NOT depend on the session's escape mode — and the advertised `literals_are_standard` is withdrawn as a quoting rule (M2, C1g).** Until this slice the bit was learned only on PostgreSQL ((at)), so every MySQL and SQLite pool advertised `nil` and the Laravel tier's `quote()` refused there — `DB::escape()`, `toRawSql()`, `->dd()`, Laravel's own `castAsJson()` testing helper, and `DB::pretend()` with a string binding (it renders each statement it did not run through `substituteBindingsIntoRawSql()`); C1f measured that as the one real gap in both MySQL-family alias columns. **The engine now learns the bit on both families, for free:** MySQL reports `SERVER_STATUS_NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES` on every OK packet and the driver keeps the last one (`None` with no packet — a parked connection, or one whose last packet was an ERR, which the driver clears it on), checked against `@@sql_mode` AND a literal's semantics (`CHAR_LENGTH('a\b')` is 3 or 2) in both states on one connection; SQLite's answer is a constant `Some(true)` (its literal has exactly one escape, a doubled `'`), checked against the property. No wire change.

  **The first design quoted BY that bit, and the adversarial review broke it.** It doubled `'` when the pool advertised `true` and applied `mysql_real_escape_string()`'s table (with `'` doubled) when it advertised `false`. The reviewer stood up a private MariaDB whose global `sql_mode` includes `NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES` and, through a real `ferrod` and `ferro-mysql`, made a literal built by the advertised rule BREAK OUT (`\' union select 0x50574e4544 -- ` returned a second row) three ways: an application turning the mode OFF inside its own transaction; an operator changing the server's global mode while the pool's cached bit (600 s TTL, and each PHP client keeps its handshake's copy for life) still said `true`; and — with no change at all — an `init_connect` that sets the mode, which runs on a fresh dial but NOT after `COM_RESET_CONNECTION`, so the probe's fresh-dial session and every recycled lease genuinely differ. That last case falsified this slice's own premise that "the probe reads a freshly set-up or freshly reset session, so the value is the server's default" — on such a server those are different states (`pdo_mysql` is wrong there too, in the other direction, since its auth OK packet predates `init_connect`). It also showed `init_connect='SET NAMES gbk'` makes a fresh dial GBK, where byte-wise backslash escaping breaks out (`0x5c` is a GBK trail byte) — equal to `pdo_mysql`'s exposure, but enough to falsify the docblock's "cannot be negotiated". **The root cause is not a missing case but the premise: a pool-level bit read from one probed session cannot describe the session a statement will run on**, and an escaping function must not stake injection-safety on it.

  **So `quote()` no longer reads the bit; it uses literal forms that mean the same bytes in every mode.** The backslash is the only mode-dependent character, so a string without one is quoted by doubling `'` on every family — byte-identical to `pdo_pgsql`, `pdo_sqlite`, and `pdo_mysql` under `NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES`. A string WITH one becomes `E'…'` (every `\` and `'` doubled) on PostgreSQL, whose `E''` strings process backslashes whatever `standard_conforming_strings` says, and `_utf8mb4 X'<hex>'` on MySQL/MariaDB, which puts no byte of the value in the SQL text at all — so neither `NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES` nor the connection charset can change it, closing the GBK class by construction (the doubled form emits no `\`, and `'` cannot be a trail byte in GBK, Big5 or SJIS). SQLite doubles only. MEASURED, not argued: every case — backslash-then-quote, quote-then-backslash, trailing backslash, the reviewer's two breakout payloads, multi-byte, NUL/CR/LF/Ctrl-Z — reads back as its exact bytes under BOTH escape modes on MariaDB (one pinned transaction turns the mode on) and under both `standard_conforming_strings` settings on PostgreSQL (`SET LOCAL … = off`), the GBK payload stays one row of the right byte length under `SET NAMES gbk`, and the output is asserted IDENTICAL whether the pool advertised `true`, `false` or `nil`. Three mutations each fail: MySQL's backslash form replaced by plain doubling (7 cases misread or break out in the default mode — the review's F1), by the first design's backslash table (8 under `NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES` on and GBK), and PostgreSQL's by plain doubling — C2g's rule — (5 with `standard_conforming_strings` off). **This also closes the same hazard on PostgreSQL**, open since C2g: a cached `standard_conforming_strings = on` with a session that has it off.

  **Costs, stated:** byte-identity with PDO wherever it would have bought nothing — on a default MySQL session `pdo_mysql` emits `'O\'Brien'` and the shim `'O''Brien'` (the same string to the server; upstream's `MySql/EscapeTest::testEscapeString` and `MariaDb/EscapeTest::testEscapeString` compare the bytes and record it), and a value with a backslash renders as `E'…'` or hex in `toRawSql()`. `quote()` now refuses only an unknown backend FAMILY. **The bit itself stays on the wire, INFORMATIONAL** — true of the connection it was read from, documented as such in `/proto/PROTOCOL.md` §4, the trait and `PoolInfo` — which withdraws (at)'s "only an unambiguous `true` may proceed" as the shim's rule (D5's "client-side, per-platform tables, no engine round trip" holds unchanged). **Not changed, and filed:** the Doctrine tier's `quote()` follows DBAL's stock platform rule, which on PostgreSQL doubles `'` and leaves `\` raw — breakable in a session with `standard_conforming_strings` off (`docs/followups/2026-10-02-doctrine-quote-is-mode-dependent-on-pg.md`).

  **Harness:** upstream's MySQL- and MariaDB-SPECIFIC directories now run as their own columns (`testkit/laravel/allowlist-{mysql,mariadb}.txt`; their base classes carry `#[RequiresDatabase('<family>')]`, so — like C2f's PostgreSQL directory — they run only under the alias and the control). RECORDED (`docs/laravel-suite/2026-10-02-c1g-results.md`, `laravel-suite` run 37011993348 on the final code, two runs per column, identical sets): the alias columns now EQUAL their controls on all 633 driver-agnostic cases — MySQL 8.4 587/587, MariaDB 11.8 588/588; **that proves the no-backslash (doubled) form only**, since none of upstream's cases quotes a string with a backslash (the first, breakable design produced the identical table), so the backslash arm rests entirely on the dedicated escape tests, whose mutations fail — and the family-specific columns are `mysql` 123/126 against 126/126 and `mariadb` 122/123 against 123/123, every non-pass one of two causes: the deliberate `''` (`testEscapeString`) and `DB::getPdo()->query()` refused by the shim (two `testLastInsertIdIsPreserved` cases, whose subject — the key surviving a query run from a `QueryExecuted` listener — holds; the call they make to prove it is what is refused). **A second adversarial round, on the redesign, found no defect**: it re-attacked every breakout and misread with live probes — `E''` under `standard_conforming_strings` and `backslash_quote` on/off, invalid UTF-8 into the hex form (a LOUD error 1300 under any `sql_mode`, never a silent truncation), GBK lead bytes before a doubled quote, collation mixing, Illuminate's `substituteBindingsIntoRawSql` scanner — and refuted all of them. Its two surviving mutations are now caught: the unknown-family refusal on the backslash path had no test, and dropping the `_utf8mb4` introducer survived every read-back test, because a bare `X'…'` reads back the same bytes; what the introducer changes is that the literal is a utf8mb4 string rather than a BINARY one (`UPPER()` is a no-op on binary, and it compares byte-for-byte to another literal), which a new live test asserts against `pdo_mysql`'s literal as the control. PostgreSQL, re-measured in the same run as a regression check on the `E''` form, is unchanged: `ferro-pgsql` 570/579, the alias 605/607 equal to its control, the PostgreSQL-specific directory 74/74 equal to its control. The review's CI nits are fixed too: the family-specific steps now assert the reset line and the control's stock class, and run 2 must equal run 1 or the job fails.

  **(cd) The caller's W3C `traceparent` reaches the engine — the first half of §13's OTLP bullet, split at its consumer (M2, C4c-1).** §13 asks for one OTLP span per EXEC *"linked to the caller's `traceparent` passed in EXEC options"*, and §6 has listed `trace?: {traceparent}` among those options since v0.2 — but the wire never carried it and nothing in the tree produced one. Checking the consumer first (the C4b-1 lesson, (bs)) split C4c: a span can only be LINKED once a producer on the client and a field on the wire exist, and once the context arrives the slow log — which exists — consumes it today. So C4c-1 is the transport plus that first consumer; the OTLP exporter is C4c-2.

  **Wire.** `ExecRequest` gained a ninth positional field, `traceparent: str | nil` — flat rather than §6's nested `trace: {traceparent}`, because the request is positional and the map would carry exactly one key. Strict arity (PROTOCOL.md §8) makes any field addition a shape change nothing else catches, so `protocol_version` moved 3 → 4 (PROTOCOL.md §1); without the bump a version-3 client would handshake and then have every EXEC refused as `Protocol`, one statement at a time. New golden vector `sql_exec_request_traceparent` in both codecs (charter rule 2); every other request vector now ends in a second bare `nil`. Bumping the arity left a guard test VACUOUS — `exec_request_oversized_params_len_refused` hand-builds an arity-8 header, so it was now refused by the arity check before reaching the `bound_len` path it exists for; it sends 0x99 now and asserts the error is NOT the arity one, so the next bump fails it instead of passing it.

  **Engine.** `ferrod`'s `trace.rs` is the one place the text is interpreted, against W3C Trace Context Level 1 with no leniency added: lowercase hex only, version `ff` refused, version `00` exactly 55 characters, a later version's known prefix parsed when the character after it is `-` (and of its flags only `sampled`, as §3.2.4 of the W3C text says), all-zero ids refused. It is parsed once per EXEC, before any shape check, on every path. **A malformed value is dropped and COUNTED (`ferro_traceparent_invalid_total`), never refused** — W3C tells a vendor that cannot parse the header not to use it and to start a new trace, and an observability field must never be why a statement fails; the counter is what makes a broken provider visible to an operator without making it visible to the statement. Uppercase is refused rather than normalised: a producer emitting it is broken for its other consumers too, and Ferro should not be the one place that hides it. **Field 9 is the one `str` the codec decodes lossily** (invalid UTF-8 becomes U+FFFD, which the ASCII grammar then rejects and the engine counts); the framing stays strict, and every other `str` field still refuses invalid UTF-8.

  **Slow log.** The record carries `trace_id` and `parent_span_id` (empty when absent, so a log query can filter without an existence check). They are random identifiers chosen by the caller's tracer, not data, so the redaction contract is untouched — and the header's RAW text never reaches the log: the e2e sends a malformed header carrying a would-be secret and asserts it absent.

  **Client.** `Ferro\Client\TraceContext::useProvider(?\Closure)` installs a process-wide PROVIDER that `ExecCodec::encode()` — the one encoder the autocommit, tx-scoped and streamed paths share — calls once per EXEC, in the fiber that issued it, so a tracer whose current context is per-fiber (OpenTelemetry PHP's is) gives each statement its own span with no plumbing through Ferro. Process-wide rather than per connection because both drop-in tiers create clients the application never sees and Illuminate replaces them on every reconnect ((bw)). A provider that throws (any `Throwable`), returns a non-string, an empty string, more than 512 bytes or anything but printable ASCII, or re-enters the client sends NO context and the statement runs; the re-entrancy guard is held per FIBER, and a context that would push a statement over the frame cap is dropped by the encoder. The client bounds the length and the alphabet and leaves the grammar to the engine, which counts what it drops. (OpenTelemetry PHP's context is per-fiber once its fiber support is initialised; without it, a provider in an uninitialised fiber can throw — no context is sent, and the statement still runs.)

  **The adversarial review found the slice's central promise false for one class of value, and the code otherwise right.** MAJOR: a `traceparent` that was not valid UTF-8 — 55 bytes, under the length cap — made the engine refuse the whole request as `Protocol` (the codec rejected the `str`), so every statement failed for as long as the provider returned it; a provider forwarding an inbound HTTP header verbatim hands an external caller that byte. Fixed at BOTH ends, each tested on its own: the client drops anything but printable ASCII, and the engine decodes field 9 lossily (a third-party client cannot fail statements this way either), with two controls proving the framing and every other `str` field are still strict. Reproduced first through a real `ferrod` (the e2e failed with `malformed ExecRequest: … invalid utf8`). MINOR: "parsed on every path" had no test — counting only on the autocommit-buffered path survived every test, because every test that sent a `traceparent` used that path; one e2e now sends a malformed context down autocommit buffered, autocommit streamed, tx-scoped, and the three request shapes refused for their own reasons (unknown pool, `query_id`, unknown `tx_id`), asserting each statement's outcome and an EXACT counter delta. MINOR: the separator checks at bytes 35 and 52 were untested (each deletion survived; only a HEX digit in the separator's place discriminates, since anything else fails a neighbouring field's hex parse). MINOR: the re-entrancy guard was process-wide, so a provider that suspended its fiber blanked every other fiber's context while parked — now a `WeakMap` per fiber plus the main stack. NIT: a 55-byte context could be what pushed a 16 MiB statement over the frame cap; the encoder now drops the context instead. Wording: the W3C section citations were corrected, and the metric's HELP no longer says "the statement ran" for requests refused on their own grounds. The review's other surviving mutation (the PHP decoder accepting arity 8) and two caught only incidentally (a removed re-entrancy guard caught by a 13.6 GB OOM; the guard reset outside `finally` caught by test order) now each have a direct assertion. Its differential test of the parser against an independent reading of the W3C text — 305 729 inputs, single-character mutations of the specification's examples plus random strings around the 55-character boundary — found zero disagreements and zero panics. All eleven mutations re-run after the fixes are killed.

  **Scope, stated.** The context rides EXEC only: `BEGIN`/`COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK`/savepoint messages are unchanged. The slow log is still emitted on the autocommit buffered path alone (C4a's scope), so a tx-scoped or streamed EXEC's context is parsed and counted but has no consumer until C4c-2's spans.

  **(ce) One OTLP span per EXEC, exported to a real OpenTelemetry Collector — §13's OTLP bullet is complete for M2 (M2, C4c-2).** C4c-1 ((cd)) carried the caller's `traceparent` to the engine; this slice turns each EXEC into a span whose PARENT is the caller's span. **The consumer was measured before the format was written**: a real `otelcol` 0.115 confirmed OTLP/JSON's lowercase-HEX ids (not base64; a wrong-length id is refused 400), int64 values as JSON strings and integer enums — and showed that **a misspelled field is dropped SILENTLY with HTTP 200** (a typo'd `parentSpanId` became a root span, a typo'd attribute value an empty one). So the end-to-end proof reads back what the collector EXPORTED: `ferrod`'s `otlp_it` spawns the collector itself and asserts every field from its file exporter. CI's integration lane fetches the collector, pinned by version AND the official release checksum through one script (`ci/fetch-otelcol.sh`) that `ci/local-gate.sh --live` shares, and its no-skip gate fails the lane if the test cannot run.

  **One `END` is one span, by construction.** The span rides the request's `Responder` and is finished by whichever `end_*` declares the terminal — the one place every request passes exactly once — so every error site (there are some twenty) produces its span without being touched; only the three SUCCESS sites record measurements, through one shared helper for the two buffered paths. A `Responder` dropped without a terminal (a handler bug) drops its span, which then exports as `error.type = NoTerminal`, matching the error `END` the supervisor synthesizes. A request refused for its SHAPE is a span too: it is opened before any shape check, like the trace context it carries.

  **Decisions, each with its reason.** (1) **OTLP/HTTP JSON, hand-rolled, `http://` only**: one request shape and one status line do not justify an HTTP stack's dependency tree in a daemon that holds every tenant's credentials (`crate::metrics` precedent); TLS would need exactly that tree, and the target deployment is a collector agent on the same host or pod. `https://` is refused by name, a URL carrying credentials is refused, and `grpc` is refused with the reason. (2) **OpenTelemetry's standard variables** (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TRACES_ENDPOINT` used as given, else `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` + `/v1/traces`; `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME`; `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER`; `OTEL_BSP_SCHEDULE_DELAY`; `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED`), so an operator's existing configuration works — but **opt-in**: an SDK exports to `localhost:4318` by default and `ferrod` exports nowhere until told to, the metrics port's reading of product-vision §5. A value it cannot honour is logged at `error` and leaves tracing OFF; it never refuses to start. (3) **The caller is the sampling authority**: a sampled context gets a child span, an unsampled one none whatever the sampler says, and with NO context the default `parentbased_always_off` exports nothing where an SDK's default would root a trace — the engine serves every application on the host, and one that does not trace would otherwise turn each statement into a root span. `parentbased_always_on` opts in. (4) **Tracing never fails, blocks or slows a statement**: a finished span goes to a bounded queue (2048) with `try_send`, dropped and counted when full (`ferro_otlp_spans_dropped_total`); exports run on their own task in batches (512, or every interval), each bounded at 5 s; a failed export is counted (`ferro_otlp_spans_failed_total`) and logged at most once a minute; at exit `main` flushes what is queued within 2 s. (5) **The redaction contract** is the slow log's: the fingerprint, never raw SQL; a pool name only when the registry knows it (an unknown pool's name is client-chosen text); an error's registry code name, never its message; no parameters at all. (6) **Span ids** are `splitmix64` of a seeded counter — a bijection, so no two in a process can collide — rather than a syscall per span. (7) The span is a CLIENT span named `EXEC` (a constant: the name never carries statement text), its status left UNSET on success as the OpenTelemetry guidance asks, its flags marking the parent as remote.

  **Not exported, and named rather than faked:** §13's *backend connection id* — no backend exposes one through the `PoolBackend` seam (PostgreSQL's backend pid would need a fork accessor; MySQL's thread id is internal to `ferro-backend-mysql`). BEGIN/COMMIT/ROLLBACK are not spanned: §13 asks for one span per EXEC, and their messages carry no trace context.

  **The C4c-2 adversarial review found the redaction contract broken on two of three families, and C4a's premise is what broke it.** BLOCKER: the fingerprint read every dialect with PostgreSQL's rules, so on MySQL a `"…"` STRING, the tail of an `'O\'Brien'` literal and a `#` comment's text, and on SQLite a `"…"` that fell back to a string, all reached a collector verbatim — reproduced live on MariaDB 10.11 and SQLite, and true of the C4a slow log too. The root cause CORRECTS (br): C4a reused the pin lexer's region pass because "one lexer is one place to be right", but the two consumers have OPPOSITE safe directions — the pin lexer keeps ambiguous text VISIBLE (a missed pin leaks session state), a fingerprint must HIDE it (a shown value is the leak). `ferro-classify` now has a separate redaction pass per dialect (`redact.rs`): MySQL's `"…"` is a literal, `#` a comment and backticks an identifier REGION (so `` `it's` `` cannot open a literal that closes on the next real one's quote); SQLite's `"…"` is a literal and `[…]`/backticks are identifier regions; PostgreSQL keeps `"…"` as an identifier and `E'…'` as precise. **Where the server's own reading depends on a session setting the engine cannot see — an ODD run of backslashes right before a closing quote, read differently under `standard_conforming_strings` and `NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES` — everything to the end is hidden**, with a control proving every other backslash keeps the shape; an unterminated region of any kind, identifiers included, hides the rest; `0x…`/`0b…`/`0o…`/`1_000` numbers are values. The stated cost: on MySQL and SQLite a double-quoted token reads `?` (Laravel and Doctrine quote SQLite identifiers that way, so their SQLite fingerprints lose table names). MAJOR: spans had no size bound — a count-bounded queue of statement-sized fingerprints, and one tenant's bulk INSERTs pushed shared batches past the collector's 20 MiB body limit, blacking out every tenant's spans (measured, with a control); and a 2 MiB statement cost 383 ms of a worker. Fixed twice over: the fingerprint reads at most 4 KiB (a cut literal stays hidden, marked ` …`), and exports are split into requests of at most 4 MiB. MAJOR (§12): a refused endpoint was logged WITH its credential — the module refused userinfo because "a credential in a URL is one log line from a leak" and then wrote that line; no error carries the URL now, and a query or fragment is refused anywhere in it. MINOR: a tx-scoped span named the pool the CLIENT claimed (the tx path ignores it) — the pool and dialect are captured on the `TxHandle` at BEGIN; `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=none` was ignored (fail-open); and the real-collector test never asserted the fields it exists to catch dropped (renaming `flags` and both timestamps passed against otelcol) — one full-shape assertion is now shared by the in-test receiver and the collector. NITs: a `100 Continue` was read as the answer; the `NoTerminal` span named an error type no END carries (it now reports the synthesized `Protocol` plus `ferro.no_terminal`); WAL sidecars leaked from every test run; and the CI fetch step masked a failed download. **Ten of its mutations had survived** — non-2xx counted as exported, the span opened after the shape checks, zero-duration spans, `queue_us`/`exec_us` swapped, `fetch:none` rows counted, the exported counter and its series, a stream's DATA bytes, a constant `readonly`, and every non-SQLite family — and each now fails: the span's measurements are asserted EQUAL to the terminal's own stats (one read), its duration to cover `exec_us`, exact counts against owned counters, and a PostgreSQL/MySQL lane in the integration job. Not every "one END, one span" claim survived either: an EXEC refused at the SESSION layer (a reused or zero request id, `max_inflight`, a body that does not decode) is answered before any EXEC exists, so it gets an END and no span.

  **(cf) The admin service exists, and its first verb is §7.6's online backup — `ADMIN`/`BACKUP`, under D15 and D14 (M2, C3-7b-1).** `[methods.admin]` was empty since M0 and `dispatch::route(ADMIN, _)` answered `Unsupported`; it now carries `BACKUP = 1`, with a new NonRetryable `errc::FORBIDDEN` (`0x300C`), golden vectors (`admin_backup_request`, `admin_backup_response`, `error_forbidden`) and both codecs (charter rule 2; `/proto/PROTOCOL.md` §11). **No `protocol_version` bump, deliberately:** the registry hash covers the whole lock file and is checked at the handshake, so a skewed engine/client pair already fails at the first frame; `protocol_version` moves when an existing message changes shape, and an older decoder classifies the new code correctly by its explicit `branch`.

  **D15 is enforced in the SESSION layer, before any handler.** The session reads its peer's kernel-attested uid from its own socket once (`SO_PEERCRED` is fixed for the socket's life, so one read is the per-verb answer), and `Route::Admin(verb)` is authorized there: a refused verb never spawns a handler, never looks up a pool, never touches a file — so a refusal is identical whether the named pool exists or not, and a non-admin cannot enumerate pools by the difference (asserted, with the admitted peer's `Unsupported("unknown pool")` as the control). Every admin method's class is an exhaustive `match`, and a test derives the method list from the registry (`method_admin::ALL`, newly generated for every method table), so a method cannot ship unclassified. **M2 builds no READ verb**: pool state already has a reader in Prometheus ((bs)/(bv)) and its admin-side consumer is `ferro top` (M4); the READ arm of the rule is implemented and unit-tested so the first READ verb inherits it.

  **The verb is shaped by four measurements, each with its control.** (1) `VACUUM INTO ?1` takes its target as a BOUND parameter, so no path is ever spliced into SQL, and the D14 authorizer still sees the resolved name. (2) An interrupted `VACUUM INTO` leaves a zero-byte file. (3) **A DANGLING SYMLINK inside the allowed directory redirected a snapshot OUTSIDE it** — D14's guard canonicalised only the target's parent (the target does not exist yet), and SQLite followed the link: `VACUUM INTO '<root>/link.db'` created `<outside>/escaped.db`. That was a hole on the DATA plane too, closed in the guard itself (the target must not be a symlink, by `lstat`), with a plain name in the same directory as the control. (4) **D14's own text was wrong**: "`VACUUM INTO 'snap.db'` beside the database works out of the box" holds only when `ferrod`'s cwd is the database's directory, because a relative name resolves against the process's cwd — corrected in D14 and pinned by a test. **Destination policy:** the client names a plain FILE NAME (`[A-Za-z0-9._-]`, ≤ 200 bytes so the temporary's name fits `NAME_MAX`, no leading `.`), placed in the pool's allowed root via the SAME resolution the guard uses (`SqliteBackend::allowed_root`), so no directory crosses the wire in either direction (§12/D8) and a name cannot traverse. **Atomic finalisation, so the engine never destroys a file it did not create:** the snapshot is written to a unique temporary in the same directory, then `hard_link`ed into place without `replace` (atomically refusing an existing name) or `rename`d over it with `replace` — so a FAILED replace leaves the previous snapshot intact, which the obvious delete-then-write would not, and the temporary is removed on every failure path. **Fate:** `VACUUM INTO` never writes the source database, and the engine itself resolves the one file it writes, so a cancelled or timed-out backup is `Cancelled`/`QueryTimeout`, never `Indeterminate`; the checkout is nonetheless NON-readonly, because `query_only` refuses `VACUUM INTO` (C3-7a property 4). `CANCEL` reaches a running backup through the interrupt handle (asserted on a backup proven to be writing). A server-backed pool is `Unsupported`: a PostgreSQL or MySQL database is snapshotted by its own tooling, which an engine statement cannot reproduce consistently. **Carried to C3-7b-2:** the client surface (`ferro/client`), since nothing outside the test suite can send `BACKUP` yet.

  **The adversarial review broke the first version in ways the slice's own 12 mutations could not have, and every finding was reproduced before it was fixed.** (1) **The D14 guard had been open since D14 shipped**, for ANY non-literal `ATTACH` target: SQLite's `codeAttach` passes the authorizer a filename only for a string literal, and NULL for a bound parameter or an expression, which rusqlite surfaces as `Unknown` and the guard's `_ => Allow` admitted — `ATTACH ?1` to an outside path plus `CREATE TABLE s1.copy AS SELECT …` copied the data out. Such an `ATTACH` is now refused wherever it points; the cost is that an application spells its target as a literal (`VACUUM INTO ?1` is unaffected, because SQLite re-issues it as a literal `ATTACH %Q` — the control). (2) **A percent-encoded `file:` URI escaped too**: the bundled `libsqlite3-sys` compiles with `SQLITE_USE_URI`, so turning off the open flag would have closed nothing, and the guard read `file:%2Ftmp%2Fx.db` as a relative name while SQLite decoded and opened `/tmp/x.db`; the scheme is refused. (3) **`replace` could overwrite the LIVE database or its WAL** — measured, over `main.db` an acknowledged write was lost (52 rows where 53 were committed), over `main.db-wal` the database was corrupted (`integrity_check`: "unable to get the page"); every SQLite pool's database name and its `-wal`/`-shm`/`-journal` sidecars in that directory are refused, with or without `replace`. (4) **The temporary was predictable and not created exclusively**, so a pre-planted hard link at `.<file>.ferro-backup-<pid>-<n>` received the snapshot outside the directory, and a planted file at the name was DELETED by the engine on failure; the temporary is now created `O_CREAT|O_EXCL`, mode `0600`, under a 128-bit random name, held OPEN until it is published or removed, and published only if its name still refers to that inode with one link. **Holding it open is load-bearing, and a test found that, not the review**: tmpfs reused a freed inode number at once, so an identity check by `(dev, ino)` alone matched a stranger's replacement file — the open handle keeps the inode allocated, so the number cannot be reused. (5) **`SQLITE_CANTOPEN` and `SQLITE_NOTADB` were "handle-fatal"**, so a refusal to open a NAMED file read as `ConnectionLost` — Retryable for a backup (a client honouring it loops forever), and §19.3 `Indeterminate` for an undeclared `VACUUM INTO '<existing file>'` that provably wrote nothing; both are now known-fate `Unsupported`, and the connection is kept. The documented name limit was also false (names past ~220 bytes failed at the temporary) — it is 200 now, with the temporary's overhead pinned. (6) The two OPERATE refusals ("disabled" vs "not a member") told any admitted peer whether OPERATE was enabled at all; they read identically now, with the reason logged server-side. And SQLite's own `CANTOPEN` message names the full path, so a failed snapshot's error is redacted to `<allowed directory>`. **Threat model, stated:** a party that can write the allowed directory — by default the database's own — can already replace the database; these checks make the engine refuse rather than be steered by such a party, not make a shared writable directory safe. The reviewer's seven surviving mutations (swapped `queue_us`/`exec_us`, a temporary name without the leading `.`, the untested not-sent fate context, the finalise-failure cleanup, a guard refusing only dangling symlinks, and two that are equivalent or inherent) each now have a test that kills them or a stated reason they cannot.

  **(cg) `ferro/client` can take a backup — `Connection::backup($file, $replace = false, $timeoutMs = null)` (M2, C3-7b-2).** The client half (cf) carried: until it, nothing outside the test suite could send `ADMIN`/`BACKUP`. It sends the connection's own pool, so an application backs up the database it already talks to and never names a directory (the engine places the file; `BackupResult` deliberately carries no path, only `bytes`/`queueUs`/`execUs`). **Fate, decided — and the first decision was WRONG:** it classified a lost backup as a lost READ (Retryable) on the grounds that the source database is never written, and the adversarial review measured that false. A backup PUBLISHES A FILE, and a reply lost after the request went out says nothing about whether it was published — a backup the client had given up on appeared 500 ms later, because the engine's interrupt cannot stop a `VACUUM INTO` that has not started yet and the engine then publishes; worse, the client's own `transaction()` re-ran its closure on that Retryable and SENT THE BACKUP AGAIN. So a sent-then-lost backup is now a lost WRITE (`IndeterminateException`, which no retry loop re-issues — pinned with the exact count of BACKUP frames sent under `transaction()` with the default policy and a reconnect loop), and only a provably UNSENT one is Retryable; an engine-reported failure stays a known fate (the engine removes its own temporary before answering). **The connection's read timeout bounds a backup**, stated rather than worked around: the reply is awaited like any other, so a snapshot slower than the connection's `ioTimeout` (5 s by default) cannot be confirmed and ends Indeterminate, and `$timeoutMs` beyond it has no effect — a per-request read deadline on a multiplexed, Fiber-shared session would change every other request's, so the documented remedy is a backup connection opened with a larger `ioTimeout`. A refusal arrives as the engine sent it — `NonRetryableException` with `ERR_FORBIDDEN` (D15, or the name policy) or `ERR_UNSUPPORTED` (a server pool); a malformed success body is a `ProtocolException`, never a fabricated result, which needed STRICT decoding (each field a non-negative native int): the coercing `toInt` the TX codecs share decoded `[nil, nil, nil]` as a 0-byte success and clamped a u64 past `PHP_INT_MAX`; and a non-UTF-8 `$file` is refused client-side rather than surfacing as `ERR_PROTOCOL`, which §22.2 (ai) reserves for wire faults. Proven live through a real `ferrod` in both D15 configurations: with the test process's uid in `FERRO_ADMIN_UIDS` the snapshot is taken (its header and size checked rather than opened, so the test assumes no `ext-sqlite3`) and replaced, and the name policy and the live database's own names are refused — the swap's ATOMICITY is the engine's property, proven in `ferrod`'s `admin_it.rs`, not here; with the DEFAULT empty list every request is `Forbidden`, including a valid name and a SERVER pool, because the gate answers before any pool lookup — and that test PINS the list blank, since the harness inherits the caller's environment and an exported `FERRO_ADMIN_UIDS` turned all three tests red. The live harness gained an `extraEnv()` hook (refusing every `FERRO_SOCK`/`FERRO_POOLS`/`FERRO_POOL_*` key) and learns the process uid as the owner of a file it creates, because the client and its tests assume no `ext-posix` (charter rule 7). **A process finding worth keeping:** a reviewer that symlinks the main tree's `vendor/` into a worktree runs the MAIN tree's `src/` — Composer's autoloader resolves `Ferro\` by absolute path — so every PHP mutation applied in the worktree is never executed and passes; the reviewer caught it on its own first round, and every mutation in this slice was re-run against the real tree (all killed). **Not built, deliberately:** a `ferro` CLI command — D10's CLI is M3's schema tooling, and an application (or an `artisan` command) calling `backup()` covers M2's operator need.

  **(ch) M2 — the §15 demo app, and the queue defect it found on its first run (2026-10-02).** `testkit/laravel-demo/` is a stock Laravel 11 application's database surface — `database` sessions under real `web`-middleware HTTP requests (register, login, logout, an `auth` route, a duplicate registration refused by the `unique` rule's query), password-reset tokens (the broker's throttle reads the row's `created_at` back), the `database` queue (two jobs, a failing one landing in `failed_jobs`, `queue:retry` re-queuing it by UUID, a three-job `Bus::batch` whose `then` callback runs once, and the batch reads Horizon's batches screen makes — `BatchRepository::get()`/`find()` and its `like` search over `job_batches`) and the `database` cache store with atomic locks. **"Only the config diff" was FALSE when this slice was first written, and its adversarial review is what caught it:** `ferro/laravel` shipped no service provider, so an application had to call `FerroConnections::register()` from PHP of its own (measured: a config entry naming `ferro-pgsql` with nothing registered fails `Unsupported driver [ferro-pgsql]`), and the harness hid it because it registers the drivers itself. The package now ships `FerroServiceProvider`, declared under `extra.laravel.providers` and found by Laravel's own `PackageManifest` reading the installed package's real composer.json (a demo test, mutation-proven by deleting the declaration); `illuminate/support` already arrives with `illuminate/database`, so no dependency is added. It registers the four `ferro-*` drivers and no stock-name alias. It runs through `testkit/laravel-suite.sh` (`FERRO_LARAVEL_ALLOWLIST=testkit/laravel/allowlist-demo.txt`), so it inherits the suite's per-family connection, contact assertion and stock-PDO control rather than inventing its own; and between requests the test drops every in-memory session store, the `session.store` singleton and the auth guards, so state can survive ONLY through the database — without that the test client's shared application passes with sessions never read back (measured: the first draft "passed" a logged-out dashboard on a stale in-memory store, on the control too). **Its first Ferro run found a real tier defect: the `database` queue worker processed NOTHING on any family.** `DatabaseQueue::getLockForPopping()` reads `getPdo()->getAttribute(PDO::ATTR_DRIVER_NAME)` on every pop to choose between `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` and a plain lock, and `FerroPdoShim` refused every attribute but `SERVER_VERSION` — so the pop threw inside its transaction, the worker swallowed it as a job-less iteration, and `queue:work --stop-when-empty` returned success with every job still queued. The control passed. It is the only `ATTR_DRIVER_NAME` caller in `laravel/framework` 11.51.0 (grep-verified). The shim now answers what PDO answers for the family — `pgsql`, `mysql`, `sqlite`, and `mysql` on MariaDB exactly as `pdo_mysql` does, since Illuminate detects MariaDB from the version string, which this tier passes through unnormalised for the MySQL family — and refuses an unknown or unadvertised family, since the one caller picks lock SQL from it. Every other attribute still refuses by number. The review also found the lock choice untested — a shim answering `postgres` instead of `pgsql` silently downgrades `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` to a plain `FOR UPDATE`, serialising every worker, and the demo still passed — so the queue test now asserts the clause `getLockForPopping()` picks (mutation-proven). RESULT, local (PostgreSQL 16.13, MariaDB 10.11 under both Laravel families — no MySQL server is available locally — and SQLite 3.53.2 through Ferro / 3.45.1 through `pdo_sqlite`): **every column OK (7 tests, 67 assertions)**, Ferro and stock control alike. Because it is seven tests and seconds per column, it is not an on-demand measurement like the framework suite but a **per-PR gate** (`ci.yml`'s `laravel-demo`, PostgreSQL 17, MySQL 8.4, MariaDB 11.8 and SQLite, Ferro and control), which asserts each column resolved the connection class it claims and passed in full — the only place MySQL 8.4 is measured. **Not claimed:** Horizon itself (Redis-only — §15 amended), Octane, and anything beyond what the seven tests exercise (requests go through Laravel's in-process test client with CSRF skipped, not a socket); the framework suite's columns (§22.2 (am), (ar), (bn), (cb)) remain the breadth measurement.

  **(ci) M2 — the doctrine/orm functional suite runs, and ORM on PostgreSQL becomes config-only (2026-10-02).** §14's acceptance named "ORM functional suite green on PG + MySQL" and every record since M1-S8b said it was not run. `testkit/orm-suite.sh` runs upstream doctrine/orm **3.7.3**'s whole `tests/Tests/ORM/Functional` directory (1627 tests, minus upstream CI's `performance`/`locking_functional` groups) through `Ferro\DBAL\Driver` over DBAL 4.4.4 — the driver's own lock — in the clone's OWN vendor tree (the ORM needs persistence/console/cache, which the driver's tree lacks), with the Ferro packages symlinked from THIS checkout and the runner REFUSING to start unless `vendor/ferro/*` resolve here (a vendor tree pointing at another checkout would measure that checkout's code). It replaces upstream's `TestUtil` (which maps `db_driver` only and creates/drops the database through a privileged connection), resets by dropping the whole database before `ferrod` opens it (`DROP SCHEMA … CASCADE` over the suite's leftovers exhausts `max_locks_per_transaction`, measured), and a bootstrap contact assertion requires a `Ferro\Client\Connection` in the Ferro column and a PDO in the control. **The measurement was built first and it named four defects; three are fixed here and the fourth was REVERTED on review:** (1) `Result::fetchFirstColumn()` delegated to `FetchUtils`, which loops on `fetchOne() !== false` — a boolean column whose first value is false came back `[]`, a silently wrong answer (`GH9230Test`; PDO answers with `FETCH_COLUMN`); it loops on `fetchNumeric()` now. (2) **An `F64` could not bind a PostgreSQL `numeric`** — `DecimalType` binds a PHP float under `STRING` (7 tests); the driver cannot stringify it because `FloatType` binds under `STRING` too and the TEXT arm refuses the float widths (§22.2 (ap)), so the engine now binds `F64 → numeric` as canonical TEXT in the SHORTEST round-trip form — the value the float actually holds — and NOT PostgreSQL's own 15-digit `float8::numeric` cast. **It is also NOT what `pdo_pgsql` sends, and the first version of this entry said it was** (the review measured it): PDO's `PARAM_STR` is PHP's `(string)` cast at `precision = 14`, so the two differ for a float needing more digits (`0.1 + 0.2`: Ferro `0.30000000000000004`, PDO `0.3`) and at a rounding boundary of the column's scale (`0.00499999999999999` into `numeric(10,2)`: Ferro `0.00`, the correct rounding of that float; PDO `0.01`, a double rounding through `0.005`). Kept deliberately — Ferro stores the value the application holds — and listed as an incompatibility; a DOMAIN over `numeric` takes the same arm (the format is decided on the resolved type, now tested). (3) **`MYSQL_TYPE_NULL` is `Unsupported` — admitted, then REVERTED on review.** It is the type of a bare `SELECT NULL` (`NewOperatorTest`), and admitting it looked safe because such a column can only hold NULL. It cannot: MariaDB declares a bare `?` in the select list (`SELECT ? AS p`) as `MYSQL_TYPE_NULL` at PREPARE time, and the classifier reads prepared metadata, so the admitted type let that statement RUN and then fail to decode its bound value — the reviewer measured `SELECT bump(), ?` applying its write and reporting a known failure, where the refusal had kept it unsent. The refusal is restored, with a both-engines live test that pins the FATE (`Com_stmt_execute` unchanged on the session: refused before execution, or answered correctly where a server declares a real type), mutation-proven against two guessed types. **CI then found the same class already on `main`, on MySQL 8.4:** that test's first run on the runners failed because MySQL 8.4 declares the same bare `?` as a STRING at prepare time while the executed cell is the bound `Int` — the statement ran, then its cell was refused. Both paths now follow one rule: REFUSE from the prepared metadata (before anything is sent), DESCRIBE and DECODE from the executed metadata whenever the executed set carries it (both paths read it before `cols`/HEAD is built). A type admitted at prepare but out of scope once executed is refused after the send — (av)'s narrow trade, known-fate, the connection recovered clean. A NULL-typed column in the EXECUTED metadata reads as NULL (it describes real cells — a NULL-bound `?` on MySQL 8.4); a NULL-typed column in the PREPARED metadata is still refused before execution, so MariaDB's bare `?` and `SELECT NULL` stay refused — conservatively, because the vendored driver negotiates MariaDB's `CACHE_METADATA`, under which an execute may omit its metadata and the CACHED prepared list is decoded against. **A focused second review found the first version of that test vacuous**: `mysql_async` caches each statement's EXECUTED metadata and returns it as the "prepared" list on the next `prep` of the same SQL on that connection, so a buffered run followed by a stream run on one connection made the stream half unable to fail, and on MariaDB every shape was refused before reaching the new code. The test now runs every case on a FRESH connection per path and adds two shapes whose prepared and executed metadata DIFFER on MariaDB (`COALESCE(?, 1)` prepares LONG, executes VAR_STRING; `? + 0` prepares DOUBLE, executes LONGLONG) — reverting either path now fails it. Its CI run then measured a SERVER semantic, not a decode one, recorded as an incompatibility: MySQL 8.4 derives a parameter's type at prepare, so `SELECT COALESCE(?, 1)` bound to `'x'` answers `0` there (MariaDB keeps `'x'`; `pdo_mysql`'s default emulated prepares send the literal) — the test accepts the server's answer on MySQL, and HEAD still matches the cell. The same review moved a stream-arm error: when handing the connection back fails after an executed-metadata refusal, the link loss is now mapped through the fate table instead of reported as a known-fate `Unsupported`. (4) **`lastInsertId()` on PostgreSQL**: DBAL 4 makes ORM 3 map `AUTO` to `IDENTITY` on PostgreSQL, so under the stock ORM configuration 1185 of the column's non-passes were `NoIdentityValue` — 397/1594. §22.2 (by) had recorded that an in-transaction `lastval()` would be correct and deferred it here: a Ferro transaction PINS one backend connection, so the follow-up runs in the same session as the INSERT, and hygiene cleared that session's sequence state at checkout (`DISCARD SEQUENCES` in the targeted profile, `DISCARD ALL` in the full one). It now answers inside a transaction with `SELECT lastval()` (DBAL 3's named form: `currval(name)`, cast through `text` because the bind matrix does not bind TEXT into `regclass`), and still throws outside one. Its semantics are `pdo_pgsql`'s — the session's most recent `nextval()` of any sequence, and a `55000` that aborts the transaction when there is none — with ONE difference the hygiene itself causes, which the first version of this entry missed: the session's sequence state starts EMPTY at the transaction's checkout, so an INSERT made BEFORE `beginTransaction()` is invisible inside it — `pdo_pgsql` (one long-lived session) answers that key, Ferro raises `55000` and the transaction is aborted. DBAL 3's named form now has a two-sequence test (with one sequence, `currval(name)` and `lastval()` agree, and a mutation that dropped the named branch survived). **The safety half is a live test, mutation-proven:** a transaction that lands on a RECYCLED connection — confirmed by `pg_backend_pid()`, so a fresh dial cannot make it pass — must get `55000`, never the previous tenant's value; with `DISCARD SEQUENCES` deleted from the targeted profile it answered `4242`, the other tenant's `nextval()`. **D-S8b-5's "ORM on PostgreSQL is NOT config-only" is withdrawn for identity generation** — the unit of work always inserts inside a transaction. (Not every ORM mapping is config-only: a database-defaulted sub-second `timestamptz` column is still unreadable through the driver, (ab).) RESULT, local (PostgreSQL 16.13, MariaDB 10.11, debug `ferrod`; `docs/orm-suite/2026-10-02-local-results.md`): PostgreSQL stock config **1571/1597** vs `pdo_pgsql` 1597/1597; PostgreSQL with the SEQUENCE preference (a labelled harness change) 1563/1594 vs 1589/1594 (the control's 5 failures are the harness change's own); MariaDB **1579/1586** vs `pdo_mysql` 1586/1586 (`NewOperatorTest`'s `SELECT NULL` is the seventh, refused by design); skip sets identical to the controls' in every column. Every Ferro-only non-pass is documented: 16 sub-second `TIMESTAMPTZ` refusals (§22.2 (ab) — at FETCH, so a bare `now()` read through the driver is refused too, which the incompatibilities page now says), 6 TEMP-table cases (§7.4; `MultiTable{Update,Delete}Executor` outside a transaction), and 4 `lastInsertId()` calls outside a transaction; on MariaDB, the 6 TEMP-table cases and `NewOperatorTest`'s `SELECT NULL`. **The adversarial review is why three of this entry's claims changed before merge**: the NULL admission (a fate regression, reverted), the `pdo_pgsql` parity of the `numeric` text (false, now a documented divergence) and of `lastval()` (one difference, now stated); it also found two surviving mutations (DBAL 3's named branch, the DOMAIN arm of the `numeric` format), both killed now, and stale README/harness text. **Not established:** MySQL 8.4, PostgreSQL 17 and two-run reproducibility come from the on-demand `orm-suite` workflow, not this record; the DBAL 3 lane and SQLite have no ORM column; §14's bar as written ("green") is not met.

  **(cj) M3-D1a — the client multiplexes its one socket, and fan-out costs the slowest query, not the sum (2026-10-06).** The engine has run every request on its own task since M0 (`ferrod` `session::mod`), but the client was single-in-flight: write one frame, read one terminal. `Session` now files every frame it reads under its `request_id` (one router, `pump`), so `submit` can write a request and return at once and `awaitTerminal` can read until that one id's terminal arrives, keeping other ids' frames for their own awaiters. `sendRequest` is `submit` + `awaitTerminal`, so every tier's synchronous surface is unchanged.
  - **Measured live:** four `pg_sleep(0.5)` statements cost about 2 s run one after another (the control, so a sleep that never slept cannot pass the test) and about 0.5 s submitted together and awaited with `Ferro\await()`. This needs **no Fiber scheduler**: under plain FPM all k requests are written before any terminal is read.
  - **Failure handling in the router:**
    - a frame for an id not in flight, or a non-terminal frame for a buffered request, is a desync: `ProtocolException`, and the session is poisoned;
    - **a session-fatal `request_id=0` terminal decides NO pending request's fate.** With several requests in flight it may concern any of them, or a control frame, so its payload is never attributed. The session stops sending and keeps reading until EOF, because the engine drains each in-flight request's own terminal before it closes (`cancel_all` + `drain_supervisors`). A request still unanswered at EOF fails with a `ConnectionLostException` carrying no server payload, so the caller's classifier decides, and a lost write is `Indeterminate`. **This also changes the single-request case**: before M3 the fatal's payload was the request's fate, and that is now never so;
    - a transport failure fails every pending request as SENT, never `requestNotSent`, because each frame was completely written and its fate is unknown. A request still waiting for an in-flight slot when the session fails was never written, so it IS `requestNotSent`. `close()` poisons the session, so a Future still pending on it fails as sent;
    - a Future dropped unawaited discards its terminal on arrival, so abandoned Futures cannot grow the session's inbox.
  - **The in-flight limit is the client's (default 256)**, below the engine's `max_inflight` (1024), because the engine answers an excess request with an error instead of running it. At the limit, `submit` reads until a terminal frees a slot and keeps that terminal. A wrapped `u32` id still in flight is skipped, never reused.
  - **The async path reuses the synchronous fate rules rather than copying them.** A lost request is classified by `FateClassifier::classifyLoss` (a lost write is `Indeterminate`), and the one re-issue the policy allows for a read runs through `dispatchAutocommit` counted as its second attempt.
  - **Three deliberate limits, recorded:**
    - **Inside an open transaction an async statement settles at once.** It is part of the transaction's order, and leaving it in flight would let the next statement overtake it in the client's view.
    - **An async statement does not update `lastInsertId()`.** "The most recent statement" has no meaning with several in flight.
    - **An open stream stays exclusive on its session.** A buffered statement on the same `tx_id` would queue in the engine behind a stream stalled on its credit window, which the client would not replenish while awaiting that statement: a deadlock. Lifting this needs per-stream window handling in the router.
  - **The adversarial review found 9 defects, and the worst was a fate violation measured live.** A malformed frame on ANOTHER request id made the engine send its `request_id=0` fatal. The first version stamped that fatal's NonRetryable payload onto a pending `INSERT`, and the row had been applied in 2 of 3 runs. It also closed the socket, discarding the terminals the engine was still draining. The review also found:
    - the two rules that stop the async path re-sending a lost write had no test: classifying the loss as "not sent", or retrying regardless of fate, left every test green;
    - a raw `TypeError` escaping on a read of a closed socket, after a fatal at the in-flight limit and after `close()`;
    - a request waiting for a slot reported as sent;
    - `lastInsertId()` touched on three async paths;
    - an unbounded inbox;
    - errors thrown at the call instead of at `await`;
    - consumer lock files missing the new `files` autoload, so `Ferro\await` was undefined in the Doctrine and Laravel tiers.

    All are fixed. The review's mutations and the fixes' own mutations were run against the main tree: 14, all killed.
  - **Not established:** Fiber suspension (D1b: `Ferro\Loop` plus a Revolt adapter, so an `await` inside a Fiber suspends instead of blocking), the `FIBERS` client feature bit (still not advertised), and §16's fan-out target measured on the D17 reference runner.

  **(ck) M3-D1b — Fiber suspension: `Ferro\Loop` (2026-10-06).** `Ferro\Loop::run(array $tasks)` runs each task as a Fiber.
  - **How a Fiber waits.** When a task awaits a `Future` returned by an `…Async` method and its terminal has not arrived, the Fiber suspends with a `Waiter` (session + request id), and the loop runs the others.
  - **How the loop reads.** It reads ONE frame at a time and files each under its `request_id`, then resumes every Fiber whose terminal is there. With one session the read blocks, exactly the synchronous behaviour, so the transport's read timeout still bounds a silent peer. With several sessions it `stream_select`s across their sockets, and after 30 empty one-second selects it falls back to a blocking read so that timeout still applies.
  - **Measured live:** four tasks each submitting and awaiting a 0.5 s sleep finish in about one sleep, on one session and across two. That is only possible if an await suspends, because each task submits only when its Fiber runs. A unit test pins the same property without timing: two tasks whose replies arrive in reverse order finish in reverse order, and both requests are written before anything is read.
  - **Each session keeps its own deadline**, its transport's read timeout, so a silent peer fails as soon as it would synchronously, whatever other sessions do.
  - **What does NOT suspend, by design:**
    - a synchronous call inside a task, and an await on a Future that settled at once (inside a transaction), block the whole loop until they finish. That stays correct, because other Fibers' frames are kept for them;
    - a Fiber the loop did not start never suspends into it, so another scheduler's Fibers are left alone;
    - a task that suspends its Fiber with anything else is refused with a `LogicException`, never resumed blindly;
    - the loop does not nest.
  - **The adversarial review found 6 defects; the worst was measured live.** Fiber A `begin()`s on a Connection and suspends; Fiber B's `execAsync` on the same Connection settled INSIDE A's transaction and reported success, and A's `rollBack()` then deleted B's acknowledged row. Fixed and pinned:
    - **an imperative transaction belongs to the Fiber that began it.** Another Fiber's statement, `begin`, `commit` or `rollBack` on that Connection is refused with `InvalidTransactionStateException`, never routed into it. The one exception is `rollBack()` once the owner Fiber has terminated, the only way to release a transaction its owner abandoned;
    - one global idle counter let a busy session keep a silent one waiting forever: each session now has its own deadline;
    - an undecodable frame escaped `Loop::run` as a raw `CodecException`: it is now a desync, so the session is poisoned and the fault is a `ProtocolException` at each await;
    - a Fiber using a Connection while another Fiber's stream is open failed with `ProtocolException`: it now waits for the stream to close. The stream's own Fiber, the main program and Fibers the loop did not start still get the refusal, because for them the wait would never end;
    - the docblock example threw;
    - three mechanisms had no test.
  - The HELLO now advertises the `FIBERS` client feature. It is informational: the engine has always served requests concurrently and does not read it.
  - **Not established:**
