    - the Revolt/AMPHP adapter. An `await` inside a Revolt-managed Fiber still blocks: correct, but serial;
    - §16's fan-out target on the D17 runner;
    - **a known limit both D1 slices inherit: the client's socket read timeout doubles as every request's deadline.** `Ferro::connect`'s default is 5 s, so a query slower than that poisons the session and fails every request in flight on it, not just the slow one.

    All three are D1c. The remedy for the third is a per-request deadline that CANCELs only its own request, with the socket timeout reduced to a liveness bound.

  **(cl) M3-D2a — the manifest is built: `ferro-manifest` + the `ferro` CLI + `ferro-queries` (2026-10-06).**
  - **`ferro-manifest`** is a library shared by the CLI and (from D2d) `ferrod`, so both compute one hash from one canonical form. The canonical bytes are pinned by a test, because a change to them would move every deployed pair's hash at once.
  - **The CLI binary is `ferro` (crate `ferro-cli`, D10).**
    - `ferro manifest --sql <dir> [--php-queries <json>] --out <file>` collects queries from `.sql` front-matter and from the JSON `vendor/bin/ferro-queries` prints for `#[FerroQuery]` attributes, then validates and writes them.
    - `ferro manifest-hash <file>` recomputes the hash.
    - Exit codes: 1 on any invalid input (every problem listed, nothing written), 2 on misuse.
    - Arguments are parsed by hand rather than adding a CLI dependency.
  - **Attributes are read by PHP, not Rust.** PHP's own tokenizer (`PhpToken`) is the only robust PHP parser available without a dependency. The extractor accepts only NAMED, LITERAL arguments: single-quoted strings, double-quoted strings and heredocs with no backslash or interpolation, nowdocs (dedented as PHP does), and `true`/`false`/`null`. A concatenation, constant or expression is refused with its location, because a query the manifest gets wrong runs the wrong SQL under a trusted id.
  - **Not established (D2b–D2e):**
    - checking queries against a real schema (`ferro check`, `ferro schema sync`);
    - code generation (`ferro gen`);
    - the engine loading the manifest, comparing the hash at HELLO and running `query_id` (DONE at D2d, (cm));
    - the client's idempotent retry licence.
  - **The adversarial review confirmed 20 findings, three HIGH, all reproduced and fixed** — and all three were the extractor recording something other than what PHP runs, which is exactly the failure this slice exists to prevent:
    - a `b'…'` binary-string prefix was read as part of the string (`'SELECT 1` recorded for `SELECT 1`);
    - the attribute's name was compared as text, so `#[ferroquery(…)]`, an alias, a group import or `#[Attribute\FerroQuery(…)]` after `use Ferro\Attribute` were silently MISSED, while a short `FerroQuery` that PHP resolves to another class was taken. Names are now resolved against `namespace` and `use` as PHP does, case-insensitively, and only a top-level attribute counts (`#[CoversClass(FerroQuery::class)]` no longer breaks a tests-directory scan). The stated "aliases are not resolved" limit is gone;
    - a nowdoc's dedent rewrote line terminators (CRLF → LF, `\f` → newline, and byte 0x85 inside a UTF-8 character split the string).
    Also fixed: a blank line inside the front-matter silently ended it (the query then ran on the DEFAULT pool); a second `-- ferro:` block was swallowed into the first query's SQL (a `DELETE` inside a query declared `readonly`); a manifest's repeated query id kept the LAST entry without a word, and a misspelt field was ignored; `fs::write` truncated a good manifest before failing; a symlink cycle hung the `.sql` walk; a non-UTF-8 argument panicked. **The extractor is now tested against PHP itself:** each case is `eval`-compiled and its attributes instantiated through Reflection, so the expected value is PHP's, not the author's belief about PHP; 17 of those cases fail against the reviewed version. The hash is now pinned as a known answer computed outside the crate, including a vector with non-ASCII and control characters, since a PHP client recomputing it (D2e) must reproduce serde_json's escaping exactly. **Remaining leniency, stated:** an attribute's TARGET is not checked, so a declaration on a function or parameter, which PHP refuses at instantiation, is still extracted.

  **(cm) M3-D2d — the engine loads the manifest, checks it at HELLO, and runs EXEC by `query_id` (2026-10-06).**
  - **Startup.** `FERRO_MANIFEST=<path>` is read once by `main` through `ferro-manifest` — the parser and validation the CLI uses — and the hash is recomputed, never read from the file. A manifest that does not load, or that names a pool the daemon is not configured with, stops startup: a deploy that cannot run its declared queries fails at start, not on the first request for one. A blank value reads as unset.
  - **HELLO.** A `manifest_hash` must be 64 lowercase hex characters and equal the engine's; otherwise the handshake is refused session-fatal `Unsupported`, also when the engine has no manifest. A client that sends none is admitted. `HELLO_ACK` sets `feature_engine::MANIFEST` when one is loaded.
  - **EXEC by `query_id`** runs the manifest's SQL, exactly as written, through the same buffered, streamed and tx-scoped paths as inline SQL (`ferrod::manifest::resolve`). The request must AGREE with the declaration rather than either side silently winning: its `readonly` (the engine trusts it for the fate it reports, and a SQLite checkout enforces it), and the pool it actually RUNS on — the request's `pool` for autocommit, the transaction's pinned pool for a tx-scoped request, whose `pool` field is otherwise ignored. A mismatch, an unknown id and an engine with no manifest are `Unsupported`; `sql` and `query_id` together is `Protocol`. A client-supplied id is echoed only once it is a well-formed id.
  - **Only a session that AGREED on the manifest at HELLO may run a `query_id`.** `readonly` and the pool travel with every request; `idempotent` — the client's licence to re-send a lost write (§9.2) — does not, so the hash is the only proof the client read the engine's value of it. The per-session handler is now built AFTER the handshake (`HandlerFactory` takes a `SessionInfo`), which is what lets the SQL service know.
  - **The adversarial review confirmed 9 findings, all fixed, and 5 of its 13 mutations survived the first version — all killed now.** The one that mattered: a session that sent NO hash could run query ids, so a stale client could apply its own `idempotent` to the engine's SQL and, once D2e lands, re-send a write the engine's manifest says must not be — the existing tests did exactly that. Also: the OTLP span fingerprinted a query declared for a MySQL pool with the request pool's PostgreSQL rules, exporting a `"…"` literal (it now fingerprints only a query declared for the run pool); a multi-megabyte malformed `manifest_hash` was echoed into a refusal larger than the frame cap, so the client saw a bare EOF (the hash is now shape-checked and a malformed value never echoed — the same bound now applies to `type_registry_hash`); the streamed tests asserted only that something streamed, so running other SQL passed them, and the tx-scoped stream and tx-scoped `readonly` check had no test; the span's query-id redaction had none either. 17 mutations were then re-run in the main tree — the reviewer's 13 plus four against the fixes — and all are killed; one only after a test change, because the hash shape check is two halves and every malformed value the first test tried was non-hex, so dropping the LENGTH half passed it (wrong-length lowercase hex is now tried). Gates: `manifest_it` 8 tests, a span unit test, workspace green on the local backends apart from four MySQL-family tests that need MariaDB ≥ 11 (the local server is 10.11; CI runs 11.8).
  - **Not established:** the client side (D2e: `…ById` methods, loading the manifest, sending its hash, and the idempotent retry licence with its chaos test); manifest-only mode (M4, E2).

  **(cn) M6-F0 and M7-G0 — Ferro HTTP and Ferro Queue get their SPEC sections, and D19–D22 are recorded (2026-10-06).** D16 required each engine to have its own SPEC section and service id, with the product-vision §3 admission test passed in writing, before any code. Both now do. The normative text lives in `docs/spec/23-http.md` (§23) and `docs/spec/24-queue.md` (§24), with stubs at the end of this file, because each is longer than any section here.
  - **What is allocated, and where.** `HTTP = 6` with the engine feature bit `0x08`; `QUEUE = 7` with no feature bit; codes `UpstreamUnavailable` `0x1007` and `RateLimited` `0x1008` (Retryable), `TlsRefused` `0x300D`, `ResponseIncomplete` `0x300E`, `LeaseLost` `0x300F` and `PoolMismatch` `0x3010` (NonRetryable); the `[http.causes]` vocabulary and `queue_wait_grace_ms = 1000`. They are allocated here and reserved in `/proto` as comments only (no keys, so no generated constant and no registry-hash change); the keys, golden vectors and both codecs land with each engine's first code slice (F2, G1). No code and no vector changed. The drafts' "whichever merges first takes the lower codes" was replaced by this fixed allocation.
  - **Both drafts were attacked by an adversarial review before adoption, and revised once.** HTTP's review journalled **26 findings** (3 HIGH, 8 MAJOR including one security finding, 9 MEDIUM, 6 LOW); Queue's journalled **21** (5 HIGH, 12 MEDIUM, 3 LOW-MEDIUM, 1 LOW), and re-verifying them found four more (R1–R4). Every finding was re-checked at its cited source before the revision relied on it. The ones that changed the design most:
    - HTTP F1: the HTTP/2 "not processed" licence the draft relied on never fires as written, and the obvious repair would have licensed a re-send of a **processed** POST. In v1 a non-idempotent request therefore never rides HTTP/2.
    - HTTP F2: the client's 5 s socket timeout is every request's deadline and poisons the session, so a slow API call would have turned a concurrent DB write `Indeterminate`. Ledger D1c is now a hard prerequisite of HTTP's client slice F8 (§23.11.0).
    - HTTP F9: idempotency by HTTP method is read/write inference under another name. Only declarations license.
    - Queue F1 and F5: swallowing `LeaseLost` could not stop a `failed_jobs` row for a job another worker runs, and engine-made phantom attempts would fail never-run jobs under Laravel's default `--tries=1`. `FerroJob::fail()` is overridden, and an undelivered reservation is unreserved.
    - Queue F4: liveness release was keyed to a session the application closes on every `DB::reconnect()`.
  - **Decided under the owner's full-freedom grant as applied 2026-10-06** (recorded in the ledger's "Owner directives and grants": on 2026-10-06 the owner redefined v1 and asked for it finished, and the freedom on engineering choices the owner granted for M2 was applied to M3 onward; none of the choices below was made by the owner personally). HTTP (§23.18): the environment-variable configuration, no `ferro.toml` first; in-process, in the crate `ferro-http` behind the cargo feature `http`, gated by `cargo deny`'s advisory check; no large-request-body path in v1 (refused before dispatch); no per-pool uid allow-lists in v1 (gap recorded in §12); an HTTP status is never an engine error; no Symfony `HttpClientInterface` in v1, so Lane S is post-v1; the acceptance bar is Lane B plus Lane C; `IDEMPOTENT_METHODS` empty by default, with product-vision §4.2 D reworded and the cost stated; no reload in v1, a 30 s HTTP drain; HTTP/1.1 by default, and non-idempotent requests never over HTTP/2 in v1. Queue (§24.17): Laravel's stock `jobs` table as the only layout; **liveness release dropped from v1**; the retry licences signed as one merged D21; **Symfony Messenger demoted out of v1** (D16's and product-vision §4.3's naming of that seam amended); `at_most_once` cut; D22 decided but **PENDING OWNER RATIFICATION** (it amends binding scope text, so no G-slice code, G1 onward, starts before the owner ratifies it); the `FerroJob::fail()` divergence accepted; PostgreSQL ≥ 12.
  - **Resolved while merging the two, because they disagreed or contradicted the tree:**
    - one D21, not two. Its wording admits protocol-defined licences, because Queue's `RESERVE` licence is keyed on the verb, which the HTTP draft's "never an inference from a method, verb or statement text" would have forbidden;
    - Queue's draft re-verified a table's shape "on `SIGHUP`", which `ferrod` does not implement (neither does it reload pools, despite §18). §18 now says so, and the shape is re-verified at the next restart;
    - Queue's client-side wait clamp was written against today's 5 s/30 s socket timeout, which D1c replaces before any Queue slice. §24.8 now states both rules, and G4 tests the one its client implements;
    - with liveness release dropped, the `liveness` field stays in the frozen `RESERVE` shape (so a post-v1 release needs no version bump), and a v1 engine refuses `true`.
  - **Amended in the same change:** §3, §5, §9.2, §11, §12, §13, §17, §18, §19.1, §19.3, §20.1 and §20.3 (each an *[Amended …]* note), D16's row, product-vision §2, §4.2, §4.3 and its decision log (P14), and the ledger's Phase F and G rows. **Not changed:** `/proto`'s keys (it gains reservation comments only), PROTOCOL.md, any code, and `CLAUDE.md` — whose copy of charter rule 6 still lacks D22's sentence.
  - **Not established, and owed by named slices:** every premise in §23.19 and §24.17. On HTTP's critical path (spike F1a, before F2): that `sent` is exact at the HTTP/1.1 plaintext write (P1), that the failure causes are distinguishable through `hyper` (P14), the head limits (P15), `try_send_request` (P19), bounded backpressure (P7), the dependency licences (P3, P16), and refusal during drain (P17); `PING` while busy (P18) is D1c's to measure. Off it (F1b): the HTTP/2 licence with its negative control (P2), resumption (P5), curl's re-send (P4), header case (P6) and curl's head limits (P20). On Queue's: the `MATERIALIZED` CTE's affected ≤ LIMIT under concurrency (G1), the dedup sequences (G4, G6), R4 in-tree (G3/G5), SIGALRM reentrancy and fake time (G5), the index plan (G3), MariaDB's `SKIP LOCKED` inside the engine transaction (G6). **Admission point A is argued for both engines and measured for neither** (HTTP's F11, Queue's G3).
  - **The adoption itself was adversarially reviewed (14 findings, R1–R14) and every finding was fixed in the same branch:**
    - PROTOCOL.md section collision: Queue's messages take §13 (§12 is HTTP's);
    - "an idempotent request is `Retryable` after a post-send failure" was false of §23.7.1's own table. The true statement is "never `Indeterminate`": a link-level failure is `Retryable`, and a timeout or cancel follows §9.2's read rule;
    - D21's revisit trigger would already have fired on HTTP's `Idempotency-Key` licence, and was reworded;
    - the grant was cited but not recorded: it is now in the ledger, and D22 is pending owner ratification;
    - §24.16 C7 misquoted the adopted §9.2 sentence, and now quotes it verbatim;
    - D22's charter sentence did not cover rule 6's "never SQL generation" for the `ferro` queue driver;
    - the numbers were reserved only in prose, and "as `ADMIN = 5` once did" was false: they are now comments in `/proto`;
    - P18 had two owners (it is D1c's);
    - §23.11.0's deadline formula was undefined for an HTTP request without `timeout_ms` and for a waiting `RESERVE`;
    - I4 said each request executes at most once, which a parked `RESERVE`'s polls contradict;
    - manifest-only pools now accept every queue verb, and the cost was unstated;
    - "`ferro_errors_total` stays SQL-only" was false of the tree;
    - the HTTP drain cap and `serve`'s hard abort fired at the same instant, and no drain signal reaches the sessions (both chassis changes are now listed);
    - product-vision §4.2 D contradicted itself on refusals made before sending.

  **(co) M3-D2e — the client runs checked queries by id, and §9.2's idempotent licence is live (2026-10-06).**
  - **`Ferro\Manifest`** loads the `manifest.json` `ferro manifest` writes and RECOMPUTES its hash from the canonical bytes, reproducing serde_json's escaping exactly (raw UTF-8, `/` and U+2028/9 unescaped, `\u00xx` control characters) — pinned against `ferro-manifest`'s two known answers, both computed with `sha256sum` outside either codebase. A file whose recorded `hash` is not the hash of its queries was edited after it was written (flipping `idempotent` is the edit that matters) and is refused. Unknown fields, a version other than 1, an empty manifest and a malformed id are refused too.
  - **`Ferro::connect(manifest: …)`** sends the hash at HELLO, so a stale client is refused at connect (D2d), and every reconnect sends it again.
  - **`execById` / `queryById` / `queryOneById` / `scalarById`** send `query_id` (with `sql` nil) on the pool and with the `readonly` the manifest declares; an unknown id, a missing manifest, or — inside a transaction — a query declared for another pool is refused before anything is sent (`ManifestException`, no fate). Inline SQL is untouched.
  - **The licence.** A query run by id and declared `idempotent: true` whose fate is `Indeterminate` — a lost reply after the send, or an engine-declared `WriteUnconfirmed` — is re-sent within the policy's `maxAttempts`, after a reconnect when the session died. It is the only path that ever re-sends a write, and it is a DECLARATION, not a policy: `RetryPolicy::retryIdempotentWrites` (default true; `false` in `RetryPolicy::none()`, which the drop-in tiers use) can only switch it off. An inline write with identical text is never licensed. The declaration acted on is the engine's own, because the engine runs a query by id only on a session whose HELLO carried its manifest hash.
  - **Measured live** (`ManifestLiveTest`, PostgreSQL through a real `ferrod` with `FERRO_MANIFEST`): with a 0.5 s client read deadline and a statement whose FIRST execution sleeps 1 s (gated on a non-transactional sequence, which also counts how often the engine ran it), a declared-idempotent upsert SUCCEEDS after exactly two sends and its row exists once; the identical loss on an undeclared write surfaces `IndeterminateException` after exactly one send; with the licence switched off the upsert surfaces too. The loss is a client-side link cut (the read deadline closes the socket), the same fate class as a daemon restart — the request was written and its answer never came; a mid-statement daemon KILL is not what this test does.
  - **Queries by id inside transactions.** An imperative transaction (`begin()`) carries `tx_id` on a by-id statement; a closure transaction runs one on its `TxHandle` (`$tx->execById(…)`), and the `Connection` REFUSES `…ById` while a closure transaction is running — it would run outside the transaction and survive its rollback. Nothing in a transaction is ever re-sent: a lost in-transaction statement is the transaction's fate.
  - **The adversarial review confirmed 5 findings; all fixed.** A by-id write inside `transaction()` ran OUTSIDE the transaction — measured live, the closure threw, the transaction rolled back, and the row survived — with the licence armed besides. A licensed re-send whose reconnect failed (the daemon still down, or restarted with another manifest) surfaced the DIAL's error, which callers read as "nothing was sent", instead of the write's `Indeterminate`; the licence made the reported fate less truthful than no licence. PHP accepted files the engine refuses (an id with a trailing newline — `$` matches before one — untrimmed SQL, any pool string, a non-string `source`) and refused a `"hash": null` the engine accepts; all aligned now, with the one remaining difference stated in `Manifest`'s docblock (a repeated JSON key, harmless because the hash decides admission). The live test asserted only the send COUNT, so "applied once" was proven by nothing: it now counts applications (`hits`), and reads 1. 29 mutations were re-run in the main tree — the reviewer's 22 and seven against the fixes — and all are killed, three of them only by the live lane.
  - **Not established:** async variants of the by-id methods (`…ByIdAsync`) and streaming by id from PHP (the engine serves both); `ferro check`/`ferro gen` (D2b, D2c) — until they land the manifest's SQL is not checked against a schema.

  **(cp) M3-D1c — per-request deadlines and PING-probed liveness: a slow statement is not a dead engine (2026-10-06).** Until now the transport's read timeout (5 s in `Ferro::connect`) was every request's deadline AND closed the whole session: any statement slower than it failed — a write as `Indeterminate` — and every other request in flight on that socket failed with it. That was the hard prerequisite of Ferro HTTP set by `docs/spec/23-http.md` §23.11.0, where minute-long upstream calls are ordinary.
  - **Silence is probed, not fatal.** The transport keeps the bytes of a frame it had partly read when a read times out (`TransportException::isReadTimeout()`; the session keeps a header whose payload timed out), so the stream stays in step. The session then sends ONE liveness PING and reads on; the PONG is consumed by the router. The session closes only when a further whole read timeout passes with NOTHING read since that PING went out — any frame proves the engine alive, because the PONG may legally follow a slow request's terminal — and every pending request then fails as sent-and-lost, as before. `Ferro\Loop` selects on a silent session first and PINGs it only if nothing is waiting on its socket, without blocking the others. After a session-fatal terminal nothing can be sent, so silence then closes the session at once.
  - **Request deadlines are opt-in, and only where the engine bounds the request.** `Ferro::connect(statementTimeout: s)` sends `timeout_ms` on every BUFFERED SQL EXEC (autocommit, in-transaction and by id). The engine enforces it over the wait for a pooled connection AND the statement, one deadline for both, and answers with the §19.3 fate it computes: a dispatched autocommit write that is cancelled is `Indeterminate`, one that never got a connection is a known not-applied (`PoolTimeout`, or `ConnectionLost` when CANCELled while it waited; both Retryable), an in-transaction statement is `Retryable` with its transaction rolled back, and a read is never `Indeterminate`. The client arms a backstop at `s` + 2 s on exactly those requests, sync and async. Past it the client CANCELs that request and waits one more read timeout for the engine's terminal, which still decides the fate; only if none arrives does the session close. The read wait is shortened to the nearest deadline and restored after the read, and a write always runs under the full timeout (PHP's socket stream bounds writes with the same timeout). `Ferro\Loop` wakes for deadlines and acts on them.
  - **What gets no deadline, and why.** Transaction control (BEGIN, COMMIT, ROLLBACK, savepoints) and admin requests carry no `timeout_ms` and their handlers do not act on a CANCEL, so a backstop could only end by closing the session — a slow COMMIT that went on to commit was reported `Indeterminate`. A stream is not sent `timeout_ms` either, because the engine would bound its WHOLE life with it, including every wait for the caller to replenish its credit window. All of them are bounded by liveness only. `backup()` keeps its own `timeoutMs`.
  - **`statementTimeout` is validated in one place** (`Connection::statementTimeoutMs`, called by `Ferro::connect` before it dials and by `Connection`'s constructor): a finite number of seconds above zero whose millisecond count fits the wire's u32 (at most 4 294 967.295 s); anything under half a millisecond is sent as 1 ms. `INF` and `NAN` used to become a 1 ms timeout, and a value past the u32 made every EXEC malformed.
  - **§23.11.0 is followed with one recorded difference.** The margin is §23.11.0's 2 s. On expiry the client does not fail the request itself, as item 1 there says: it CANCELs and waits one liveness interval for the engine's terminal, because that terminal carries the exact fate (a statement that finished as the CANCEL arrived is reported as the success it was). §23.11.0 carries the matching amendment.
  - **Measured live:** a `pg_sleep(1.6)` read with a 0.5 s read timeout succeeds on the same session (previously a session-killing failure); a 0.3 s statement timeout is answered by the ENGINE's `Cancelled` in under 1 s and leaves the session usable; a SIGSTOPped `ferrod` fails a request in about two read timeouts instead of hanging; a COMMIT slowed to 2.2 s by a deferred trigger commits under a 0.3 s statement timeout and a 0.5 s read timeout; with every pooled connection pinned, a write under a 0.3 s statement timeout is a `PoolTimeout` in under 1 s; a stream consumed for longer than the statement timeout completes. Engine side, `chaos_fate_it::checkout_wait_is_bounded_by_timeout_ms_and_cancel_live` pins the checkout bound, a CANCEL during the wait (write: `ConnectionLost{Retryable}`, read: `Cancelled`), the one shared deadline, and that the write never applied. Offline, a timeout-capable fake transport and a scripted engine in a forked process pin the probe, the unanswered-probe close, a timeout in the middle of a frame, a deadline that cancels only its request, a CANCEL that goes unanswered or cannot be written, a late PONG, a silent session beside a busy one, and the backstop on the sync and Loop paths; a real Unix socket pins the transport's kept bytes and its write timeout.
  - **Cost stated:** with no statement timeout configured, a request waits as long as the engine keeps answering PINGs — as long as the statement runs, like PDO — where it used to fail at 5 s; the same holds for every COMMIT, BEGIN, savepoint and stream even when one is configured. A dead engine now takes about two read timeouts to detect, not one.
  - **The merge with M3-D2e moved its chaos test.** `ManifestLiveTest` lost a reply by a 0.5 s READ timeout closing the socket mid-statement; under this slice that statement simply succeeds. The test now loses it to a 0.5 s statement timeout, i.e. the engine-declared `WriteUnconfirmed` half of the same licence, which the client treats identically ((co)).
  - **The adversarial review confirmed 8 findings; all fixed.** The backstop was armed on requests the engine never bounds — BEGIN, COMMIT, ROLLBACK, savepoints and BACKUP carry no `timeout_ms` and their handlers ignore a CANCEL — and the engine did not race the wait for a pooled connection against `timeout_ms` or the CANCEL, so a slow COMMIT that committed was reported `Indeterminate` with the session closed, and a write that never got a connection became `Indeterminate` instead of the pool timeout the engine would have answered (F1: the backstop is now armed only on a buffered SQL EXEC, and the engine races the checkout). A deadline-shortened read wait leaked into the next write's timeout and was never restored, so a large request after a deadline read failed mid-frame and closed the session; a broken pipe after a read timeout was reported as "write timed out" (F2). `INF`/`NAN` became 1 ms and a value past the u32 rejected every EXEC (F3). A CANCEL whose write failed escaped `Ferro\Loop::run` past every task's catch, from two call sites (F4, HIGH). A stream was sent `timeout_ms`, so the engine cut off a slowly consumed one (F5). A PONG arriving after the slow request's terminal sat unread, and the Loop judged the probe by its age and closed the healthy session — a write sent just before became `Indeterminate` (F6). Nine of 21 mutations survived (F7), and the slice's own live timeout test was a false green: `SELECT pg_sleep(5)` returns a `void` column, which the engine refuses BEFORE it runs, so it "answered at the timeout" with `Unsupported`. The documentation overstated all of this and cited a §23.11.0 that did not yet exist on the branch (F8). The reviewer's 21 mutations were adapted to the fixed code and re-run with one mutation reverting each fix (12) and three against the engine change: **all 36 are killed**, M13 now across both `Ferro::connect` factories.
  - **Not established:** the Revolt/AMPHP adapter and the §16 fan-out bench (ledger D1d/D1e); a deadline for transaction control, which needs `timeout_ms` on the TX wire messages and handlers that act on a CANCEL.

  **(cq) M3-D2b — `ferro check` and `ferro schema-sync`: queries are prepared against a shadow schema (2026-10-06).**
  - **`PoolBackend::describe`** prepares a statement WITHOUT running it and reports its parameters and columns — the default refuses, so a backend that cannot describe says so. PostgreSQL reports each parameter's type and each column's §9 tag through the same `oid_to_tag` the engine uses, so a column type the engine cannot carry fails at check time instead of on the first request (`point` is refused, pinned). MySQL counts parameters (it infers no usable types at prepare) and tags columns through `column_to_tag`, including the prepared-`NULL` refusal; a `CALL` describes as columnless, because MySQL declares its columns only on execution. SQLite counts parameters and passes each column's DECLARED type through (rusqlite's `column_decltype` feature, no new crate), with no §9 tag — SQLite types values, not columns. Each backend has a live test that also proves describing a `DELETE` leaves the row in place.
  - **`ferro schema-sync`** empties a database and applies migrations in file-name order (empty files skipped; a migration that leaves a transaction open FAILS, because the transaction would roll back at exit and lose it and every later migration). The guard is the database NAME — the one thing a shadow database reliably has that a production one does not — and it is the name the **connected server reports**: `current_database()`, `DATABASE()`, or the file SQLite's `main` is actually open on. The reset is then **verified** empty, and a failure after the reset says the database WAS emptied rather than "nothing was written".
    - **PostgreSQL:** every non-system schema ENUMERATED from `pg_namespace` (a hand-kept list measurably rotted in the DBAL harness), every publication, every large object, and the database's own settings (`ALTER DATABASE … RESET ALL`; a stale `search_path` would change how `check` resolves names); `public` is recreated with PUBLIC's `USAGE`. Residue stated: roles and other databases are untouched, and `public` is owned by the CLI's role.
    - **MySQL/MariaDB:** `DROP DATABASE` + `CREATE DATABASE` with the original character set and collation — the only reset that also removes routines, events, sequences and versioned tables; it needs the database-scoped grant (testkit's `ferro_cli_shadow`).
    - **SQLite:** every table, view and trigger dropped IN the database the backend opened (foreign keys off); nothing deletes a file, so a path trick cannot point the guard and the drop at different files. Built as `ferrod` builds it (the pool's D14 `allow_dir`, a busy timeout).
  - **`ferro check`** connects once per pool, describes every query, and lists every failure (the statement's own SQL error); with `--write` it records `params` and `columns` per query, outside the hash (pinned: the hash is identical before and after, and a misspelt column field is refused on load).
  - **Secrets:** both commands read `FERRO_POOLS`/`FERRO_POOL_<NAME>_DSN` through `ferrod::config`, the daemon's own parser, and name POOLS in every message — pinned by a test whose unreachable DSN carries a password that must not appear in any output.
  - **Cost stated:** `ferro-cli` now depends on `ferrod` (for that parser) and the three backend crates, so it is no longer a small binary.
  - **MySQL expression columns are untagged.** A table column's prepared type is its executed type; an expression's is not (`SELECT ? + 1` prepares as a DOUBLE and the engine, which reads the EXECUTED metadata, sends a LONGLONG), so describe reports no tag for a column without an `org_table` rather than a wrong one.
  - **The PHP `Manifest` loader** accepts `params`/`columns` and shape-checks them; a manifest written by `ferro check --write` loads with the hash the CLI prints (cross-checked).
  - **The adversarial review is why the guard asks the server.** It confirmed 12 findings, two BLOCKERS: the first guard parsed the DSN itself, and the drivers parse differently — on PostgreSQL `…/rv_shadow?dbname=victim` (tokio-postgres applies `dbname` over the path), a `@`/`/` inside a query value, and a libpq key=value DSN; on MariaDB `…/victim/x_shadow` and `…/victim#_shadow` (mysql_async reads the first path segment and ignores the fragment) — each EMPTIED a non-shadow database with exit 0, reproduced against a throwaway victim. Also fixed: the SQLite "reset" was a file delete whose failure was ignored (and a `?` in the path pointed the delete and the open at different files); the refusal message printed DSN text including a password; the MySQL reset left routines, events, sequences and versioned tables (re-sync failed); a migration's open transaction was silently lost; the PostgreSQL reset left publications, large objects and database settings; the pool name was printed twice and the backend's reason was lost (`RUST_LOG=warn` now shows it). Both bypass shapes are pinned against the real servers in CI, along with a non-trivial reset (a publication; a procedure). 13 mutations re-run in the main tree, all killed.
  - **Not established:** `ferro gen` (D2c), which turns the recorded `columns` into DTOs; checking binds against parameter types (nothing in PHP declares a bind's type).

  **(cr) M3-D2c — `ferro gen`: DTOs and query-id constants from a checked manifest (2026-10-06).**
  - **DTOs are generated for the hydrator that will fill them, by porting its rules rather than describing them.** `ferro/client`'s `HydrationPlan` resolves a constructor parameter `$p` to the column `camelToSnake($p)` (`strtolower(preg_replace('/([a-z0-9])([A-Z])/', '$1_$2', $p))`, over bytes) if one exists, else to the column named exactly `$p`, last-wins on duplicate names. `ferro gen` ports both rules and checks every generated name against them over the query's WHOLE column list: a column gets its camelCase spelling where `camelToSnake` restores it (`created_at` → `$createdAt`; `x_y_z` → `$xY_z`, because no `_` is put back between two capitals), else its own name (`userId`, `ID`, `a__b`, `__id`, `é_x` hydrate through the fallback), and is REFUSED with an "alias it" message only when neither reaches it: `userId` beside a `user_id` column (its `camelToSnake` is the other column, which the hydrator reads first), a duplicate name, a non-identifier (`count(*)`), or a name PHP forbids as a parameter (`$this`, the auto-globals). The first version described the rule as `(?<!^)[A-Z]`, which `HydrationPlan` has never used, and so generated `x_y_z`/`__id` DTOs that threw on every row while refusing `userId`/`a__b`, which would have hydrated. The port is now pinned three ways: a table of `camelToSnake` outputs recorded from PHP itself, a test that calls the client's own `HydrationPlan::camelToSnake` (via reflection) when `php` is on PATH, and one that runs `HydrationPlan::build` over the generated names and asserts column `i` lands in parameter `i`.
  - **Types are exactly what the default policy RETURNS, not what it usually returns.** `M1ValuePolicy` hands a `TIMESTAMP`/`TIMESTAMPTZ` sentinel (`infinity`, `-infinity`, MySQL's `0000-00-00 00:00:00`) back as its canonical TEXT, so those two are `\Ferro\NaiveTimestamp|string|null` and `\DateTimeImmutable|string|null` — the first version typed them without `string`, and a PostgreSQL `infinity` row failed hydration (measured live through `ferrod`). The others: `?bool`, `?int`, `?float` (NaN and ±Infinity are floats), `?string` for TEXT and BYTES (`Ferro\Bytes` is bind-side only), `int|\Ferro\U64|null`, and `?\Ferro\{Decimal, Date, Time, Uuid, Json}` (their sentinels live inside the value object); no tag, or the NULL tag, is `mixed`; a tag the client cannot decode is refused. **Every property is nullable**: a prepare reports no nullability. All fourteen are asserted in one table, and a test fills generated DTOs through the REAL client (`ExecCodec::decodeRow` under the default policy, then `hydrateDto`) from hand-built wire cells — every tag, an all-NULL row, PostgreSQL's sentinels and MySQL's zero datetimes — when `php` is on PATH. Those rows are built by hand, not read through `ferrod`; the live read was a manual measurement and is not a committed test.
  - **Names PHP refuses are refused.** Class names are checked against PHP's reserved words for a CLASS name (verified with `php -l` on PHP 8.4, including `parent`, `exit`, `die`, `eval`, `include_once`, `insteadof`, the `end*` keywords, `__halt_compiler` and the magic constants; `enum` and `from` are legal and accepted), while reserved words are allowed as namespace segments, as PHP 8 allows them, except a namespace starting with `namespace` (a relative name). A query id whose constant would be `CLASS` (forbidden as a class constant) or `MANIFEST_HASH` (already defined) is refused, as are two ids sharing a constant.
  - **Class identity is PHP's, and file names cannot collide.** PHP class names are case-insensitive, so `App\user` and `App\User` are one class: spelled two ways, they are refused. Output is one flat directory of `<Short>.php`, so two classes with the same short name (`App\A\Row`, `App\B\Row`), compared case-insensitively, or a dto that is the `--queries-class`, are refused — the first version silently wrote one over the other. Two queries may share a dto when their column NAMES and TAGS match in order (server type names may differ: `int4` and `int8` are both `int`); the same columns in another order are refused with that reason, since the constructor follows the column order.
  - **Writing is staged, and the report is true.** Every file is written and synced beside its target before any target is replaced; a target that is not a regular file is refused first, so a failure there leaves the directory untouched and says so. A failure while renaming into place, once any target has been replaced, is reported as PARTIAL, naming the files written and not written — the first version printed "nothing was written" over a half-replaced directory. A `.ferro-gen` marker lists what the run generated, and the next run deletes the files the previous marker lists that it no longer generates — and only those, only plain files, only names `gen` could have produced.
  - **The reserved-word list is the UNION over the PHP versions the client supports (8.2–8.4).** Generated code must load on every one of them, so `__property__` (a magic constant only since PHP 8.4) is refused even though PHP 8.2 and 8.3 accept it as a class name. CI caught the first version of the `php -l` comparison asserting exact agreement, which failed on the runner's preinstalled PHP 8.3; the test now reads the running version and, for a word reserved only by a newer PHP, asserts that `php -l` accepts it while the check still refuses it. That arm was itself vacuous at first, because its cases came from the list under test, so dropping the word also dropped its case; it now names those words independently. Verified on 8.2.34, 8.3.35 and 8.4.19 locally, with the dropped-word mutation failing on all three.
  - **The PHP-backed proofs cannot go quiet in CI.** Five tests run the REAL client (its `camelToSnake`, `HydrationPlan::build`, `decodeRow`+`hydrateDto`) or `php -l`; without a `php` on PATH they print `skip:`, and the `integration` lane now installs PHP 8.4 and runs `ci/assert-no-skips.sh` over them, so a lane that stops running them fails instead of passing.
  - **`Queries`** holds one constant per query id plus `MANIFEST_HASH`, so application code names ids through constants a static analyser can check.
  - **Not established:** PHPStan stubs (§11 names them; a generic `queryById` return type keyed on the id literal needs a PHPStan extension); generation for a non-default §9.1 policy (`decimal: string` etc. would need different property types); nullability from the catalog (PostgreSQL's `attnotnull` could narrow table columns — a later refinement); PSR-4 subdirectories (output is flat, so DTOs from different namespaces need distinct short names); a committed test reading rows through `ferrod` into a generated DTO; and the PHP tests run only where `php` is on PATH (the recorded `camelToSnake` table and the type table run everywhere).

  **(cs) M3-D1e — the §16 fan-out bench, and a D17 bench workflow (2026-10-06).**
  - **`ferro-bench --scenario fanout`** times, per iteration on one connection, one synchronous `SELECT pg_backend_pid() FROM pg_sleep(0.010)`, the same statement fanned out 10 ways under `Ferro\Loop::run` (§16's "under Fibers"), and the same 10 awaited with `Ferro\await()` (plain FPM, no scheduler). A fixed server-side sleep keeps the measurement about OVERLAP: ten sequential statements would cost ten sleeps. It writes its own record, `bench/results/<UTC>-<env>-fanout.json`, with the raw samples.
  - **The verdict carries its uncertainty, because the first version's did not.** The adversarial review measured the p99 `fanout − single` on a 4-core container with a 95% interval WIDER than the 2 ms budget, saw the same code flip from MET to MISSED between runs, and found one statement through the async path alone drawing +2.6 ms at p99 — so a point estimate's MET/MISSED was noise. Each estimate now carries a deterministic 95% bootstrap interval, the budget is MET at a percentile only when the interval's UPPER bound is within it, and §16 is met only when both D17 runs meet it.
  - **The comparator is stated, and it is the conservative one.** One single call's distribution stands in for "max(single query)" since all k statements are identical; the literal max over k independent single calls is strictly larger (locally p50 12.4 ms / p99 ~35–38 ms against 11.1 / 16.4 ms for one call) and is recorded beside the verdict as context. Judging against it would turn every local p99 MISSED into a MET by more than 10 ms; choosing the stricter reading means a MET cannot be an artefact of the comparator.
  - **Overlap is proven, not inferred from timing.** Each statement returns its backend's pid and every fan-out records how many DISTINCT pids answered it; `validate()` refuses a record in which any fan-out was answered by fewer than k sessions. The first version's timing check (`p50 < k × sleep`) accepted a deliberately half-serialized fan-out (two waves of five, p50 22.6 ms); and its pool-size check read the daemon's configured CEILING through the metrics port (a near-tautology with a bind race), so it is gone.
  - **`bench.yml`** runs on demand and on a PR that changes the bench, twice on two runners, with `FERRO_BENCH_ENV=gh-ubuntu-latest`. Three review findings were about the workflow never producing an honest record: setup-php enables Xdebug on GitHub's images and Xdebug disables the JIT the harness requires (`coverage: none`); a run that could not reach Postgres SKIPPED with exit 0 and an `ignore`d empty upload — green with nothing measured (a skip on a runner now fails, and the upload refuses an empty set); and a PR checkout is GitHub's synthetic merge commit, so the manifest's `git_sha` named a commit on no branch (it now checks out the PR head). `reference` is read from the runner's own `GITHUB_ACTIONS`/`RUNNER_ENVIRONMENT`, must agree with the env label, and the trivial D12 record's tags now follow it too (it was hard-wired provisional, so a D17 trivial number could never be recorded as the reference — E8 needs it). Every record carries D17's caveat that a shared-runner p99 is an upper bound.
  - **The review's mutation round found the first version's tests weak** (8 of 26 killed; `main.rs` untested). The env/reference rules moved into the tested library, and 27 mutations re-run in the main tree are all killed — including the three that survived the first fix (p50 estimated from p90, an off-by-one in the overlap check, the interval's upper bound taken from the median).
  - **Locally (not the record):** 4-core container, PG 16: fibers p50 +0.85 ms [+0.82, +0.88] MET, p99 +3.99 ms [+0.07, +7.60] MISSED; await p50 +0.49 ms MET, p99 +3.96 ms [+0.35, +6.67] MISSED; 10 distinct backends in every fan-out.
  - **RECORDED on the D17 runner, and §16's fan-out target is MET** (`bench/results/20261006T081902Z-gh-ubuntu-latest-fanout.json`, `…081928Z…`; two runs on two runners, PostgreSQL 17, PHP 8.4.26 with the tracing JIT, release `ferrod`, measured commit `3f081e2`). Run 1 (AMD EPYC 9V45, 4 cores): fibers over single p50 +0.82 ms [+0.81, +0.83], p99 +1.30 ms [+0.89, +1.65]; await p50 +0.57 ms [+0.57, +0.58], p99 +0.77 ms [+0.37, +1.16]. Run 2 (Intel Xeon Platinum 8573C, 4 cores): fibers p50 +0.67 ms [+0.65, +0.68], p99 +0.76 ms [+0.62, +0.93]; await p50 +0.47 ms [+0.45, +0.48], p99 +0.53 ms [+0.41, +0.65]. Every 95% upper bound is within the 2 ms budget in both runs, under the conservative comparator, with 10 distinct backends in every one of the 4000 fan-outs per run. A test (`every_committed_fanout_record_validates`) re-validates every committed record against the current shape. The 4-core container's MISSED p99 above is that host's noise, not the engine's.
  - **Not established:** fan-out on MySQL or SQLite (the bench is PostgreSQL-only, like D12); any number with `ext-msgpack` (the pure packer is measured, as in D12).

  **(ct) M6-F1a — the Ferro HTTP premise spike: none of §23.19's v1-critical premises is refuted, but its adversarial review amended §23.7.1's fate table. Four premises hold only with caveats (2026-10-06).** `engine/crates/ferro-http-spike/` ships no library code (the `ferro-sqlite-spike` precedent, (ba)). It tests §23.19's F1a premises (P1, P14, P15, P19, P7 (h1), P3, P16, P17) against the real `hyper` 1.11.1, `h2` 0.4.19 and `rustls` 0.23.45 on `ring`, and against `ferrod`'s real `serve`. There are 25 tests, and every premise has a negative control. Of 22 mutations, 21 were killed and one is equivalent. The crate's README carries every verdict, measurement and mutation. P18 stays with D1c.
  - **The review found the spike's own first draft wrong in two places, and the spec in a third.**
    - **`sent` was under-reported through TLS (HIGH).** `tokio-rustls` accepts a plaintext write into rustls and then writes the records in a loop. If a later record's write fails, the plaintext call returns `Err`, although earlier records already reached the socket. Reproduced: the upstream decrypted the request head and 16 KiB of body while the plaintext tracker said `sent = false`, which would have made an applied POST Retryable. §23.7.1's "bytes buffered into rustls count as sent" was false on exactly that path. The fix is a second counter below TLS: `sent` is now plaintext accepted OR ciphertext written since dispatch, which is conservative. The repro is now a P1 control, and it asserts the plaintext half alone says "not sent", so it provably exercises the hole.
    - **P7's stall probe passed a "merely slow" read-ahead.** A stall was declared after one 300 ms quiet window, so a credit-ignoring reader at one frame per 350 ms looked like backpressure. The first draft's claim to have applied (bj)'s rule rested on a 4 ms-per-frame throttle. The probe now holds for 3 s after the stall and requires the upstream not to move at all. The slowest read-ahead it catches is one frame per ~3.3 s (measured: caught at 3.2 s, missed at 4 s), and a permanent control runs 350 ms and 2 s.
    - **The fate table had no cell for "dispatched, not sent"**, the very state P1's controls and P19's case C produce. §23.7.1 gains that phase: an I/O failure is Retryable `ConnectionLost` (`unsent_write`, `unsent_closed`), a deadline is Retryable `PoolTimeout`, and a `CANCEL` is `Cancelled`, for idempotent and non-idempotent alike. The engine reads `sent` only after it has aborted `hyper`'s spawned connection task, because that task writes independently of the response future.
    - Also fixed:
      - The read count that separates `eof_empty` from `eof_partial_head` now starts at dispatch, tested on a reused keep-alive connection. Over the connection's life, it would have classified every reused `eof_empty` as `eof_partial_head`.
      - §23.5.4 said `bytes_received` counts wire bytes. It is the plaintext count.
      - P3's control injects every forbidden crate, one at a time.
      - P14's "write vs reset" observation has a control proving the classifier can name `write`.
  - **`sent` is exact on HTTP/1.1 (P1 holds; the tracker amended, as above).**
    - No byte reaches the I/O between handshake and dispatch.
    - After arming, on a successful exchange, the plaintext count equals what the upstream received (TCP) or decrypted (TLS). On a failed TLS write it does not, which is why the ciphertext half exists.
    - The required controls report `sent = false`: dead at the first write, and reset before dispatch.
    - A third control proves the probe is not blind: on `hyper`'s HTTP/2 client it sees the preface written before any request.
    - `hyper` writes vectored on both transports. A tracker that counted only `poll_write` sees nothing, and a mutation proves it.
  - **`try_send_request` is a cross-check in one direction only (P19 holds with a caveat).** It returns the message only if the dispatcher never dequeued it. A request already serialised into `hyper`'s buffer, whose first I/O write then fails, comes back with no message and no byte sent. So *message ⇒ not sent* holds, and *no message ⇒ sent* does not (§23.7.1 amended).
  - **The "sent, no head" causes are distinguishable, but not from `hyper`'s error alone (P14 holds with caveats; §23.5.6 amended).**
    - `eof_empty` and `eof_partial_head` are the same `IncompleteMessage`. The tracker's read count since dispatch separates them, and they map to different Guzzle classes.
    - `is_parse_too_large()` is compiled only with `hyper`'s `server` feature, which D20 leaves off. **The spike's first draft called it and did not build.** The oversize/malformed split is therefore `hyper`'s `Display` text, pinned by a test.
    - A reset during the body write is seen on the read side (5 of 5 runs), so `write` and `reset` are not reliably separable. Both are the same class and the same fate, so §23.11.3 is unchanged.
    - A 101 comes back from `hyper` as an ordinary head, which the engine must intercept.
  - **The head limits are settable, but `max_buf_size` is not an exact limit (P15 holds with a caveat; §23.9.1 amended).**
    - The tests run over a scripted in-memory I/O, so chunking is fixed and the results are deterministic.
    - `max_headers` = 256 is exact at every chunking.
    - `max_buf_size` = 256 KiB is exact only for small reads. **In one read, heads up to 507 904 B were delivered.** `hyper` reserves only from a capacity below the limit, and `Vec::reserve`'s `max(2 × capacity, required)` growth keeps a delivered head strictly below 2× the limit (the review found the same maximum after a preceding 1xx). §5.2's rule therefore still holds by more than 30×.
    - F4's one-byte-over test must rest on an engine-side check of the parsed head.
    - On HTTP/2, `hyper`'s own default `max_header_list_size` is 16 KiB, not `h2`'s 16 MiB.
    - An over-limit HTTP/2 head is a library `RST_STREAM(PROTOCOL_ERROR)`, identical to a malformed block. (`h2`'s comment promising `REFUSED_STREAM` is stale.) Far over the limit, it is a connection-level `GOAWAY(ENHANCE_YOUR_CALM)`.
  - **Backpressure is bounded (P7 (h1) holds).**
    - Both sockets pin 128 KiB buffers. A credit-gated reader lets the upstream write only ~0.6 MB past the credit, for both body framings, and new credit resumes it.
    - The same probe on a read-ahead client sees 511 MiB.
    - A read-ahead throttled to ~25 MB/s is caught because it never stalls (118 MiB in 5 s). One slower than the 300 ms stall window is caught by the 3 s hold, down to one frame per ~3.3 s. That window, not the 25 MB/s one, is what (bj)'s "merely slow" rule needed.
  - **The dependency set (P3 holds with a caveat; §23.13 amended).**
    - **`tokio-rustls`'s default features select `aws_lc_rs`**, the backend D20 rejects, so `default-features = false, features = ["ring", "tls12"]` is load-bearing.
    - With it, the lock holds no `aws-lc-*`, `cmake`, `webpki-roots`, OpenSSL crate or `httpdate`.
    - `ring` rebuilt from clean with a failing `cmake` shim on `PATH` and as `$CMAKE`, and never invoked it.
    - Every licence, read from the crate manifests, is on `deny.toml`'s list, unchanged.
  - **P16 holds.** `unicode-normalization` is `MIT OR Apache-2.0`. NFKC folds U+FF0E, U+2024 and U+2025 into dots, and NFC does not, so §23.4.2 step 8 is right to name NFKC.
  - **P17 holds, and its bound is the measurement.**
    - Through the real `serve`, busy and idle sessions read and answer new frames after the drain starts. A handler that captured the `Drain` refuses with `draining`, and a service-6 frame is answered `Unsupported` today.
    - After `drain_deadline`, `serve` aborts the sessions and a request is lost. That is the case for §23.6.1's chassis change 2.
    - Chassis change 1 needs no session-layer plumbing for HTTP: `main` can hand the `Drain` to the `HandlerFactory`, which today it builds first.
    - Two of the mutations edited `ferrod`'s `serve.rs` itself, and both were killed.
  - **`cargo deny check` (0.20.2, built from crates.io at the review round) passes in full, but only after a gate fix.** The first draft deferred this check to CI's `deny` job, on the claim that the job covers "this crate's dev-dependencies included". It did not. cargo-deny's licence check skips crates reachable only through dev-dependencies unless `[licenses] include-dev = true`. Measured: with `ISC` deleted from the allow-list, the check still passed, because `ring`, `rustls-webpki` and `untrusted` are dev-only in the spike. `deny.toml` now sets `include-dev = true`, and the allow-list is unchanged. The same deletion then fails on exactly those three, and the real allow-list passes advisories, bans, licences and sources. A yanked `chacha20` (through `tokio-postgres`) is a pre-existing warning.

  **(cu) M3-D3 — the memfd large-payload path behind `MEMFD_RX` (2026-10-06).** A buffered success terminal at or above `FERRO_MEMFD_THRESHOLD_BYTES` (default 1 MiB) reaches a client that advertised `MEMFD_RX` as a sealed memfd passed with `SCM_RIGHTS`; §5.1 is amended with the scope.
  - **`/proto`.** The `OOB_FD` flag and both feature bits were allocated in M0; what was missing was the frame's payload. `OobRef [fd_index: u32, len: u64, encoding: u8]` (a `msg!` fixarray(3)) and a registry table `[oob_encoding] FRAME_PAYLOAD = 0` — a table rather than a documented byte for the reason `[outcome]` is one (charter rule 2). Registry hash moved (`b1d69ba2485a43aa`); no `protocol_version` bump, by the ADMIN precedent: no existing message changed shape, a new frame appears only after a client opts in, and the hash already refuses a skewed pair at the handshake. Golden vector `oob_ref` (an `END | OOB_FD` SQL/EXEC terminal, `len` past u16), byte-locked in both codecs. `PROTOCOL.md` §1.1.
  - **What the memfd holds** is exactly what the frame would have carried inline — the whole `Outcome::Ok` envelope — so the receiver substitutes it for the frame's payload and nothing above the frame reader changes. `fd_index` is always 0 (one fd per `OOB_FD` frame); a receiver refuses any other value, any other encoding, and a `len` above `MAX_FRAME_PAYLOAD`.
  - **Pairing an fd with its frame is the classic bug, decided by a rule rather than by position, and it was measured first.** On a `SOCK_STREAM` Unix socket a `recvmsg` returns the fd-bearing bytes TOGETHER with earlier fd-less ones — measured in PHP: `"XX"` written plainly, then `"HDR1body"` sent with an fd, arrive as ONE `recvmsg` of `"XXHDR1body"` carrying the fd — so "the fd belongs to the first frame in this read" is wrong. The engine's writer flushes every earlier frame, then sends the `OOB_FD` frame with a `sendmsg` that starts at the frame's first byte and carries its one fd, and never attaches an fd to anything else; the receiver reads every byte with `recvmsg`, queues fds FIFO, and gives the n-th `OOB_FD` frame the n-th fd. A receiver must never read that socket with plain `read(2)`, which discards the fd — pinned by a control test.
  - **Engine.** `session::oob` (memfd create + write + `F_SEAL_SHRINK|GROW|WRITE|SEAL` + rewind — the fd shares its file OFFSET with the receiver's); the supervisor decides per terminal, from a per-session threshold that is `Some` only for a `MEMFD_RX` client; the writer is now a `FramedWrite` over the socket's owned write half (a `Sink` cannot `sendmsg`), and `ControlMsg` carries the fd. `supervisor::build_terminal_frame` is still the ONE place an `END` is built (the metrics guard holds). `HELLO_ACK` sets `MEMFD` when the path is enabled. A memfd that cannot be made, and an fd the kernel refuses (`ETOOMANYREFS`, nothing sent), fall back to the same terminal inline — exactly one `END` either way. Counters `ferro_oob_payloads_total`, `ferro_oob_bytes_total`, `ferro_oob_fallbacks_total`. `nix` gained its `fs` and `uio` features; no new crate.
  - **PHP.** `Transport` reads with `socket_recvmsg` (exact lengths, so no userland buffer and `stream_select` stays truthful; `SO_RCVTIMEO` as the read deadline; `MSG_CMSG_CLOEXEC`; a truncated control message is refused, because the kernel closes the fds that did not fit; M3-D1c's two read rules hold on it too — `setReadWait` moves `SO_RCVTIMEO` with the stream timeout, since `stream_set_timeout` does not bound `recvmsg`, and a read that times out mid-frame keeps its bytes and any fd that came with them) when `ext-sockets` is loaded on Linux over a UDS — auto-detected, optional (`connectUnix(receiveFds: false)` keeps `fread`), never required (charter rule 7). `Session` advertises `MEMFD_RX` exactly then, and resolves an `OOB_FD` frame in its one frame reader: `fstat` size must equal `len`, the memfd is read from offset 0 (`stream_get_contents` on the stream `ext-sockets` returns, not `php://fd/N` — the same single copy) and closed. Any disagreement is a desync that poisons the session. `Session::oobPayloadsReceived()` and `Transport::fdsReceived()` are the diagnostics the live test asserts on.
  - **Decided here, with their costs.** (1) **Results only**, not params: an inbound `OOB_FD` stays session-fatal, so a >16 MiB bind is still the M2 chunked `LARGE_OBJECT` deferral (B6b). (2) **The ceiling is unchanged** (`MAX_FRAME_PAYLOAD`), so memfd does not let a buffered result be larger than inline — lifting it for `MEMFD_RX` clients only would be the observable difference §5.1 forbids. (3) **Every success terminal qualifies**, not only EXEC's, because the decision is made at the one terminal site; only EXEC's buffered result is ever large, and `0` as a threshold makes BEGIN and a stream's small terminal travel by memfd too (which the e2e uses). (4) **Not written directly into the memfd**: the result is encoded into the heap as before (the `stats.bytes` fixpoint needs the encoded length) and copied once; the seal-and-write runs synchronously in the supervisor task, the same order of work as the `Outcome::encode` copy it replaces. (5) **Unparseable threshold keeps the default** with a warning rather than disabling or refusing to start — the two paths are observably identical. (6) The writer is Unix-only by type; a future TCP listener (FB-8) could not reach `sendmsg`.
  - **Measured** (no throughput number is claimed — charter rule 5; §16 was not run): `ferrod`'s `oob_it` (SQLite, no server, runs in every lane) — a 2 MiB result arrives as `END | OOB_FD` with all four seals set, writes/grows/shrinks refused through a fresh writable open, identical (timing stats aside) to the same statement inline on a session without `MEMFD_RX`; three distinct large results interleaved with fifteen small requests on ONE session, read in 7-byte `recvmsg`s, each memfd's CONTENT matching its own request; the threshold, `off`, a tx-scoped statement and a stream (DATA never OOB). PHP `OobLiveTest` (real `ferrod` + PostgreSQL): the same three properties through `Connection`, asserting the path was TAKEN (fd and OOB counts), not just that the rows are right — the inline fallback would produce the same rows. With `ext-sockets` loaded the whole client live tier (88 tests, after merging M3-D1c), the Doctrine tier (43) and the Laravel tier (140) pass on the `recvmsg` path, and the client tier passes on the `fread` path (`-d disable_functions=socket_recvmsg`, which CI now runs as its own step). CI's PHP job gained `sockets`.
  - **Mutation round: 24 mutations, 23 killed.** Killed (Rust): threshold made exclusive, OOB never taken, `MEMFD_RX` ignored, fd never attached, no `F_SEAL_WRITE` (first survived the unit test — a write at EOF is refused by `F_SEAL_GROW` too; the test now overwrites inside the size), no rewind, no `MEMFD` bit, `off` read as default, uncounted send, wrong `len` in the `OobRef`. Killed (PHP): `MEMFD_RX` always advertised, OOB frame not resolved, no size check, read from the current offset, fd not closed, LIFO pairing (only the transport-level test sees it: with exact-length reads at most one fd is ever queued, so the live test cannot), `MSG_CTRUNC` ignored, fd receive never enabled, timeout misreported, any encoding / any `fd_index` accepted, a D1c read wait not applied to `SO_RCVTIMEO`, a timed-out partial read dropped. **Survived, equivalent:** removing the writer's flush before `sendmsg` — `FramedWrite::send` already flushes every frame, so nothing is ever buffered there; the flush stays as the guard a future batching writer would need.
  - **Not established:** the `ETOOMANYREFS` inline fallback has no test (the kernel skips the in-flight-fd limit for a privileged sender, and the dev container runs as root); client→engine OOB (params); §16 throughput with the path on; any client but PHP's.

### 22.3 M2 exit record (2026-10-02)

**M2's deliverables are built and on `main`. Its acceptance bars are NOT met as written.** Both halves are stated here, because a milestone record that restates a bar without its measurement, or drops the part not met, is how this project's records have gone wrong before (§22.2 (z), (bq)).

**Every suite number below was measured on the exit commit `7902867`, twice per column, with identical outcome counts and failure sets between the two runs.** Each row names where it ran. CI rows come from on-demand workflow runs:

- `orm-suite` 37067176160
- `dbal-suite` 37069674974 (4.4.4) and 37069678072 (3.10.6)
- `laravel-suite` 37069681124

The Laravel SQLite columns ran in the dev container, because the workflow has no SQLite option. All of these reproduced the previously recorded numbers exactly, with two exceptions. The ORM row is new. The Laravel SQLite alias column moved from C3-6b's 549 / 595 to 551 / 595 (two fewer errors, reproduced in both runs); C3-6b kept no junit file, so the two tests that changed are not identified. The suites run from on-demand workflows, not nightly (§20.3, amended). The per-push `ci` workflow carries the engine, client, tier and demo-app gates.

**§17's M2 bullet, item by item:**

| §17 item | State | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Eloquent tier + PDO shim | **built**, see the details below this table | §15; §22.2 (al)–(as), (bn), (bw), (bx), (cb), (cc), (ch) |
| Illuminate suite green | **NOT green as written** (below) | `docs/laravel-suite/` |

| DBAL `^3.8` bridge | **built**, see the details below this table | §14; §22.2 (by), (bz), (ca) |
| Incompatibilities doc page (§14–15) | **built.** Both tiers, every entry cited, and a CI gate that fails on a stale citation or on a resolved follow-up cited as live | `docs/known-incompatibilities.md`; §22.2 (bq) |

What "built" covers in the three summarised rows:

- **Eloquent tier + PDO shim.**
  - Four drivers: `ferro-pgsql`, `ferro-mysql`, `ferro-mariadb` and `ferro-sqlite`, plus an auto-discovered `FerroServiceProvider`.
  - The `FerroPdoShim` surface Illuminate reaches: `quote`, `lastInsertId`, `inTransaction`, `exec`, and `getAttribute(SERVER_VERSION | DRIVER_NAME)`.
  - Reconnection, and `cursor()` streaming.
  - The shim refuses `getPdo()->query()`/`prepare()`, a documented incompatibility.
- **Observability.**
  - One OTLP span per EXEC, read back from a real collector.
  - §13's Prometheus list, with three metrics named as not exportable in M2.
  - The redacted, dialect-aware slow log.
- **DBAL `^3.8` bridge.**
  - A second `driverClass` over one shared core.
  - The live suites run on 3.10.6.
  - At the 3.8.0 floor, CI runs PHPStan and the bridge's unit tests (`tests/Dbal3`), not the live suite.

**§14's acceptance.** §14 has two versions of this bar:

- **v0.1 wording:** "DBAL functional suite green on PG + MySQL + SQLite; ORM functional suite green on PG + MySQL". **It is NOT met.**
- **The bar as restated at M1 under §22.2 (z):** the curated subset, through the real `DriverManager` with driver identity asserted, "green modulo a triaged and recorded list". **The DBAL rows meet it**, because every non-pass is triaged in its results doc and none is a driver defect.

Each cell is passed / executed:

| Suite (Ferro column) | PostgreSQL 17 | MySQL 8.4 | MariaDB 11.8 | SQLite |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| doctrine/dbal 4.4.4 (curated subset) | 365 / 375 | 374 / 385 | 373 / 384 | 333 / 361 (`pdo_sqlite` control 361 / 361) |
| doctrine/dbal 3.10.6 (curated subset) | 305 / 314 | 296 / 303 | 300 / 307 | 224 / 267 (control 262 / 267) |
| doctrine/orm 3.7.3 Functional, stock config | 1571 / 1597 (`pdo_pgsql` control 1597 / 1597) | 1583 / 1594 (`pdo_mysql` control 1590 / 1594) | 1579 / 1586 (`pdo_mysql` control 1586 / 1586) | not run |

**Not every column has a recorded stock-driver control.**

- The ORM columns and the DBAL SQLite columns are measured against a control in the same workflow run. For the ORM, no test the control runs is skipped under Ferro on any family (`docs/orm-suite/2026-10-02-ci-results.md`).
- **The DBAL server-family columns (PG, MySQL, MariaDB) have no control in the workflow.** C5b compared their SKIP sets against stock drivers locally (`docs/dbal-suite/2026-10-02-c5b-dbal3-results.md`), and MySQL 8.4 has never had a DBAL control.

**The ORM non-passes.**

- The Ferro-only ones are all documented incompatibilities:
  - sub-second `TIMESTAMPTZ`, refused at fetch (§22.2 (ab));
  - §7.4's TEMP tables across statements;
  - `lastInsertId()` outside a transaction on PostgreSQL;
  - a NULL-typed select-list column on the MySQL family.
- MySQL 8.4's 4 shared failures fail identically through `pdo_mysql`, so they are not Ferro's. They are not identified by name.
- The DBAL rows are curated subsets (§22.2 (z)), not upstream's whole suites.
- SQLite has no ORM column, and the ORM suite's DBAL 3 lane is run nowhere.

**§15's acceptance:** "the `illuminate/database` integration suite green on MySQL, PG, SQLite via Ferro connections; a Laravel demo app … runs with only the config diff; Octane supported in sync mode".

**The suite is NOT green.** The quoted columns are upstream's 89 driver-agnostic files (633 cases), which are a curated subset, not upstream's whole suite.

| Driver-agnostic subset | PostgreSQL 17 | MySQL 8.4 | MariaDB 11.8 | SQLite 3.53 (dev container) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ferro driver name (`ferro-*`) | 570 / 579 | 578 / 579 | 578 / 579 | 575 / 579 |
| Stock name (opt-in alias) | 605 / 607 | 587 / 587 | 588 / 588 | 551 / 595 |
| `pdo_*` control | 605 / 607 | 587 / 587 | 588 / 588 | 586 / 588 |

The family-specific directories run as separate columns. Their base classes require the stock driver name, so they run under the alias and the control only:

| Family directory | Alias | Control | The difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL | 74 / 74 | 74 / 74 | none |
| MySQL | 123 / 126 | 126 / 126 | the deliberate `''` in `EscapeTest::testEscapeString`; two `testLastInsertIdIsPreserved` cases refused at `DB::getPdo()->query()`, a recorded incompatibility (§22.2 (cc)) |
| MariaDB | 122 / 123 | 123 / 123 | `EscapeTest::testEscapeString` only |
| SQLite | not run | not run | — |

How the Ferro-named non-passes break down:

- **On PostgreSQL, MySQL and MariaDB, every Ferro-named non-pass and every Ferro-only skip is upstream code branching on the driver name** (§22.2 (am), (ar); C1f's triage). The checks take four forms: `$this->driver === …`, `in_array($this->driver, …)`, `match ($this->driver)` and `#[RequiresDatabase(…)]`.
  - The MySQL-family workflow lists the Ferro-only skips by name (8 on MySQL, 9 on MariaDB).
  - **The PostgreSQL job has no skip comparison, so its Ferro-only skips (54 skipped against the control's 26) are not listed by name.** They are attributed from upstream source: the class-level `['pgsql','sqlsrv']` gate on `SchemaBuilderSchemaNameTest`, and `RequiresDatabase('pgsql')` cases.
- **On SQLite, the same is true of the non-passes except one real incompatibility**: `compileAlter()`'s foreign-key toggle (§22.2 (bn)). C3-6b's triage named the wrong test for another non-pass, and its results doc is corrected.
- **SQLite's 11 Ferro-only skips are name-caused**: eight `SchemaBuilderTest` table-rebuild cases, `RequiresDatabase('sqlite')`, and three `testBasicUpdateForJson` data sets, `RequiresDatabase(['sqlite','mysql','mariadb'])`. The alias column runs all 11:
  - 7 pass.
  - `testSetJournalModeOnSqlite` fails in the control too, because the pool pins WAL.
  - **3 fail through Ferro only**: `testAddForeignKeysOnSqlite`, `testDropForeignKeysOnSqlite` and `testAddAndDropPrimaryOnSqlite`. Each fails with `foreign key mismatch - "__temp__posts"` at the rebuild's copy step.
- **Those 3 are the same incompatibility as the one above, not new ones.** The rebuild relies on a `PRAGMA foreign_keys = OFF` sent as a separate statement, which does not survive to the copy on a pooled connection.
- **So that one SQLite incompatibility shows up in 4 tests, 3 of them visible only through the alias column**, which C3-6b had counted as hijack errors.
- Upstream's `Sqlite/` directory is not run.
- The alias column is the proof for every case except SQLite. On SQLite the alias is not an escape hatch, because upstream's own throwaway connections carry no `ferro_socket`.

**Demo app: met, as amended by §22.2 (ch).**

- `testkit/laravel-demo/` is one testbench application, and it exercises:
  - `database` sessions under real `web` requests;
  - login and registration through hand-written routes, not starter-kit scaffolding;
  - password-reset tokens;
  - the `database` queue, with failed jobs, retry and a batch;
  - the `database` cache with locks.
- It is a per-PR CI gate on all four families, for Ferro and the control alike.
- **Horizon has no database workload to demonstrate.** §15 was amended to say so.
- **"Only the config diff" is proven by a separate test that resolves `FerroServiceProvider` through Laravel's own `PackageManifest`.** The running demo's harness registers the connections explicitly.

**Octane in sync mode: NOT tested.** No Octane harness exists, and nothing in M2 measured it. Octane guidance is M5's (§17).

**Open, carried past M2 (none blocks a deliverable):**

- FB-8 (MEDIUM): `FERRO_ADDR` is specified and implemented nowhere.
- FB-6 (MEDIUM): `ci/local-gate.sh` has no `php/laravel` lanes, so the local gate is weaker than CI for M2's own tier.
- FB-9 (LOW): one batch shape can undercount `ferro_pin_cause_total{cause="tx"}`.
- FB-10 (LOW): the client poisons a session only on a transport failure.
- FB-11 (LOW): a connect-time `TransportException` reaches a Doctrine caller unconverted. (The FB items are in `docs/dev-loop/LEDGER.md`.)
- The Doctrine tier's PG `quote()` breaks out with `standard_conforming_strings` off (`docs/followups/2026-10-02-doctrine-quote-is-mode-dependent-on-pg.md`, OPEN).
- Through `transactional()`, an indeterminate COMMIT reaches the caller masked under DBAL's own message (`docs/followups/2026-10-02-transactional-masks-a-failed-commit.md`, OPEN, upstream behaviour).
- A database-defaulted sub-second `timestamptz` is unreadable through the DBAL driver (§22.2 (ab)).
- `getPdo()->query()`/`prepare()` are refused by the Laravel shim.
- Chunked `LARGE_OBJECT` (B6b) and multi-result-set `selectResultSets()` (C1a) are deferred `/proto` changes.
- The tracker-clean hygiene skip (B7) waits on SPEC §7.2's own condition.
- Replica lag and `ferro top` are M4.

The development loop's hourly routine was disabled at the exit (2026-10-02, after this record's two audit rounds). M3 is not started.

## 23. Ferro HTTP

Ferro HTTP is the outbound HTTP transport engine (M6, D16): a PHP worker hands `ferrod` a request addressed to an operator-declared upstream, and the engine dials, pools, originates TLS, attaches daemon-held credentials, sends the request at most once and streams the response back over the credit path, classifying each failure's transport fate on the §9.2 tree. Service id `HTTP = 6`. **Normative text: [`docs/spec/23-http.md`](docs/spec/23-http.md).** Decisions: D19 (the contract), D20 (the dependency set) and D21 (retry licences per service).

## 24. Ferro Queue

Ferro Queue is the job transport engine (M7, D16): its verbs move jobs between PHP producers and workers as rows in an operator-declared table in the application's own database — leases, fenced acknowledgement, release with a client-chosen delay, waiting on the host without holding connections, and transactional enqueue on a business write's `tx_id` — over PostgreSQL ≥ 12 first, then MySQL ≥ 8.0.1 and MariaDB ≥ 10.6. Service id `QUEUE = 7`. **Normative text: [`docs/spec/24-queue.md`](docs/spec/24-queue.md).** Decisions: D21 (retry licences per service) and D22 (the §3 / charter-rule-6 scope exception).
